--- Page 1 ---
Pather Panchali

Chapter One

At the far northern edge of the village of Nischindipur stood the small, thatched house of Harihar Ray. Harihar was a man of modest means, a simple householder who managed his family’s affairs with the meager income from a few ancestral plots of land and the annual offerings from two or three devoted students and servants.

The previous day had been Ekadashi, the sacred fasting day. Early in the morning, Harihar’s distant cousin-sister, Indir Thakrun, sat on the veranda of the house, eating her breakfast of powdered puffed rice. Harihar’s six-year-old daughter sat quietly by her side, her eyes following each handful of the snack with a pitiful intensity—from the moment Indir Thakrun lifted it from the bowl until it disappeared into her mouth. Every so often, the girl cast a hopeless glance at the steadily emptying brass bowl. She seemed on the verge of speaking a few times, but the words never quite came.

At last, after finishing the last handful, Indir Thakrun looked at the girl and said, “O ma, didn’t I save a couple for you?—Look, there they are!”

The little girl replied in a plaintive voice, “That’s all right, pisi, you eat—”

Indir Thakrun broke a ripe, large beeche-kola banana in half and handed a piece to her. The girl’s face lit up with joy—she took the gift from her pisi’s hand and began to suck on it slowly, savoring every bit with utmost attention.

From inside the house, her mother called out, “Are you sitting there again, hanging around like that? Come here, now!”

Indir Thakrun said, “Let her be, bou—she’s just sitting with me, she’s not doing anything. Let her sit—”

Still, her mother insisted in a tone of authority, “No, why should she be sitting around like that at mealtime? I don’t like such things, come here, I say—get up now—”

Frightened, the little girl got up and went inside.

--- Page 2 ---
The relationship between Indir Thakrun and Harihar was a distant one. Some kind of cousin, on the maternal uncle’s side. Harihar Ray’s ancestors’ original home was in the neighboring village, Yashra-Bishnupur. When Harihar’s father, Ramchand Ray Mahashoy, was widowed for the first time at a young age, he noticed with great vexation that his own father showed not the slightest inclination to arrange a second marriage for him. After spending about a year in awkward, embarrassed waiting, and still seeing no sign of enthusiasm from his father, Ramchand, growing desperate, was forced to employ various direct and indirect means to press his case.

In the afternoons, when there was nothing else to do, simple-hearted Ramchand would toss and turn on his bed after eating. If anyone sat nearby and asked what was wrong, Ramchand would break into a plaintive tune: who else did he have, who would look after him now—what did it matter if he had a headache—such were his laments. As a result, in this very village of Nischindipur, Ramchand’s second marriage took place, and shortly after the wedding, when his father passed away, Ramchand left Yashra-Bishnupur for good and settled here permanently.

This was in his youth—after coming to this village, under his father-in-law’s care, Ramchand began to study Sanskrit at the local tol (traditional school), and in time, became a well-regarded scholar in the region. Yet he never took up any real employment, and whether he was fit for such work at all is a matter of grave doubt.

For nine months of the year, his wife and son stayed at her father’s house. Ramchand himself spent most of his time at the dice game gatherings in the neighborhood, at Patiram Mukherjee’s place, and only showed up at his in-laws’ house at mealtimes. If anyone asked, “Panditmoshai, you have a wife and child—shouldn’t you think about their future?” Ramchand would reply, “No worries, brother. Even if they live off the grain that falls beneath Brajo Chakkotti’s rice granary, they’ll get by for two generations, laughing all the while.” After that, he would return to pondering how best to pair the dice in chaak-ka and panjuri to break up the house.

--- Page 3 ---
Here is your requested translation, following your instructions:

---

Braj Chakraborty’s faith in the daily abundance of his rice granary was so recklessly misplaced that, after his father-in-law’s death, it did not take Ramchand long to realize the truth. In this village, he had neither land nor much in the way of ready cash. A handful of disciples and attendants had gathered around him here and there, and with their help he somehow managed to keep the household running and raise his son. Before him, a cousin-brother of his had married into this very family. They too had lived here. Ramchand received much help from them as well. The cousin’s son, Neelmoni Ray, worked in the commissioner’s office, but his job kept him posted in distant places, so in the end he left this home, taking his elderly mother with him to his place of work. Now, there is no one left in their homestead.

It is said that Indir Thakrun had once been married to a renowned kulin (high-caste) from the east. After the wedding, her husband would rarely set foot in this village. He would spend a night or two, collect his travel expenses and the honor due to his kulin status, make a mark in his ledger, and then, accompanied by his porter, set off for the next in line among his many fathers-in-law. Small wonder, then, that Indir Thakrun could never bring herself to think fondly of her husband. After her parents died, she survived on the charity of her brother, but fate was cruel—he too died young. Soon after, Harihar’s father, Ramchand, built a house on this very plot, and from that time Indir Thakrun first entered this household. But all that is not of today.

Since then, many years have passed. In the pond of the conch-bangle makers, generation after generation of water-lilies have bloomed and faded away. In the empty fields of the Chakrabortys, Seetanath Mukherjee planted a new orchard, and now those trees too are growing old. On some homesteads, new families have settled; others have fallen silent, deserted. How many Golok Chakrabortys, how many Braj Chakrabortys have died and vanished without a trace? The restless, crystal-clear current of the Ichhamati river, racing alongside the endless flow of time, has swept away—like bits of straw, like foam on the waves—so many Johnsons and Thompsons from the village’s old indigo house, so many Majumdars—who knows where they have drifted now?

--- Page 4 ---
Only Indir Thakrun is still alive. She is no longer the slender, smiling young woman of 1240 (Bengali calendar); now she is a seventy-five-year-old crone, her cheeks sunken, her back slightly bent so that her body leans forward, distant things no longer clear to her eyes. Raising her hand, as if to shield her gaze from the harsh sunlight, she calls out, “Who’s there? Nabin? Behari? No, it’s you, Raju…”

How many changes have unfolded before Indir Thakrun’s eyes on this very homestead! That plot of land belonging to Braja Chakraborty, now overgrown with jungle—on the night of Kojagari Lakshmi Purnima, the whole village would gather there for a feast. In the grand Chandimandap, what lively games of dice would take place, morning and evening! Was there ever such a bamboo grove back then? On the day of Poush Parbon, a whole maund of rice would be pounded in that dheki-shala (rice-pounding shed) for making Poush pitha (winter rice cakes)—if she closes her eyes, Indir Thakrun can still see it all! The middle daughter-in-law of the Ray family would come with her attendants to pound rice, the dheki thudding rhythmically, her golden bangles flashing on her crimson-painted hands as she moved forward and back, her beauty and bearing like Jagaddhatri herself. When Indir Thakrun was newly widowed, every Dwadashi morning, that lady would prepare breakfast with her own hands and come to feed her. Where have they all gone? Not a soul from those days remains, no one with whom she can share a word of joy or sorrow.

Then Ramchand, her protector in this household, passed away. His son Harihar was just a boy then—leaping and playing along the path to the ghat, breaking his arm one day after falling from the Mukherjees’ tamarind tree while trying to pluck a half-ripe fruit, bedridden for months. He was married off with great pomp at a young age—after his father’s death, he left his ten-year-old bride at her parents’ house and disappeared from the village. For eight or ten years there was hardly any news—now and then a letter, sometimes a money order for two or five rupees in the old woman’s name. How many hardships she endured, how many days she went hungry, peering anxiously at neighbors’ doors, just to keep this house together.

--- Page 5 ---
After many years, Harihar has now, after six or seven years, settled down and built a household; he has a daughter—she too is nearly six years old now. The old woman had thought that at last, the household of childhood had been restored. In her narrow life, she desired no other happiness, nor was she capable of imagining any other kind of joy or sorrow—if only the old, familiar path of her lifelong habits could turn back upon itself, that would be enough for her; to her, that alone was the ultimate tale of happiness.

She cannot bear to let Harihar’s little daughter out of her sight for even a moment—she herself once had a daughter, her name was Bishweshwari. She was married young, and soon after her marriage, she died. In Harihar’s daughter, it is as if Bishweshwari has returned to her orphaned mother’s lap from the land beyond death, after forty years. The dormant, extinguished motherhood of forty years awakens in an instant—in the girl’s anxious, bashful gestures, in the innocent laughter of her eyes—aroused with a sudden, eager interest, with the desperate hunger of a life nearing its end.

But what she had hoped for did not come to pass. Though Harihar’s wife is strikingly beautiful, what of it? She is terribly quarrelsome; she cannot stand the old woman, cannot bear the sight of her. Who knows where she is from, what her background is, what relation she truly has to them—she just sits around, wasting food.

Over every little thing, she starts arguments with the old woman twice a day. After a long bout of quarreling, the old woman would sling her own brass water pot under her arm, hang a small cloth bundle from her right hand, and say, “I’m leaving, new bou (daughter-in-law), if I ever set foot in this house again, then—” Leaving the house, the old woman would spend the whole day in the bamboo grove, nursing her wounded heart. Toward evening, Harihar’s little daughter would find her, tug at her sari’s edge, and begin to plead—“Get up, pitima (grandmother), I’ll tell Ma not to scold you, come pitima.” Holding the child’s hand, the old woman would return home in the gathering dusk. Sarbojaya would turn her face away and say, “There she comes. Where else would she go! Is there any other hearth for her but this one?... All her spirit is spent here, every last bit!”

This had started within a year of their coming home—has happened many times, and still happens, now and then, even now.

--- Page 6 ---
On the eastern homestead of Harihar, the thatched hut has lain in disrepair for many days. In this hut lives the old woman. On a bamboo rack hang two dirty, torn saris. The torn edges are knotted together. These days, the old woman can no longer thread a needle, so sewing is beyond her; when the tears grow too large, she simply ties them in knots. On one side lies a tattered mat and a few ragged quilts. In a small bundle, an entire kingdom of torn cloth is bound together. It seems these have been carefully hoarded for years as materials for quilting, though never once have they been needed; and now, even if they were, her eyes no longer hold the sharpness for such work. Yet she keeps them with utmost care, and when the sun shines after the Bhadra rains, the old woman unties the bundle and lays the scraps out in the courtyard to dry in the sun.

Inside her cane chest is another bundle: a few torn red-bordered saris—these belonged to her daughter Bishweshwari—a brass lota (water pot) with a lid, a clay bowl, and a couple of earthen cups. The brass lota is filled with puffed rice, which, at night, she pounds in a mortar and pestle and eats from time to time. In the earthen cups, one holds a little oil, another a pinch of salt, another a scant bit of date palm jaggery. Since Sarbojaya does not always give her these things when asked, the old woman secretly brings them from the household and stores them away in the wedding cane chest.

Sarbojaya herself rarely comes to this hut, only once in a great while. But in the evenings, her daughter sits on the torn-quilt-and-leaf bedding spread on the hut’s veranda, listening for a long time, utterly absorbed, as pishima (father’s elder sister) tells her fairy tales. After a while, when one story or another has been told, the little girl says, “Pishi, tell me the story of the dacoits!”—the tale of how, fifty years ago, dacoits had robbed the only well-off house in the village. Though it has been told countless times before, every few days it must be repeated; the little one will not let it go. After that, she listens to pishima recite rhymes. In her youth, Indir Thakrun had memorized many rhymes. In those days, reciting these by heart to her friends at the riverbank or along the paths, Indir Thakrun had earned much praise. Since then, she has not had such a patient listener for many years, and fearing her memory might rust, she now recites all the rhymes she knows to her little grand-niece every evening, keeping them sharp.

She chants them out, drawing out the words—

“O Lalite, O Champak bud, listen to a tale,

A thief has entered Radha’s house—”

--- Page 7 ---
Here is your translation, following your instructions to preserve the lyrical, evocative style, the sense of childhood wonder, and the cultural context:

---

Having said this much, she looks at her niece with a laughing face and eyes full of anticipation. Khuki, brimming with enthusiasm, declares—“Churōbāndhā ek—minse.”—She puts an odd emphasis on the syllable “mi,” tilting her small head forward in rhythm, as if to keep time, and finishes pronouncing the word. Khuki finds it all terribly amusing.

Her pishi, in her attempts to trick her niece, recites all sorts of rhymes and then leaves a gap for Khuki to fill in—ones perhaps not spoken for ten or fifteen days—but Khuki remembers them all exactly; it is no easy task to fool her.

After a while, when her mother calls her to eat, she gets up and goes.

---

**Pather Panchali—Chapter Two**

Harihar Ray’s ancestral home was in Yashra-Bishnupur. In the old days, the wealthy Chowdhurys of Yashra-Bishnupur, after granting rent-free land, had settled a handful of Brahmin families in the village. Among them was Harihar’s forebear, Bishnuram Ray.

British rule had not yet taken deep root in the land. All the roads and paths were fraught with peril, teeming with thugs, footpads, river pirates, and the like. These bands of dacoits were often drawn from the Goala, Bagdi, and Bauri castes. They were immensely strong—expert in wielding the lathi and the sorki. In many a secluded corner of the countryside, the ruins of temples dedicated to Dakait-Kali, built by these very bandits, still remain. By day, they would roam about disguised as respectable folk; by night, after offering worship to Kali, they would set out to plunder the homes of householders in distant villages. In those days, even many prosperous householders amassed their wealth through dacoity. That the foundation of many a zamindar’s fortune, and of well-to-do families in Bengal, lies in this ancestral hoard of plundered gold and jewels—those who know the tales of old Bengal know this as well.

--- Page 8 ---
The son of Bishnuram Ray, Biru Ray, was infamous in such a way. Under him served a band of salaried thugs. To the north of the village of Nischindipur, the dirt road that comes from Chuadanga, passes through Nawabganj, and continues on to Taki—beside that road, in the midst of the vast, horizon-spanning golden fields of Sonadanga, by the edge of an old, large pond called Thakurjhi Pukur, was the haunt of these thugs. Beneath the sprawling banyan tree by the pond, they would lie in wait, hidden, and pounce upon unsuspecting travelers, beating them and robbing them of everything they possessed.

The methods of these thugs were of a peculiar kind. Striking the traveler on the head with a stick, they would first kill him, and only then search for money—often, after the deed was done, it would turn out that the murdered man did not have even a quarter-anna on him. Stuffing the corpse into the pond, the thugs would return, with innocent faces, to the shade of the tree by the pond, hoping that the spoils from the next victim would make up for this wasted effort.

That great banyan tree still stands today, in the midst of that vast field to the north of the village, and the low-lying land by the roadside is still called Thakurjhi Pukur. The pond bears no special mark now, fourteen annas of it have silted up—when the farmers plough the land for paddy, even today, sometimes a human skull turns up from that soft earth.

It is said that an old Brahmin from the east, accompanied by his young son, was returning to his homeland beyond Taki-Sripura from the region of Kaliganj. It was the end of the month of Kartik. The Brahmin had gone abroad to collect money for his daughter’s wedding, and was carrying some money and belongings with him. After cooking and eating at the roadside inn in the market of Haridaspur, they set out again in the early afternoon, intending to spend the night at the inn in Nawabganj bazaar, five krosh ahead. They were not unaware of the dangers of the road, but how mistaken their calculations proved to be—the short days of Kartik, and long before reaching Nawabganj bazaar, they saw the sun sinking over the Sonadanga fields and began to walk quickly. But as soon as they reached the edge of Thakurjhi Pukur, they fell into the hands of the thugs.

--- Page 9 ---
As soon as the dacoits landed a blow with their stick on the first Brahmin’s head, he, terrified for his life, began to scream and fled the road, running out towards the fields, his son chasing after him. But how long can an old man and a boy—one elderly, the other a child—keep up a race with a band of hardened thieves? In no time, the dacoits caught up, encircled their prey, and trapped them. Helpless, the Brahmin supposedly pleaded that they might kill him if they must, but begged for his son’s life—the only son of his lineage, the one to perform the ancestral rites, the pinda-daan and all that. As fate would have it, Biru Ray himself was said to be among the gang that day. Recognizing him as a Brahmin, the terrified old man fell at Biru Ray’s feet, imploring him again and again to at least spare his son’s life. But the simple Brahmin did not understand that no one else would be troubled by the threat to his family’s pinda-daan; rather, if they let them go, the dacoit band would have other worries to contend with. In the darkness of evening, the unfortunate father and son’s bodies were buried together in the cold Hemanta night, beneath the water of Thakurjhi pond, among the clusters of floating tokapana and the tangled roots of shyama grass. Having arranged all this, Biru Ray returned home.

It was not long after this incident—just the following year, during the time of the puja. The year was 1238 in the Bengali calendar. Biru Ray, with his whole family, was returning by boat from his in-laws’ house in Haludber. After crossing the great salty canal below Nakipur and entering the Madhumati, they would ride the tide for two days before reaching the Ichhamati near Dakshin Sripur. From there, it was another four days’ journey to their own village.

They traveled all day, and by afternoon, the boat was moored at the ghat in Taki. There was to be a puja at home. After buying the necessary items for the puja from the Taki bazaar, they spent the night there, and at dawn, they set off again, heading homeward. Two days later, towards evening, they anchored the boat on a lonely sandbank at the confluence of the big canal of Dhabalchit and the Ichhamati, waiting for the tide, and began to prepare for cooking. It was a vast sandbank, with little vegetation save for occasional clumps of kash grass. In one spot, the boatmen, and in another, Biru Ray’s wife, were busy with the cooking. Everyone’s spirits were high—only two more days and they would reach home. Especially with the puja so near, the joy was palpable.

--- Page 10 ---
The moonlight had risen. The brackish river’s water glittered and shimmered. In the howling wind, the masses of kash flowers on the sandbar seemed to merge sky, moonlight, and the waters of the estuary into one swirling, silvery blur. Suddenly, at some sound, a couple of boatmen left their cooking, stood up, and looked around. Behind the clumps of kash grass, there was a sudden commotion—a muffled, frightened cry that was cut short as soon as it began. The curious boatmen, eager to see what had happened, had barely reached the other side of the kash thicket when something large crashed from the sandbar into the water and disappeared with a splash. That part of the sandbar was deserted—no one saw anything clearly.

Before anyone could understand what had happened, before they could grasp what was lost, the rest of the boatmen arrived. Hearing the commotion, Biru Ray came, his servant with him. Biru Ray’s only son had been on the boat—where was he? It was learned that, growing impatient with the delay in cooking, he had gone out a little while earlier to wander the sandbar in the moonlight. The boatmen’s faces went pale; from long experience with these brackish rivers, they understood—a great crocodile had been lying in wait behind the kash grass on the sandy bar. It had taken Biru Ray’s son from the shore.

After that, of course, what happens always happened. With poles from the boats, they searched here and there, leaving the boats to scour the mid-river until deep into the night—then came the wailing, the beating of hands and feet in grief. Last year, around this very time, something similar had happened in the field by Thakurjhi’s pond; it was as if an invisible judge had now delivered his verdict on the lonely sandbar of the Ichhamati. Foolish Biru Ray learned the hard way that the punishment of that unseen court could not be cheated by the price of Thakurjhi’s pond’s dark grass; even in darkness, it finds its own path.

Returning home, Biru did not live much longer. Thus began a strange fate in his family. Though his own line ended, his brother’s descendants remained. But the eldest child of the family never survived—before coming of age, each would die of some illness or another. Everyone said a Brahma-shap—a curse of Brahma—had entered the family. Harihar Ray’s mother, on a pilgrimage to Tarakeshwar, wept before a sadhu and received an amulet. Whether by the power of the amulet, or because the force of the Brahma-shap faded away like camphor after two generations, Harihar, even at this age, is still alive.

**Pather Panchali, Third Chapter**

Some days later.

--- Page 11 ---
Khuki had gone to bed after dusk. Her pishima (father’s sister) was not at home; it had been over two months now since, after some quarrel with her mother, pishima had left in anger to stay at a relative’s house in a distant village. Her mother’s health had also been frail for some time, so there was no one to look after Khuki. Lately, ever since her mother entered the confinement room for childbirth yesterday, no one really noticed when she ate or when she slept.

Lying in bed, Khuki cried for pishima until sleep finally came. Every night, she cried. Later in the night, she woke up to the sound of voices and saw, through half-closed eyes, Kuruni’s mother and the midwife standing in the shadowy corner of the kitchen, talking in low tones. The old grandmother from the neighborhood, and some others, were there too. Everyone seemed anxious and busy. Khuki, half awake, soon lay down again.

The wind rustled through the bamboo grove, making a whispering sound. Light glowed in the confinement room, and voices drifted from within. Moonlight spilled onto the veranda; in the cool breeze, she soon fell asleep. Sometime in the night, a vague commotion and muffled noises stirred her from her dreams. She saw her father hurrying out of the house, rushing toward the confinement room, calling out anxiously—“How is khuri (aunt)? What’s happened?” From inside the confinement room, she heard voices—one of them was her mother’s. In the darkness, half asleep, she couldn’t make sense of anything and sat quietly for a while. A strange fear crept over her. Why was Ma speaking like that? What had happened to her mother?

She sat for a while longer, unable to understand, then lay down again and soon drifted back to sleep. She didn’t know how much time had passed—somewhere, the mewling of a kitten woke her. Suddenly she remembered: in the evening, she had hidden the fluffy little kittens of the tabby cat inside the broken oven on pishima’s veranda—tiny, soft, their eyes not yet open. She thought—oh no, what if the tomcat comes and eats them all… yes, that’s right.

Still half asleep, she got up quickly and, in the darkness, went to pishima’s veranda. She reached into the oven and found the kittens sleeping peacefully, safe and sound. There was no sign of the tomcat anywhere. Relieved and a little surprised, she went back to bed and soon fell asleep again.

But in her dreams, somewhere, the kittens were still crying.

--- Page 12 ---
The next morning, as she rubbed the sleep from her eyes, Kuruni’s mother, the midwife, said, “O khuki, last night you got a little brother—won’t you come see him? Oh my, such a commotion last night, so much shouting and running about—where were you? What a night it was! We’ve promised to offer sweets at the shrine of the Pir in Kalpur—he truly protected us through the night.”

Khuki dashed off at once, running to the door of the birthing room and peeking inside. Her mother lay sleeping, pressed against the fence of date-palm leaves that made up the walls of the confinement room. There, wrapped in a quilt, lay a tiny, impossibly small being—barely bigger than a large glass doll—also fast asleep. The faint smoke from the fire of dried cow-dung cakes made it hard to see clearly. As she stood there for a while, the little creature opened its eyes, blinking, and waved its impossibly small hands, then, in a voice so thin and weak, began to cry. Only then did Khuki realize that the sound she’d heard in the night and thought was a kitten’s mewling had been this—exactly like a kitten’s cry, impossible to tell from afar. Suddenly, Khuki’s heart brimmed with sorrow, tenderness, and sympathy for this helpless, impossibly tiny, utterly fragile little brother. But when Nera’s thakuma (grandmother) and Kuruni’s mother, the midwife, forbade her, she couldn’t enter the birthing room, no matter how much she wanted to.

When her mother finally came out of confinement, Khuki would rock her little brother’s cradle and sing all sorts of rhymes. With each song, memories of evenings past, of pishima (paternal aunt), would rise up and fill her eyes with tears. How many such rhymes pishima used to recite! People from the neighborhood would come to see the baby. Everyone would say, “A child to light up the house! Look at that hair, that complexion!” As they left, they’d say, “Have you seen his smile, n’di (sister)?”

Khuki could only think, if only her pishima could come and see him just once. Everyone else had come, but where had their pishima gone—would she never return? Even as a child, Khuki understood this much: in this house, neither Baba nor Ma loved pishima, no one would bother to bring her back. In the daytime, when she glanced toward pishima’s room, her heart would ache; sometimes the door would hang open all day. Dust gathered on the veranda. The courtyard was no longer swept as before; here a sheora sapling, there a taro plant—would pishima have allowed that? Khuki’s big eyes would fill with tears—how could she ever forget those rhymes, those stories of pishima?

--- Page 13 ---
That day, Hari Palit’s daughter Asiya said to her mother, “On the path to your old ghat, I saw someone coming from the fields, carrying a brass pot and a small bundle—she’s gone and sat herself down inside Chakkotti moshai’s house. Go, send Dugga to fetch her, have her bring her in by the hand, then her anger will cool—”

Sitting in Hari Palit’s house, the old woman was then listening to the neighborhood girls chatter about Harihar’s son being born.

Oh dear!

The old woman looked up in surprise and saw Durga, panting as if she had run all the way. The old woman eagerly reached out to catch Durga—and at once Durga flung herself into the old woman’s lap—her face was smiling but her eyes were wet with tears. Among the women and girls gathered in the courtyard, many eyes filled with tears as well. The venerable wife of Hari Palit said, “Take her, thakurjhi (auntie), she was your daughter in a former life, and now she’s come back to you again—”

When they reached home and the old woman saw Khoka (the little boy), she wept and laughed all at once. After so many days, the moon had risen again over her homestead.

In the morning, the old woman rose and, brimming with joy, swept the inner courtyard, cleared the weeds and wild growth. To Durga, it felt as if at last the household was running as it should, as if for so long something had been not quite right.

After lunch, the old woman sat behind the kitchen door, on the path through the bamboo grove, cutting bamboo splints. Beyond that, all the way to the riverbank, there were no houses—the river itself wasn’t very close, nearly a quarter mile away—this whole stretch was nothing but sprawling mango orchards, tangled bamboo thickets, and other wild jungle. As the old woman cut the bamboo, Durga would come and sit beside her, chattering nonsense. When a small bundle of cut splints had gathered, Durga would carry them into the house. As she cut, in the lazy midday hush beneath the cool shade of the bamboo, old memories would come drifting back to the old woman.

All those things from so long ago!

--- Page 14 ---
He came those two or three times—like a dream, I remember. Once, he brought some food wrapped in a little bundle. Bishweshwari was only two years old then. Everyone exclaimed, Oh—like a lump of sugar candy! Mixed with water from the earthen pot, she too was fed a little. 

That man came—right by the old guava tree, just at dusk, standing there. He had come from the land of her in-laws, carrying a letter. There was no one to read the letter; brother Golok had died the previous year. So, in Braja-kaka’s Chandimandap, amidst the gathering for dice, she herself took the letter there. 

The memory of that day is still vivid—N’jetha, Mej jetha, Braja-kaka, from the other side of the village Patit Ray’s brother Jadu Ray, and there was Golok’s relation Bhojohori. Sej jetha read the letter aloud. Amazed, he asked—Who brought this letter, Indir? 

After that, Indir Thakrun had to come home right then, take off the iron bangle from her wrist and the beloved pair of silver anklets her parents had given her in her first youth, wipe the vermilion from her forehead, and go to bathe in the river. 

How many ages ago it was—all of it has become a dream, and yet, it feels as if it were only yesterday!...

--- Page 15 ---
Nibaran comes to mind—Nibaran, Nibaran! Braja Kaka’s son, Nibaran! A boy of sixteen years, what a radiant complexion, what hair! There, where the Chandimandap lies half-hidden in the wild undergrowth, in the midst of the bamboo grove—that is the house where he lay bedridden with a fierce fever, lingering on the edge between life and death for two or three days.

Ah, the boy would always cry out for water, water, but Ishan Kabiraj (the village healer) had forbidden it—only a little pouch of fennel seeds would be given for him to suck on, just a bit at a time. That same Nibaran died on the fourth night, and even a moment before his death, that “water, water” was still on his lips—yet not a single drop was allowed to touch his mouth.

After that boy died, for five days no one could persuade Boro Khuri (elder aunt, father’s elder brother’s wife) to take even a sip of water. On the fifth day, her brother-in-law Ramchand Chakraborty himself went to her room, folded his hands, and said, “If you go, what will become of me? Where will I go in this old age, Ma?” Boro Khuri was a daughter of an old, wealthy family—her beauty was like Jagaddhatri herself; there was no other bride in the region as lovely as she. She had never drunk water without first drinking the water that had washed her husband’s feet—a true housewife of the old days, cooking for her kin and only taking a modest meal herself in the third watch of the day. In charity and in feeding others, she was the very image of Annapurna (the goddess of nourishment). She loved to cook and feed people.

So, perhaps her brother-in-law’s words struck some tender place in her heart—after that, she rose and took water, it is true, but she did not live long; within a year and a half of her son’s death, she too followed after him.

—Just a little water, Ma—just a little—

—No water, shh, Baba—the Kabiraj-moshai (healer) has forbidden it—no water—

—Just a little—just a swallow, Ma—I beg you at your feet...

From across fifty years, in the call of the midday birds, the soft, sighing sound of the bamboo grove drifts to the ear.

Khuki (little girl) says—Piti, are you awake? Come, let’s go to sleep.

Setting down the sickle in her hand, the old woman says—Look, there it is again, that cursed drowsiness—no, Ma, I won’t sleep at this hour—let me finish these up—will you bring that big basket over here?

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Four**

--- Page 16 ---
Khoka is almost ten months old now. He looks rather frail, his little face impossibly small. Only two tiny teeth have sprouted on his lower gums. For no reason at all, at any moment, he flashes those two little milk teeth in a wide, gleeful grin. People say, “Bouma, your Khoka’s smile is something to bargain for.” If you so much as pick Khoka up for a moment, there’s no stopping him—he’ll start laughing like a madcap, so much so that his mother says, “All right, Khokon, that’s enough for today, you’ve laughed so much, save a little for tomorrow!” 

He has learned to say only two things! When he’s happy, he says, “Je—je—je—je,” and flashes his milky teeth in a smile. When he’s upset, he says, “Na—na—na—na,” and starts wailing in a most dreadful way. Whatever he finds in front of him, he tests the strength of those two new teeth on it—lumps of clay, a piece of wood, his mother’s anchal (the loose end of her sari). Sometimes, when his mother sits to feed him, he suddenly grabs the brass shell-bangle in her hand and, in great delight, bites down hard with his new teeth. His mother bursts out laughing and says, “Oh my, Khoka, why are you biting the bangle? Let go, let go—what are you doing! Those two teeth are all you have—if they break, how will you smile, tell me?” But Khoka won’t let go. His mother has to pry the bangle from his mouth with her finger, after much effort.

Since one cannot always rely on Khuki (his older sister) to watch him, his mother has raised the kitchen veranda a little and fenced it in with a bamboo barrier, sitting Khoka inside while she goes about her chores. Khoka, confined like a defendant at a hearing in a criminal trial, sometimes laughs to himself, sometimes babbles in an incomprehensible language to invisible listeners, sometimes stands up holding the bamboo fence and gazes out at the bamboo grove. When his mother returns from bathing at the ghat (riverbank), the sound of her wet sari makes Khoka look up from his play, searching here and there, and when he sees her, he beams, standing up holding the bamboo fence. His mother says, “Well, well, I put on your kajol (kohl) and wiped your face, and here you are, sitting like a harichacha bird (a local bird, known for its dark eyes)? Come here, let me see you.” She rubs his nose and face hard to remove the kajol, turning Khoka’s rosy cheeks bright vermilion—he protests loudly, “Je—je—je—je,” but his mother pays him no heed.

--- Page 17 ---
After this, whenever Khoka saw the gamchha (thin cotton towel) in his mother’s hand, he would babble and scamper away on all fours, trying to escape in a hurry. Some days, returning from the bathing ghat, Sarbajaya would call out––

“Khokon bole tu––u––u! Dolo to khoka? Dole dole khokon dole––!”  
(“Little Khoka says tu––u––u! Swing, Khoka, swing! Swing, swing, Khoka swings––!”)

At once, Khoka would plop down and begin to rock back and forth with great enthusiasm, waving his tiny hands in delight and breaking into song––

(Song)

Je––e––e––je––je––je––e––e––i  
Je––je––je––je––e  
Je––je––je––je––je––je

His mother would say, “All right, stop now, don’t swing anymore, Khoka, that’s enough, that’s enough, you’ve swung plenty.”

Sometimes, while working, Sarbajaya would pause and listen, straining her ears—no sound came from inside Khoka’s enclosure, as if he had suddenly gone quiet! Her heart would leap in fear—had a jackal taken him away? She would rush in to find Khoka, like a heap of overturned champa flowers, lying sprawled on the ground, his tiny hand awkwardly flung out, fast asleep. From all around, sluggish ants, flies, and swarms of tickling ants would come hurrying over in greedy excitement. Khoka’s thin, rosy lips would tremble ever so slightly in sleep, and in his dreams, he would sometimes swallow and breathe heavily—as if he were about to wake, but then would slip back into such deep slumber that even the sound of his breathing could not be heard.

From morning till dusk, and from dusk till deep into the night, their lonely house by the edge of the bamboo grove would ring with the meaningless songs and innocent laughter of the ten-month-old child.

A mother raises her child with love, and through the ages, the glory of mothers has been sung in every heart. But what the child gives to the mother—is that any less? He comes empty-handed, it’s true, but who can measure the worth of his captivating smile, his childhood softness, his moon-shaped face, his babbling nonsense words? That is his treasure, and in exchange for it, he receives care—not as a beggar with empty hands, but as one who gives in return.

Some days, when Harihar was busy with the accounts from the market or his own writing, Sarbajaya would bring the child and say, “Ogo, can’t you hold the boy for a bit? Where’s the girl gone—Thakurjhi (the maid) has gone to the ghat… Just hold him for a while, will you? I need to bathe, no, it’s fine if the boy just sits on your shoulders?”

Harihar would reply, “Uh-uh, don’t bring all that trouble here now, I’m very busy.”

--- Page 18 ---
Sarbojaya, in a fit of anger, leaves her son behind and walks away. Harihar, busy with his accounts, suddenly notices his son chewing on the strap of his chappal! Snatching the shoe away, Harihar exclaims—“Ah! Look at this mischief, and here I am trying to get some work done.”

Suddenly, a sparrow flutters down and perches at the edge of the veranda. The little boy, gazing in wonder at his father’s face, points excitedly at the bird and waves his hand—“Je—je—je—”

Harihar’s irritation melts away, replaced by a deep tenderness.

A memory from long ago surfaces in his mind.

He remembers a night many years past.

He had come from the west, newly arrived, and at the advice of the villagers, had gone to his in-laws’ house to bring back his wife. It was afternoon when the boat reached the ghat of his in-laws’ village. Since the wedding, he had visited only once before; the roads and paths were unfamiliar, so he had to ask his way before finally arriving at the front of his in-laws’ house. At his call, a slender, fair-skinned young woman came to the outer door to see who was calling, and as their eyes met, she quickly slipped away into the house—Harihar wondered, who is this girl? Could it be his wife? Had she grown so much?

That night, the mystery was solved.

Late at night, Sarbojaya entered the room, draped in her mother’s red-bordered motka sari, preserved from poverty. Harihar looked at her in astonishment. The little girl-bride of ten years ago was nowhere to be seen in this beautiful young woman—someone seemed to have broken her down and remade her anew. The childish softness in her face was gone, but in its place had blossomed a beauty that was rare—Harihar realized this without delay. The shape of her hands and feet, the way she moved—everything was perfect and new.

Entering the room, Sarbojaya was at first flustered. Though she had grown up, she had hardly ever met her husband face to face. The shyness of a newlywed seemed to seize her all over again. Harihar spoke first. Taking her right hand in his, he seated her on the bed and said—“Sit here. Are you well?”

Sarbojaya smiled softly. The shyness seemed to ease a little. She said—“After all this time, you finally remembered me? Tell me, what kept you away for so long?” Then, with a laugh, she added—“Why, what wrong did I do, tell me?”

--- Page 19 ---
In his wife’s words, Harihar found the lilt and cadence of a remote village—something new and deeply sweet to him. Later, he noticed that on her hands, apart from a few lac bangles and glass bracelets, she wore no other ornaments. A poor man’s daughter, with no one to give her such things—he realized how unjust he had been, never inquiring after her all this time. Sarbajaya, too, kept glancing at her husband. All day, she had stolen four or five looks at him from behind doors—on every limb of Harihar’s strong, youthful body, health and vigor had lent a heroic bearing, rare in the villages of Bengal.

Today, from her parents’ conversation, she had learned that her husband had returned from the West, having studied much, and that he was not without means either. At last, her sorrow had lifted; perhaps God had finally turned His gaze upon her. Everyone used to say her husband had become a sannyasi—a wandering ascetic—and would never return. Though she had never truly believed it in her heart, his return had always seemed a distant, hopeless dream. How many nights had she lain awake in worry, unable to join fully in the village’s weddings and sacred thread ceremonies—everyone would sigh, “Ah, poor thing,” and offer her their sympathy, and tears of wounded pride would spring to her eyes. The golden dreams of her unspoiled youth had, until now, fallen quietly as tears in the solitude of night. She had never spoken of it to anyone, but often she would sit and wonder—this is the way of the world; if her husband truly never returned, then after her parents’ death, where would she stand—who would give her shelter?

At last, she had found her shore.

Harihar smiled and asked, “Well, when you saw me outside the door earlier—did you recognize me? Tell me the truth now—”

Sarbajaya smiled and replied, “No, why would I recognize you! At first I couldn’t quite tell, and then, just then—”

“—You guessed—”

“—Not guessed, no, not guessed at all—truly. Didn’t you see, I covered my head and slipped inside the house right away? Then, after a moment’s silence, she asked again, ‘Tell me, did you recognize me? Swear on your heart and tell me.’”

In such idle and earnest talk, the night grew late. When the subject of her departed elder brother came up, Sarbajaya’s tears would not be held back. Harihar asked, “Where did Bina get married?” He did not know the name of his younger sister-in-law; only today had he heard it from his father-in-law’s lips.

--- Page 20 ---
––Her marriage took place at Kurule Binodpur––you know, by the big river, what’s it called? Madhumati.––Yes, on the banks of that very Madhumati.

A question kept rising again and again in Sarbajaya’s mind––Will my husband take me with him? Or will he just come to see me, then leave again for those distant places, Kashi, Gaya? She wanted to ask, the words hovered on her lips, but somehow she could not bring herself to speak them aloud––deep inside, a voice of rebellion declared––Let him go without me, then––why should I make myself so small by asking to be taken along?

Harihar solved the problem himself. He said, “Tomorrow, let’s go. I’ll take you home to Nishchindipur.”

Sarbajaya’s heart gave a sudden thud, as if a pestle had dropped on it––gathering herself, she said, “Why tomorrow? You’ve come after so long––can’t you stay a couple of days? Will Ma and Baba let you leave so soon? Day after tomorrow, my Bakulphul’s family has invited you to their house again––”

“Who is your Bakulphul?”

“She lives here in the village––in this neighborhood, though she’s married across the river now.” Then, smiling, she added, “She said she’ll come to see you tomorrow morning––”

The current of conversation flowed on––the night grew deep. By the house, in the drumstick tree, a night-waking bird called out in a strange voice. Harihar felt that here, in the shadow of the bamboo groves of Bengal’s quiet countryside, a corner of the home, anxious with love, had been waiting for months, for years, dressed in welcome for his return––and yet, what was he searching for, wandering like a homeless, shelterless man among the barren, unfamiliar deserts and hills of the west?

The night-waking bird called monotonously, the moonlight outside slowly faded. In a way, this night seemed full of mystery to him; before them stretched the path of a new life, leading into the vast unknown future––and tonight was its beginning. Who knows what that life would be? Who knows what gifts Lakshmi, goddess of fortune, had set aside for them, as provisions for that uncertain journey ahead?

Perhaps the same vague thought stirred in both their hearts. Both sat in silence, gazing through the window at the moonlit night outside.

And then, how many days have passed since? Back then, where was this child’s place in the world?

---

**Pather Panchali**  
Fifth Chapter

--- Page 21 ---
Indir Thakrun returned some six or seven months ago, yet Sarbajaya has not spoken kindly to the old woman even once in all this time. Lately, it seems to her even more that this old witch, this devourer of seven lineages, is loved by her daughter more than by herself. Jealousy, of course, she feels, but also anger. Her own daughter is being turned away from her. Twice a day, in conversation after conversation, she hints to the old woman that it is time to find her way—time to leave. But which way is that? In all her seventy years, since she first became aware of herself, the old woman has never found that path; how will she find it now, after so many years? She cannot even imagine.

At the end of the monsoon, the old woman finally arrived at a decision. Her son-in-law’s house is in Bhandarhati, six krosh (about twelve miles) away. Her son-in-law, Chandra Majumdar, is still alive. He is well-off, a prosperous householder. Of course, the moment her daughter died, all ties with her son-in-law were severed—that was thirty-five or thirty-six years ago—and since then, there has been no contact, no exchange of news. Still, if she were to go there, would her son-in-law refuse her a little shelter?

Before evening, the cart entered the village of Bhandarhati and stopped in front of a large Chandimandap (a pavilion for worship of the goddess Chandi). At the carter’s call, a young man of about twenty-four or twenty-five came and asked, “Where is this cart from?” Following him, an elderly man emerged from the house, asking, “Who is it, Radhu? Ask where they have come from.”

The old woman recognized him—and yet she stood amazed—this was her son-in-law, Chandra! She compared, in her mind, the robust, well-built, handsome young man of forty years ago with this gray-haired, aged figure, and she felt as if she could hardly breathe. In the next moment, a strange mixture of emotions—neither quite laughter nor quite sorrow—overwhelmed her, and she burst out crying, calling her daughter’s name for the first time in many years.

Chandra Majumdar, utterly bewildered at first, searched the heavens and the earth for an explanation. Then, understanding, he came forward and bowed to touch his mother-in-law’s feet in reverence. Collecting herself a little, the old woman pulled her sari over her head and, in a broken voice, said, “I have come to you, babaji, after all these years—just for a little shelter—how many more days will I live anyway? There is no one left for me in this wide world—at this age, just for a little rice and cloth—”

--- Page 22 ---
Majumdar Mahashay asked his eldest son to unload the goods from the carriage and sent his mother-in-law inside the house with the boy. The widow daughter from the second marriage and the eldest daughter-in-law are the mistresses of the household. There are three more daughters-in-law. Three or four grandchildren—boys and girls—run about.

The house is a grand, high, eight-roofed structure, with two enormous verandas propped up by the trunks of palm trees, cross-beamed and sturdy. Every inch is packed with things—boxes, trunks, chests—there’s hardly space to set your foot. Majumdar Mahashay’s widowed daughter is named Haimabati. A very good girl—she herself sliced the fruit, arranged the afternoon snack, and, with great warmth, seated the guest close and fed her, asking this and that. She said, “Didima, you’ve never seen me before, have you? You’ve never set foot here before! Shall I cut the fruit for you, Didima? Do you still have your teeth?”

In the adjoining kitchen, the children gather for their evening meal, raising a ruckus. One shouts, “O Ma, look, Umi is dumping all the dal onto my plate!” The daughter-in-law yells back, “Why do you sit next to her to eat? Haven’t I told you every day to sit separately—Umi, you’re getting too naughty, aren’t you?”

But after ten or twelve days, everything began to feel strangely new to the old woman, and she could not find comfort—the unfamiliar rooms, the new roads and lanes, the different ways of the household. It all felt as if it did not truly belong to her, as if everything was someone else’s. Every evening, she remembered her own quiet veranda and the faces of her little Khuki and Khoka. After about twenty days, the old woman grew restless to leave. Her heart would not settle here. The arrival of the first wife’s mother-in-law and her intentions had never pleased the eldest daughter-in-law, who was not unhappy to see her go—if anything, she was quietly glad. What Chandra Majumdar himself wished, only God knows, but out of fear of his eldest son and daughter-in-law, he said nothing.

After many days, once again, sitting on her own veranda with Durga by her side and Khoka in her lap, watching the gentle trembling of moonlit coconut fronds, the old woman felt the drowsy bliss of happiness return.

At first, Khuki had sulked terribly—she would not speak, would not come near. But after much coaxing and comfort, she had softened. Now, the old woman stroked her niece’s head and said, “A bright red pair of long dangling earrings would suit you so well, don’t you think? Or is something else in fashion these days—what do they call those things now—?”

--- Page 23 ---
Winter arrived. The old woman—she went to the Ganguli house across the neighborhood and said to old Ramnath Ganguli Mahashoy, “Oh Ram, this chill has come down so hard again—there’s not a single piece of cloth on my body that I can wrap around myself in the mornings and evenings, just to sit for a bit. If you could give me just one—”

Ram Ganguli replied, “Alright, didi, come another day. This month it won’t be possible—but maybe next month I’ll see what I can do.”

After many days of walking back and forth, one day he brought out a cotton shawl, red-printed from Kushtia, and handed it to her, saying, “Here you go, didi, this is a very warm thing—cost me nine and a half annas—you won’t find anything better than this in all of Nawabganj—brought it on Wednesday—won’t you open it and see?”

The old woman could hardly believe it even then. Her face broke into a wide, delighted grin as she unwrapped it and draped it over her shoulders—“Wonderful, what warmth—thick and sturdy, such good cloth—ah dada, may you live long—may Kanai Bolai live long, may Akshay Pramai be blessed—no one gives to the poor and hungry, I’ve been asking Annada for a piece of cloth to wear for three years now—always says she’ll give, but never does—let me enjoy this while I can, how many days do I have left anyway?”

Showing it off to Sarbojaya with delight, she said, “Look, thakurji, you won’t be able to walk out of this house with seven doors between you, I’m telling you straight. If you have to beg, make your own arrangements—”

The old woman swallowed those words. She had to swallow many such words ten times a day. She still hadn’t forgotten the rhyme from the old days—

A kick, a broom underfoot,
A stone of rice pressed to the chest—

Durga, full of mischief, said, “How much did it cost, pitima—how naked you were—no?”

With a reassuring tone, pishi said, “When I die, I’ll leave it for you, you can wear it when you’re grown.”

The earthy, starchy scent of the new shawl was a rare delicacy to the old woman, it felt so refined, so precious. In the morning, wrapping the shawl around herself as she swept, she would sometimes glance at her own reflection. For no reason, she would stand by the path to the ghat, call out to the passing, gentle housewives, “Who goes there? Rajir’s mother?—so late in the day?—” And with a little laugh, looking at her own body, she’d say, “This cloth I’m wearing—Ramchand from the other side gave it—nine and a half annas—”

A couple of mischievous girls would say, “Oof, how grand thakuma looks in her red shawl! Is thakuma getting married?”

Pathar Panchali, Chapter Six

The maid-thakurun from the other side came by with a smiling face and said, “I came for two coins, bou, Indir pishi took a lump of salt from me yesterday, said, ‘I’ll come by tomorrow and pay you’—”

--- Page 24 ---
Sarbojoya was busy with the household chores. She exclaimed in surprise, “You bought nôna (a type of wild fruit) from her?”

Dasi-thakrun was a shrewd businesswoman. From a mere tamarind or hog plum to a bunch of greens, she never gave anything away without taking money. The warmth in the maid’s manner vanished. She said, “Ask your nanad (husband’s younger sister) whether she bought it or not! Did I come here in the morning to tell lies for two paisa? I won’t give it for less than four paisa—she said the old woman wanted to eat—well, let it be, even for two paisa—”

Sarbojoya was so angry that she couldn’t utter a word. Nôna, a fruit that grows so abundantly in the wild that even the cows and calves get tired of eating it—she could not imagine that in a village like this, there would be people who would actually buy it with money.

Just then, Indir budi (old Indir) appeared from somewhere. Sarbojoya seemed to pounce on her, saying, “Hey you, three lifetimes have passed, once you too were in need—when you eat at someone’s house, shouldn’t you have a little compassion for their money? Did you go to buy nôna? Where will I get nôna and rice to feed you today? If you’re so fond of it, go buy it yourself with your own money—aren’t you ashamed to indulge your whims at others’ expense?”

The old woman’s face shriveled, yet she tried to muster a faint smile and said, “Well, give it, bou (daughter-in-law)—the ripe nôna, I thought I’d eat it, how many days do I have left anyway? Give me two paisa for it—”

Sarbojoya shouted four times louder, “You think money grows on trees? You have your own pots and pans—go sell them and buy it yourself—”

Then, taking her water pot, she left through the back door towards the ghat (riverbank).

The maid stood for a while and said, “I swear on my nose and ears, I’ve never had such trouble selling things! And I tell you too, Indir pisi (auntie Indir), if you didn’t have your own money, you shouldn’t have brought the nôna yesterday, don’t bring things on credit like this anymore. Well, you all quarrel among yourselves, I’m a poor woman, I’ll come back later, but don’t forget my money—”

Khuki (the little girl) followed the maid out to the outer courtyard. She kept talking as she went, “Pisimā, she’s an old woman, she brought just one nôna, is that something to scold her for? Don’t you feel like eating it, dasi-pisi? It’s good nôna, she gave me half of it yesterday—do you have a tree at your house, pisi?” Then she called out, “Listen, dasi-pisi, I’ll give you a paisa now, it’s in my doll’s box. Ma locked the house and went to the ghat, when she comes back I’ll secretly give it to you, but don’t tell Ma, pisi.”

--- Page 25 ---
Just before noon, Indir budi was leaving the house. In her left hand, a small bundle wrapped in a dirty cloth; in her right, a brass water pot dangling from its handle; tucked under her arm, an old mat, its frayed edge trailing strips of cane.

Khuki called out, “O pisi, don’t go—O pisi, where will you go?” Then she ran up and tugged at the back of the mat. “If you leave, I’ll cry, pisi—truly—”

From the veranda, Sarbajaya spoke: “Well, go if you must, but why leave cursing the household? We have children, we make a home—after all these years of feeding you, shouldn’t we wish for your welfare? If you leave at such an hour, hungry and angry, and misfortune befalls us, is that what you want? If your heart weren’t so twisted, would you be in such a state now?...”

The old woman did not turn back. Khuki, weeping, followed her a long way down the path.

Indir budi made her way to the far side of the village, to the house of Nabin Ghoshal. Hearing her story, Nabin Ghoshal’s wife pressed her hands to her cheeks and exclaimed, “Oh my, I’ve never heard such a thing, khuri! Stay, you must stay here, with us.”

After staying there for a month or two, budi left and sought shelter at the house of Tinkari Ghoshal, and from there, at Purna Chakraborty’s. In every house, the warmth of the first welcome faded after a few days, and the householders began to show their irritation in various ways. They advised her to settle her quarrel and return home. Budi wandered to a couple more houses, always trusting that if no one else, at least Harihar would send for her. But three months passed, and no one came with an eager invitation. Durga did not come either. Budi knew—the distance from that side of the village to this was great; a little girl could not come so far. Still, she went to the other side once or twice in hope, but did not meet Khuki.

One cannot live in others’ homes for twelve months of the year. On the eastern edge of the village, Chinte the milkman’s old thatched hut stood abandoned—after a couple of months, the villagers together decided to give that hut to budi, and agreed that everyone would help her a little. The hut was tiny, its walls patched with bamboo slats, far from the village, nestled in a bamboo grove. Sarbajaya was heard to have said, “Let everyone see how fierce she is. Not this house, not anymore—she never cared for my children, she’ll never darken my doorstep again, let her rot in the dump for all I care.”

--- Page 26 ---
Those who were supposed to help, at first, for a few days, joined in with great enthusiasm, but gradually their interest, too, faded away. The old woman wonders, Why did I leave that day in such anger? The daughter-in-law tried to stop me, Khuki cried so much, tugged at my hand, pleaded with me––! In deep sorrow for herself, tears stream down her two sunken cheeks. She murmurs––Was it fated that my last days would be so full of suffering––If only my daughter were here today––

It is the last day of the month of Chaitra. All day the sun has blazed fiercely; by evening, a gentle breeze begins to stir, the drums of the Charak festival still beat in the Gosainpara, the fair is not yet over.

From house to house, under the harsh sun and in constant worry, every evening the old woman now develops a slight fever. She lies quietly on a mat spread out on the veranda, a clay pot of water by her head. The little brass pitcher has already been pawned for four annas to buy rice. Parched with fever, she sips water from the clay pot now and then.

––Pisimā! (aunt)... The old woman drops her quilt and leaps up; Khuki is climbing onto the veranda, behind her is Raji, the daughter of Behari Chakkatti from their neighborhood. Khuki wears a clean dress, at the end of her scarf are little bundles tied up. The old woman cannot say much. With overwhelming eagerness, she stretches out her thin arms and draws Khuki to her feverish chest.

––Don’t tell anyone, Pisi, let no one find out, I slipped away quietly after watching the Charak, Raji came with me too, look, I’ve brought all this for you from the fair––

Khuki unties her bundles.

––Murki (puffed rice sweet), Pisimā, two paisa worth of murki for you, and two kadma (sugar candies), and for Khoka, a wooden doll––

The old woman sits up properly. Turning the little gifts over in her hands, she says––Let me see, let me see, oh my precious, look at all the things you’ve brought! May you become a queen, to have such kindness for your poor Pisi! Let me see Khoka’s wooden doll! Ah, what a fine doll––how much did you pay for it?...

After a rush of chatter, Khuki says––Pisi, your body feels so hot?

––Wandering about all day in the sun has made me like this, that’s why I thought I’d lie down a bit––

Though still a child, Durga understands why Pisimā has been wandering in the sun. With gentle affection, she strokes her thin, hungry aunt and says, You must go home, you know––I miss hearing your stories in the evenings, that’s all––go tomorrow, will you?

The old woman, overwhelmed with joy, says, Did Bou (daughter-in-law) tell you something today?

--- Page 27 ---
Raji said—Khurima hasn’t said anything, Pishima. She doesn’t let her come here. If we ask, she scolds us. But you should go too, Pishima. If you just say a little something, then Khurima won’t say anything more—

Khuki said—You must go tomorrow, Pisi. Ma won’t say anything—so let’s go home now, Pisi, don’t tell anyone, all right? But tomorrow morning, you must go, promise.

In the morning, the old woman woke to find her body a little lighter. When the day grew a bit, she tied up two torn, tattered pieces of cloth and a dirty gamchha (thin cotton towel) in a small bundle and set off towards home. On the way, Gopi Bostom’s wife said, “Didi Thakurun, so you’re going home, are you? Has Boudidi’s anger gone away, then?”

The old woman smiled broadly and said—Yesterday Durga came to call me at dusk, how she cried! She said, “Ma said—come, Pisi, come home—” So I told her—You go today, let the morning come, I’ll come home then—my girl cried so much, do you think I want to go!... That’s why I’m going this morning.

The old woman entered to find no one at home. After a night of fever, to walk so far under the sun with her frail body—perhaps she had grown exhausted. Setting down her bundle, she sat on the edge of her own veranda.

Soon after, Sarbajaya, having bathed in the river, pushed open the back door and returned. Her eyes fell on the old woman sitting there, and she stood for a moment, struck silent with surprise. The old woman smiled and said—O bou, are you well? After all this time, where else would I go, leaving you all at this age—so I say—

Sarbajaya stepped forward and said—What made you come to this house?

Her manner and the tone of her voice left the old woman no more courage to smile. Without letting her answer, Sarbajaya said—This house will never be your place again—I told you that day—you come back again, with what face?

The old woman went stiff as wood, no words came from her mouth. Then suddenly, she broke down crying and said—O bou, don’t speak to me like that—give me just a little place—where else will I go, let me spend my last days here—at least on this bit of earth—

—No, don’t start pleading about the land again, you lose sleep over this patch of earth, do you? Leave at once, or I’ll make trouble—

--- Page 28 ---
The old woman had never expected things would come to this. Like a drowning man who, as he sinks, clutches at whatever he can find, the old woman clung to her bundle, searching for shelter, her gaze wandering aimlessly this way and that—today, it seemed to her as if the refuge she had known for so many years was truly slipping away from beneath her feet, and there was no way to hold on to it any longer.

Sarbojaya said, “Go on, don’t just sit here, Thakurji (a respectful term for an elderly woman), it’s getting late, I have my chores to do. I simply can’t give you a place here—not in any way.”

With great difficulty, the old woman picked up her bundle once more. As she reached the outer door, her eyes fell upon her old broomstick, propped up in the corner against the wall; for three or four months now, no one had touched it. This patch of yard grass, that carefully planted lemon tree, this beloved broomstick, Khuki, Khoka, Braja Pishi’s homestead—in her seventy years of life, she had known nothing else, understood nothing else.

Now, forever, all of these were slipping away from her.

As she walked past the sajne tree, bundle under her arm, the lady of the Ray household called from behind, “Thakuma (grandmother), where are you going back to? Won’t you come home?” Getting no reply, she muttered, “Thakuma’s hearing has completely gone these days.”

In the evening, someone from the other neighborhood came and said, “O Ma-Thakrun (another term for an elderly woman), it seems your old woman is dying—she’s been lying by the Palit family’s granary since noon, tried to walk back in the sun, but couldn’t make it—go and see her once, won’t you? Isn’t Dada-Thakur (elderly man) home? Can’t you send someone?”

It was true that under the big platform beside the Palit family’s granary, Indir Thakrun was dying. On her way back from Harihar’s house, her body gave out, and unable to go any further in the sun, she lay down right there. The Palits had carried her to the Chandimandap (a communal pavilion for worship). They had massaged oil into her chest and back, fanned her, done everything they could, but as her condition worsened late in the day, they laid her down again. Many from Palit-para (the Palit neighborhood) stood around her. Some said, “But why did she go out in the sun? The sun is blazing today!” Others said, “She’ll come around soon, must have just fainted—”

Bishu Palit said, “It’s not a faint. The old woman won’t survive. Harijetha (elder uncle Hari) probably isn’t home, word has been sent, but who will come all this way?”

--- Page 29 ---
Hearing the news, Dinuchakraborty’s eldest son, Phani, came to see what had happened. Everyone said—“Give, Dada Thakur, thank goodness you’ve come, give her a little Ganga water in her mouth, will you? Just look at this—no one from the Brahmin neighborhood, no one at all—who will give her a drop of water?”

Phani handed his bamboo walking stick to Bishu Palit and sat down by the old woman’s face. Taking the Ganga water with care, he called out—“O Pisimā!” (father’s sister, a term for an elderly woman)

The old woman opened her eyes and stared, wide and unblinking, at his face, but no answer came from her lips. Phani called again—“How are you, Pisimā? Are you feeling ill?”

Then he poured a little of the Ganga water into her mouth. But the water did not go in; Bishu Palit said—“Try once more, Dada Thakur—”

A little while later, as Phani gently closed the old woman’s eyelids, a good amount of water, pooled in the hollows of her eyes, rolled down her withered cheeks.

With the death of Indir Thakrun, an era in the village of Nischindipur came to an end.

---

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Seven**

Four or five years have passed since the death of Indir Thakrun. It is the end of the month of Magh, the cold still lingers. Along a narrow earthen path, hemmed in on both sides by thickets and tangled shrubs, a few people from Nischindipur are making their way, on the afternoon of Saraswati Puja, to the fields outside the village to see the blue-throated nilkantha bird.

One of the group said, “Hey Hari, have you folks handed over Bhushon the cowherd’s banana grove again?”

The man addressed doesn’t look at all like the Harihar Ray of ten years ago. Now he is middle-aged, a full-fledged householder, father of children. Harihar goes from village to village collecting rent, sits among the disciples and servants of his ancestral line, dispensing wisdom, haggles with the vegetable sellers over the price of ridge gourds and pointed gourds at the market. Gone is the free-spirited, open-hearted, wandering youth that he once was. That life of the west has gradually receded far, far away—sitting on the wide ramparts of the Chunar fort watching the sun set behind distant hills, spending nights in the laurel forests on the road to Kedarnath, plucking sour oranges from the garden of Shah Kasem Sulemani’s dargah, the clear, bright, icy-cold heavenly river Alakananda, like a stream of molten silver, the king of the waters at Dashashwamedh Ghat—all these come back to him, little by little, like dreams glimpsed long ago.

Harihar, about to say something in reply, turned around and called, “Where’s the boy gone off to again? O Khoka, Khoka-a-a—”

--- Page 30 ---
From behind the bend in the path, a bright, beautiful boy of six or seven, slender and lively, came running to catch up with the group. Harihar said, “Falling behind again already? Come on, keep moving—”

The boy asked, “What went into the forest, Baba? With the big, big ears?”

Harihar paid no attention to the question and instead began discussing fishing plans with Nabin Palit.

Harihar’s son, his voice eager, asked again, “What ran into the forest, Baba? With the big, big ears?”

Harihar replied, “How should I know, Baba? I can’t keep answering your questions. Ever since we left, you’ve been asking—what’s this, what’s that—did I see what went into the forest? Now come on, keep walking!”

The boy, chastened, walked ahead of his father.

Nabin Palit said, “Actually, Hari, if you really want to catch fish, let’s go to Boyshar’s bil [a large wetland] one day. Nepal Parui from the east bank is letting people fish—every day they’re catching one and a half to two maunds [old Bengali weight measure]—not a fish under five seers! I heard that one night, just before dawn, right in the middle of the bil, in the deep water, there was a sound—like a young calf calling—can you imagine?”

Everyone moved closer, gazing at Nabin Palit’s face.

“—An ancient bil, deep water, you’ve seen how in the middle the water’s as black as ink, thick with lotus plants, some say it’s a giant catfish, some say a yakshi [a supernatural being]—until it’s light, everyone just sits in the boat, shivering—”

The story was reaching its peak when suddenly Harihar’s son, bursting with excitement, pointed at a clump of reeds nearby and shouted as he ran, “There it goes, Baba! Look, Baba, there it goes! Big, big ears, there—”

His father called after him, “No, no, no—thorns, thorns, thorns—” Then he hurried over, grabbed the boy’s hand, and said, “Ah, you’re really testing my patience, I see. I’ve told you a hundred times, but you just won’t listen. This is why I didn’t want to bring you.”

The boy, his face bright with enthusiasm and curiosity, looked up at his father and asked, “What, Baba?”

Harihar said, “How would I know what it was! Probably a pig or something—come on, walk right in the middle of the path—”

“Not a pig, Baba, it was small!” Then he crouched down to show how high the creature had been from the ground.

“Come on, come on—yes, I understand, you don’t have to show me—let’s go!”

Nabin Palit said, “That was a rabbit, little one, a baby rabbit. There are rabbits in these reed thickets, that’s why.”

--- Page 31 ---
The boy had seen a picture of a rabbit under the letter ‘kho’ in his primer, but that such a creature could leap away alive before his very eyes—or that one might ever see it with ordinary eyes—this was something he had never imagined.

A rabbit!—alive—bounding away right in front of you—not a picture, not a glass figurine—but a real rabbit, ears perked up, flesh and blood! In thickets of wild vāt (vetiver) grass and baichi shrubs like these!—How could such a thing be possible in this perishable world of water and earth? The boy simply could not fathom it, no matter how hard he tried.

Everyone left the narrow path through the woods and stepped out onto the field. Beyond the babla (acacia) and jiol (waterberry) trees by the riverbank, something caught their eye—a large, brick structure, like a stack of bricks. It was the crumbling remains of the old indigo factory’s boiler house. In the days of the indigo planters, this Nishchindipur had been the head office of the Bengal Indigo Concern, and the manager of the Nishchindipur factory, John Larmer, had ruled over the fourteen factories of the region with an iron fist. Now, the factory’s broken vats, the boiler house, the sahib’s bungalow, and the office had all become heaps of brick overrun by jungle. Once, at the mere mention of the mighty Larmer Sahib’s name, tigers and cattle alike would drink from the same ghat in fear; nowadays, except for a few ancient elders, no one even remembers the man’s name.

The field’s thickets were filled with reeds, wild kalmi (water spinach), sodal, and jujube trees. The kalmi vines had spread their large green leaves over the tops of all the bushes, covering them in a lush canopy—inside, there was cool shade, small cowherds, thorny natakanta, and blue wild aparajita (butterfly pea) flowers blooming with their faces lifted to the sunlight. In the mellow light of late afternoon, the woodland’s greenery glowed with a gentle radiance; birds called out, and all around, nature’s bounty was scattered with a king’s generosity—nowhere was there even a trace of poverty, no sign of the middle-class stinginess. In the enchantment of day’s end, the field, river, and forest shimmered with a magical beauty.

--- Page 32 ---
As they wandered through the fields, Nabin Palit Mahashay began to recount how, once upon a time, he had cultivated sweet potatoes on the northern plot of this very field and reaped such profit from it. Someone else chimed in, “I heard the bricks from the kuthi (colonial bungalow) are going to be sold—Moti Da from Nawabganj is apparently negotiating the deal.” At the mention of Moti Da, the conversation turned to how he had risen from humble beginnings to become a wealthy man. Gradually, from there, the talk drifted to the soaring prices of the present times, to how the Kundu family’s gol-dari shop had burned down in the Asharh (monsoon) market, to when the wedding date was set for Dinubabu Ganguli’s daughter—thus flowed a lively exchange of all the essential news and gossip of the village.

Harihar’s son asked, “Where’s the nilkantha bird, Baba?”

“Just wait and see, look there—soon it’ll come and perch on the babla tree,” replied his father.

The boy craned his neck, gazing up at the crowns of all the nearby babla trees. Here and there, the low-slung kul trees (bearing Indian jujube) were heavy with ripe fruit, and the boy stared at them with wide-eyed, hungry wonder. More than once, he had tried to pick the fruit, only to be scolded by his father and forced to desist. How could such small trees bear kul? The kul tree in their own yard was so tall that, even if he wanted to, he couldn’t reach its branches. Even gripping the heavy, hooked fruit-picker with both hands, he couldn’t lift it; sneaking forbidden treats was a struggle—he knew this well. If his mother found out, she would come and drag him home, exclaiming, “Oh, what will I do with you! How naughty you’ve become! Just the other day you got up from your fever, and now you’re already wandering under the kul trees! I turn my back for a moment, and you vanish from the house! How many kul did you eat? Let me see your mouth!”

He would protest, “I didn’t eat any kul, Ma, there wasn’t a single one on the ground—I couldn’t have picked them, could I?”

Then, bringing his rosy little face very close to his mother, he would open his mouth wide. His mother, inspecting him closely, would kiss his sweet, ghee-scented face and say, “Never eat them secretly, Khoka (dear little one)! Wait till you’re well again—I’ll gather kul and make pickle for you, and keep it in a jar—then, in the month of Boishakh or Joishtho (summer), you can have it. But never, ever eat them in secret again—promise?”

Harihar said, “You were talking about the kuthi—look there, Khoka, do you see? That’s the sahib’s kuthi. Have you seen it?”

--- Page 33 ---
Along the riverbank, sprawling wide, lay the old indigo bungalow of a bygone era—its ruins scattered like the bones of some monstrous, ferocious beast from prehistory. Over it, the silent afternoons of a wintry, desolate season—emblems of time’s ceaseless flow—spread their pale, ashen shroud, layer by layer.

Not far from the bungalow’s corridor, in a state of abandonment and overgrown with wild thickets, rests the grave of the indigo planter Lermor Sahib’s young son. Of the vast Bengal Indigo Concern’s grand headquarters, nothing else remains intact above the earth—no other sign but this. If you approach, you can still read, on the weathered stone darkened by ages, the inscription:

Here lies Edwin Lermor,

The only son of John & Mrs. Lermor,

Born May 13, 1853, Died April 27, 1860.

Amidst the other wild trees and shrubs, a solitary sōdal tree has grown tall, spreading its leafy branches in shade above the grave. In the months of Chaitra and Baishakh, when strong winds sweep in from the mouth of the Arai-Banki river, its yellow flower-clusters rain down, day and night, in heaps upon the broken tomb of the forgotten foreign child. Though all others have forgotten, the trees of the forest have not yet let go of the little one.

The boy gazed around him in wonder, his eyes wide with astonishment. In his six years of life, never before had he ventured so far from home. Until now, the world’s boundaries for him had been the neḍā’s house, the patch before their own home, at most the house of the Ranu-didis—nothing beyond. Only sometimes, when he came to bathe with his mother at the neighborhood ghat, he would look from the bathing steps toward the distant, broken windowed-room of the bungalow—pointing with his finger, he would ask, “Ma, is that the bungalow?” He had heard tales of the bungalow’s field from his father, from his didi, from so many in the neighborhood, but today was his first time here! Beyond that field—was that, perhaps, the fairy-tale kingdom from his mother’s stories? In the land of Shyam-Lanka, beneath the tree of Bengma and Bengmi, where the exiled prince lies alone through the night, sword at his side? Beyond this, no humans dwell—the very edge of the world is here. Beyond this begins the land of the impossible, the country of the unknown.

--- Page 34 ---
On the way home, by the side of the path, he reached out his hand to pluck a cluster of bright-colored fruits hanging from a low bush. His father said, “Hey, hey, don’t touch, don’t touch—alkushi, alkushi!”
