“What are you doing, baba! You’re such a bother, really. I’m telling you, I won’t ever take you out anywhere again—right now your hand will itch and blister—how many times have I told you to walk in the middle of the path—but you never listen!”

“Why will my hand itch, baba?”

“Your hand will itch, it’s poisonous, poisonous—who touches alkushi, baba? The prickles will sting and burn, and then you’ll start screaming.”

Through the village, Harihar led his son by the hand, entering the house through the side door. Hearing the sound of the latch, Sarbajaya came out and said, “It’s so late! And you took him out—didn’t even put a shawl on him, nothing!”

Harihar replied, “Ah, taking him out is such a headache! He goes here, he goes there, I can’t keep him in check—he tried to pull at the alkushi fruits. Then, looking at his son, he said, ‘You wanted to see the kuthir field, the kuthir field—well, did you see the kuthir field now?’”

**  
From Pather Panchali, Chapter Eight

--- Page 35 ---
Morning. Eight or maybe nine o’clock. Harihar’s son sits on the veranda, lost in his own world, playing. He has a small tin box, its lid broken. He’s upended all the treasures from the box onto the floor. There’s a faded wooden horse, worth four paisa, a dented tin whistle with peeling paint, a handful of cowrie shells. These he had secretly taken from his mother’s Lakshmi puja cowrie pouch, and, always afraid someone might find out, he keeps them carefully hidden. There’s a two-paisa toy pistol, a few dried nataa fruits. His didi, thinking them pretty, had gathered many from somewhere—some she gave to him, some she kept in her own doll’s box. There are also a few shards of broken tiles. He keeps these in the box with special care, believing his aim with them is unfailing when playing Ganga-Jamuna—a game of skill—and so they are his most precious possessions. 

Of all these things, he’s only played the tin whistle a few times, and, his curiosity spent, set it aside. The wooden horse has been handled enough; it too lies off to the side, like a prisoner in a shelter. Now, holding the tile shards, he closes his eyes and, imagining the Ganga-Jamuna grid drawn on the veranda, tosses the pieces to see if his aim is true.

Just then, his didi, Durga, calls out from beneath the jackfruit tree in the courtyard—“Apu! O Apu!” She hadn’t been home all this while; she’s just come back from somewhere. There’s a note of caution in her voice. Hearing someone’s footsteps, Apu, like a wind-up doll, hurriedly hides the cowries from Lakshmi’s pouch. Then he calls out, “What is it, didi?”

Durga waves her hand, beckoning—“Come here—listen—”

Durga is about ten or eleven years old. She’s slender, her complexion not as fair as Apu’s, a little dusky. Glass bangles on her wrists, a dirty dress, hair rough and flying in the breeze. Her face is well-shaped, and her eyes, like Apu’s, are wide and bright. Apu climbs down from the veranda and comes close. “What is it?” he asks.

In Durga’s hand is a garland of coconut flowers. She lowers it to show him, and in her hand are some slices of tender mango. In a hushed voice she asks, “Ma hasn’t come back from the ghat yet, has she?”

Apu shakes his head—“No—”

Durga whispers, “Can you bring a little oil and a little salt? I want to pickle the mango slices—”

Apu, delighted, bursts out, “Where did you get them, didi?”

--- Page 36 ---
Durga said, “It was lying under the sindurkota (a bush with red berries) in the Potols’ garden—bring a little salt and oil, will you?”

Apu, looking at his didi, said, “If I touch the oil jar, Ma will scold me! And my clothes are dirty!”

“You just go quickly, Ma won’t be back for a long time—she’s gone to wash the ash—hurry up—”

Apu said, “Give me the coconut garland. I’ll pour it in there and bring it. You go to the back door and see if Ma is coming.”

Durga whispered, “Don’t spill the oil on the floor, be careful, or Ma will find out—you’re such a silly boy—”

Apu slipped out of the house, and as he returned, Durga took the garland from his hands and mixed the mangoes well—she said, “Here, hold out your hand.”

“Will you eat all that, didi?”

“Is that so much? This is nothing—alright, take two more—ah, they look so good! Can you bring a chili? I’ll give you another one then—”

“How can I reach the chili, didi? Ma keeps them on the shelf, I can’t reach that high!”

“Then leave it, we’ll get it later—there are so many little mangoes on the Potols’ tree by the pond—they fall in the midday sun—”

All around Durga’s house was jungle. Harihar Ray’s cousin, Neelmoni Ray, had passed away just last year, and his wife, with her children, had gone back to her father’s house. So that neighboring homestead too was overgrown with wild thickets. There were no other houses nearby. Only after a five-minute walk would you reach Bhuban Mukherjee’s home.

Harihar’s own house hadn’t been repaired in many years—the front veranda was broken, wild herbs and kalmegh (a medicinal plant) grew in the cracks—the doors and windows were all broken, tied to the bars with coconut rope.

The back door rattled open, and a moment later Sarbajaya’s voice was heard—“Dugga, oh Dugga—”

Durga said, “Ma is calling, go see—eat the rest over there—there’s salt powder on your mouth, wipe it off—”

--- Page 37 ---
Even as Ma’s call reached her ears once more, Durga had no chance to reply—her mouth was full. Hurriedly, she began to eat the half-ripe mango slices she had gathered. Seeing there was still plenty left, she slipped over to the jackfruit tree and, hiding behind its trunk, began to gulp them down greedily. Apu stood beside her, swallowing his share as fast as he could, for there was no time left to chew. As he ate, he glanced at his didi with a sheepish, guilty grin. Durga, finishing off the last slice in one pull, flung the string of mango peels over the fence of the verenda tree, into the wild thicket by Neelmoni Ray’s homestead. Looking at her brother, she said, “Wipe your mouth, you little monkey! There’s still salt stuck to it…”

Afterwards, Durga slipped innocently into the house and called out, “Yes, Ma?”

“Where had you gone off to, may I ask? How many places do I have to go alone? I’ve been scrubbing with lye since morning—my whole body aches. If only I could get a little help from you two… Such a big girl, and you can’t even break a twig for the household fire—just running wild from lane to lane, begging for scraps… And where’s that little monkey gone?”

Apu came in and said, “Ma, I’m hungry!”

“Wait, wait, just a moment, bapu… Let me catch my breath! All day and night it’s hunger with you two, and all day and night it’s errands! O Dugga, see why the calf is bawling?”

A little later, Sarbajaya sat on the kitchen veranda, setting the boti (a traditional curved blade) to slice cucumber. Apu sat beside her and said, “Ma, can’t you bring out a little more flour? My mouth feels all puckered.”

Durga, holding out her hand for her share, asked shyly, “Is there no more chaler bhaja (puffed rice), Ma?”

As he ate, Apu said, “Ugh, I can’t chew anymore. My teeth are all on edge from the mangoes—”

But Durga’s frown and warning wink cut him off mid-sentence. Their mother asked, “Where did you get mangoes?”

Not daring to tell the truth, Apu looked at his didi for help. Sarbajaya turned to her daughter and said, “You went out again, didn’t you?”

Durga replied, flustered, “Ask him, not me! I was—just now, standing under the jackfruit tree—when you called, I was right there—”

But the arrival of Swarna the cowherd woman, coming to milk the cow, cut the conversation short. Their mother said, “Go, catch the calf—been calling and calling—Kamala, calf, oh Sanno, do you think it’ll survive if you come this late? If you don’t come a bit earlier, the calf will be tied up till midday—”

--- Page 38 ---
Following didi, Apu too went to watch the milking of the cow. The moment he set foot on the outer courtyard, Durga landed a solid, resounding punch on his back and exclaimed—“You little monkey, you Lakshmi-less brat!” Then, making a face, she said—“My teeth are all sour from eating mangoes—don’t you ever ask me for mangoes again—I’ll give you ashes instead—just now I’ll sneak over to the kakurtali* by the Patolis and gather mangoes, the fruits have grown so big, sweet as jaggery—want me to give you some? Want to eat now? Silly thing—if you had even a bit of sense!”

(*kakurtali: the base of a cucumber trellis, often a shady spot in rural Bengal)

A little after noon, Harihar finished his work and returned home. These days, he worked as a steward at the house of Annada Ray, a villager. He asked—“Have you seen Apu?”

Sarbajaya replied—“Apu is sleeping inside.”

“Durga, then, I suppose—”

“She’s gone out after eating—when is she ever home? Her only connection to the house is those two meals! She’ll only come back when she’s hungry again—who knows in whose orchard, under whose mango or jamun tree she’s wandering—in this blazing Chaitra* sun, and then just watch, she’ll come down with a fever again—such a big girl, how many times must I explain? Does she ever listen, does she ever pay heed?”

(*Chaitra: the last month of the Bengali calendar, corresponding to March-April, known for its harsh, dry heat)

A little later, as Harihar sat down to eat, he said—“Today I’d gone to Dashghara to collect dues, you know? There’s a man there, very influential, has five or six granaries at home, quite a wealthy fellow—on seeing me, he bowed down and said—‘Dada-thakur, do you recognize me?’ I said—‘No, my friend, I don’t recall—’ He said—‘Back when your father was alive, he would always come to our house for every puja and ceremony, would bless us with the dust of his feet. You people are like our gurus, this time we’re thinking of taking mantras for the whole family—so if you permit, I’d like to ask—why don’t you give us the mantra yourself?’ So I told them, I won’t say anything today, let me think it over for a day or two—do you understand?”

Sarbajaya, holding a bowl of dal, had been standing; now she set the bowl down on the floor and sat in front of him. She said—“Yes, well, what’s wrong with that? Give them the mantra, what’s their caste?”

Harihar lowered his voice and said—“Don’t tell anyone—Sadhgop. You, with your habit of spinning stories everywhere—”

--- Page 39 ---
––Whom shall I go to and tell again? Let it be, Sadgop, just give it and be done—this pain is becoming unbearable. Those eight rupees from the Ray family are my only hope, and even that comes only every two or three months—while on this side, debts are piling up. Yesterday, on the path by the ghat, Sej Thakrun said—Bouma, I don’t lend money without a pledge—but since you pleaded so much, I agreed. Now it’s been five whole months, I can’t keep the money any longer. And Radha Boshtom’s wife is tearing me apart, pestering me twice a day. The boy has no clothes—patched up in two or three places, yet my darling wears them and dances around with a smiling face—sometimes I feel like just leaving it all behind and running away—

––And there’s something else they were saying, did you know? They said, there’s no Brahmin in the village, if you come and settle here, they’ll give you land and a place to live—they very much wish to have a Brahmin family in their village. They’re even willing to give some paddy fields—money is no issue! These days, Lakshmi resides in the farmers’ homes—it’s the respectable folk who are left begging for rice—

In her eagerness, Sarbajaya almost stopped speaking—right then and there. So why didn’t you agree? You could have just said, all right, we’ll come! Shelter under such a great person—do you have anything like that in this village? Just clinging to this homestead—

Harihar smiled and said—Silly! Is one to agree right away? They’re common folk, they’ll think the priest’s pot has gone up on the shelf—no, that would be too hasty—let me quietly consult with Majumdar Mahashoy first—and even if we decide to leave, can we just get up and go? All those fellows will come and demand money, or they won’t let us leave—let’s see how things stand after I’ve had a word with him—

At that moment, their daughter Durga came tiptoeing from somewhere, and, peeking cautiously from behind the outer door, saw that everyone inside was fully alert. So she slipped along the wall to the outer veranda of the house. She gently pushed at the door of the main house—it was shut. Standing on the veranda was impossible; the sun’s heat scorched her feet, so she stepped down and stood beneath the jackfruit tree in the courtyard. Her face was flushed from wandering in the sun, and in the corner of her sari she had carefully tied up a few treasures. She had come hoping that if the outer door was open and Ma was asleep, she could slip quietly into the house and lie down for a while. But she didn’t have the courage to enter the house through the front door in front of Baba, and especially Ma.

--- Page 40 ---
Stepping down into the courtyard, she stood beneath the jackfruit tree, uncertain what to do next. Listlessly, she glanced this way and that. Then, sitting down right there, she untied the corner of her anchol (the loose end of her sari) and took out a handful of dried rãḍã fruit seeds. For a while she gazed at them, then, lost in her own thoughts, began to count them—one—two—three—four...twenty-six in all. Next, she placed two or three seeds at a time on the back of her hand, tossed them up, and tried to catch them with her palm turned upwards. In her mind she thought—these I’ll give to Apu—these I’ll keep in the doll’s box—how glossy and oily the seeds are—they must have fallen from the tree just today. Lucky I came early, otherwise the cows would have eaten them all. That Rangi cow of theirs is a real rakshas (ogre)—she goes everywhere. Last time I brought some, and now with these, I have quite a collection.

She stopped playing and carefully tied up all the seeds once more in the corner of her anchol. Then, as if struck by a sudden thought, she tossed her rough hair in the breeze and, brimming with happiness, strode straight out of the house again.

*Pather Panchali*, Chapter Nine

A little distance from Apu’s house stood a great aswattha (sacred fig) tree. Only its crown could be seen from their veranda or through the window. Apu would often gaze in that direction. Each time he looked, it seemed to him as if it belonged to a land far, far, far away—a country he could not quite imagine—somewhere, some place—he only heard of such lands from his mother’s lips, in tales of princes from distant realms.

--- Page 41 ---
A sense of wonder-laced joy would blossom in his child’s heart at tales of faraway places. The blue sky—that was so far away, the kite—the field by the hut, so distant—he could not explain it, could not tell anyone, but these words would make his mind soar off somewhere far away. And the strangest thing of all was that, just as this imagination of distant places pressed down upon his heart and seemed to carry him off somewhere, at that very moment he would be overcome with longing for his mother. Wherever he was going, his mother was not there, and immediately his heart would ache to run back to her. How many times this had happened! A kite would be flying far away against the sky—growing smaller, smaller, smaller—slipping past the tall head of Neelu’s palm tree, slowly vanishing into the distant blue. As he watched, the flying kite would disappear from sight, and at once he would lower his eyes, dash out from the outer house, and in a single sprint leap onto the kitchen verandah and throw his arms around his mother, busy with household chores.

Ma would say—“Look, look, see what your son is up to—let go—let go—can’t you see my hands are all sticky? Let go, Manik, my precious, my gold, see, I’m frying shrimp for you—don’t you love shrimp? Now don’t be naughty—let go—”

After lunch, in the afternoons, sometimes his mother would spread her anchal (the loose end of her sari) by the window and lie down, reading aloud in a sing-song voice from the tattered Kashidasi Mahabharat. A shankhachil (brahminy kite) would call from the coconut tree near the house, and Apu, sitting nearby, would practice writing ka-kha (the Bengali alphabet) while listening, entranced, to his mother’s recitation. She would call out to Durga, “Make me a paan, Dugga.” Apu would say, “Ma, tell the story about collecting cow dung cakes?” His mother would reply, “Which story about collecting cow dung cakes? Oh, that one about Harihor? But that’s in the Annadamangal, not in this one.” Then, slipping a paan into her mouth, she would resume reading in her melodious tone—

“The king said, listen, listen, O sage’s son,
I shall tell a wondrous tale, beyond description.
A king named Somdatta lived in the land of Sindhu,
Ever envious of gods and Brahmins—”

Apu would at once hold out his hand near his mother’s face and say, “Give me a bit of paan?” Ma would take the chewed paan from her mouth and place it in her son’s outstretched palm, saying, “Ehh, it’s so bitter—these are the fault of those catechu bits, every day I tell them at the market not to bring this catechu, but still—”

--- Page 42 ---
Outside the window, gazing toward the noon sunlight-drenched sheora and ghetu groves beyond the bamboo thicket, he becomes utterly absorbed as he listens to tales from the Mahabharata—especially the stories of the Kurukshetra war. Of all the characters in the Mahabharata, it is Karna whom he loves the most. There is a certain tenderness he feels for Karna, a kind of inexplicable affection. The image comes to him: the wheel of the chariot is stuck in the earth—Karna, with all his might, is struggling with both hands to pull it free from the ground—unarmed, helpless, desperate. Ignoring Karna’s pleas and entreaties, Arjuna lets loose his arrow and kills him! As he listens to this part from his mother’s lips, little Apu’s heart fills with sorrow; he cannot hold back his tears—slipping past his eyes, they roll down his soft, downy cheeks—and along with them comes that strange joy, the joy of weeping for another’s pain, which begins to awaken in his inner world as a vivid new feeling.

That path of life—where human existence is steeped in tears, in poverty, in death, in disappointed failure, in sorrow, in compassion—Apu’s childish gaze begins to sense its faint outlines: in the musty scent of the old, tattered pages of the book, in the sweet cadence of his mother’s voice, in the sun-drenched enchantment of the afternoon, all pointing the way with invisible fingers.

When the afternoon wanes and his mother rises to her household chores, he steps outside to the veranda and, standing there, gazes some days at that distant ashwattha tree—sometimes, in the harsh sun of late Chaitra or Baisakh, the tree’s crown shimmers in a smoky haze, sometimes the languid red glow of evening wraps itself lazily around its branches. Of all these, it is the ashwattha tree bathed in the red light of dusk that stirs his heart the most.

It is as if Karna, somewhere far beyond that ashwattha tree, beneath the sky, is still, with both hands, straining every day to lift the chariot wheel from the earth—every day he tries—every day—Karna, the great hero, yet forever the object of fate’s cruelty! Not Arjuna, the triumphant warrior—who won the kingdom, won honor, who, from atop his chariot, destroyed his desperate enemy with a well-aimed arrow—but Karna, the vanquished. He who remains ever awake in the tears of humankind, who dwells as a companion in our sorrow and feeling—that is the one he loves.

--- Page 43 ---
Some days, as he listens to tales of the Mahabharata’s great war, it seems to him that the battles are scarcely described at all in that epic. To fill this lack, to finally satisfy his longing and truly savor the thing called war, he has devised a way of his own. Grasping a bakhari stick or a slender branch from some light tree as his weapon, he wanders along the path behind the house, through the bamboo grove, or circles the open yard outside, narrating to himself—  
“And then Drona—he let loose ten arrows at once! And what did Arjuna do? Why, he shot two hundred arrows in a single burst! And then—oh! What a battle! What a battle! The force of the arrows turned the world dark all around!” (Here, in his mind, he imagines as many arrows as it takes to satisfy his heart’s desire, though the course of his imagination never strays beyond what he’s heard from Ma’s lips about the battles as described in Kashidas’ Mahabharata.)  
“And then what did Arjuna do? He leapt from his chariot, shield and sword in hand—then began this battle! Duryodhan came—Bhima came—the sky itself was darkened with arrows—nothing could be seen anymore.”  
The warriors of the Mahabharata won their fame after fighting for only eighteen days, but had they lived in flesh and blood, they would have realized how ever more arduous the path to glory has become. Could they have continued, month after month, wielding their weapons with the same vigor, just to satisfy a boy’s yearning?

It is a summer day, midway through the month of Baishakh.

Near the edge of the jungle, by Neelmoni Ray’s homestead, just before noon that day, Dronacharya is in grave peril—the monkey-bannered chariot is nearly upon his neck, the Brahmastra is but a blink away from flying from Gandiva’s string, a wail rises from the Kuru army—when suddenly, from the direction of the sheora grove, someone calls out in a teasing voice—  
“Ore, Apu?”  
Apu starts, releasing the taut string of his imaginary bow, and looks up to see his didi standing among the trees, laughing at him, her eyes bright with mischief.  
As soon as Apu looks, she says—  
“Hey, you little madcap, what are you mumbling to yourself, waving your arms and legs about like that?”  
Then she runs over, plants a loving kiss on her little brother’s soft cheek, and says—  
“Crazy! What a little lunatic—what were you babbling to yourself just now?”

Apu, abashed, keeps repeating—  
“Oh, come on…was I really babbling? All right, just stop…”

At last, Durga stifles her laughter and says—  
“Come with me…”

--- Page 44 ---
Afterwards, she took Apu by the hand and led him into the woods. A little way in, she smiled, pointed with her finger, and said—“Do you see?... How many nôna (wild plums) have ripened?... Now tell me, how can we reach them?”

Apu said—“Uff, there are so many, didi!—Can’t we knock them down with a stick?”

Durga replied—“You do one thing, run home and bring the ankushi (hooked stick), will you? If we pull with the ankushi, they’ll fall down, you’ll see—”

Apu said—“You wait here, didi, I’ll go get it—”

When Apu brought the ankushi, the two of them tried together for a long time, but couldn’t get more than four or five fruits down—the tree was so tall, and the ripest fruits were on the highest branches, even Durga couldn’t reach them with the ankushi. At last she said—“Let’s take these for today, we’ll bring Ma with us at bath time—Ma’s hand will reach for sure. Give me the nôna, you take the ankushi. Want to wear a nôlk (nose-ring made from flowers)?”

On the top of a low bush, clusters of white buds bloomed on the orkolmi (morning glory) vines. Durga set down the fruits in her hand and began to pick the flower buds nearby. She said—“Come here, let me put on your nôlk—”

Her didi loved to wear orkolmi flower nôlk, and often wandered through the woods searching for them, making her own and, more than once, putting them on Apu as well. Apu, however, secretly didn’t like wearing a nôlk. He wanted to say he didn’t need it. But he said nothing, afraid of didi. He never wanted to upset her, because it was didi who roamed the wilds, gathering kul (jujube), jam (black plum), nôna (wild plum), amra (hog plum), and secretly fed them to him—things they were probably forbidden to eat as “unwholesome,” so even if it was wrong, he never dared disobey didi.

Breaking off a bud, Durga squeezed out the milky sap and used it to stick the bud to Apu’s nose, then put one on herself. Then, turning her brother’s chin towards her, she said—“Let me see, how does it look? Ah, it’s perfect! Let’s go show Ma—”

Apu, embarrassed, said—“No, didi—”

“Come on—don’t take it off—looks wonderful—”

Back home, Durga set the nôna fruits down on the kitchen veranda. Sarbajaya was cooking—seeing the fruits, she was delighted and said—“Where did you find these?”

Durga said—“In the litchi grove—there are lots, will you come pick some tomorrow, Ma? So ripe—red as sindur (vermilion)—”

Stepping out from behind the corner, she said—“Look, Ma—”

--- Page 45 ---
Apu, wearing a nose-ring, stands behind didi. Sarbajaya laughs and says—“Oh my! And who is this now?—I can’t seem to recognize—”

Apu, embarrassed, quickly removes the flower bud from the tip of his nose. He says—“Didi put it on me—”

Suddenly Durga exclaims—“Come on, Apu, listen, somewhere a dugdugi is playing, let’s go, the monkey show must have arrived, hurry—”

Durga runs out of the house first, with Apu following close behind. Down the path ahead, it isn’t a monkey show, but Chinibas Moyra from the other side of the village, carrying sweets on his head, out hawking his wares. His shop is across the way, and besides that, he also trades in jaggery and paddy. But with little capital, he never quite manages to make a go of it, failing after only a short while. Then, perhaps, he goes from market to market with potatoes and gourds on his head, sometimes selling betel leaves. When even that doesn’t work out, he slings a bag over his shoulder and starts peddling whatever he can. Later, all of a sudden, one day you might see him again, hawking blocks of lime on his head. People say there’s nothing—except fish—that he hasn’t tried to sell. Tomorrow is Dashahara, so people are already buying up murki and sandesh to keep at home. Chinibas Harihar Ray passes by the door, but doesn’t enter the house. He knows the people here never buy anything. Still, seeing Durga and Apu standing at the door, he asks—“Want some?”

Apu looks at didi’s face. Durga shakes her head at Chinibas—“No—”

Chinibas goes to Bhuban Mukherjee’s house and sets down his tray, and at once the children of the house gather around him, clamoring. Bhuban Mukherjee is a well-off man, with five or six granaries in his house; in this village, after Annada Ray, his name is the one spoken of when it comes to land and property.

Bhuban Mukherjee’s wife has been dead for many years. Now, his younger brother’s widow is the mistress of the household.

The younger bou must be over forty, and she is known for her extremely strict temperament.

--- Page 46 ---
Here is your requested translation, maintaining the lyrical, evocative style and all cultural terms as specified:

---

Sej-bou took a polished brass tray and, going to Chinibash’s shop, bought murki, sandesh, and batasha for the Dashahara puja. Bhuban Mukherjee’s children and her own son, Sunil, were standing there; she bought treats for them as well. Later, seeing Apu standing in the courtyard with Durga, following behind Chinibash, Sej-bou placed her hand on her son Sunil’s shoulder, gave him a gentle push, and said—Go on, climb up onto the veranda and eat there. These are offerings for the gods; if you drop crumbs here, it will become impure.

Chinibash balanced his tray on his head and set off again for another house. Durga said—Come, Apu, let’s go see Tunudi’s house—

As they crossed the main doorway, Sej-bou turned her face and called out—Can’t bear to watch, bapu. What a greedy little thing she is—has her own home, why not go sit and eat there? No, she must go door to door, just like her mother—

Once they were outside the house, Durga, in a reassuring tone to her brother, said—Chinibash’s food is nothing special! Just wait, when Rath festival comes, we’ll get four coins from Baba—two for you, two for me. You and I will buy murki and eat together—

After a while, thinking it over, Apu asked—How many days until Rath, didi?

---

Pather Panchali  
Chapter Ten

Several months have passed.

Sarbajaya drew water from the well at Bhuban Mukherjee’s house; Apu followed behind, clutching the end of his mother’s sari, and came back from that house. Setting down the pitcher, Sarbajaya said—So tell me, why do you keep trailing behind me like this? I have to finish the housework before I can go to the ghat, don’t I? Will you let me work or not—hmm?

Apu said—That’s fine—you can do your work later, Ma, you go to the ghat now. Then, hoping to win his mother’s sympathy, he said in a most pitiful voice—Don’t I get hungry too? It’s been four days since I’ve eaten.

—What can I do if you haven’t eaten? Roaming about in the sun, you’ll catch a fever, do you even listen when I tell you? I have to finish my chores before I can go to the ghat. Am I just sitting idle? Don’t be so naughty—I can’t do everything just the way you children want, go on now—

Apu gripped his mother’s sari even tighter and said—I’ll never let you work again. You work every day, can’t you skip it just once? Go to the ghat right now—no, I won’t listen… let’s see how you manage to work!

--- Page 47 ---
Sarbajaya looked at her son and smiled, saying—“Don’t be so naughty, chi! That’s enough now, just wait a little longer—let me go down to the ghat, I’ll come running back and serve your rice—why are you being so mischievous? Let go of my anchal (the loose end of her sari), tell me, how many palta’r bara (a kind of fried snack) will you eat?”

About an hour later, Apu sat down to eat, brimming with excitement.

He picked up the glass and gulped down half of it in one go, then after a few more bites, he scattered some rice beneath his plate, finished the rest of the water, and sat back, raising his hands.

—“Where’s your eating gone, where? All this time you were panting for rice—rice, rice—palta’r bara—palta’r bara—and now you’ve left it all, did you even eat anything?”

Sarbajaya sat down with a bowl of dudh-bhaat (rice mixed with milk) to feed her son. “Let’s see, open your mouth—look at your forehead—neither monda nor mithai (types of sweets), just a couple of mouthfuls of rice and more rice—look at the state of this boy—every day, as soon as it’s time to eat, your face turns all sour—will you survive eating like this? Did you come here to live, or just to trouble me? Don’t turn your face away like that, chi! Open your mouth, my little Lakshmi—let’s see, just finish this last morsel and you’ll be done—later, at Tunudi’s house, they’ll immerse the idol of Manasa (the snake goddess)—don’t you know? Eat quickly, quickly, and then we’ll go. All of us—”

Durga entered the house. Who knows where she’d been wandering. One foot dust-covered, a lock of hair at her forehead standing straight up, nearly four fingers high. She was always wandering in her own world—she didn’t play much with the other boys and girls of the para (neighborhood). Where the baichi (wild berries) had ripened in some thicket, which tree in whose garden had set tiny mangoes, where the shiyakul (a type of sweet wild fruit) tasted best under the bamboo grove—all these things she knew like the back of her hand. As she walked along the path, her eyes always roamed alertly on either side—was there a glassy-winged insect perched somewhere? If she spotted a ripe fruit on the thorny kantikari bush, she’d immediately squat down to pick it, to use as an eggplant in her make-believe kitchen. Sometimes, she’d sit by the roadside, testing different pieces of broken pottery, seeing which made the best target for her Ganga-Jamuna game—whichever piece proved best in her trials, she’d carefully tie it into her anchal. She was always busy, bustling about with her box of dolls and the tools for her little house.

--- Page 48 ---
He entered, casting a guilty glance at his mother. Sarbajaya said––So you’re back! Come, the rice is ready. Eat and grant me deliverance––then, if you must, go off again wherever you please. It’s a day in the month of Boishakh––look at all the other girls, Sanchuti is weaving, some are worshipping Shiva––and you, such a grown girl––all day and night, just wandering about. Out since the crack of dawn, and now, after midday has long passed, you finally return home––just look at the state of your hair! Not a drop of oil, not a touch of a comb––who would believe you’re a Brahmin’s daughter? You look just like one of the Dule or Bagdi girls––you’ll end up marrying into one of their houses, I’m sure––and what treasures have you tied up in your sari’s end––untie it––

Durga, trembling with fear, began to untie the knot in her sari and said––over by the Ray-kaka’s house, on the kalka-sunda tree––then, swallowing hard, she added––these are all for Benebou.

There are many in this world whose hearts would not melt at the mention of Benebou. But Sarbajaya, fuming with anger, said––Curse your Benebou! All you do is wander about day and night, gathering ashes and rubbish––if I don’t snatch your doll’s box and throw it into the bamboo grove’s pond today––

Before Sarbajaya could finish, an incident occurred. First came the second mistress of Bhuban Mukherjee’s house, and behind her, her daughter Tunu and her brother-in-law’s son Satu, and behind them, four or five more children, all entering through the front door. Without looking at anyone or exchanging a word, the second mistress marched straight to the inner veranda. Turning to her son, she said––Well, bring it––bring out the doll’s box, let me see––

Before anyone in this house could say a word, Tunu and Satu together brought out Durga’s tin doll’s box from the room and set it down on the veranda. Tunu opened the box and, after rummaging a bit, pulled out a string of beads and said––Look, Ma, here’s my necklace––the one that went missing the other day while we were playing, she stole it that day.

Satu searched a corner of the box and pulled out a handful of mango stones, saying––Look, Jethima, she picked these from our Sonamukhi tree.

The whole thing happened so suddenly, and their movements seemed so mysterious to everyone in the house, that for a while no one could utter a word. At last, Sarbajaya managed to find her voice and said––What is it, Khurima? What’s happened? Then, anxiously, she hurried up from the kitchen veranda.

--- Page 49 ---
––Just look at this, will you, just see what your daughter has done––the other day, while playing, she stole this bead necklace from Tunur’s doll box––the poor girl has been searching for days, worn out with worry. Then Satu went and said, “I saw your bead necklace inside Durgadidi’s box”––just look at this business––as if your daughter is any less! A thief––a thief among thieves––and look over there––the mangoes from the garden, they can’t even wait to ripen––she’s stolen them and hidden them away in her box.

Caught off guard by the sudden accusation of two thefts at once, Durga leaned against the wall, sweating nervously. Sarbajaya asked, “Did you bring this necklace from their house?”

Before Durga could answer, Sej-bou chimed in, “If she hadn’t, would I say so for nothing? And look at these mangoes, will you? Do you know Sonamukhi mangoes or not? Is that a lie too?”

Sarbajaya, flustered, replied, “No, Sejkhuri, I never said you were lying! I’m just asking her.”

Sej-thakrun waved her hand sharply and said with heat, “Ask all you want, but I’m telling you, this girl will never turn out straight––if she’s taken to thieving at this age, you’ll see what comes next! Come on, Satu––tie up those mangoes––if the garden’s mangoes can survive the mischief of this good-for-nothing girl, I’ll be surprised! Tunu, did you get your necklace back?”

Something like anger stirred in Sarbajaya––she was not one to back down from a quarrel. She said, “I don’t know about the bead necklace, Sejkhuri, but the mangoes, whether she plucked them or just picked them up from the ground, there’s no name written on them, Sejkhuri––and even if a child did bring them––”

Sej-thakrun, now blazing with fury, said, “Well, you’re speaking quite sharply, aren’t you? Maybe there’s no name written on the mangoes, but can you say which garden they came from? Money doesn’t have names written on it either––but you managed to take it, didn’t you? It’s been over a year now, always ‘I’ll give it tomorrow, I’ll give it the day after’––‘I’ll come by in the afternoon, give me the money then’––I can’t keep waiting any longer––I’m telling you, have the money ready.”

--- Page 50 ---
With her whole retinue, Sej-thakrun stepped out through the door. Sarbajaya overheard her, on the path outside, replying to someone in a voice loud enough to carry—“That girl from this house, she stole this string of glass beads from Tunu’s box and hid it away in her own box—what else do you expect? And look at these mangoes—there’s the orchard right next door, she just picks as many as she pleases—so I went to say something, and then, oh my, she starts cutting me off, saying—” (here Sej-bou mimicked Sarbajaya’s way of speaking) “—well—children bring things—children always bring such things—was your name written on it or something?” (her voice dropped) “As if the mother’s any less a thief—how else would the daughter learn? The whole house is nothing but thieves—”

Humiliated, wounded, Sarbajaya’s eyes filled with tears. She turned and, grabbing a fistful of Durga’s rough, tangled hair, began to rain blows and slaps on her back with her rice-and-dal-smeared hand, scolding all the while—“Some calamity has latched onto us—if only this trouble would die, we’d be rid of it—she won’t die, won’t let us live in peace—get out of the house, go away—go, get out this instant—”

Durga, terrified and beaten, fled out the back door, slipping through the little wicket gate. A few strands of her torn, coarse hair remained in Sarbajaya’s hand.

Apu, still eating, watched the whole scene in astonishment. He didn’t know whether didi had really stolen the string of beads—he’d never seen it before—but he knew for certain that mango stones weren’t stolen things. Just yesterday evening, didi had taken him along to Tunu’s orchard to gather fallen mangoes, and there, beneath the golden cassia, a few mangoes lay on the ground; didi had picked them up. He knew, too, that since yesterday, didi had said again and again—“O Apu, this time let’s string those mango stones, shall we?” But because Ma was inconveniently at home, that plan had never come to pass. Those mangoes, so precious to didi, were taken away like this, and on top of that, didi was beaten so cruelly. Seeing Ma tear out didi’s hair, a fierce anger rose in him against her. And yet, when he saw that tuft of rough hair standing up on didi’s head, fluttering in the breeze, he felt a sudden, overwhelming tenderness for her—as if, somehow, didi had no one, nowhere—she had come here all alone, with no companion in this world. Again and again, he thought, how could he take away all didi’s sorrows—how could he fill every want, every lack? He would never let his didi suffer, not even a little.

--- Page 51 ---
After eating, Apu sat down inside the house, afraid of his mother. But again and again, his mind kept running outside. As the afternoon wore on, he searched one by one—at Tunu’s house, at Patal’s house, at the potters’ house—everywhere. Didi was nowhere to be found. Rajkrishna Palit’s wife was returning from the ghat [riverbank] with water—he asked her, “Jethima [aunt, father’s elder brother’s wife], have you seen my didi? She hasn’t eaten today, hasn’t eaten anything—Ma beat her badly today—after being beaten, she’s run away somewhere—have you seen her, Jethima?”

Walking along the path beside the house, he wondered—what if she’s sitting in the bamboo grove? He went there and searched every corner. Entering the house through the back door, he saw that no one was home. His mother had probably gone to the ghat or somewhere else. The shadows of evening were beginning to fall across the house. Near the front door, where the bamboo thicket leaned over, his familiar yellow bird with the trailing tail had come and perched on a dry, drooping bamboo branch. Every day, just before dusk, it would come from somewhere and sit on that very branch of the bamboo grove—every day—every day—every day. All around, so many other birds were chirping in the woods. The abandoned homestead of Nilmoni Ray was thick with the deep shade of trees and shrubs. Standing on the veranda, Apu looked toward the top of that distant aswattha [sacred fig] tree—there was still a little red sunlight smeared on its crown, and something white swayed at the very tip, perhaps an egret, or maybe someone’s kite, torn and caught, hanging there. Across the whole sky, it seemed as if shadow and darkness were slowly descending. All around, silence… no one anywhere… On Nilmoni Ray’s abandoned land, the new, dark green leaves of the taro plants gleamed. Suddenly, his heart ached with longing. How long had it been since she left, hadn’t come home, hadn’t eaten—where had didi gone?

At Bhuban Mukherjee’s house, the children were playing hide-and-seek in the yard, running and shouting. Ranu saw him and ran over—“Bhai, Apu’s here… he’ll be on our side, come on, Apu!”

Apu pulled his hand away and asked, “I won’t play, Ranudi—have you seen didi?”

Ranu asked, “Dugga? No, I haven’t seen her! Isn’t she under the bakul tree?”

--- Page 52 ---
He hadn’t even thought of the bakultala. Durga often stays there, after all. From Bhuban Mukherjee’s house, he went to the bakultala. Evening was falling—the bakul tree stood there, its branches stretching far and wide, thick and tangled, casting deep shadows underneath. Darkness pooled at its roots. No one was anywhere… unless she was hiding behind some tree or bush! He called out—“Didi, O didi! Didi!”

In the darkness of the tree, only a few egrets fluttered their wings. Apu looked up, anxious and afraid. Not far from the bakultala, by the edge of a pond, stood a date palm. It was the season of ripe dates, and sometimes his didi could be found there too. But it had grown too dark, and bamboo groves lined both sides of the pond—he didn’t have the courage to go and look there. Moving closer to the trunk of the bakul tree, he called out once or twice, his voice echoing. In the thicket of wild shrubs, some creature, startled by his cry, rustled away towards the pond.

On his way back home, he suddenly stopped in his tracks. There, right in front of him, stood the gab tree! To pass beneath this gab tree alone, after dusk—disaster! Goosebumps prickled his skin. He didn’t know why he was so afraid to walk under this tree. There was no reason, yet the fear was all the greater for it. He had never stayed out so late before—today, that thought didn’t even occur to him. If his mind hadn’t been so restless and distracted, he would never have come this way.

Apu stood for a moment, gazing into the darkness beneath the gab tree, then turned back. There was another way home—if he took a little detour through Potol’s courtyard, he could escape the unknown terror lurking under the gab tree.

Potol’s thakuma (grandmother) was sitting on the veranda at dusk, telling stories to the children as the evening breeze drifted by. Potol’s mother was in the kitchen, busy with the cooking. In the courtyard, Bidhu the fisherwoman stood by the machatala (raised bamboo platform), pressing for payment for the fish she’d sold.

Apu said, “I was looking for didi, thakuma—on my way back from the bakultala—”

Thakuma replied, “Durga just went home. Only a little while ago—run along now—she probably hasn’t reached home yet—”

He dashed off towards home at once. Behind him, Potol’s little sister Raji called out, “Come tomorrow morning, Apu—we’ve made a new house for Ganga-Yamuna (a children’s game)! Behind the rice-pounding shed, under the neem tree—tell Durga too—”

--- Page 53 ---
Arriving near their house, he suddenly stopped short—Durga was running out through the doorway, shrieking in pain—behind her, their mother was chasing after, brandishing something in her hand, striking as she ran. Durga fled down the path by the gabtala grove, her mother shouting after her from the doorway—“Go on, get out—leave for good, as if you were never born—never come back to this house again—let all misfortune and calamity be gone—let me leave you at the very edge of the village, beneath the shatim tree!”

The shatimtala was the village cremation ground. All of Apu’s body seemed to freeze, heavy and stiff as stone. His mother had just stepped inside, reaching for the clay lamp from the edge of the veranda. As he tiptoed quietly into the house, his mother looked at him and said, “And where have you been till so late, let me hear? You’ve barely eaten any rice today!”

A thousand questions stirred in his mind. Why did didi get beaten again? Where had she been all this time? What did didi eat at midday? Had she stolen something again? But out of fear, he said nothing, moving like a wind-up doll, doing as his mother told him, slipping into the room. Then, trembling, he trimmed the lamp’s wick, took out his little schoolbag, and sat down to read. He was only in the third reader—but in his bag were two thick, heavy English books, a list of Ayurvedic medicines, a tattered copy of Dashurayer Panchali (a folk ballad), and an old almanac from the year 1303 (Bengali calendar). He had gathered these from various places, and though he could not read most of them, every day he would open them, leaf through, and look.

For a while he gazed at the wall, lost in thought. Then, trimming the lamp wick again, he absentmindedly opened the tattered Dashurayer Panchali and began to turn its pages. Just then, Sarbajaya entered, carrying a bowl of milk, and said, “Come, have some, won’t you!”

Without protest, Apu took the bowl and began to sip the milk. On any other day, it would have been difficult to persuade him to drink so easily. After just a few sips, he lowered the bowl from his lips. Sarbajaya said, “What’s this? Come on, finish it all—how will you survive if you leave even this little bit of milk—”

--- Page 54 ---
Apu, without protest, once again lifted the bowl of milk to his lips. Sarbajaya saw that he was holding the bowl to his mouth, but not sipping from it—his hand, bowl and all, was trembling... After holding it to his lips for a long while, suddenly he lowered the bowl, looked at his mother, and burst into tears out of fear. Sarbajaya, astonished, said, “What happened, re? What’s wrong, did you bite your tongue?”

As soon as his mother finished speaking, Apu, unable to hold back his fear, sobbed and cried out, “I miss didi so much!...”

Sarbajaya sat silently for a moment, then moved closer, stroking her son’s body as she spoke in a gentle tone, “Don’t cry, don’t cry like that—she’s sitting at the Potolys’ house with the neighbor’s mongrel—where will she go in the dark? Isn’t she a naughty girl? Went out at noon—haven’t seen the tip of her braid all day—no food, no water, who knows where she was, sitting in the Palits’ garden across the way, eating raw mangoes and wax apples, I’m sending someone to call her right now—don’t cry like that—you’ll get a fever again—shame on you.”

Then, wiping away her son’s tears with her anchal (the loose end of her sari), she lifted the bowl to his lips to feed him the rest of the milk. “Open your mouth, my precious Lakshmi, as soon as she comes, I’ll call her for you—completely mad—where did such a mad child come from—one more sip—yes—”

It was late at night. In the northern room, Apu and Durga lay side by side on the taktaposh (wooden bedstead). The space beside Apu, where his mother usually slept, was empty. For Ma had not yet finished her work in the kitchen. His father, having finished his meal, sat in the next room smoking his hookah. Baba had gone out to the neighborhood to bring Durga home.

Since returning, Durga had not spoken a word to anyone. She had finished her meal and come to lie down quietly. Apu, reaching out to touch Durga, asked, “Didi, what did Ma hit you with in the evening? Did she pull your hair?”

Durga said nothing.

He asked again, “Are you angry with me, didi? I didn’t do anything.”

Durga replied softly, “Of course not! But how did Satu find out that the beaded necklace was in my box?”

--- Page 55 ---
Apu, fired up with protest, sat up abruptly.  
—No—truly, I swear on your life, didi, I didn’t take it. I didn’t even know it was in your box—yesterday, Satu came by in the afternoon, we were playing with his big red hibiscus flower—then, you know, didi, Satu opened your doll’s box to look at something—I told him, “Hey, don’t touch my didi’s box”—didi will scold me—he must have seen it then—

Later, he gently stroked Durga’s arm and asked,  
—Does it hurt a lot, didi? Where did Ma hit you?

Durga replied,  
—Right beside my ear, Ma gave me such a slap—blood came out, it’s still stinging, here, here, feel it with your hand! Here—

—Here? Oh, you’re right! It’s cut, isn’t it? Should I put a little lamp oil on it, didi?

—Let it be—Tomorrow afternoon, shall we go to the Palit’s garden? The starfruit are so ripe! This big, big ones—don’t tell anyone! You and I will sneak away quietly—I plucked and ate two at noon today—sweet as jaggery—

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Eleven**

This is how the events of that day unfolded.

At his father’s command, after finishing seven pages of handwriting practice on palm leaves, Apu wandered through the house, wondering what to do next, and went looking for his sister. Durga, afraid of their mother, had skipped her morning bath and was performing the ritual of Punyipukur under the papaya tree in the inner courtyard. She had dug a small square pit in the yard, scattering chickpeas and peas all around it—the damp earth had already coaxed out tiny sprouts. She’d planted little banana saplings all around and was decorating the edges with patterns of pithuli grain—lotus vines, birds, stalks of rice, the newly risen sun.

Durga said,  
—Wait, recite this mantra with me, then we’ll go somewhere.

—Where, didi?

—Come on, I’ll take you, just wait and see— Then, after finishing the necessary rituals, she began to chant in one breath:

*Punyipukur’s garland of flowers,  
Who worships at noon hour?  
I am Sati Leelabati,  
Brother’s sister, blessed with destiny—*

Apu stood listening, then, with a mocking smile, said,  
—Ehh.

Durga stopped her rhyme and, with a hint of embarrassed laughter, said,  
—Why are you acting like that? Go away—what are you doing here?—Go.

Apu laughed and walked away. As he went, he chanted,  
—I am Sati Leelabati, brother’s sister, blessed with destiny, hee hee—brother’s sister, blessed with destiny—hee—hee—

Durga said,  
—You’ve gotten too cheeky, haven’t you? I’ll tell Ma and have your silly faces sorted out—

--- Page 56 ---
After the brata ceremony was over, Durga said, “Let’s go to the pond by the old fort—there are lots of water chestnuts there. Bhoda’s mother was saying so. Come, let’s go fetch some.”

At the very northern edge of the village, the path wound through bamboo groves, tangles of wild undergrowth, and an ancient orchard of mango and jackfruit trees. Far from the bustle of the homesteads, where the deep forest finally thinned out, there by the edge of the fields lay a stagnant pond. Once, long ago, the original inhabitants—the Majumdars—had a moat encircling their house here. Most of that moat had long since filled in, but in this one hollow water remained all year round. This was called the pond of the fort—gôr-er pukur. No trace of the Majumdars’ house remained now.

When they arrived, they saw there were indeed plenty of water chestnuts in the pond, but hardly any near the bank; all of them floated far from shore. Durga said, “Apu, see if you can find a bamboo stick—if we get one, we can pull them in.” Then, from a sheora tree growing in the thicket by the pond, she plucked some ripe fruit and began to eat. Apu, searching the woods for a stick, spotted her and called out, “Hey didi, don’t eat those!—”

“Oh, silly,” Durga replied, “who eats sheora fruit! Only birds do—”

But Durga, squeezing out the seeds from the ripe fruit, said, “Come here—just try it—see how sweet it is, like jaggery! Who says you can’t eat it? I’ve eaten them plenty of times.”

Apu, abandoning his search for a stick, came over to his sister and said, “But they say if you eat it, you go mad! Give me one, didi—”

He tasted it, and his face scrunched up a little. “It’s a bit bitter, didi—”

“Well, of course it’s a bit bitter! But tell me, isn’t it sweet too?” Durga, delighted, stuffed several large, ripe fruits into her mouth at once.

Since birth, they had never tasted anything truly good. Yet they were new to this world, their tongues fresh and eager—hungry for the many flavors of the earth, especially for sweetness. They never had the chance to buy sandesh or sweets to their heart’s content—so, among the endless treasures of the world, the compassionate forest goddesses filled the humble wild trees with sweet nectar, for these greedy, poor children who gathered what they could from the woods.

After a while, Durga waded a little into the pond and said, “Look how many water lilies there are, Apu! Wait, I’m picking some.” She waded in deeper, grabbed two flowering vines, and tugged them free—then tossed them up onto the bank. “Catch, Apu!”

Apu said, “But the water chestnuts are way out in the deep—how will you get there, didi?”

--- Page 57 ---
Durga tried to pull the distant water chestnut plants closer with a bamboo stick, but she couldn’t manage it. She said, “This pond slopes so much—I'm slipping into the deep water—how will I ever reach? Do one thing, you hold on to the end of my sari from behind and pull me back, and I’ll try to hook that cluster of water chestnuts with the stick.”

Somewhere in the woods, a yellow bird sat at the tip of a mynakanta bush, fluttering its leaves and whistling a sweet, enchanting tune. Apu, watching intently, asked, “What bird is that, didi?”

“Forget the bird for now—hold on tight to my sari, or I’ll slip—hold it hard—”

Apu held onto her sari from behind. Durga, stepping carefully, stretched the bamboo stick as far as she could. Her clothes got soaked, but still she couldn’t reach. She stepped a little further, grasping the very end of the stick with her fingertips, trying to pull the cluster closer. As Apu strained to keep her from slipping, his strength began to give out, and he started laughing from behind. With his laughter, his grip loosened, and Durga toppled forward toward the water, but she caught herself just in time and laughed, saying, “Oh, you! If only you were any use—hold on again.” With great effort, she managed to draw one cluster of water chestnuts near—Durga examined it with curiosity to see how many chestnuts it bore. Then, tossing it onto the bank, she said, “Too young, the insides haven’t turned milky yet. Try again.” 

Apu once more held her sari from behind. After a while, as Durga leaned out again, Apu, tugging hard, was pulled forward a step or two toward the water—then, seeing his clothes getting wet, he gave up and burst out laughing.

Durga laughed and said, “Oh, you!”

For a while, the quiet bamboo grove by the pond brimmed with the siblings’ bright laughter. Durga said, “Is that all the strength you’ve got? Useless as a gaver’s pestle!”

A little later, Durga waded into the water to try again, while Apu stood on the bank. Just then, Apu pointed at a nearby shaora tree and shouted, “Didi, look over here!” Then he ran over, dug at the ground, and started to pull something out.

Durga, still in the water, called out, “What is it?” Then she too came over to her brother.

By then, Apu had dug something out of the earth and was wiping the dirt off with the edge of his shirt. Holding it in his hand, he showed it to his sister with delight, “Look, didi, see how it shines—what is it?”

--- Page 58 ---
Durga took it in her hand and examined it—a roundish, one-sided, pointed, straw-cut, glistening thing. For a while, she turned it over and over with keen interest, peering at it from every angle.

Suddenly, as if struck by a thought, her rough, tangled hair-framed face lit up. She glanced around nervously to see if anyone was watching. In a hushed whisper, she said, “Apu, I think this might be a diamond! Shh, don’t shout.” Again, she looked around, wary and cautious.

Apu stared at didi in astonishment. The thing called ‘diamond’ was not unknown to him—he had heard many times from Ma’s lips, from didi’s lips, tales of fairy-tale princes and princesses adorned with diamond and pearl ornaments. Yet, what a diamond actually looked like—he had a slightly mistaken idea. In his mind, a diamond resembled fish eggs: yellowish, but not soft—hard...

Sarbojaya was not at home. When she returned from the neighborhood, she saw the children standing by the door inside the house. As she approached, Durga whispered, “Ma, we found something. We went to the fort pond to gather water chestnuts, Ma. It was buried in the jungle there.”

Apu said, “I saw it and told didi, Ma.”

Durga unwrapped the thing from her anchal (the loose end of her sari) and handed it to her mother, saying, “Look, Ma, see what this is?”

Sarbojaya turned it over and over, examining it. Durga whispered, “Ma, this is surely a diamond—isn’t it?”

Sarbojaya’s idea of a diamond was not much clearer than theirs. In a doubtful tone, she asked, “How do you know it’s a diamond?”

Durga said, “Weren’t the Majumdars rich, Ma? Someone once found gold coins in the jungle by their old homestead—pishi (aunt) used to tell the story. This was buried right by the pond, in the wild, glinting in the sunlight—this is definitely a diamond, Ma.”

Sarbojaya said, “Let him come home first, we’ll show him.”

Durga, stepping out into the courtyard, said to her brother with delight, “If it’s really a diamond, just wait—we’ll become rich people!”

Apu, not understanding, laughed foolishly—hi hi.

--- Page 59 ---
When the children had left, Sarbajaya took out the thing and began to examine it closely. Round in shape, with sharp edges and a sail-like fin, one end pointed like the tip of a sindoor box’s lid. It gleamed brightly. Sarbajaya felt as if she could see many different colors shimmering within it. Yet it was certainly not glass—that much was clear. She couldn’t recall ever seeing glass quite like this before. Suddenly, a strange current seemed to rush through her whole body, and pushing aside the barricade of doubts in a corner of her mind, a deep, trembling hope peeked out—what if it truly was a diamond?

Her idea of a diamond was something like a philosopher’s stone or the jewel from a serpent’s hood—things of legend, rarely seen in the real world. And even if one did find such a thing, perhaps all the world’s riches could be had in exchange for a single piece of diamond.

A little while later, Harihar entered the house with a small bundle in his hand.

Sarbajaya said, “Ogo, listen, come here a moment! Look at this—what do you think it is?”

Harihar took it in his hand and said, “Where did you find this?”

“Dugga found it while picking water chestnuts in the pond at Dugga’s fort. She picked it up off the ground. What do you think it is?”

Harihar turned it over and over in his hand for a while and said, “Glass, or perhaps just a bit of stone—some small thing, I can’t quite tell.”

A faint glimmer of hope appeared in Sarbajaya’s heart—if it were glass, wouldn’t her husband have recognized it? Then, quietly, as if afraid he might counter her with logic, she said, “Could it be a diamond? Dugga was saying that people have found all sorts of things at the Majumdar family’s fort! What if it’s a diamond?”

“Yes, if diamonds could be found lying about on the roadside, what worries would there be? You’re just too much sometimes!”

In his mind, he decided it was glass. But the next moment, he thought—maybe, just maybe, it could be something more. Who can say? The Majumdars were wealthy. Who knows, perhaps once upon a time this was set in their jewelry, somehow buried in the earth. As the saying goes, if it’s not in your fate, even if you find treasure, you won’t recognize it—in the end, will this turn out like the tale of the poor Brahmin?

He said, “Alright, wait, let me just take it over to the Ganguli house and show them.”

As she cooked, Sarbajaya kept repeating in her mind—By your mercy, Thakur, so many people find so many things! Let this suffering end for our family—look kindly upon the children—by your mercy, Thakur!

Her heart thudded in her chest.

A little later, Durga came home and asked eagerly, “Baba hasn’t come back yet, has he, Ma?”

--- Page 60 ---
At once, Harihar entered the house and said—“Hmph, I told you right away, this is nothing at all. Ganguli Mashai’s son-in-law, Satyababu, has come from Calcutta—he took one look and said, this is a kind of Belwari glass—the sort that hangs from chandeliers. If diamonds and jewels could be found lying on the roadside, then—just like you!”

**Pather Panchali**

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Sixteen**

This time, when leaving the house, Harihar took his son along with him. He said—“He doesn’t get enough to eat at home, but if I take him out, at least he’ll have some milk, some curd—his health will improve now.”

Since the day he was born, Apu had never gone anywhere. The bakultala (cluster of bakul trees) in this village, the Gosai’s orchard, the grove of wild olive trees, the riverbank—at most, the paved road leading to Nawabganj—these marked the limits of his world. Sometimes, in the sweltering heat of Baisakh or Jyoishtho (summer months), his mother would stand by the river’s ghat in the evening. Across the river, in the straw fields, yellow blossoms would bloom on the babla trees, cows would graze, and the thick, liana-draped shimul tree looked as if it had stood there for ages. The cowherds would bring their cattle to drink by the river, and a small fishing dinghy, rowed by Atrur Majhi from their village, would drift by as he set his fish traps. Here and there in the fields, clumps of sodali flowers would sway in the gentle evening breeze—at just such a time, suddenly, on some days, looking toward the far edge of the green straw fields where the blue sky seemed to bend down and touch the distant line of the village woods, Apu’s heart would fill with a strange longing—a feeling he could neither express nor explain. Only when his didi (elder sister) climbed up from the ghat would he say—“Didi, didi, look, look over there—” Then, pointing to the far edge of the field, he would ask—“See? Behind that tree? Doesn’t it seem very far away?”

Durga would laugh and say—“Very far—that’s what you’re showing me? Silly, you’re such a dreamer!”

Today, for the very first time, that same Apu set foot outside the village. For days, excitement had kept him awake at night, counting down the days until finally, the day to leave arrived.

The path from their village curved away from the Nawabganj road to the right, and joined the dirt road to Ashadhu-Durgapur at the edge of the fields. As soon as they stepped onto the Durgapur road, he asked his father—“Baba, which way is the railway track, the one the train goes on?”

His father replied—“It’s just ahead, come on. We’ll cross the railway line now—”

--- Page 61 ---
That time, their rangī (reddish-brown) cow’s calf had gone missing. They searched in many places, but for two or three days, no trace of it could be found. He had come with his didi (older sister) to the southern fields to look for the calf. It was the month of Poush (winter), and in the fields, the kalai (black gram) plants had begun to fill their pods with grain. He and his didi, crouching low, would now and then pluck a pod from the plants and eat the tender beans—before them, a little distance away, lay the paved road of Nawabganj, and along it, a line of bullock carts laden with date-palm jaggery creaked and groaned their way toward the Ashadhu market.

His didi was gazing fixedly across the paved road, far off into the blurred expanse of the fields, as if she saw something. Suddenly, she said, “Let’s do one thing, Apu—let’s go see the railway tracks. Want to go?”

Apu looked at her face in astonishment and said, “The railway tracks? But that’s so far away! How will we get there?”

His didi replied, “So far, is it? Who told you that? It’s just across the paved road—see?”

Apu said, “If it’s close, we should be able to see it, right? You can see from the paved road—come on, didi, let’s go and look.”

The two of them climbed up onto the Nawabganj road and looked around for a long time. His didi said, “It’s really very far, isn’t it? We can’t go—”

“Can’t see anything at all—if we go that far, how will we come back?” Yet his thirsty gaze remained fixed on the distance, torn between longing and fear. Suddenly, his didi said, with a reckless determination, “Let’s go see, Apu—how far can it be, really? We’ll come back before noon, maybe a train will pass by now—if Ma asks, we’ll say we were delayed looking for the calf—”

First, they glanced this way and that, to see if anyone was watching them. Then, slipping down from the paved road, brother and sister dashed straight southward, across fields, marshes, and waterlogged patches, under the midday sun. Run, run, run—the red road of Nawabganj fell farther and farther behind—Rowar’s field, the waterside rest-house, Thakurjhi’s pond on the left, on the right, distant landmarks passed by—up ahead, a small marsh came into view. His didi looked at him with a smile and said, “If Ma finds out—she’ll skin us alive!” Apu grinned—a reckless grin. And again, run, run, run—for the first time in their lives, their young, fresh blood surged with the wild joy of unbounded, borderless freedom—who had time to think what might happen next?

--- Page 62 ---
What happened next was not at all convenient. After going a little further, they came upon a large marsh right in front of them—thick with hogla reeds and shola plants—and there, his didi lost her way. Not a single village was in sight—only endless stretches of paddy fields, marshes, and tangled cane thickets ahead. It was impossible to make their way through the dense cane groves; their feet sank into the muddy water, and the sun grew so fierce that even in winter, sweat began to pour down their bodies. Didi’s sari was torn in several places by thorns, and he himself had to pull out two or three thorns from his feet. In the end, forget the railway line—even getting back home became a struggle. They had wandered so far that the paved road was nowhere to be seen; only after wading through water and crossing paddy fields with great difficulty did they finally reach the paved road again, by which time noon had long passed. When they got home, his didi saved both their skins by spinning basketfuls of lies.

But today, the railway line would appear before him so easily—there would be no need to run, no need to lose their way—no scolding to endure!

After walking a little way, he looked with wonder to see a high road, just like the paved road at Nawabganj, cutting straight through the middle of the fields, stretching far to the right and left. Heaps of red gravel were piled high along the edges in neat rows. Upon white iron posts, it seemed as if many ropes were strung together—stretching as far as the eye could see, those white posts and taut ropes ran on and on—

His baba said, “Look, Khoka, that’s the railway line—”

Apu dashed through the gate and climbed up onto the road. Then, standing there, he gazed in awe at both sides of the railway track. Why were there two iron rails laid side by side? Did the train run on top of them? Why? Why did it travel on iron and not on the ground? Didn’t it slip and fall? Why? Were those called wires? What was that humming sound within the wires? Was news traveling through them? Who was sending the news? How did they send it? Was there a station over there? Was there a station on this side?

He asked, “Baba, when will the train come? I want to see the train, Baba.”

“How will you see the train now? The train will come around noon, there’s still two hours to go!”

“That’s all right, Baba, I’ll wait and watch, I’ve never seen it—yes, Baba—”

“Don’t be like that, that’s why I don’t want to bring you anywhere—how will you see it now? You’d have to sit here in the sun till noon if you want to see it. Come, I’ll show you on another day.”

--- Page 63 ---
At last, with eyes brimming with tears, Apu had to move forward, trailing behind his father.

You are walking on, unaware… You know nothing of what might catch your eye by the wayside, your wide, fresh eyes devouring everything around you with a world-consuming hunger—on your own journey of delight, you too are a discoverer of lands. To taste the joy of the unknown, one must wander the earth; there is no other way. Today, I set foot where I have never been before, bathed in the waters of a river new to me, felt my body soothed by the breeze of a village unknown—what does it matter to me if someone else has come here before? In my own experience, this is an undiscovered land. Today, with mind, wit, and heart, I am the very first to savor its freshness!

Amdoba! The little hamlet of peasant folk—what a name! The women are cutting straw in the yard, tying up goats, feeding rice to chickens, the elders are drying jute, chopping bamboo—watching all this, the village slips away behind, and suddenly it is the open fields… the wetlands shimmer with water… egrets sit in the ripe paddy fields… on the water, the leaves and blooming flowers of water lilies are so thick you cannot see the water at all.

At the edge of the Khalsemari wetlands, above the lush green fields of aus paddy, stretches the rain-washed, blue expanse of the Bhadra sky. All around the horizon, the sunset paints its wondrous colors—mountains of clouds, islands of clouds, seas of clouds, dream-palaces of clouds… Never before had he met the open sky like this; the distant land beyond the fields now, at last, unveiled its veil of mystery to the eight-year-old boy.

It grew very late as they walked on. His father said—"You are such a wide-eyed boy, why do you always gape at everything you see like that? Walk faster."

After dusk, they reached their destination. The disciple’s name was Lakshman Mahajan, a prosperous farmer and well-off householder. In the big, open-roofed outer room, he made a place for them to stay with great care and affection.

In the morning, Lakshman Mahajan’s younger brother’s wife came to the pond’s ghat to bathe—stepping down to the water, she noticed, in the plantain grove by the pond, an unfamiliar little boy, a stick in hand, pacing back and forth, muttering to himself like a madcap, lost in his own world. Setting down her water pot, she came closer and asked, “Whose house have you come from, khoka (little boy)?”

All of Apu’s tricks and cleverness were for his mother. Outside, he was terribly shy and tongue-tied.

--- Page 64 ---
The first thing that came to Apu’s mind was to turn and run. Then, in a timid voice, he said—“That’s their house—”

The young bride said, “The house of the Bottakurs? The son of Bottakur’s gurumoshai (spiritual teacher)? Oh!”

The bride took him along with her to her own house. Their home was separate—a little distance from Lakshman Mahajan’s house, but with a pond lying in between.

With the bride’s gentle manner, Apu’s shyness melted away. He entered the house, his eyes wide with curiosity as he gazed at everything inside. Oh! So many things!... There was nothing like this in their own home. These people must be very wealthy! A string of cowrie shells, colorful hanging tassels, a bird made of wool, glass dolls, clay dolls, trees made of shola (a spongy plant)—and so much more!—He picked up one or two things with trembling hands, turning them over and over, afraid he might break them.

Until now, the bride hadn’t really looked closely at the boy’s face—seeing him up close, she realized how very much a child he still was, his expression as innocent as a five-year-old’s. She had never seen such a look of pure wonder in any other child’s eyes—such color, such form, such a beautiful face, such large, painterly, innocent eyes—an unknown tenderness welled up in the bride’s heart for this unfamiliar boy.

Apu sat and told all sorts of stories—especially about the railway tracks laid down yesterday. After a while, the bride prepared mohanbhog (a sweet semolina dish) and served it to him. In a bowl, a generous helping of mohanbhog, so rich with ghee that his fingers were slick with it. Apu took a small bite and was astonished—he had never tasted anything so exquisite!—Why were there raisins in the mohanbhog? His mother’s mohanbhog never had raisins. At home, he would plead with his mother—“Ma, you have to make mohanbhog for me today!” His mother would smile and say, “All right, I’ll make it for you later”—then she would simply boil some semolina in water, mix in a little jaggery, and lovingly serve the pulpy mixture to her son on a brass plate. Apu had always eaten that with happiness, never knowing mohanbhog could be like this. But today, he felt there was a world of difference between this mohanbhog and his mother’s. Suddenly, his heart filled with compassion and sympathy for his mother. Perhaps his mother didn’t even know that mohanbhog could be made this way!—He seemed to realize, in a vague, shadowy way, that his mother was poor, that they were poor—that was why there was no good food in their house.

--- Page 65 ---
One day, Apu received an invitation to lunch at the home of a Brahmin neighbor from the neighborhood. At noon, a girl from that house came and called Apu, taking him along. In their kitchen courtyard, she carefully laid out a mat, sprinkled water to purify the spot, and made a place for Apu to sit and eat. The girl who had come to fetch Apu was named Amala—her complexion was bright and fair, her eyes large, her face lovely, her age about the same as didi’s.

Amala’s mother sat nearby and fed him, placing on his plate chondropuli (a sweet rice cake) she had made with her own hands. After the meal, Amala accompanied him back home.

That evening, while playing, Apu’s toe suddenly slipped into the gap between two bamboo poles in the garden fence and got stuck. The fence was made of freshly split bamboo; his toe was cut and started bleeding. Had Amala not come running and freed his foot from the bamboo, the whole toe might have been severed. He could not walk, so Amala picked him up in her arms, gathered some pathorkuchi leaves (medicinal succulents) from beside the granary, crushed them, and bound them to his toe. Fearing he might get scolded by his father, Apu told no one about what had happened.

That night, lying in bed, Apu dreamed only of Amala. He was wandering about in Amala’s lap, sitting beside her, playing with her, Amala was tying a bandage on his toe, he and Amala were running along the railway tracks—Amala’s smiling face and sparkling eyes hovered close to him all through his sleep. At dawn, he kept searching—when would Amala come? Other children arrived, and the games began; the morning wore on, but there was still no sign of Amala. From inside the house, the bou (young wife of the household) called him to eat—every morning and afternoon, bou would prepare food with her own hands and feed him. When he finished eating and was about to leave, he asked bou, “Did Amala-didi come this morning?” “No, she didn’t come.” As the day wore on, the games broke up. His father called him to bathe. Still, where was Amala? Hurt and sulking, his heart swelled—fine, let her not come! He decided he would never speak to Amala again, as if they were born enemies. In the afternoon, the games began again; everyone else came—Amala did not. Even though five or six children came to play, Apu felt—who was there to play with? None seemed a worthy playmate. Listlessly, he played for a while, but still, there was no sign of Amala.

--- Page 66 ---
The next morning, Amala came. Apu didn’t say a word. Wherever Amala sat, he wouldn’t go anywhere near her, not even within three paces, yet from time to time he would steal glances at her from the corner of his eye, as if to see whether she understood that he was sulking. Had Amala realized why he was acting this way? At first, Amala truly didn’t understand, but later, when she sensed that something must have happened, she came closer and asked, “What is it, Khoka, why aren’t you talking?... What’s wrong?”

Apu didn’t really understand all of it, but with his lips puffed out in wounded pride he replied, “As if nothing’s wrong! Of course something’s wrong! Why didn’t you come yesterday?”

Amala, surprised, said, “I didn’t come, is that it?—Is that why you’re angry?”

Apu nodded, letting her know that was exactly it. Amala burst out laughing, took Apu by the hand, and led him inside the house. There, the bou (the young wife of the house) listened to everything and at first laughed so hard she nearly choked, then, stifling her laughter, said, “Well then, Amala, looks like you can’t go home now—what else can you do, when Khoka simply can’t bear to be without you, you’ll just have to stay here—unless—”

At something in the bou’s tone, Amala seemed to catch on to something, and replied in a bashful, protesting voice, “Oh, stop it, boudi—if you tease me like that, I’ll never come to your house again—”

A little while later, Apu went with Amala to her house. Amala opened up their almirah (cabinet) and showed him her big glass mem-puppet (European-style doll), a wax bird, trees, and so many other things. She said they’d bought them all at the fair during the Snan Yatra festival in Kaliganj, Apu learned when he asked. So many new toys—a rubber monkey, which, no matter where you went, would blink its eyes and look right at you—a strange doll, which, if you pressed its belly, would suddenly flail its arms and legs like someone with epilepsy and play a khanjani (a small tambourine)—but the most marvelous thing of all was a tin horse. If you wound it up, just like Ranudi’s uncle winds the clock in their house, and set it down, it would rattle and run across the floor—going quite far—just like a real horse. Seeing that, Apu was amazed. He picked it up, turned it over and over in wonder, and looking at Amala, asked, “What kind of horse is this, it’s so nice! Where did you buy it, how much did it cost?”

Then Amala opened a little box of sindur (vermilion) and showed it to him—inside was a small, shiny red thing, like a bit of tinsel. Apu asked, “What’s that? Tinsel?”

Amala laughed and said, “Why would it be tinsel? Haven’t you seen gold leaf, Apu?”

--- Page 67 ---
Apu had never seen a golden leaf before. Is the color of gold really so bright, so radiant? He turned the golden leaf over and over in his hands, examining it closely. As he walked home with Amala, he thought—ah, didi has none of these toys—she spends her days gathering only dry nut shells and tamarind seeds, and gets scolded for stealing other children’s dolls!... He had never seen any girl of didi’s age with such riches of playthings; today, having the chance to compare, his heart melted with a deep pity for his sister. If he had money, he would buy didi a wind-up horse— and a rubber monkey… one that blinks its eyes at you, wherever you go, always looking your way…

--- Page 68 ---
The bride had an old set of playing cards—not really a complete set, just a gathering of discarded cards from various decks, all bundled together. Apu would sometimes take them out and shuffle through them, turning them over in his small hands. At Ranudi’s house, sometimes in the afternoons, a card game would gather.
