He would sit quietly, watching the play unfold. Tekka, Golam, Saheb, Bibi—their Bengali names for the cards—hands would clash over the paper soldiers, the air thick with playful rivalry. What a game it was! He didn’t know how to play, nor did his mother, nor did any of his didis. 

Some days, his mother would be invited to play, but no one really wanted her on their team. “She doesn’t know how to play at all,” they would say. On rare days, she would sit down at the table, putting on the air of a seasoned player—but soon enough, she would be found out. Someone would exclaim, “O bou, what’s this? You played a tekka here? Didn’t you see the Golam of that suit was cut in the other hand—right before your eyes?” His mother would quickly try to cover her ignorance, laughing, “Oh, is that so! What a mistake, Thakurjhi, I just didn’t remember at all.” Then she would go on playing, pressing her lips together to hide a smile, glancing this way and that, as if she knew all the secrets of everyone’s hands, as if she wouldn’t let this round pass without doing something clever. But soon enough, someone would cry out in astonishment, “What’s this, Bouma, let me see? Oh dear, what will happen now! You had the Binti all along and didn’t show it?” 

His mother, still smiling, would put on a wise face and say, “Yes, yes, there’s something special about this! I didn’t show it on purpose.” In truth, she didn’t really know what made a Binti. The real players would scold her, “What’s this ‘something special,’ let’s hear it? You’ve ruined the hand! Give the cards to Sejbou, give them, you don’t need to play anymore—you’ve played enough.” 

His mother, trying to hide her embarrassment, would laugh again—as if nothing had happened, as if it was all just a joke, and they were only teasing her, and she was taking it in the same spirit…

--- Page 69 ---
If he gets a deck of cards, then he, Ma, and Didi play together. After lunch, in the afternoon, by that window at the edge of the forest near their house… the one whose shutters—what insect has gnawed them, turning them to powder like mustard seed—if you shake them, the dust falls in a soft shower, and the scent of old wood rises. From the forest by the window, the afternoon breeze brings the sharp smell of gandhabhadali creepers; in the jungle of kalmegh bushes by the veranda, Didi’s familiar green beetle flits up, settles, flits up again, settles again—on that lonely afternoon, the three of them will sit by that window on a mat, lost in their own world, playing cards. Who wins or loses—does it matter if no one knows? Even if no one shows their hand, the game will go on—no one will force another to get up, no one will speak a word of insult, no one will laugh or mock; each will play as they can, in their own way. Let the game be about playing, not about showing off a win!

After evening, Apu was invited to the bride’s room. Sitting down to eat, he was astonished by the array of food and the grandeur of the arrangements. Why, in a small flower-patterned dish, were salt and lemon served separately? Ma always puts salt and lemon on the plate herself. For every curry, a separate bowl!—and how many curries there were! Was that enormous prawn head really meant just for him?

Luchi! Luchi! Beyond his and Didi’s dreams and desires, in the blue-tinted haze of a fairy-tale land, it appears… How many nights and days, eating rice with ol’er daata chachchari (a curry of yam stems) and lau chhenchki (grated bottle gourd), through so many mornings and afternoons empty of snacks or treats, his absent-minded mind would suddenly, in a wistful rush, race away to that place—where, in the hot noon sun, their neighborhood’s master cook Biru Ray roams with a gamchha (cotton towel) on his shoulder, a large iron kadai (wok) sits on the big oven with ghee heating, the incomparable aroma of frying luchis fills the air, children in their best clothes come and go, the Ganguli family’s great natmandir (temple stage) and the olive grove are spread with mats, and on a single day each year, the address of that land is found—on that day of Ramnavami Dol (spring festival) in the month of Chaitra or Baisakh—their invitation comes from the Ganguli house across the way. But today, how unexpectedly has that blessed day dawned! Sitting down to eat, again and again he thought, Ah, Didi has never eaten like this, not even once!

--- Page 70 ---
The next morning, the games began again. As soon as Amala arrived, Apu ran up and grabbed her hand—“I’ll be with Amala-didi on one side, and all of you on the other—”

After a little while, Apu began to feel that Amala was more eager to have Bishu on her team than him. He didn’t quite understand why—Apu was a complete novice at games, having him on your side almost guaranteed defeat—while Bishu was a mischievous, quick-footed boy, hard to catch or beat at any sport. Once, Amala even showed her annoyance quite openly. Apu tried with all his might to win, to make Amala happy—but despite all his efforts, he lost again.

That time, when it came to forming teams, Amala leaned towards Bishu.

Apu’s eyes filled with tears. Suddenly, the game seemed utterly tasteless to him—Amala was turning to Bishu for everything, laughing and chatting with him. After a while, when Bishu said he needed to go home for something, Amala kept urging him to come back again and again. Apu felt a sharp pang of jealousy, the whole morning seemed empty and hollow! Then he tried to reassure himself—Bishu is leaving the game—if he goes, there’ll be fewer players, that’s why Amala-didi is asking him to stay; if I were to leave, she’d ask me too, maybe even more than him. Suddenly, he pretended to leave and said, “It’s getting late, I’m going, need to bathe.”

Amala said nothing, only Narugopal, the blacksmith’s son, called out—“Come again in the afternoon, brother!”

Apu walked a little way off and looked back once—no one seemed to miss him, the game was going on in full swing, Amala was standing by the post, playing the old woman with great enthusiasm—not even glancing his way.

Wounded and sulking, Apu made his way home, not speaking a word to anyone.

Amala-didi is something else! Didn’t want him—so what?...

A couple of days later, Harihar returned home with his son.

It had only been a few days, but already Sarbajaya could not bear being apart from her boy any longer.

Durga’s games hadn’t really taken off for some days; a quarrel over a boat made from a dried country gourd shell had broken out a few days before Apu’s journey, and since then the two hadn’t even looked at each other—now, even more gourd shells had piled up, but Durga no longer took them to float in the water—Why did I twist her ear over such a silly quarrel? Let her come back, I’ll never fight with her again, she can have all the shells she wants.

--- Page 71 ---
Returning home, Apu spent fifteen days wandering about, recounting his strange adventures. Oh, the marvelous things he had seen in those few days! The railway tracks—where real trains actually run! Earthen custard apples, papayas, cucumbers—so lifelike, they seemed real fruit! That doll—when you pressed its belly, it would suddenly thrash its arms and legs like an epileptic and start playing the khanjani (a small tambourine)! Amoladi! How far he had gone, crossing unknown, new villages, walking along lonely paths over fields, past ponds blooming with lotuses. In one such village, when Baba took him to the blacksmith’s shop by the roadside to drink water, they had called him inside, seated him with care on a mat, and served him milk, flattened rice, and batasha (crispy sugar sweets)! Which story should he tell, and which should he leave out!

Hearing the tale of the railway tracks, his didi (elder sister) was spellbound. Again and again, she asked, “How big was the noaguno (steam engine), Apu? Was it tall? Really long? Did you see the train? Did it pass by?”

No—Apu had not seen the train. That was the only thing missing—and all because of Baba. If only they had sat quietly for four or five hours by the railway line, they would have seen the train—but he could never convince Baba.

One afternoon, in a rush and distracted, Sarbajaya (Apu’s mother) entered through the front door. The moment she stepped onto the courtyard, something thin as a string caught her chest, and at once there was a sharp snapping sound—something broke with a “patang!” and two things fell limply to the ground from either side of her. The whole event happened in the blink of an eye, before she could see or understand what it was.

A short while later, Apu came home. As he crossed the threshold and stepped into the courtyard, he stopped short—he could not believe his eyes—what’s this! Oh no, who broke my telegraph wire?

At first, stunned by the suddenness and enormity of the loss, he could not quite grasp what had happened. Then, regaining his composure, he noticed the wet footprints still visible on the courtyard floor. Something inside him called out—it could be no one but Ma. Never anyone else, only Ma. Entering the house, he saw Ma sitting, quite at ease, washing jackfruit seeds. Suddenly, he stood up straight, and, leaning forward like the Abhimanyu of the jatra (folk theater) troupe, he spoke in a high, plaintive, flute-like tone:

“Tell me, Ma, did I go to all that trouble, searching through the woods and gardens to bring those little things for nothing?”

--- Page 72 ---
Sarbojaya turned around, astonished, and said—“What have you brought? What’s happened—”

—“Do you think I don’t feel pain? Haven’t my hands and feet been scratched by thorns?”

—“What are you babbling about like a mad child? What’s happened?”

—“What’s happened! I worked so hard to string up the telegraph wire, and now it’s been torn down, hasn’t it?”

—“You can’t go a single moment without some outlandish mischief, can you! In the middle of the road—what’s been strung up there—who knows if it’s a telegraph or some other-graph, I was coming quickly and it just snapped—so what should I do now, tell me—”

With that, she returned to her work.

Oh! What terrible heartlessness! Once, he used to think his mother loved him. Of course, that mistaken notion had faded long ago—but never, not even in his wildest dreams, had he imagined his mother could be so cruel, so stone-hearted. All yesterday, where was he? At Neelmoni jethar’s (elder uncle’s) homestead, at the big mango orchard of the Palits, at the bamboo grove of the ever-cheerful Guru-mahashoy—roaming alone through those fearsome, tangled jungles, he had struggled so hard to gather the swaying gulancha vines from the highest branches. Now, at last, the train game would begin, everything was ready, and yet—

Suddenly, he wanted to say something sharp, something harsh, something that would pierce her heart to his mother—and standing there, perhaps unable to think of anything else, he declared, even more fiercely and purely than before—“I won’t eat rice today, go away—I’ll never eat again—”

His mother replied—“Don’t eat, don’t eat, go on—will you be made a king just by eating rice? Here I am, can’t even finish cooking in peace—don’t eat, go on, let’s see who gives you food when you’re hungry!”

That’s it! In the blink of an eye—everything is there, I am here, you are here—his mother is washing jackfruit seeds as always—but where is Apu? He seemed to vanish like camphor! Just then, as Durga entered the house, she saw him slip past her at the doorway and rush outside like a storm. Startled, she called out—“Hey Apu, where are you going like that, what’s happened, hey Apu, listen—”

His mother said—“I don’t know, all your nonsense, you children, I’m just skin and bones now—something or other strung up in the middle of the road, I was coming, it snapped—so what now? Did I tear it on purpose? That’s why the boy’s angry—I won’t eat rice—don’t eat, go on, will you all ring bells in heaven just because you eat rice?”

--- Page 73 ---
In such a play of mother and son’s wounded pride, it is Durga who must act as mediator—after much calling and searching, it was she who finally found her brother around two in the afternoon. He sat there, dry-faced and absent-eyed, upon the fallen trunk of an old mango tree in the Ray family’s garden on the far side of the village.

Had anyone come to Apu’s house that evening and seen him, they could never have guessed that this was the same Apu who, in the morning, had left home in a huff, aggrieved with his mother. From one end of the courtyard to the other, wires had been strung up. Apu gazed at them in wonder, as if nothing was left out, as if it were a real railway telegraph line.

He went to Satu’s house and said—“Satuda, I’ve strung up telegraph wires in our courtyard. Come, let’s play train-train—will you come?”

“Who put up the wires?” Satu asked.

“I did it myself. Didi brought me the string,” Apu replied.

Satu said, “You go play, I can’t come right now.”

Apu understood in his heart that gathering the older boys and organizing a game was not his task. Who would listen to him, anyway? Yet, he went to Satu once more. Gripping the edge of the veranda with a disappointed face, he said without much hope, “Come on, Satuda, won’t you come? You, me, and Didi can play now?” Then, trying to tempt him, he added, “I’ve picked so many pomelo leaves to use as tickets. Look!” He spread his hands to show the quantity. “Will you come?”

Still, Satu did not want to come. Apu, shy and awkward outside, said nothing more and went home. His eyes were nearly brimming with tears from sorrow—after all his pleading, Satuda still wouldn’t listen!

The next morning, he and his Didi together built a large shop out of bricks and set out to gather items for their store. Durga knew more about the treasures of the wild—together they collected salt-leaf betel, red potatoes, wild yams, radhalata flowers for fish, teasel gourd, chichchiri beans, clods of earth for rock salt—so many things, and by the time they finished arranging their shop, it was already late in the day. Apu asked, “What will you use for sugar, Didi?”

Durga replied, “There’s good sand on that mound by the bamboo grove—Ma brings it to roast rice! Let’s go get some—it shines white, just like real sugar!”

--- Page 74 ---
Searching for sugarcane in the bamboo grove, they wandered off the path and slipped into the forest by the roadside. It was a tall, dense thicket—at the very top of a chotka tree, behind a curtain of thick, dark green vines, hung some bright red, round, plump fruits, swaying gently. Both Apu and Durga stared in astonishment. After much effort, they managed to tug at the vine until a few fruits tumbled down to the ground, tearing off a bit of the stem. With great delight, the two of them rushed together and picked them up from the earth.

There were only three ripe fruits. Mainly for display, they arranged them in the shop so that any customer entering would notice them at once. The business began in earnest. Durga herself bought betel leaves and nearly finished off all the shop’s supply. The game was in full swing when, through the front door, Apu saw Satu coming in. Overjoyed, he ran to greet him—“O Satuda, just look at our shop! See what fruits we’ve got. Didi and I picked them ourselves—can you guess what they are? Do you know?”

Satu said, “Oh, those are makal fruits—we used to have so many in our garden!”

With Satu’s arrival, Apu felt a deep sense of fulfillment. Satuda hardly ever came to their house—and besides, Satuda was a leader among the older boys. With him there, the childishness of the game seemed to vanish.

After a long while, when the play had run its course, Durga said, “Bhai, give me two maunds of rice, the fine kind. Tomorrow is the formal engagement for my doll’s wedding—a lot of people will come to eat.”

Satu replied, “Are we invited, or not?”

Durga shook her head and said, “Of course you are! You’ll be part of the bride’s party—tomorrow morning, come and bring the nakuṭo (a ceremonial gift). Satuda, tell Ranu to grind a little sandalwood paste tonight. I’ll come fetch it in the morning.”

Durga had barely finished speaking when Satu, snatching something from among the shop’s wares, suddenly dashed for the door. Instantly, Apu followed, shrilling in his thin, sweet voice, “Didi! He’s taken it! He’s taken it!” and raced after him.

Before the bewildered Durga could grasp what had happened, Satu and Apu had already darted out the door! Glancing toward the playhouse, Durga saw—not a single one of those ripe makal fruits was left…

--- Page 75 ---
Durga, in a rush, came to the doorway and saw that Satu was running ahead along the path to the gabtala, with Apu chasing after him, not far behind. Satu was three or four years older than Apu, and besides, he was not a slender, girlishly built boy like Apu—he was strong, with sturdy arms and legs. Competing with Satu in a race was out of the question for Apu—yet he had managed to keep up, only because Satu was running off with someone else’s belonging, and Apu was chasing for dear life.

Suddenly, Durga saw that as Satu ran, he bent down and glanced back over his shoulder—at the same moment, Apu suddenly stopped in his tracks—by then, Satu had already run out of sight, disappearing down the path beneath the chalta tree.

By then, Durga had run up and reached Apu. Apu, eyes squeezed shut, was bent forward, rubbing his eyes with both hands. Durga said, “What happened, Apu?”

Without properly opening his eyes, Apu, in a voice full of pain, kept rubbing his eyes and said, “Satuda threw dust in my eyes, didi—I can’t see anything—”

Durga quickly pulled down Apu’s hands and said, “Move, move, let me see—don’t rub your eyes like that, let me see—”

But Apu immediately raised his hands to his eyes again and cried out desperately, “No, didi—it feels so strange in my eyes—I’ve gone blind, didi—”

“Let me see, let me see—don’t put your hands in your eyes like that—move—” Then she blew on a corner of her cloth and began to gently press it to his eyes. After a while, Apu slowly began to open his eyes—Durga lifted his eyelids, blew on them several times, and said, “Can you see better now? All right, you go home... I’m going to their house to tell his mother and thakuma (grandmother) everything—I’ll tell Ranu too—such a naughty boy—go on, I’ll be right back—”

--- Page 76 ---
Durga advanced as far as Ranu’s backdoor, but she did not dare go any further. She was afraid of Sejthakrun (second eldest aunt). For a while, she lingered near the backdoor, hesitating, then turned back home. Entering through the main door, she saw Apu standing silently behind the left panel, which he had pushed open just a little, crying quietly. He was not a crybaby; he never cried over anything, no matter how big—he would get angry, sulk, yes, but never cry. Durga understood that today he was deeply hurt, all those cherished fruits were gone… And on top of that, to be so insulted, having dust thrown in his eyes! She could not bear to see Apu cry—it made something ache inside her chest.

She went and took her brother’s hand—in a tone of consolation, she said, “Don’t cry, Apu—come, I’ll give you all my cowrie shells—come—does your eye still hurt?... Let me see, did you tear your cloth?”

After lunch, in the afternoon, Apu did not go outside but stayed in the house. The old, timeworn rooms of the ancestral home. Furniture, wooden chests from another era, cane baskets painted in faded colors, a rack for cowrie shells, water stools cluttering the room. There were boxes Apu had never seen opened, inside which were jars and pots whose contents were a complete mystery to him.

All together, the room exuded a certain old, old smell—he did not know what it was, but it seemed to bring memories of long-ago days. In those days, Apu did not exist, but this rack for cowrie shells did, that grandfather’s cane trunk did, that big wooden chest did, that sodali tree’s crown still peeped out from the forest, in that place now overgrown with wild shrubs there had once been someone’s grand Chandimandap (a ceremonial pavilion for worship), so many children with so many names once played in this yard—where had they all vanished, melting away like shadows, how many ages ago!

--- Page 77 ---
When she is alone in the house, when Ma goes down to the ghat, an irresistible longing comes over her—for that box, for that cane basket. She yearns to open them, to bring them out into the daylight, to examine what strange mysteries are hidden inside. On the highest shelf of the house, in a large wooden chest, lies a heap of palm-leaf manuscripts and old notebooks. She had asked Baba and learned that these belonged to her grandfather, Ramchand Tarkalankar. How she wishes those treasures would come within her reach—then she could bring them down just once, turn them over in her hands, explore them to her heart’s content.

Some afternoons, she sits by the window at the edge of the woods, reading that tattered copy of Kashidas’s Mahabharata. She has taught herself to read so well now—no longer does she need to listen to others recite; she can read fluently, like water flowing, and she understands every word. Her mind is sharp when it comes to studies. Sometimes, Baba takes her to the Gangulibari Chandimandap, into the assembly of elders. He hands her the Ramayana or a panchali ballad and says, “Read, baba, let everyone hear you just once.” The elders praise her warmly. Dinu Chatujje says, “And my own grandson—he’s about your Khoka’s age—he tore up two copies of Barnaparichay, would you believe it? Still can’t recognize the letters properly. He’s taken after his father—while I’m alive, the moment I close my eyes, he’ll have to take up the plough.” Pride swells in Harihar’s chest at his daughter’s accomplishments. Inwardly he thinks, “Will she ever be like them? All their lives, they’ve been moneylenders! So what if we’re poor? Ours is a family of scholars, after all. My father didn’t fill those palm leaves with falsehoods—he left a legacy, a lineage. Where could that ever go?”

--- Page 78 ---
Just a few hands’ distance from their window stood the boundary wall, and right beyond it, hugging the wall’s edge, began a wild, sprawling jungle of weeds! Sitting at the window, one could see only the tops of the bhat-sheora trees, rising and falling like waves in a green sea, and here and there, all manner of vines swaying in the breeze. The ancient bamboo grove, heavy with age, leaned over the sodali and bon-chalta trees, and beneath, on the dark earth, wagtail birds danced! Under the great trees, dense green jungles of holud, bon-kochu, and kattu-ol jostled and pressed, straining desperately to turn their faces toward the sunlight. The plant that, helpless in this battle for life, had been smothered under the proud shadow of its neighbor—its leaves had faded, its death-pale stems melting away—before its dying gaze, the full, dazzling sunlight of late autumn flooded the forest, the world steeped in the yearning, moist fragrance of parasitic blooms, all its beauty, mystery, and vastness slowly slipping away into the shadows.

From the edge of their house, this wild jungle stretched unbroken on one side to the kuthir field, on the other all the way to the riverbank. To Apu, this forest seemed endless; how far he and didi had wandered within it, yet never glimpsed its end—always these paths beneath the titiraj trees, the thick, swaying guloncho vines, the plump bon-chalta fruits dangling all around. The winding path ended in a mango grove, but again, threading beneath this tree and that, through tangles of bon-kolmi, nata-kanta, and dense moyna bushes, it led on and on, who knew where, as somewhere the wild dhundhul vines swung in the emptiness, and on the mossy branches of ancient shirish trees, clusters of parasitic plants caught the eye!

--- Page 79 ---
This forest, with the fresh touch of its emerald greenness, brushed its magic over her and her didi’s hearts. Since birth, this forest had been their intimate companion, filling their thirsty souls with a silent joy at every moment, in every heartbeat—so many strange, so many exquisite flavors of delight.

The monsoon-soaked, deep green thickets crowned with the yellow-tipped buds of fragrant nata-kanta flowers; the fleeting movements of a squirrel darting along the thick, thorny branches of the moyna-kanta tree in the shadow of the approaching sunset; the abundance of leaves, blossoms, and fruits—when, at the edge of the dense forest, on a crooked, solitary branch among the tangled undergrowth, some unknown bird of the woods would perch, then the wild, rare, and profound joy that filled her heart was something she could never explain to anyone. It was like a dream, like an enchantment—birds singing all around, blossoms tumbling down in a gentle shower, the light of sunset deepening into shadow.

Somewhere within this forest lies an old, half-dried pond, and on its bank stands a broken temple. These days, Panchananda Thakur is the village deity, but once upon a time, the goddess Vishalakshi of that temple held the same place in people’s hearts. She was the tutelary goddess of the village’s Majumdar family. Once, for some grave reason, the family, desperate for success, offered a human sacrifice at her temple. Angered by this, the goddess appeared in a dream and declared she would abandon the temple and never return.

That was long ago. No one who witnessed the worship of Vishalakshi lives anymore. The temple has crumbled to ruins; the pond before it has silted up and become a marsh; the forest has crept in on all sides, swallowing everything. There is no one left in the Majumdar family to even light a lamp in her name.

--- Page 80 ---
Only––and this too was long ago––Swarup Chakraborty of the village was returning from a feast in a neighboring hamlet. At dusk, as he descended to the river ghat, he saw a beautiful sixteen-year-old girl standing by the roadside. The place was far from any settlement, evening had deepened, not a soul was anywhere on the path. To see such a young, lovely girl alone by the silent forest at this hour, Swarup Chakraborty was, as anyone would be, astonished. But before he could utter a word, the girl spoke, her voice tinged with a gentle pride yet sweet as honey––

“I am this village’s Bishalakshi Devi (the presiding goddess). Soon, a terrible outbreak of olautha (epidemic fever) will begin in the village––tell them, on the night of Chaturdashi (the fourteenth lunar day), to perform Kali Puja at Panchanand Tala, offering one hundred and eight pumpkins in sacrifice.”

No sooner had she finished speaking than, before the stunned eyes of Swarup Chakraborty, the girl seemed to slowly dissolve into the winter evening’s mist all around. And sure enough, a few days after this incident, a dreadful epidemic did strike the village that year.

How many times he has heard these stories! Whenever he stands by the window, thoughts of Bishalakshi Thakur (the goddess) rise in his mind. Might he not see Devi Bishalakshi just once? Perhaps, wandering the forest path, plucking a strand of guloncho vine––at that very moment––

So beautiful to behold, clad in a red-bordered sari, wrists and neck adorned with bangles and necklaces like Ma Durga herself.

––“Who are you?”

––“I am Apu.”

––“You are such a good boy, what boon do you wish for?”

He goes and lies down on his bed. Sometimes, in the whispering breeze, the bittersweet scent of leaves and vines drifts in; at high noon, from far away atop some great tree, a seagull calls, drawn out and plaintive, as if above all the small joys and sorrows, the peace and strife, past and present of this little village, in the sun-drenched, blue, solitary sky of autumn midday, the melodious gift of some wistful, wandering god drifts farther and farther away.

He never knows when he falls asleep; when he wakes, the day is already gone. Outside the window, shadows have fallen across the whole forest, and at the tips of the bamboo grove, the red sunlight lingers.

--- Page 81 ---
Every day at this time—precisely in this shadow-drenched twilight—when I gaze toward the lonely forest, all its strange tales come alive in my mind. My heart fills with an exquisite joy; it feels as though these sweetly fragrant days, heavy with the scent of vines and leaves, had once come before, long ago. The faint memory of happiness from those days returns, filling these present afternoons with the hope of some vague, future delight. It seems as if something is about to happen, as if these days will not pass in vain—some great joy awaits at their end!

How many mysterious, dreamlike messages are woven into this ever-familiar, ever-changing forest, companion since birth, brimming with joy! Looking up at the shadowy sky above the bamboo grove, she sees: someone, taking advantage of a young hero’s generosity, stretches out a hand to beg for his eternal armor and earrings; somewhere, after drinking a bowl of pithuli (a simple rural drink), a poor little boy dances in delight before the other children, crying, “I’ve had milk, I’ve had milk!”—and there, beneath the old bel tree by the ruined homestead, it was right there that the aged hero Bhishmadev, lying on his bed of arrows, had Arjuna pierce the earth with a sharp shaft to draw forth the sacred Bhogavati stream to moisten his dying lips. In his youth, King Dasharath, hunting in the blossoming groves by the Sarayu, once killed a poor boy fetching water, mistaking him for a deer—that happened right by the pond beneath the great jamun tree in Ranu-didi’s garden.

They have a book at home, its pages all yellowed, part of the cover missing, the title reads “Birangana Kavya” (Epic of Heroines), but no one knows the author, for the first pages are torn away. The book is dearly loved—within it, she has read:

Not far I saw a lake, and on its shore  
A royal charioteer lay rolling,  
His thigh shattered! I rose, weeping,  
O Lord, what evil dream is this you show me?

--- Page 82 ---
On the day of the Kaluichondi brata, when Ma would go to the northern fields of the village for a picnic by that old, delightful pond—no one knew—only she and Ma. That little pond, ringed by woods on all sides, became for her the Dvaipayana Lake of the Mahabharata. In the heart of that lonely field’s pond dwelled the broken-thighed, dishonored hero, all alone—no one saw him, no one came searching. When the farmers returned from the banana and eggplant patches of the northern field, there was no trace of people anywhere—the thick darkness of a moonless night slowly spread over the undiscovered, uninhabited, unknown land at the edge of the golden fields. Then, the ancient sorrow of humankind, thousands of years old, would sometimes awaken in her as the innocent joy of a child enchanted by a poor father’s deception, sometimes as the image of a luckless, solitary, helpless prince—brought to life and made meaningful by the growing, eager sympathy of her heart. How many times had her eyelids grown wet, reading that book by the nameless author!

Her baba was not at home. If he were, she would have to sit quietly inside, open her books, and study. The entire day would pass, and still there would be no respite. Her heart would grow restless. How much longer must she sit and memorize Shuvankari’s Arya? Would she not play today? Was there any day left at all? She felt such anger, such hurt, toward Baba.

Then, suddenly, unexpectedly, she would be given leave. Tossing her books and satchel in a heap, she would run out to the shade-filled courtyard and dance with joy.

What an exquisite, strange afternoon it was... By the dense, shadowy trees, the playhouse... garlands of gulancha vines strung up... swings made from date palm fronds... a cool, fresh scent drifting in from the woods... the last red rays of sun glinting on the pomelo tree above Jethamashai’s abandoned homestead... a glossy brown-winged teḍo bird fluttering in to perch on the wild morning glory bush... the smell of fresh earth... the world of children overflowing with happiness—how could she ever explain such joy to anyone?

After dusk, Sarbajaya had set the rice to cook. Apu sat on a mat on the veranda. It was very dark, the steady chorus of crickets filling the air.

Apu asked, “How many days till Pujo, Ma?”

Durga was slicing vegetables with the boti (traditional curved blade). She said, “Twenty-two days left, isn’t it, Ma?”

She had counted it all out. Her baba would come home, and for Apu, for Ma, for herself—there would be dolls, new clothes, alta (red dye for feet).

--- Page 83 ---
These days, because she’s grown up, her mother no longer lets her go to the other neighborhood to attend feasts. She’s almost forgotten what it’s like to eat luchi. On radiant nights of Kojagari Purnima, when the moonlight spills over everything, she used to wander from lane to lane, weaving through the dappled shadows of the bamboo grove, her sari’s end brimming with puffed rice and crispy khoi from the Lakshmi Puja offerings. Conch shells would sound from every house, the scent of frying luchi would drift down the lanes, and sometimes someone from the neighborhood would send a piece of the Puja’s cool naivedya—offerings—over to their home. She would bring back heaps of khoi and puffed rice, and for two days her mother would serve them as their afternoon snack, eating some herself too. That year, Sej Thakrun had said—“A gentleman’s daughter, going house to house like a farmer’s child, collecting khoi and puffed rice—what will people think? It doesn’t look good… Don’t send her out like that again, Bouma.” Since then, she never went again.

Durga said, “Want to play cards?”

“Go on, fetch the cards from the other room, let’s play a bit—”

Durga glanced at Apu, her face uncertain.

Apu grinned and said, “Come on, I’ll stand by you—”

Their mother said, “Oh, I can’t bear this girl’s fears! All day she runs wild, never afraid of anything, but come night, she’s paralyzed just going from one room to another…”

It was that same deck of cards Apu had brought from the shishya-bari—the tutor’s house. All three of them were equally skilled at the game. Apu still didn’t know all the suits—sometimes he’d show a card to his mother, his opponent, and ask, “What suit is this, Ruiton? Look, Ma—”

Durga’s heart was light today. Usually, they didn’t cook at night; there’d be leftover rice and curry from earlier. But today, fresh rice was on the boil, curry would be cooked, and that filled her with delight. Today felt like a festival day. Apu said, “While we play, tell that story, Ma—the one about Shyamlanka?”

Suddenly, he lay down with his head in his mother’s lap. Stroking her arm, he pleaded, “Say that rhyme, Ma—the one—Shyamlanka grinds the mortar, her hair tumbles to the ground.”

Durga said, “How can we play if you start reciting rhymes, Apu? Get up—”

Sarbojaya said, “Durga, where did you find the patalkochu (taro leaves) today?”

“That big garden the Gosains have, you know? When you and I went looking for the red cow, Apu? There were so many growing there, no one noticed, Ma, it’s such a wild place! Otherwise, people would have picked them all—”

Apu said, “You went there? Oh, that’s such a deep jungle, Didi!”

--- Page 84 ---
Sarbojaya gazed at her son again and again, her eyes brimming with affection. That very Apu of the other day—who, when she would say, “Come moon, come moon, leave a silvery mark on Khoka’s forehead,” would, like a clockwork doll, tilt his moon-like brow towards her finger-locked hands—he was now sitting down to play cards! To her, the sight felt utterly novel and strange. If Apu failed to play well, or if he hoped to win a trick and didn’t, or if the bad cards landed in Apu’s hand and the good ones in her own, even as a player on the opposing team, she felt a pang in her heart.

Durga said, “Do you know what happened today, Ma—”

Apu interrupted, “Oh, then I’ll be cross with you, just watch—”

“Go on, be cross then—listen, Ma, he doesn’t even know what poppy seeds are called! Today at Rajidi’s house they put poppy seeds out in the sun to dry, and he asked, ‘What’s that, Rajidi?’ Rajidi said, ‘Licorice root, taste it and see’—and he actually ate it, Ma, standing right there, never realizing it was poppy seeds—such a fool, isn’t he, Ma?”

Apu said the words, but in truth, he could never really be cross with his didi. That day, when Satuda had run off with his ripe makal fruit, it was his didi who had spent the whole day searching through the woods and gardens, and at dusk, came back with her anchal (the loose end of her sari) tied full of makal fruit, and laid them out before him, saying, “How’s this now? You cried so much this morning, didn’t you?” That evening, what had delighted him more—the makal fruit, or the tender, gentle smile in his didi’s large, loving eyes—he could not say.

“Apu, do you play the game of sixes properly?” Durga, delighted, began to gather and arrange the cards.

“What’s that scent of flowers, didi?”

Their mother said, “There’s a chatim tree (alstonia scholaris) behind your Jethamoshai’s (paternal uncle’s) homestead, that’s where the fragrance is coming from.” Both Apu and Durga asked eagerly, “Yes, Ma, didn’t you say a tiger once came under that chatim tree?” But their mother, hastily dropping her cards, stood up and said, “Oh no, the rice is burning, I can smell it—let me take it off the stove, wait just a moment—”

As they sat down to eat, Durga said, “Ma, the patal-kerai curry (pointed gourd and beans) tastes wonderful today!”

Her face glowed with a heavenly satisfaction.

Apu chimed in, “Wow, it tastes just like meat, doesn’t it, didi? Ma, there are so many patal-kerai growing in one spot, I thought they were toadstools, so I didn’t pick them—”

--- Page 85 ---
Certainly! Here is your translation, preserving the lyrical, evocative prose and all cultural terms as instructed:

---

With the exuberant words of praise from both, Sarbajaya’s heart swelled with pride and satisfaction. Yet, had she truly found the right ingredients? At others’ houses, they call Sejthakrun to cook for feasts—let them call her just once and see, let them see what cooking really means, not Sejthakrun—yes.

Sarbajaya said, “Pour water over Apu’s hands, Dugga. What kind of mischief is this from the boy? Washing his face right in the middle of the road? Every night, you’re always on that path—”

But Apu refuses to move even a single step. Ahead lies the gap in the broken wall, the darkness of the bamboo grove, the shadowy thickets, black as the seeds of a ridge gourd. The ruined homestead… and who knows what other nameless terrors! He cannot understand—when one’s very life is at stake, is it really so much worse to squat right there on the path? Afterward, everyone goes off to sleep. The night deepens, the autumn air, heavy with the sharp scent of sheuli and the dew-soaked touch of Hemanta, fills the darkness. At midnight, the wan moonlight of the dark fortnight rises above the bamboo tops, glistening on the dew-drenched leaves and branches. In the enchanting play of light and shadow, the edge of the forest seems like a land of sleeping fairies, brimming with mystery. Suddenly, with a whispering rush, a gust of wind shakes the snehadal branches, sets the tendrils of the telakucha creeper trembling, and sweeps on.

Sometimes, at this very hour, Apu would awaken from his sleep.

It is as if the goddess herself has arrived, that forgotten presiding deity of the village, Bishalakshi.

On the banks of the Ichhamati, where the water is clear as the rind of a pomegranate, in the cool mud thick with green moss, the footprints of those who once walked there have long since vanished, even the ancient saptaparni tree on the shore may never have seen them. In the olden days, it was they who offered flowers, fruits, and naivedya at the goddess’s temple. Who among the people of today even remembers her?

But she has not yet forgotten this village.

When the village lies deep in midnight silence, she wanders through the woods, making flowers bloom, tending to the fledgling birds. In the last watch of a moonlit night, she fills the tiny hives of wild bees, natkan, and puyo flowers with sweet honey.

She knows where the head of the hidden bask flower peeks from a corner of the thicket, where in the secret heart of the forest the clusters of sheuli flowers lie sleeping in the shade of the trees, where at some bend of the Ichhamati, among the green moss, the blue-petaled kolmi flowers gather in a crowded tangle, and where, in a little straw nest among the thorny branches, the tun-tuni bird’s chicks are just awakening from sleep.

--- Page 86 ---
In the gentle radiance of her beauty, the forest seems to have filled to the brim. In the silence, in the moonlight, in the fragrance, in the enchantment of indistinct light and shadow, the night’s exquisite grace unfolds.

Yet, before the first light of day breaks, the Lady of the Forest vanishes somewhere—after Swaroop Chakraborty, no one has ever seen her again.

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Seventeen**

Annada Ray Mahashoy of the village has recently fallen into great trouble.

With the arrival of the land survey, tents have been pitched in the northern fields. The chief survey officer has set up his office by the riverbank in the middle of the fields, and a host of minor officials have accompanied him. All the gentlemen of the village own some land—property earned by their forefathers—and, anchoring their boats of life on the secure shores of these possessions, they had been passing their days in a state as inert and unmoving as lifeless matter. But now, all have found themselves in a bit of a predicament. Ram, perhaps, has long enjoyed Shyam’s land as his own without dispute; Jadu, paying tax for ten bighas, has quietly occupied twelve. What had been settled in perfect peace for so long has now been thrown into confusion. Though the trouble is, in a way, universal, Annada Ray’s predicament is somewhat different—perhaps even more grave.

One of his kinsmen has long been living in the west. All these years, Annada Ray had enjoyed that absent relative’s mango and jackfruit orchards and lands without hindrance, and he was entirely confident that, once the survey was over, he would have it all—or at least a good portion—officially recorded in his own name. But who knows which villager wrote what letter to that distant kinsman—now, for the past ten days, the kinsman’s eldest son has arrived to oversee the property during the survey. Not only has Annada Ray lost his daily bread, but there are further troubles. The best rooms in the house belong to that relative’s share; for the past twenty years, Ray Mahashoy has occupied them himself, but now he must give them up. The kinsman’s son is a stylish college boy—he sleeps in one room, studies in another. From the upstairs room, Annada Ray has had to remove the iron safe, pawned goods, and documents. The downstairs room, where cheap bundles of firewood bought from the Palit neighborhood were stored, must soon be vacated as well.

Evening. In Annada Ray’s chandi-mandap (a pavilion for worship), a few men from the neighborhood have gathered—the game of dice usually begins at this hour. But today, the day’s work is not yet done. Annada Ray was bidding farewell, one by one, to the assembled debtors.

--- Page 87 ---
Right beneath the edge of the courtyard veranda, a young farmer’s wife had been sitting for a long while, her head covered with her sari’s end, a small boy by her side. Now, thinking her turn had come, she stood up. Rai Mahashoy, head slightly bowed, peered at her over his glasses and said,  
—Who is it? What is it with you now?

The farmer’s wife, loosening the knot of her anchal, spoke in a low voice,  
—I have managed to gather a little money, with much hardship. Please take my money… and unlock the granary for me, Master Thakur, we’re suffering so much, what more can I say—

Annada Rai’s face brightened. He said,  
—Hari, count her money. Check the date in the ledger, and recalculate the interest once more—

The farmer’s wife took the money from the corner of her sari and placed it on the edge of the veranda before Harihar. After counting, Harihar said,  
—Five rupees?

Rai Mahashoy said,  
—Alright—deposit it—where’s the rest of the money?

—I’ll bring it soon, then I’ll pay the rest—I’ll work hard—pay it off, but for now please unlock my granary, let me at least feed my mother two meals and keep her alive, the house is falling apart, but that can wait—

She spoke so calmly, as if the granary key was already in her palm. She was slow to recognize Rai Mahashoy.

Before Rai Mahashoy could finish, he said,  
—Oh, what demands this woman has! Nearly forty rupees owed with interest—she brings five rupees, says unlock the granary, this is just how these small folk are. Now go, don’t loiter here at midday—

The farmer’s wife was no stranger to anyone at the Chandimandap. Dinu Bhattacharya, whose eyesight was poor, asked,  
—Who is that, Annada?

—That’s Tamaraj’s wife from the other side—hasn’t it been four days since Tamaraj died? With interest, she owes forty rupees, so the very day he died I locked the granary, now she wants it opened—pleading this way and that—

Even if the earth had slipped from beneath her feet, Tamaraj’s wife wouldn’t have been more startled—now she understood much of the matter, and stepped forward,  
—Don’t say that, Master Thakur, my boy had a little silver coin, a nimphol, last year I rolled it away for him! But I had to sell it at Bhoda Sekra’s shop, he gave me five rupees—didn’t want to sell a child’s keepsake, but what could I do, now I can feed us two meals, I thought, when days get better, I’ll get my boy another nimphol! So please, Master Thakur, give me back the key—

--- Page 88 ---
––Go, go––now go––do you think all these troubles with money will be solved just by crying? They won’t. What would you understand of such things? If your husband were here, he’d have understood. Now go, don’t make a scene––those five rupees are kept in your name––bring the rest of the money, then we’ll see.

Annada Ray took off his spectacles and, slipping them into their case, stood up and prepared to go inside the house. Tamarjez’s wife cried out in a desperate tone––Where are you going, O Monib Thakur (respected master), please find a way for my little boy, what will I feed him, I don’t even have a paisa to buy him a handful of puffed rice––either open my granary, or return my hard-earned money––

Ray Mahashay (a respectful form of address) grimaced and said––Go, go, woman, don’t whine in the evening––a handful of money is sinking and you don’t care, open the granary, return the money––what do you even have in your granary? Four shelis (a small measure) of paddy at most, will that repay the debt? Even those five rupees are still outstanding, will I never see my money! What will her boy eat, she asks––how should I know what her boy will eat? Go, if you can, complain wherever you like––

As Ray Mahashay went inside the house, Dinu Bhattacharya said––Hey, boudi (sister-in-law), how long has it been since Tamarjez passed––let’s see––

––It was Wednesday, father Thakur, he brought fish from the market, I cooked it with onions––served him rice––he was a simple man, happy with whatever I gave him––after eating he said he was feeling cold, asked for a quilt, I gave it to him––and then, before the stars had even risen, the man was silent, not a sound from him, and before noon he left me destitute––left my boy destitute––her voice choked with tears, she pleaded––please, you all say something––please, give me back the key to the granary, it’s so hard to run the household––do you think I want to leave a debt unpaid––no matter how, I’ll repay it––

At that moment, the newly arrived cousin entered and the conversation ceased. Dinu said––Come, Niren Babaji, you went for a walk in the fields, did you? So, this is your ancestral land, what do you think of it?

Niren smiled a little. He was not more than twenty-one or twenty-two, well-built, handsome. He studied law at a college in Calcutta, a man of very quiet nature––though sent by his father to look after things, he neither understood nor cared for any of the work, spending his days and nights reading novels and shooting his gun. He had brought a gun with him, for he was quite passionate about hunting.

--- Page 89 ---
Niren, on his way into his own room, saw Gokul’s wife sitting on the floor, picking at something bit by bit. As he reached the doorway, his eyes fell upon his precious imported lamp, its glass shade shattered into pieces, shards of glass scattered across the entire floor. At the sound of footsteps near the door, Gokul’s wife started and turned around, her handkerchief spread out as she carefully picked up the fragments of glass—one by one. It seemed she had come, as usual, to tidy the room and had tried to light the lamp; somehow, she had broken it, and before the owner of the lamp could arrive, she was hurriedly trying to erase all traces of her crime. Suddenly caught red-handed, she was utterly flustered.

To ease the weight of her embarrassment, Niren laughed and said, “Well, boudi, looks like you’ve broken the lamp, haven’t you? See, you’ve been caught! You know I study law, don’t you? All right, now why don’t you make me a cup of tea, boudi—quickly, let’s see how good you are at your work. Wait, let me light the lamp first—luckily, there’s another shade in the box.”

Gokul’s wife replied in a shy tone, “Shall I bring a matchbox, thakurpo?” (thakurpo: younger brother-in-law)

Niren, with a teasing note, said, “If you hadn’t brought a matchbox, what were you doing with the lamp, may I ask?”

This time the young bride laughed, and in a low voice said, “There was dust on it, so I thought I’d wipe it clean. But as I tried to take down the glass, who knows with these English lamps—” She broke off, laughing bashfully, and lowered her gaze.

Niren had been here for ten or twelve days, but though she was his boudidi by relation, he hadn’t really spoken much with Gokul’s wife. But from that evening of the broken glass, the awkwardness of their new acquaintance melted away. Niren, the son of a well-off father, had come to this village in Bengal for the first time, and in this lonely, joyless exile, he hadn’t wanted the days to drag on. Once the path of friendship with his boudi of similar age was opened, the morning and evening tea-times became filled with the sweetness of easy conversation and quiet joy.

That afternoon, Durga came by for a visit. Peeking into the kitchen doorway, she called, “What are you cooking, khurima?” (khurima: father’s younger brother’s wife, or aunt)

The young bride replied, “Come in, dear, will you help me a bit? I can’t manage all this alone…”

Whenever Durga visited, she often helped khurima with her chores. As she cleaned the fish, she asked, “Say, khurima, where did you get these crabs? We don’t eat this kind of crab.”

--- Page 90 ---
––Why won’t you eat it, really! Bidhu the fisherman said everyone eats these crabs.

––Yes, khurima (aunt), oh my, did you buy them?

––Of course I did, Bidhu gave me all those for just five paisa.

Durga said nothing. In her heart she thought—khurima is good in every way, only a little foolish! Who ever buys these crabs with money, or eats them anyway? Bidhu found a good soul and cheated her. At the same time, Durga’s affection for this simple khurima grew even deeper.

They say that day Gokul-kaka (uncle) hit khurima on the head with his wooden sandal—Swarna the cowherd woman tells the story at their house. She too had gone to bathe at the riverbank that day. Khurima came to bathe but didn’t dip her head under, afraid it would sting. That day, Durga’s heart was breaking with sorrow, but she said nothing, lest khurima feel embarrassed—in front of all the people at the ghat, she’d be ashamed. Still, Rai-jethi (elder aunt) had asked—Bouma (daughter-in-law), aren’t you bathing?

Khurima replied with a smile—Not today, didima (grandmother), I’m not feeling well.

Khurima thought no one knew about her being beaten. But as soon as khurima left the ghat, Rai-jethi said—Did you see how Gokul beat his wife? Blood is stuck in her hair, all matted! Rai-jethi is so unfair. You know, but then why ask, and why tell everyone else?

As Durga was about to leave, having washed the fish, she asked timidly—Khurima, do you have any chire’s rice (rice for making flattened rice)? Ma was saying Apu wanted to eat chire, but we haven’t bought any paddy this year.

Gokul’s wife whispered softly—Come by after noon… She pointed toward the house—Come when everyone’s asleep!

Durga asked—Khurima, who’s come to your house? I’ve never seen anyone.

––Haven’t you seen Thakurpo (younger brother-in-law)? He’s not here now, gone out somewhere, but come in the evening, you’ll see him then. Then Gokul’s wife laughed and said—If you married Thakurpo, you two would make a lovely pair.

Durga blushed red and said—Oh, stop!

Gokul’s wife laughed again—Why, why stop? Is our boy not good enough? Let me see… She lifted Durga’s chin with her hand and said—Look at this face, as beautiful as a Durga idol! So what if your father has no money?

--- Page 91 ---
Durga gave herself a little shake, freed herself, and said—“Go, Khurima (aunt, father’s sister-in-law) is always like this…” Then, almost running, she slipped out through the back door. As she went, she thought—Khurima is good in every other way, only a little foolish, otherwise, just look at her…? Oh, never mind!

No sooner had Durga left than Swarna the milkmaid arrived to milk the cows. From inside the women’s quarters, the daughter-in-law called out—“O Sanno, I beg you, the calf is tied to the pituli tree in the outer courtyard—bring it here, and look, the water pot is scrubbed and left on the veranda.”

By now, Sakhi Thakrun (elderly lady of the house) had finished her midday worship. Coming outside, she turned her face northward toward the local Kali temple, and, bowing, began to chant in a drawn-out, sing-song voice—“Mercy, Mother Siddheshwari, grant us the day, Mother, help us cross the ocean of the world—Mother Rakshakali, protect us, Mother, O Mother!”

Gokul’s wife called from the kitchen—“O Pishima (father’s aunt), I’ve set aside some coconut naru (sweet balls), have a couple and drink some water.”

Suddenly, Sakhi Thakrun called from the veranda—“Bouma (daughter-in-law), come see over here.”

At the sound of her voice, Gokul’s wife’s heart nearly flew out of her chest. She feared Sakhi Thakrun as one fears Yama, the god of death. When it came to distributing affection or kindness, the Lord had shown no partiality toward Sakhi Thakrun—that much could be said for certain. Leaning over the pile of scrubbed utensils gathered in the corner of the veranda, she pointed with her finger and said—“Look with your own eyes, can you see? Do you see the clear water marks? Sanno took the water pot from here, and then that low-caste girl’s touched, soiled pot was brought back into the kitchen and mixed with the rest! Ha! All purity, all lineage, utterly ruined!”

Sakhi Thakrun sat down on the veranda in despair. She could hardly have been more disappointed had she received news of a worthy son’s death.

—“This is what happens when you bring a daughter from a family of good-for-nothings and fools into the house. How would she ever learn the customs of gentlefolk—how would she even know? You scrubbed the utensils, but did you notice if they were defiled or not? It’s already three hours past noon, I thought I’d have a sip of water! Touched by a low-caste, I’d have had to bathe and purify myself at once—thank goodness I didn’t touch the pot.”

Gokul’s wife stood there, face clouded with sorrow, thinking—Why on earth did I ask Sanno, that cremation-ground girl, to fetch the pot? I could have done it myself.

--- Page 92 ---
Sakhi Thakurun, her face twisted in irritation, said—“Standing there gawking like a dhingi*? Go, throw out the hearth-ash. Scrub the pots and pans and bring them back. Smear the kitchen floor with cow dung and bathe. All you good-for-nothing girls have turned this household into a shambles!” Muttering with anger, Sakhi Thakurun went back inside; she could no longer bear the harsh sunlight outside.

[*dhinki: a term for a gawky, awkward girl]

By the time all the chores were done as ordered, the day was nearly spent. When she went to bathe in the river again, her face had shriveled and grown small from the sun, hunger, thirst, and exhaustion!

At the ghat, the evening shadows were thick and deep. On the far bank, sunlight glinted off the tall shimul tree. Around the bend of the river, a sailboat, its sail unfurled, was rounding the curve with slow strokes of the oar. Near the plough, a man stood drying his cloth, letting it loose so it fluttered in the wind like a flag. In the middle of the river, a large turtle raised its head, took a breath with a soft sough—so-o-o-o-bhoosh!—and then disappeared beneath the water.

The river water carried a cool, lovely scent. On the opposite sandbar of the little river, a cormorant sat perched atop a bamboo fish-trap.

At this hour, every day, memories of her childhood would come flooding back—

Pankouri, pankouri, come ashore now...

Gokul’s bou* gazed at the cormorant for a while, her mother’s face rising in her mind. There was no one left in the world who looked at her face. Was it really time for her mother to die? In her poor father’s house, only a ganja-smoking brother remained, and who knew where or when he wandered—there was no telling. Last year, during the Puja, he had come here for four days. Secretly, she would slip him whatever little savings she had in her box—a coin or two. Then, one day, he suddenly vanished from here. Only after he left did it come to light that he had bought a quilt on credit from a Kabuli quilt-seller, writing her husband’s name in the seller’s ledger. There was much commotion over this. Much criticism, much humiliation for her father’s family! Since then, there had been no word of her brother.

[*bou: daughter-in-law, young wife]

In the evening, as she worked, her heart ached for her helpless, wayward brother. Looking out at the lonely path across the fields, it seemed to her that her homeless brother might be walking alone down some distant, deserted, dark country road, with no place to rest his head at night, no one to look upon his face...

A surge rose in her chest; tears blurred the river’s shadowy waters, the fields, the ghat, the shimul tree across the way, the big boat at the river’s bend—all faded into a mist.

--- Page 93 ---
**Pather Panchali – Chapter Eighteen**

That day, Apu had gone to the fishermen’s quarter to play with shells. It was no earlier than two or two-thirty in the afternoon, and the sun blazed fiercely overhead. First, he went to the house of Teen-kori the fisherman. Teen-kori’s son, Bonka, was trimming branches under the guava tree. Apu called out, “Hey, want to play shells?”

Though Bonka wanted to, he replied that he had to go to the boat right away—if he played, his father would scold him! From there, Apu went to the house of Ramcharan the fisherman.

Ramcharan was sitting on the veranda, smoking his hookah. Apu asked, “Is Hriday home?”

Ramcharan replied, “Why do you want Hriday, Thakur? To play shells, I suppose? Off you go now, Hriday isn’t home.”

Wandering about in the heat of midday, Apu’s face grew flushed. After several more fruitless attempts, he wandered on and, near the tamarind grove close to Baburam the sparrow-catcher’s house, his face suddenly lit up with joy. The shell-playing circle under the tamarind tree was in full swing. All the boys were from the fishermen’s quarter, except for one—Patu from the Brahmin quarter. Apu didn’t know Patu very well, for Patu’s home was quite far from his own. Patu was a little younger than Apu; Apu remembered, on the very first day he went to enroll at Prasanna Gurumoshai’s pathshala (village school), he had seen this boy sitting quietly, chewing on a palm leaf.

Apu went up to him and asked, “How many shells do you have?” Patu took out his little pouch of shells to show him. It was a small pouch, woven with red thread—his prized possession. “I’ve brought seventeen,” he said. “If I lose the seven gold-tipped ones, I’ll bring more.” Then, showing off his pouch with a smile, he asked, “How does it look? This pouch holds a whole handful of shells.”

The game began. At first, Patu was losing, but soon he started to win. Only a few days ago, Patu had discovered that his aim in the shell game had become unerring, and that’s what had tempted him to come all this way, lured by dreams of conquest. According to the rules of the game, Patu would drop the big shell from above, take careful aim, and strike—just as a shell would go spinning out of the circle, Patu’s face would light up with boundless delight. Then, gathering up the shells he’d won, he’d tuck them into his pouch, and, out of greed and joy, glance again and again at the pouch—how much more till it’s full!

A few of the fishermen’s boys began to confer. One said to Patu, “You’ll have to shoot from a hand’s length farther away, Thakur—your fingers have too much snap!”

--- Page 94 ---
Patu said—“Well now, why, is it a crime to have more shells? You all could have won too, I never stopped anyone.”

Then he looked around; the fishermen’s boys had all gathered on one side. Patu thought—Never before have I won so many shells. I won’t play anymore today—if I do, will I even be able to take these shells home? And again, playing with one extra hand! I’ll lose everything!... Suddenly, clutching the little pouch of shells in his hand, he said—“I won’t play with an extra hand, I’m going home.”... Then, seeing the expressions and the cruel glint in the eyes of the fishermen’s boys, he unconsciously gripped his pouch of shells tightly in his fist.

One of them stepped forward and said—“That won’t do, Thakur, you think you’ll win the shells and run away?”... At once, he grabbed Patu’s hand, pouch and all. Patu tried to pull free, but couldn’t; with a troubled face he said—“Hey, let go of my hand, will you?”—From behind, someone shoved him and he fell, but he did not let go of the pouch. He understood now—this is what they’re after! As he fell, he tried desperately to hold the pouch close to his stomach, but he was just a child, and not very strong either—how long could he struggle against the sturdy, older boys of the fishermen’s quarter? Before long, the pouch slipped from his hand and landed to one side—the shells scattered in all directions.

At first, Apu hadn’t exactly been unhappy at Patu’s misfortune, for he too had lost many shells. But seeing Patu fall, especially seeing him helplessly beaten as he lay on the ground, something stirred in Apu’s chest. Pushing through the crowd, he said—“He’s just a child, why are you beating him? Hey, let him go—let go!” Then he tried to help Patu up from the ground, but a fist struck him from behind and for a moment he saw nothing; then, in the jostling, he too fell to the ground.

--- Page 95 ---
Certainly! Here is your translation, following your instructions:

---

Apu too would surely have been beaten badly that day, for his delicate, girlish hands and feet had no strength in them. But just at that moment, as Niren happened to come down this path, the opposing gang scattered. Patu had been hurt the most—Niren lifted him up from the ground and brushed the dust from his body. As soon as he regained his composure, he looked all around—except for one or two scattered shells, the rest had vanished, almost the entire pouch of shells gone.

Later, coming closer to Apu, Niren asked, "Apuda, you’re not hurt too badly, are you?"

At this hour, right at noon, Niren scolded both of them for coming so far to play shells with the fishermen’s boys. To pass the time, Niren had started a little school with the neighborhood boys at Annada Ray’s Chandimandap, and he kept urging the two of them to come study there from tomorrow. As Patu walked along, he kept thinking—how beautiful my string of shells was, I begged Chibas so much for it that day—and now it’s gone! If I don’t want to play shells anymore, what is it to them? That’s my wish!

As soon as he entered the house, Apu said to Durga, "Didi, by the shiuli tree, near the log, I left a curved stick—didn’t you break it into two pieces?"

Durga had indeed broken it. "Oh, what a precious curved stick! You and your nonsense—if you search the bamboo grove, won’t you find another stick? As if sticks are so rare!"

Apu said, embarrassed, "Not rare, you say? Go on then, bring me another stick just like that! I search so hard to find them, and you go and break everything—fine then!"

Tears welled up in his eyes.

Durga said, "I’ll bring you as many as you want right now—what’s the use of crying?"

--- Page 96 ---
A crooked stick—what a strange thing in Apu’s life! The moment he holds a dry, light stick, thick at the base and slender at the tip, bent just so, Apu’s heart thrills with delight, and the strangest imaginings awaken in his mind. With such a crooked stick in hand, he might spend an entire morning or afternoon wandering alone along the bamboo groves or by the riverbank—sometimes a prince, sometimes a tobacconist, sometimes a traveler, sometimes a general, sometimes Arjuna from the Mahabharata—imagining and muttering to himself all sorts of fantastical events that, if they were to happen to him, would fill him with joy. The lighter and more perfectly curved the stick, the more complete his happiness and the more vivid his imaginings. But Apu knows well how difficult it is to find just such a stick. How many times must he search before he finds the right one!

That Apu wanders about like this with a crooked stick in hand—he tries very hard to make sure no one ever hears of it. If anyone were to see him talking to himself like that, they’d think he was mad, or something else entirely; so, to avoid this, he steers clear of crowded places or anywhere someone might suddenly appear. Instead, he roams by the riverbank, along the lonely bamboo groves, or beneath the tamarind tree behind their house. He is always extremely careful that no one sees him like this. If, by chance, someone does appear, he instantly clicks his tongue in embarrassment and tosses the stick away—lest anyone think anything of it—for he feels terribly ashamed.

Only his didi knows. Didi had caught him like this once or twice, so what was the point of hiding it from her anymore? That’s why he asked her openly about the crooked stick. If it had been anyone else, Apu could never have mentioned it out of sheer embarrassment. Even though no one knows the mysterious bond between Apu and his crooked stick, he always feels as if everyone does, and that if he ever spoke of it, they’d all laugh and call him mad. Who could ever understand—that with a crooked stick in hand, he could spend an entire day, without eating or drinking, wandering alone by the river or through some deserted forest, in a joy beyond compare!...

He pleaded with didi—Don’t tell Ma about any of this, didi!... Durga never did.

She knows—Apu is a little mad! She feels such tenderness for him, her silly, innocent, beloved little brother—what would be the point of telling Ma about all this?...

--- Page 97 ---
On the eve of the Madhu Sankranti vow, Sarvajaya said to her son, “Tomorrow, invite your master-moshai (respected teacher) for lunch—tell him to come eat here at noon.”

Rice of coarse grains, papaya dalna (a spiced curry), dumur’er shuktoni (a bitter gourd and fig medley), thor’er ghonto (banana stem curry), chingri mach’er jhol (prawn stew), banana fritters, and payesh (rice pudding).

Durga’s mother had assigned her to serve the food. Utterly inexperienced—she placed the bowl of dal before the guest with such timid caution, as if afraid someone would scold her at any moment. Niren wasn’t used to such coarse rice, nor did he know how people could eat curries cooked with so little oil or ghee. The payesh was bland—made with watery milk; after just one taste, his enthusiasm for the dessert was cut in half. But Apu was overjoyed, eating with great gusto—such delicacies had only appeared at their home once or twice before—today was a day of celebration to remember! “Please, have a little more payesh, master-moshai…” He kept asking didi (elder sister) for this or that, again and again.

When they returned home, Gokul’s wife said with a smile, “Do you like Dugga, thakurpo (younger brother-in-law)? She’s so pretty, so sweet to look at! Ah, the daughter of a poor family, her father has no money. Who knows whose hands she’ll end up in?—She’ll suffer her whole life. So why don’t you marry her, thakurpo? She’s from your own kin, after all—the girl and your sister both look just like dolls, don’t they?…”

Returning from the surveyor’s tent that day, Niren took the path behind the village, through the mango orchard. Walking along a narrow, forest-lined trail, he saw a girl emerge from the orchard onto the path ahead—he recognized her: Apu’s sister, Durga. He asked, “So, little one, is this your family’s orchard?”

Durga, glancing back, blushed and said nothing.

Then, standing to the side of the path, she stepped aside to let Niren pass. Niren said, “No, no, little one, you go ahead. I’m glad I ran into you. I’d wandered off to the pond over there, then got lost looking for the way back. What a forest you have in your village!”

As Durga walked on, she suddenly stopped and tried to glance back at Niren’s face. At that moment, a few fruits tumbled from inside her sari onto the path.

Niren said, “Something fell, little one! What fruit is that?”

Bending down to gather them, Durga replied shyly, “Oh, it’s nothing—just mete alu (wild potatoes).”

“Mete alu? Do you like eating them? How do you eat them?”

--- Page 98 ---
This question struck Durga as exceedingly curious. Something that a five-year-old boy knows, a bespectacled learned man does not! She said, “But this fruit isn’t eaten—it’s bitter.”

“Then why do you—”

Durga replied in a shy tone, “I’m just taking it… to play with.” She still remembered how, only the other day, Khurima (paternal aunt) had teasingly brought up the topic of her marriage with this bespectacled boy. She was intensely curious, wanting to observe the boy closely. Yet, even on the day of the Makar Sankranti vow, she had not managed it, nor could she today.

“Tell Apu to bring his books tomorrow morning—will you remember to tell him?”

Durga nodded in agreement as she walked on.

After going a little further, she pointed to a side path and said, “If you take this way, it’ll be much easier for you.”

Niren said, “Alright, I’ll remember the way now. Let me walk you a little further. Will you be able to go alone?”

Durga pointed with her finger, “There’s our house, just a little ahead. I’ll go this short distance by myself now. You—”

Niren had never really looked at Durga closely before—he had only seen such beautiful expression in the eyes of her brother, Apu. As if the deep, shadowy lushness of a secluded rural lane lined with plum and bakul trees lay half-asleep in those wide eyes. Dawn had not yet broken; the languid darkness of the night still lingered. Yet, it hinted at the coming morning—the awakening of so many sleeping eyes, maidens going to the riverbank, the nectarous festival of new beginnings in every home—incense wafting from every window.

Durga stood for a moment, shifting restlessly. Niren felt as though she was struggling to find words. He said, “No, Khuki (little girl), let me walk you a bit further? Come, I’ll go right past your house.”

Durga hesitated, then bit her lip and smiled. Niren thought, now she would speak. But the next moment, Durga shook her head, indicating she didn’t need him to come along, and set off toward home.

Noon. Coming up to the roof to gather the laundry, Gokul’s wife peered into Niren’s room. In the heat, Niren had tossed and turned on his bed for a while, then, abandoning hope of sleep, spread a mat on the floor and was writing a letter home.

--- Page 99 ---
Gokul’s wife said with a smile—“You haven’t slept yet, thakurpo (younger brother-in-law)? I thought you’d fallen asleep. Today you barely touched the mocha’r ghonto (banana blossom curry)—left it right on your plate. The other day you finished it all!”

“Come, boudi (elder brother’s wife). How could I eat the mocha’r ghonto? You Bengalis make everything so spicy that once I sit down to eat, I can’t even see straight—how am I to tell what’s ghonto and what’s what?”

Gokul’s wife leaned her head against the doorframe, pressing her chin into her sari’s anchal (end of the sari) in her familiar way.

“Tsk, thakurpo, you’re putting on such airs—so city-bred! That little bit of spice, and you people over there can’t handle it—can you?”

“Forgive me, boudi, but if this is just ‘a little bit,’ then I’m not leaving here until I’ve tasted your idea of ‘a lot’! Whatever’s in my fate—jah baannano tah tipanno! (Bengali proverb: whatever is destined will happen!) One day, I’ll cast aside all shame and eat as much chili as I please.”

“Oh my, what will become of me! Do you think I’m sitting here quietly, having put away the shil-nora (grinding stone) out of modesty, thakurpo? Listen to him—‘jah baannano’…!” She laughed so hard that tears came to her eyes. After a moment, regaining her composure, she asked, “Tell me, thakurpo, how hot does it get where you live?”

“Where? In Kolkata, or out west? You couldn’t possibly imagine the heat out west from here! You can’t understand it from Bangladesh. In the month of Boishakh, can anyone sleep indoors at night? In the afternoon, we pour water over the roof to cool it, and only then can we sleep up there at night.”

“All right, how far is it from here to where you live?”

“By train, it’s almost two days’ journey from here. If you board the morning train at Majherpara station, you’ll arrive by the next day, around noon or night.”

“Tell me, thakurpo, I’ve heard they’ve cut through mountains to lay the railway towards Gaya and Kashi—is it true?”

“It’s true. Huge mountains, forests on top—when the train passes through, it’s pitch dark inside, you can’t see a thing, they have to light lamps in the carriages.”

Gokul’s wife asked eagerly, “But doesn’t it ever collapse?”

“Why would it collapse, boudi? Great engineers have built those tunnels, spent so much money—would it just fall apart? This isn’t like the steps at your Raypara ghat that crumble away every other day!”

Gokul’s wife didn’t quite understand what an engineer was. She asked, “Are the mountains made of earth or stone?”

“Some are earth, some are stone. Ah, boudi, you’re truly a village soul. Tell me, how far have you ever traveled by train?”

--- Page 100 ---
Gokul’s wife burst out laughing again, a teasing, playful laugh. Eyes half-closed, face tilted just a little upwards, she said in a childlike tone— “Oh, I’ve traveled so far, all the way to Kashi, Gaya, even Mecca! That year I went with my pishashashuri (husband’s paternal aunt-in-law) and Satu’s mother to see Jugalkishore’s fair at Aranghata. That’s the only adventure of my life—riding on a train!”

This girl, within moments, could take the slightest thread and weave around herself a web of laughter and mischief—something Niren found utterly delightful. There are some people whose hearts hold an inexhaustible store of joy, so much so that, for no reason at all, the wellspring of their inner happiness overflows and spills into others, infecting them too—this village wife was one of those rare souls. Lately, Niren found himself secretly waiting for her arrival—if she didn’t come, he felt disappointed, and perhaps even harbored a little secret resentment.

“Boudi, why don’t you all come along, let’s go out west for a change?”

“The people of this house, going out west for a stroll! You’re just as silly, thakurpo (younger brother-in-law)! And then who’ll keep watch over the brinjal patch in the north field?”

At the end of her words, she let out another round of laughter, laced with mockery and good humor. After a moment, she grew serious, her voice dropping low—“Listen, thakurpo, will you promise me something?”

“What is it? Tell me first.”

“I’ll tell you only if you promise!”

“Oh, I can’t sign on a blank sheet, boudi! You know I study law. Let me hear it first, then I’ll answer.”

Gokul’s wife left the doorway and came inside the room. From within her sari, she took out a paper-wrapped bundle and said, “If I leave these two makri (homemade sweets) with you, will you give me five rupees?”

Niren asked in surprise, “Why, tell me?”

“I won’t say just now. Will you give it, thakurpo?”

“First tell me what the money’s for. Otherwise—”

Gokul’s wife replied in a low voice, “I want to send it somewhere. Can you read what’s written in English on the top of this letter’s address?”

Niren read it and asked, “Your brother, isn’t it, boudi?”

“Hush, hush, don’t tell anyone in this house. He’s written asking for five rupees, where will I get it, thakurpo, you know how helpless I am? So I thought, these two makri—give me five rupees, thakurpo—does that poor wretch have anyone in this whole wide world?...” Gokul’s wife’s voice grew heavy with tears.

Niren said, “I’ll give you the money, boudi, five or even ten if you need, and you can pay me back whenever you can—but I can’t take the makri…”

--- Page 101 ---
Gokul’s wife, with a playful tilt of her head and a smiling face, said—  
“That won’t do, thakurpo! Oh, how clever you are! So I’ll die leaving your debt unpaid, and you—no, that won’t do, you simply must take it. Well then, I’ll be off, thakurpo, so much work waits for me downstairs—”

She hurried out of the room, but reaching the stairs, she turned back and, in a low voice, added—  
“But don’t mention the money to anyone, thakurpo! Not to a soul—do you understand?”

From beneath the quilt, Durga called out with great delight—  
“Apu, hey Apu!”

Apu was already awake, but hadn’t spoken yet. He said—  
“Didi, will you close the window? The cold wind is coming in.”

Durga got up, closed the window, and said—  
“Do you know when Ranu’s didi’s wedding is? Not much longer now. It’ll be a grand affair, with English band music. Have you ever seen an English band?”

“Yes, they all wear hats when they play, with those big flutes—huge drums, I’ve seen it—and another kind of flute, black, not so big, they call it a ‘fulot bashi’—it sounds wonderful! Have you heard a fulot bashi?”

Durga was thinking of something else.

Yesterday afternoon, she had gone to visit Khurima (auntie from the other side of the village). After some small talk, Khurima asked,  
“Durga, where did you meet thakurpo?”

She replied—  
“Why, Khurima?…” Then she recounted what had happened that day. With a teasing tone, she said,  
“Lost my way, Khurima, that’s all—right by the pond at the fort—in the middle of the woods—”

Khurima laughed and said—  
“Yesterday I was telling thakurpo about you—told him—she’s a poor man’s daughter, thakurpo, her father can’t really afford to give much—such a good girl—almost as if she doesn’t belong to this age—so, won’t you take her? That’s why thakurpo was asking about you—said, where did we meet on the path by the ghat—thakurpo lost his way and ended up somewhere—things like that. And for three days now I’ve been saying I’ll have your father told by the father-in-law thakur. Thakurpo seems to agree, seems you’ve made an impression on him—”

--- Page 102 ---
Durga did indeed lead the calf out from the cowshed and tie it up in the sun, but though on other days she would at least do some of the household chores, today she felt no such urge at all.
