There are days when her mind is seized by such a mood that she simply cannot remain confined within the boundaries of home—who knows what spirit leads her wandering from path to path, from one neighborhood to another. Today, the air itself seemed so lovely, the morning neither hot nor cold, a gentle fragrance of lemon blossoms drifting by—something stirs in her heart, though she cannot say what it is.

Stepping outside, she went to Ranu’s house. Bhuban Mukherjee is a well-to-do householder, and this is the wedding of his eldest daughter, to be celebrated with great pomp. The firework-seller had come and was haggling over prices. Seetanath, the famous rasunchouki (a type of drum) player of the region, had also been booked, and relatives from various places had already begun to arrive for the wedding. The courtyard bustled with their children’s laughter and play.

Durga’s heart brimmed with joy—how many fireworks would be set off in this very house in just a few days! She had never seen fireworks before, except once during the Ganguli family’s Phul Dole (spring swing festival), when she had glimpsed one—whoosh, it soared into the sky, seeming to touch the very clouds, then fell back down, such a marvelous sight!... Apu says, it’s called a haowi baaji (skyrocket).

After noon, when Ma spread her anchal (sari end) on the veranda and dozed off, Durga slipped out of the house once more. It was mid-Phalgun (late winter/early spring), the sun’s heat had grown fierce, and in the steady, warm wind, the big neem tree in Ranu’s garden was shedding its yellow leaves, spinning as they fell—no one was about, from the direction of the Nedas’ house someone was beating on a tin sheet. Buuuu—a strange sound rang out. A beetle! Almost unconsciously, Durga gathered her anchal into her fist and looked around with quick, searching eyes.

But it was not a beetle, it was a handsome insect.

Her clenched anchal loosened of its own accord—stepping softly, full of curiosity, she approached the creature. It sat on the path ahead, its wings marked with tiny spots, like drops of white and blood-red sandalwood paste.

--- Page 103 ---
Sudorshon Poka—not quite an insect—Thakur. To see it is a matter of great fortune—she had heard this from her mother’s lips, and from many others too. She sat down carefully on the dust, then touched her hand once to her forehead, then once to the insect, and began to chant, over and over, with quick urgency—Sudorshon, keep all things beautiful… Sudorshon, keep all things beautiful… Sudorshon, keep all things beautiful (exactly as she had heard others say it). Then she added some words of her own into the mantra—keep Apu well, keep Ma well, keep Baba well, keep Khurima from the other neighborhood well—then, after a moment’s thought, hesitantly, she said—keep Nirenbabu well, let my wedding be there, Sudorshon, let there be drums and music like at Ranu’s didi’s wedding.

With the excess of the devotee’s offerings, the little insect spun helplessly in circles on the dust; Durga, her heart’s desires fulfilled, finished her prayer and stepped aside with reverence.

Along the winding lanes within the village, overhead, the first Phalgun sky—so blue, even with a peacock’s-throat tint—peeks through the gaps in the trees.

Through the middle of the shaora grove runs the narrow path to the river’s ghat. On either side of the winding path, mango orchards. The warm air is sweet with the scent of amra blossoms; in the woods, the hum of bees and green beetles, in the deep shade of the mango groves, the koel calls, and everything is turning gentle.

Beyond the gardens, the field at the charak-tala. Shadows have fallen on the grass-filled meadow. Durga wandered among the bushes, searching for sheyakul berries—sheyakul are never large now, at the end of winter they fall away. On that high mound, in a bushy tree, she had eaten many sheyakul just the other day, but now there are none, all have fallen, and under the thick bushes, the dry sheyakul, shriveled like black pepper, are scattered. A flock of shalik birds were chattering in the bushes; as Durga approached, they flew away.

A fresh wave of happiness surged in her heart. The nearness of the festival, the hope of staying up all night in Ranu’s didi’s wedding room and listening to songs—

In her joy, she wanted to run from one end of the field to the other.

Once, she stretched out both arms, made them long like wings, spun around a little, then ran a little. She wanted to fly!… The body is such a light thing—if only, arms outstretched like wings, she could cut through the air and go!

--- Page 104 ---
Simply for the joy of making noise, she stomped hard, again and again, on the heap of dry, fallen leaves, walking along with a crackling, crunching sound. As the leaves broke beneath her feet, the place filled with the dry, dusty scent—partly earthy, partly bitter.

A little ahead, the dirt road led towards the golden fields of Sonadangar. A bullock cart was creaking its way down the path to the fields. Freshly cut bamboo poles were tied to form a frame, over which a quilt and a tattered red-bordered cloth had been draped to make a canopy. Inside the canopy, a little girl was crying in a monotonous, unbroken wail—like only children can. Perhaps she was the daughter of some village farmer, being taken from her father’s house to her in-laws’ home.

Durga stood still, gazing in wonder at the cart.

After marriage, would she too have to leave her mother, father, Apu—everyone—and go far away, just like that? Would they let her come back whenever she wished? She had never thought of this before—leaving behind this garden, the clump of basak flowers, the red cow, the jackfruit tree by the yard, all the things she loved so dearly, the scent of dry leaves, the path to the pond—would she have to leave all this forever? Perhaps the little girl inside the canopy was crying for that very sorrow.

Beyond the dirt road, just across a small, fallow field, lay the river.

Were the fishermen on the other side catching fish? Khoyra, perhaps? If they came to this side, she could buy a few paise worth of fish to take home. Apu loved to eat khoyra fish.

After returning home, she spent a long time in the evening arranging her doll’s box. Her mother, while pouring oil, had spilled quite a bit of kerosene on the floor, and the smell was wafting through the air, making it feel a little warm. The doll-arranging was almost done when Apu came in and said, “Did you take the little mirror from my box, didi?”

“Hm—The mirror is mine—I found it first, it had fallen under the bed. Go away, I’m keeping the mirror in my box. What would a boy do with a mirror anyway?”

“Oh really, your mirror? Ma brought that mirror from the house of the khurima (paternal aunt) across the river, and I had asked for it from Ma first. No, didi, give it—”

Even before finishing his sentence, he sat down beside didi’s doll box and began searching for the mirror inside.

--- Page 105 ---
Durga slapped her brother on the cheek and said—“You little rascal—here I am, trying to tidy up my dolls, and there you are, making a mess with your toys—don’t you dare touch my box—no, I won’t give you the mirror.”

But before she could finish, Apu leapt at her, clinging to her neck, grabbing a fistful of her rough hair, pulling, scratching, biting—driving her to distraction. In a voice choked with tears, he cried out—“Why do you hit me? Don’t you think it hurts me?—Give it back—I'll tell Ma—you stole red dye from Lakshmi’s pouch—”

At the mention of stealing the red dye, Durga flared up. She grabbed her brother by the ear, shook him, and landed a flurry of slaps, saying—“I took the dye?—I took the dye, you wretched, motherless monkey! And what about you—didn’t you pry the cowrie shells off Lakshmi’s pouch and hide them? Shall I not tell Ma about that?”

The shouts, the crying, the sounds of scuffling—hearing it all, Sarbajaya came running.

By then, Durga had Apu by the ear, nearly pinning him to the ground—Apu, in turn, was clutching a fistful of her hair so tightly that Durga couldn’t lift her head.

Apu had gotten the worst of it. Through his tears, he wailed—“Look, Ma, she took my mirror out of my box and put it in hers—she won’t give it back—she slapped me so hard—”

Durga protested—“No, Ma, look, I was just arranging my doll’s box, and he came and—”

Sarbajaya, without another word, landed a few hard blows on her daughter’s back and said—“You big, unruly girl—why do you keep hitting him for no reason? Don’t you know there’s a world of difference between you and him?—A mirror—what do you even need a mirror for? Always picking fights with him for every little thing. What a curse! Dolls’ boxes—always dolls’ boxes—enough!”

Without finishing, she snatched up Durga’s carefully arranged box of dolls and, with a single motion, flung it out into the yard.

—“Good-for-nothing girl, does nothing but eat and wander from house to house in the neighborhood—always with her dolls’ box, dolls’ box! I’ll drag the whole lot to the bamboo grove and throw it away right now. I’ll put an end to your games, once and for all—”

--- Page 106 ---
Durga could not bring herself to speak. Her soul lived inside her box of dolls. Ten times a day, she would arrange and rearrange it—her dolls, the bits of colored foil, scraps of patterned cloth, the little bottle of red alta dye, the hard-won natphal seeds, the tin-framed mirror, the bird’s nest—so many treasures gathered with such effort, now scattered somewhere across the dark courtyard. That Ma could so mercilessly throw away her box of dolls—Durga had never imagined such a thing! So many things, collected from so many places with such care, all inside that box!

She stood there, stunned, unable to muster the courage to say a word.

Even to Apu, the punishment seemed too harsh. Without another word, he quietly went and lay down.

Night had deepened. The smell of kerosene oil drifted up from the floor, and inside the room, the mosquitoes from the bamboo grove whined and buzzed. After sitting for a while in silence, Durga too went and lay down quietly.

Through the broken window, the light of the Phagun (spring) moon spilled across the bed. From the direction of the ruined homestead, the heady scent of lemon blossoms drifted in, thick and sweet.

For a moment, she thought of getting up, going out to gather her box of dolls and the scattered things—would she find them in the morning? All those treasures, gathered with such pain! But she did not dare. What if Ma beat her again if she tried to bring them in?

A long time passed. Suddenly, she felt a hand on her body. Apu called out, his voice trembling—“Didi?” Before Durga could answer, Apu buried his face in the pillow and burst into sobs—“I won’t do it again—I promise, don’t be angry with me, didi—I beg you!” His voice caught in his throat, choked by his tears.

At first, Durga was surprised—then she sat up and tried to soothe her brother’s sobs. “Don’t cry, hush, hush, if Ma hears she’ll scold me again, quiet, you mustn’t cry—alright, I won’t be angry, don’t cry, shh—”

She was afraid that if Ma heard Apu crying, she might beat her again.

With much coaxing, she managed to calm her brother. Then, lying beside him, she began to tell him stories—especially the story of Ranu’s didi’s wedding. After a while, Apu reached out, touched his sister’s arm, and whispered, “Can I tell you something, didi?—You’ll marry Master-moshai (the village tutor) someday—”

Durga blushed, but at the same time, a deep curiosity stirred within her. Yet, embarrassed to speak of such things with her little brother, she remained silent.

--- Page 107 ---
Apu spoke again—“Khuri-ma was telling Ranu’s mother this afternoon. Apparently, Master-moshai has no objections—”

The urge of curiosity made it impossible to remain silent. She replied in a tone of scorn—“Yes, she was saying—oh, all your stories are always like this—”

Apu almost sat up on the bed, “I’m telling the truth, didi, I swear on you! I was standing right there, and the whole thing started when they saw me. They’ll have Baba write a letter to Master-moshai’s father, wherever he lives—”

“Does Ma know?”

“I thought I’d ask Ma when I got home—but I forgot. Should I ask her, didi? Maybe Ma didn’t hear—Khuri-ma said she’d call Ma and tell her—”

Then he said, “Just wait, you’ll see how many train rides you’ll take. Master-moshai’s people live far away from here—you have to go by train—”

Durga remained silent.

She had seen pictures of trains—there was one in one of Apu’s books. Very long, with many wheels, a boiler at the front, with fire inside, smoke billowing out. The whole train was made of iron, even the wheels—not like the wooden wheels of a bullock cart. There are no thatched houses by the railway tracks—there can’t be, they’d burn down. When the train runs, does fire really come out of its pipe! Stroking her brother’s arm, she said—“I’ll take you with me too.” After that, both of them quietly settled down to sleep. As she drifted off, one thought kept circling in Durga’s mind—Thakur Sudarshan has heard her prayers! Just today she had gone to Sudarshan—Thakur is so merciful—Ma always tells the truth!

Apu said, “Such a beautiful sari has been bought for Leela-di, today Leela-di’s uncle brought it from Ranaghat for her wedding, Sej Jethima said—it’s a Baluchar sari—”

Durga replied with a smile—“Do you know a rhyme?...Pisi used to say,

On the sandy banks of Baluchar, let me tell you a tale—

I saw a peacock chick in the belly of a buffalo, my friend.”

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Nineteen**

Apu had not told anyone about this yet—not even his didi.

That day, in the quiet of noon, when he had secretly opened his father’s book-laden wooden chest, he had found this strange thing inside one of the books!

The shadow of the bamboo grove in the courtyard had not yet stretched east to west; it was exactly noon, and like the ancient banyan tree’s shade on the endless golden fields of Sonadanga, a dense patch of shadow had gathered in one spot.

--- Page 108 ---
One day, in the afternoon, while his father was away, he quietly closed the door to his room and, in secret, opened his book box. With eager anticipation, he began to open this book and that, looking at the pictures here and there, and searching to see if any of the books held a good story within their pages. Opening the cover of one book, he saw the title written: “Sarva-Darshan-Sangraha” (A Compendium of All Philosophies). What did that mean? What was the book about? He could not understand a single bit of it. As soon as he opened the book, a troop of paper-cutting insects silently emerged from beneath the faded marble-patterned endpaper and scurried off in all directions, as fast as their legs could carry them.

Apu brought the book up to his nose and inhaled—what an old smell! He loved the scent of those thick, earth-colored pages—this smell always reminded him of his father. Whenever he caught this scent, for reasons he could not explain, he always thought of his father.

The ancient marble-paper binding was worn and frayed in many places. It was this very oldness that most enchanted him about the book. For that reason, he hid the book under his pillow, put the other books away, and closed the box.

Reading in secret, it was in this very book that one day he came across a most curious thing! At first hearing, anyone would be astonished—but there it was, printed in the book, and he read it with his own eyes. Describing the properties of mercury, the author wrote: if you place mercury inside a vulture’s egg and leave it in the sun for several days, then later, if you put that egg in your mouth, a person can, if he wishes, gain the power to travel through the sky.

Apu could not believe his own eyes—he read it again—and again.

Then, hiding the book away in his battered old box, he went outside, and as he wandered, the thought kept circling in his mind, leaving him amazed.

He asked his didi (elder sister), “Didi, do you know where vultures build their nests?”

His didi could not say. He asked all the neighborhood boys—Satu, Nilu, Kinu, Patal, Nera—everyone. Some said not here, but in the northern fields, at the tops of tall trees. His mother scolded him—“Where are you wandering off to in the middle of the afternoon!” Apu came inside and pretended to lie down, but secretly opened the book again and read that passage once more—astonishing! Does no one know such an easy way to fly? Perhaps no one else has this book, perhaps only his father does, perhaps no one else has ever read this passage, perhaps only his eyes have fallen upon it after all this time.

--- Page 109 ---
He buries his face in the book again, breathing in that scent of Agrahayan—the same old, old smell! Whatever is written in this book, he no longer has any doubt about its truth.

There’s no worry about mercury—he knows what mercury is. Mercury means *para*; he knows that. The back of a mirror is coated with *para*—there’s a broken mirror at home, he can manage to get that now. But where on earth will he find a vulture’s egg?

Sometimes, after lunch, his didi calls him—“Come, listen, Apu, come see something fun!” Then she takes a handful of rice on a leaf and goes out to the bamboo grove by the back door, calling out—“Ay Bhulo—tu-u-u-u!” As soon as she calls, Durga looks at her brother with a smiling, secretive face, as if the gates to some wondrous land of mystery are about to swing open before their eyes! Suddenly, as soon as the dog appears from somewhere, Durga raises her hand and exclaims—“There he is! Did you see where he came from?” She bursts into delighted laughter.

Every day, feeding the dog rice is a great amusement for Durga. “You call him—no one’s around, everything is quiet!” She sets the rice on the ground and closes her eyes, her heart pounding with hope and curiosity—“Maybe Bhulo won’t come today, let’s see, where does he come from? Did he hear today at all?”

Suddenly, there’s a sound in the thick undergrowth—

In the blink of an eye, tearing through the vines and leaves, panting, Bhulo appears out of nowhere, swift as a shooting star.

At once, a current rushes through Durga’s whole body. Her face and eyes glow with wonder and delight! She thinks to herself—He really does hear, but from where does he come? Tomorrow, I’ll try calling very softly, let’s see if he hears then too.

To enjoy this little pleasure, she even endures Ma’s scolding, eating a little less herself at mealtime so she can save some rice for the dog.

But Apu cannot find any amusement in didi’s dog-calling. He has no part in these girlish affairs of hers. Even as he glances at the skinny, eager dog eating, he doesn’t really see it—he’s only thinking about the vulture’s egg.

At last, a clue is found. At Hiru the barber’s jackfruit tree, the cowherds tie up their cattle and go to fetch oil and tobacco from the landlord’s house. Apu goes to one of the cowherds from their para (neighborhood) and says—“You roam across so many fields—have you ever seen a vulture’s nest? If you bring me a vulture’s egg, I’ll give you two paisa.”

--- Page 110 ---
A few days later, Rakhāl came to their house and called out to him, then, reaching into the pouch at his waist, brought out two small, black-colored eggs and said—“Look, Thākur, I’ve brought these.”

Apu quickly reached out his hand and said—“Let me see.” Then, turning them over and over in delight, he exclaimed—“Vulture’s eggs! Aren’t they?”

Rakhāl produced a wealth of evidence on the matter. There was no reason to doubt that these were vulture’s eggs—he had risked his life to fetch them from the very top of some tall tree somewhere—but he wouldn’t let them go for less than two ānas.

Hearing the price, Apu saw darkness before his eyes. He said, “I’ll give you two paisa, and will you take my cowrie shells too? I’ll give you all of them, a whole tin packet of cowries—all of them. Look, such big, golden ones—want to see? I’ll show you.”

Rakhāl seemed much shrewder than Apu in worldly matters. He would not agree to anything but cash. After much bargaining, they settled at four paisa. Apu, after asking and pleading with didi, managed to scrape together two paisa and paid him, taking the eggs. Besides that, Rakhāl took some cowries too. These cowries were Apu’s very soul—he would never part with them, not for half a kingdom or a princess. But what are cowrie games compared to the thrill of flying in the sky!

Holding the eggs in his hand, his heart felt as light and swollen as a balloon puffed full of air. Yet, along with it, a shadow of doubt crept in—one that hadn’t been there before, but seemed to appear from nowhere the moment the eggs were in his grasp, faint but present. Before dusk, he sat alone on the cut stump of the shepherds’ jamun tree, pondering—would he really be able to fly? And if he could, where would he go? To his uncle’s home in the next village? To where his father was? Across the river? To where the shālik and myna birds fly, up to where that star hangs in the sky?

That very day, or perhaps the next. Just before evening, Durga was searching for scraps of cloth to make lamp wicks. Rummaging through the pile of torn and ragged cloth tucked beside the jars and pots on the shelf, something suddenly rolled off behind her and fell to the floor with a thud. The room was dark inside, hard to see; Durga picked it up from the floor and brought it outside, saying—“Oh my, what are these two big eggs here? Oh, they’ve fallen and smashed to bits—look, Ma, what bird has laid eggs inside the house!”

--- Page 111 ---
What happened after that, it is better not to speak of. That night, Apu did not eat… tears… an uproar, a commotion. His mother gossips at the ghat—“What a business with this boy, oh my, I’ve never heard such a thing before—have you heard, Sej Thakurjhi (elderly female cousin-in-law), apparently people can fly if they have a vulture’s egg—yes, that rascal of a cowherd boy from their house, the little scoundrel. Seems he told him, brought him two eggs—maybe crow’s eggs, who knows—and said, ‘Here, these are vulture’s eggs.’ And would you believe it, he bought them from him for four paisa! The boy is such a fool, what more can I say to you, Sej Thakurjhi—what am I to do with this child of mine!”

But how could poor Sarbajaya know, after all, not everyone has read the ‘Collected All-Seeing Knowledge,’ nor does everyone know the properties of mercury.

If it were so easy, everyone would be flying in the sky!

*Pather Panchali*  
Twentieth Chapter

For a long time, there had been a close bond between Apu and the village elder, Narottam Das Babaji. The pride of Ganguli Para, with his fair, radiant face, the old Sadananda lived in a humble thatched hut. He did not care much for noise or crowds, often kept to himself, and sometimes, after dusk, would go sit at the Gangulis’ Chandimandap (a pavilion for worship and gatherings). Since Apu’s childhood, Harihar would sometimes take his son along to visit Narottam Das—and from then on, the two had become fast friends. Now and then, Apu would go to the old man, call out, “Dadu, are you there?” The old man would quickly come out of his hut, spread out a mat of palm leaves on the veranda, and say—“Come, dadu bhai (dear grandson), come, sit, sit—”

--- Page 112 ---
Elsewhere, Apu is shy, words do not easily leave his lips—but with this simple, serene old man, he mingles without the slightest hesitation. His conversations with the old man are as intimate, unrestrained, and joyous as those with his playmates! Narottam Das has no one—he lives alone. A girl from a fellow Vaishnava family comes to do his chores and then leaves. Many afternoons, Apu sits with him for hours, listening to stories and telling his own. He knows that Narottam Das Babaji is much older than his own father, older even than Annada Ray—yet, perhaps because of this very old age, Apu feels as if the old man is his peer. Here, all his shyness and embarrassment seem to melt away on their own. As they talk, Apu laughs with his heart wide open, saying things he would never dare utter elsewhere, for fear that some elder might scold him as a “jetha chhele”—a cheeky boy.

Narottam Das says, “Dadu, you are my Gaur—when I see you, I feel as if my Gaur, at your age, was just as beautiful, graceful, and innocent as you—he had eyes filled with that same gentle devotion—”

Elsewhere, such words might have embarrassed Apu, but here he laughs and says, “Dadu, then show me the pictures from that book again!”

The old man brings out the ‘Prembhakti-Chandrika’ from his room—his most cherished book, which he reads in solitude, lost in rapturous delight. There are only two pictures in it; when he has finished showing them, the old man says, “When I die, I’ll leave this book to you, dadu. In your hands, the book will never be dishonored—”

One of his disciples sometimes comes by, composing songs and reciting them to him. The old man, annoyed, says, “It’s good you’ve written songs, but don’t recite them to me, bapu. The true composers were Vidyapati and Chandidas—after them, such things ring hollow in my ears—go and sing them elsewhere.”

Through the simple, humble, unadorned flow of life here, there seems to run a secret, subterranean current of liberation, and somehow Apu’s heart catches hold of it. To him, it feels as intimate and joyful as the companionship of fresh earth, birds, and trees—and that is why the pull to come to dadu is so strong within him.

--- Page 113 ---
When it was time to return, Apu gathered a heap of muchukund-champa flowers from beneath the tree in Narottam Das’s courtyard. He placed them carefully on his bed. Then, as soon as the evening lamps were lit, he had to sit down to study at his father’s command. Never for more than an hour, but to Apu it always felt as though the night had grown very old! When he was finally set free, he would go to bed, lie down, and at once—the fragrance of the muchukund-champa flowers lying on his bed, saturated with the memories of all the day’s games and joys, would overwhelm his tired body and soul, filling his boyish heart with a sweet ache for those vanished hours of play. Lying face down, he would bury his face in the pile of flowers and breathe them in for a long, long time.

That day, his didi whispered softly—“Will you come for a choruibhati, Apu?”  
(*Choruibhati: a children’s picnic, where everyone brings a little food to share*)

Through their doorway, all the neighbors of the para (village neighborhood) went off to the kului-chandi brata’s forest feast in the field behind the village, and his mother went too. But they never took him along. There, everyone brought their own things. They didn’t have so much rice or dal to spare. And at the picnic, everyone would take out all sorts of things—fine rice, dal, ghee, milk—while his mother would bring out only coarse rice, ground motor dal, and a couple of brinjals. Sitting nearby, Bhuvan Mukherjee’s second grandmother’s children would eat rice mashed with milk, bananas, and slabs of new sugarcane jaggery, and his mother’s heart would ache for her own children.

Apu too loved to eat rice mashed with milk, bananas, and patali jaggery, just like that!...

The red dusk would descend on the quiet woods at the edge of the field, and as she walked back along the bamboo grove path, only the thought of her son would fill her mind.

On the far side of Neelmoni Ray’s jungle-covered homestead, Durga had cleared a patch of forest herself with a dao (curved village knife), and told her brother, “Stand there under the tamarind tree and see if Ma is coming—I’ll get the rice out quickly—”

From a good coconut garland, she quietly poured out two measures of oil from the oil jar. Smuggling the loot outside, she entrusted it to her brother, saying, “Quick, take this, Apu—run and leave it there, but make sure no cows or anything eat it—”

Just then, Mator’s Ma entered the courtyard through the back door, her little boy trailing behind. Durga said, “Where did you come from, Tomrej’s bou?”

Mator’s Ma wasn’t very old, nor was she bad-looking, but ever since her husband’s death, hardship had worn her down, leaving her pale and thin. She said, “I’d gone to the kuthir field to gather firewood—will you take a garland of buincha flowers?”

--- Page 114 ---
Durga herself often goes searching through the woods and gardens, picking handfuls of baichi fruit, shaking her head as she says—she won’t buy any.

Mator’s mother said—Take some, didi thakuron, they’re very sweet, I picked them from the edge of Madhukhali’s pond—she pulled out a bunch of garlands from her sari’s end and showed them—Look how big they are! If I took them to the market with the firewood, sold them, got some coins, it would be so late by then. I could’ve bought Mator a coin’s worth of puffed rice in the meantime. Take them, I’ll give you two bunches for a paisa—

But Durga wouldn’t agree, she said—Apu, there’s a handful of puffed rice in the pot, bring it and give it to Mator!

As soon as they slipped out the back door, the two of them set off with their things.

All around, the place was ringed by forest. From outside, you couldn’t see in. In a tiny clay pot, no bigger than the ones in their playhouse, Durga set the rice to cook and said—Look, Apu, see how big the wild potatoes I’ve brought from one spot! Under the palm at the Puti’s place, there’s a bush with plenty more. I’ll put them in the rice...

Apu, full of excitement, gathered dry twigs and sticks. This was their first real forest picnic. Apu still couldn’t quite believe that real rice and curry would be cooked here, not like the playhouse picnics they’d had so many times before—dust rice, broken-tile fried potatoes, jackfruit-leaf luchis?

But oh, what a beautiful afternoon!—what a perfect spot for a forest feast! All around, thickets and wild groves, over there the swinging tendrils of ivy gourd, beneath the wood-apple tree a tangle of jungle shrubs blooming with sheora flowers, wagtail birds dancing and darting across the half-burnt patches of tough durba grass, the secluded, hidden spot behind the silent bushes. On this first day of spring, new tender leaves sprouted on every shrub, clusters of ghetu flowers lit up the old abandoned homestead, and though many blossoms had fallen from the pomelo tree in the past days’ mist, bunches of white flowers still caught the eye on the upper branches.

These days, Durga seemed to cling ever more tightly to these trees and plants, these paths and corners of the village she knew so well. Some sorrow of coming separation seemed to shroud the beloved path by the gabtala, the bamboo grove behind their house, the shaded riverbank. Her Apu—her golden little brother, whom she could not bear to be without for even a day, whose absence made her heart ache—how far would she have to go, leaving him behind?

And what if she never returned—if she became like Nitam pisi (the aunt who never came back)?

--- Page 115 ---
On this very homestead lived Nitom pisi (father’s younger sister), who, after her marriage, left for somewhere far away long ago, and never returned to her father’s land. It is a story from ages past—one heard since childhood, woven into the fabric of family tales. Everyone says she was married off to Murshidabad district—but how far is that? Where is it? No one keeps track of her, whether she is alive or not, no one knows. Nitom pisi never saw her father again, nor her mother, nor her brothers and sisters. One by one, all of them have passed away. Oh Ma, how can people become so cruel? Why did no one ever look for her again? How many times, in lonely moments, has she wept thinking of Nitom pisi! If she were to return suddenly today—what would she think, seeing this father’s homestead, now overgrown with dense jungle and deserted by all?

What if the same fate awaits her? What if she too, like Nitom pisi, leaves behind her father, mother, Apu—never to see them again—never, ever again—this house of theirs, the gabtala (cluster of wild guava trees), the path to the bathing ghat?

The very thought sends a shiver through her. No, she does not want that. Yet, for some reason, lately it seems to her that something is about to happen in her life, something momentous and imminent, something that comes only once and never again. In the midst of play and chores, day and night, this thought keeps returning to her mind… She cannot quite grasp what it is, or how the thought arises, but still, she feels it, always feels it—it is coming… coming… coming soon…

In the middle of the choruibhati (children’s picnic) in Apu’s yard, a voice was heard calling out. Durga said—That sounds like Bini’s voice—go call her, Apu. A little while later, a dark-skinned girl of Durga’s age followed behind Apu—smiling a little, and with a hint of respectful awe in her tone, she said—What’s happening, Durga didi?

Durga said—Come, Bini, we’re having a choruibhati—sit down—

The girl was the daughter of Kalinath Chakkatti from the other side of the village—she wore a half-soiled sari, thin glass bangles on her wrists, her figure a bit tall, her face utterly simple and unadorned. Her father, being a yogi Brahmin, meant that their family was never invited to social events in the village; they lived in a cramped corner on the edge of the village. Their circumstances were not good. Bini set to work eagerly on Durga’s requests. Having come out to wander, she had suddenly found herself in the midst of something delightful and rewarding, and now, whether these children would accept her as a part of their festival or not—a kind of hesitant joy was evident in her words and gestures. Durga said—Bini, see if you can find two more dry sticks—the fire isn’t burning well—

--- Page 116 ---
Bini at once ran off to fetch wood, and a little while later she appeared, bearing a bundle of dry bel branches, and announced, “This will do, Dugga didi—should I bring more?”

When Durga said, “Bini has come—she’ll eat here too—Apu, bring two more handfuls of rice—”

Bini’s face lit up with joy. A little later, she brought water. In a tone full of curiosity, she asked, “What curries are there, Dugga didi?”

Taking the rice off the fire, Durga poured a little oil into the pan and tossed in the eggplant, frying it. After a while, she gazed at the pan in surprise, then called out to Apu, “Look, Apu! The color’s turning out just like real begun bhaja (fried eggplant)! Doesn’t it look just like Ma’s cooking?”

Apu, too, found it astonishing. Until now, he could hardly believe that their forest picnic would have real rice, real begun bhaja! Then the three of them, in great delight, sat down to eat on banana leaves—just rice and fried eggplant, nothing else. As Apu lifted a mouthful to his lips, Durga watched him eagerly and asked, “How is it, the begun bhaja?”

Apu replied, “It’s good, didi, but I think there’s no salt—”

Salt, as an ingredient, had been entirely forgotten today; they hadn’t bothered with it at all. Yet, in the midst of their great happiness, the three of them sat down to their choruibhati (picnic meal), eating rice with bits of roasted potato and bland, half-burnt begun bhaja. This was Durga’s very first attempt at cooking, and with a mixture of wonder and joy, she savored her own handiwork! Here, in the midst of this wild thicket, among heaps of dry leaves, sitting beside the fallen fronds under the date palm, they were eating real rice and curry.

As they ate, Durga looked at Apu and burst into a peal of delighted laughter. She was so happy that the lump of rice seemed to get stuck in her throat. Bini, eating timidly, asked, “Is there a bit of oil left, Dugga didi? I’d mix it with the rice and roasted potato.”

Durga said, “Apu, run and fetch a little oil—”

Life, with its endless store of delights, its moments bright with the light and moonshine of joy—this was only the beginning of their sweet journey. The endless path of life, stretching far beyond the horizon, was still unknown to them; they were but tiny travelers on that road, greeted by every turn with new flowers, fruits, sorrows, and joys. For them, every welcome was entirely new.

--- Page 117 ---
Joy! Joy! The joy of expanse, the joy of those hidden spaces in life, the joy of that unseen path beyond the vast, snow-crowned mountain’s chasm! Today’s joy! The joy found in the smallest, most trivial things!

Apu said—“Will you tell Ma, didi? Will you eat rice again this afternoon?”

“Pfft, when do I ever tell Ma! Just wait, you’ll feel hungry after evening—right now—”

Because Jugir Bamun (the Brahmin of the neighborhood) is considered an outsider, if he wants to drink water in the village, people give it to him in a separate vessel, and even then, he has to wash it himself. Bini hesitated a little, then, showing Apu the glass, said—“Apu, could you pour a little water on my cheek? I’m so thirsty!”

Apu said—“Take it, Binidi, just take it and have a sip!”

Still, Bini seemed not to have the courage. Durga said—“Go on, Bini, take the glass and drink!”

After they finished eating, Durga said—“Don’t throw away the pot, okay? We’ll have another picnic some other day—what do you think? Let’s hang it up on that kul tree (Indian jujube tree) over there.”

Apu said—“Yes, but will it stay there? Mator Ma comes to gather firewood—if she sees it, she’ll take it, didi—such a thief—”

Durga placed the basket inside a hole in a broken wall.

Apu’s heart was thumping. On the other side of that hole was another small crevice, where Apu had hidden a cigar box—what if didi went over there and found it!

--- Page 118 ---
A few days ago, Nerā’s brother-in-law and one of his friends had come to their house. Their home is somewhere near Calcutta. Very much a city gentleman, always smoking cheroots. He would smoke one, and then, almost immediately, light up another. In Apu’s heart, a great longing had awakened—he too wanted to try a cheroot, just once, to see what it was like.

After consulting with Nerā, he went to Harish the Yogi’s shop in the village and bought ten cheroots wrapped in red paper for three paisa. That day, sitting alone in the dense jungle, he had quietly lit one and smoked it. He hadn’t liked it at all—a bitter taste, a harshness, a strange burning. After two puffs, he couldn’t go on. Yet, he couldn’t bring himself to throw away the remaining four cheroots from his share. He’d hidden them, tucked away in an empty cheroot box he’d collected from Nerā’s brother-in-law, stashed in the crevice of a broken wall amid the overgrown ruins.

The day he smoked his first cheroot, when it was finished, his heart had thudded with fear—what if Ma noticed the smell on his breath? He’d eaten a lot of ripe jujube fruit, cupped his hand to his mouth and checked his breath again and again, before daring to re-enter the world of people. What if, today, he was caught red-handed with the evidence!

But there was no need to go over the wall to Didi’s side. Everything could be managed on this side itself.

*Pather Panchali*, Chapter Twenty-One

Sarbojaya heard the news at the ghat, from the lips of the neighborhood women.

--- Page 119 ---
For the past few days, there had been a rift between Niren and Annada Ray, especially with her son Gokul. Yesterday afternoon, it seems, there was quite a quarrel and shouting. As a result, Niren packed up his things and left here last night. Annada Ray’s neighbor, Harimoti, wife of Jagneshwar of Dighari, was saying—“I don’t know what’s true or false, but I’ve been hearing all sorts of things these days—I don’t really believe it, boudi (sister-in-law), she’s not like that. Then again, I heard Niren secretly gave her money, and boudi sent the money somewhere, and the receipt in Niren’s handwriting came back and ended up in Gokul’s hands—all this. Well, what’s the use of listening to others’ gossip? I heard Niren said—‘You all together can torment one person and that’s not wrong?—Think what you will, boudi, if you just give the word, I’ll carry her on my head like my lost mother and take her away—then you can do as you please.’ After that, there was quite an uproar for a while—before evening he called a cart from Goylapara, took his things, and left.”

Sarbojoya was deeply shaken when she heard this. In the meantime, she had already urged her husband to write to Niren’s father, through Annada Ray, about the marriage proposal. She had invited Niren home two more times—the boy had greatly pleased her. Harihar had tried to explain to her many times, “Niren’s father is a wealthy man—would he ever agree to marry his son into our family?” But Sarbojoya had not given up hope; somewhere in her heart she had found a courage—this marriage proposal did not feel like a hopeless dream, it would happen. Even if Harihar did not believe it in his heart, at his wife’s urging he had pressed Annada Ray about it several times. But now, what a great calamity had occurred!

Meanwhile, one day on the road, Durga met Gokul’s wife. She quietly told Durga many things—the whole story of why Niren had left. As she spoke, tears streamed down her cheeks.

“Days will just pass with this kind of sweeping and scolding—there’s no one, Dugga—so how will my brother grow up? There’s nowhere to rest even for two days—nowhere at all—”

--- Page 120 ---
Moved by sympathy, Durga’s heart swelled, and at the same time, a fierce protest against Khurima’s (paternal aunt’s) disgrace and a jumble of comforting words for her sorrow began to form, indistinctly, in her mind. Unable to arrange all her thoughts into words, she simply said, “Oh Sakhi Thakuma (old woman, friend), what people! Let them talk, what can they do? Don’t cry, Khurima, my precious one, I’ll come to you every day—”

Hearing this, Sarbojaya asked eagerly, “Daughter-in-law, what did Durga say? And—was there any talk about Niren?”

Durga replied in a shy tone, “Why don’t you ask at the ghat tomorrow? I don’t know—”

Apu asked once, “What did you hear from Khurima, will Mastermoshai (the teacher) not come anymore?”

Durga scolded, “How should I know—go on—”

In the slanting afternoon sunlight, the shadow-filled path felt strangely wistful. It was for her brother. This happened to her often—how many times it had happened—if she stayed away from home too long, or didn’t see her brother, her mind would fill with all sorts of imagined sorrows for him, and her heart would ache.

Her little brother, with his milky complexion like a golden doll, was sitting alone in front of the house, lost in his own world, playing with cowrie shells and eggplant seeds, wearing a dirty, half-torn piece of cloth. He would ask her for coins to buy this or that, but she couldn’t give him any—how it pained her heart—

A few days later. At Bhuban Mukherjee’s house, Ranu’s didi’s (elder sister’s) wedding was over, but the guests and relatives had not all left yet. There were still many children. Durga had become quite friendly with a little girl named Tuni. Her father had come too. That afternoon, after leaving his wife and daughter here for a few days, he had gone back to his workplace. About an hour later, as Sej Thakrun (the second-oldest grandmother) was busy with something in this room, Tuni’s mother’s voice reached her ears. Sej Thakrun came out to the verandah and asked, “What is it, Hasi?” Tuni’s mother, flustered and anxious, was rummaging through the bedding, searching under pillows, turning over the mattress, and said, “Just a little while ago, I left my gold sindur (vermilion) box right here beside the bed, Khoka (the little boy) started crying in the cradle, he came in from outside—and in the rush, I forgot to pick it up—now it’s gone, I can’t find it anywhere—”

Sej Thakrun said, “Oh my, really? You didn’t take it with you?”

“No, didima (grandmother), I left it right here. I remember clearly, exactly here—”

--- Page 121 ---
Everyone gathered and searched around for a while, but there was no sign of the tin. Sej Thakrun (the second eldest grandmother) inquired and learned that, at first, it was the children of this house who were in the verandah; then, when the call for food came, all the children went to eat, and among the outsiders, only Durga was left. Sej Thakrun’s younger daughter, Tenpi, whispered quietly—“When we came to eat, Dugga-di (Durga-didi, elder sister Durga) was slipping out through the back door, just then she came back again—”

Sej Thakrun whispered some advice, then, in a harsh tone, said to Durga—“Give back the tin, Dugga. Where have you kept it? Bring it out right now, I’m telling you—”

Durga’s face shriveled up, she seemed to shrink into herself; at Sej Thakrun’s manner, her tongue seemed to curl up inside her mouth. She mumbled something indistinctly, it was hard to make out what she said.

Tuni’s mother had not said a word all this while—she was a little surprised that everyone was ganging up to call a girl from a respectable family a thief, especially since she had seen Durga here for a few days, and liked her for her good looks—was it possible that she would steal? She said—“I don’t think she took it, Sejdi (elder sister-in-law)—why would she—”

Sej Thakrun said—“Why don’t you keep quiet! What do you know about her, whether she took it or not, I know very well—”

Someone else said—“If you have it, bring it out, or at least say where it is—let’s get this trouble over with. Give it back, dear, why lie—”

Durga seemed to be in a strange state—her legs were trembling, she leaned against the wall and said—“I don’t know, Kakima (aunt)—I really don’t—”

Sej Thakrun said—“You think I’ll believe you just because you say so? She definitely took it—I can tell by her manner. All right, I’m telling you nicely, where have you kept it, give it back, if you return the thing I won’t say anything else—just give me my thing—”

The aforementioned relative said—“I’ve never heard of a girl from a respectable family stealing. Is her house in this very neighborhood?”

Sej Thakrun said—“You’re no one to talk sense! You’ll see, just wait, you thought you could get away with taking something from my house—did you think you’d found just anything?—Today I’ll—”

Then she grabbed Durga’s hand and, dragging her, brought her to the very center of the verandah and said, “Durga, tell me now, where have you kept it?... Won’t you say?... No, you don’t know, you silly girl—you know nothing—speak quickly, or I’ll smash all your teeth to pieces right now! Speak up—say it now, I’m telling you—”

--- Page 122 ---
Tunir ma (Tuni’s mother) was coming forward, reaching out her hand, when one of the kutumbini (female relatives by marriage) said, “Don’t cry, can’t you see she’s taken care of it? The only cure for a thief’s mother—just give it to her, it’ll be settled right away—why make a fuss—”

Durga’s head felt strange. Helplessly, she looked around, and with great difficulty, her dry tongue stumbling, she managed to utter, “But I don’t know, Kakima (auntie), they all left, I too—” As she spoke, fear stiffened her, and she kept her eyes fixed on Sej Thakrun (the second eldest grandmother), inching closer to the wall.

Afterwards, everyone together tried to reason with her for a while longer. But she kept repeating the same thing—she didn’t know.

Someone said, “A seasoned thief—”

Tepi said, “The mangoes from the garden never fall to the ground, Kakima—”

Perhaps it was this last remark that struck some old wound in Sej Thakrun’s heart. Suddenly, she let out a thunderous cry and shouted, “So, you wretch, you shameless thief’s brat, you won’t give it up? Let’s see if you do or not!” Before she finished speaking, she pounced on Durga, grabbed her head, and began banging it hard against the wall. “Tell me where you’ve hidden it—tell me now—tell me quickly—tell—”

Tunir ma rushed forward and grabbed Sej Thakrun’s hand, crying, “What are you doing—what are you doing, Sejdi (elder sister-in-law)—let it be, it’s only my box—why beat her like that? Let go—enough, let her go, shame!”

Seeing her mother, Tuni began to cry. The earlier kutumbini exclaimed, “Oh! She’s bleeding—”

No one had noticed that blood was streaming from Durga’s nose. A patch of her bodice was turning crimson.

Tunir ma said, “Quick, Tepi, bring some water—look, there’s a bucket on the verandah—”

Hearing the shouting and commotion, the blacksmith’s wives from the neighboring house came to see what was happening. Ranur ma (Ranu’s mother) hadn’t been there all this time—after lunch, she’d been sitting at the blacksmith’s house, gossiping—she came too.

From the beating, Durga’s head was ringing; dazed, she looked once into the crowd—what did she see?

When the water came, Ranur ma splashed it on her face and eyes, then helped her to sit down. Her head was still spinning, and she sat, lost and bewildered. Ranur ma said, “How can you beat her like that, Sejdi?… The poor girl is so frail—shame—”

“You all don’t know her yet. There’s no cure but a thief’s mother’s beating, I tell you—she hasn’t had enough yet—if it’s not found, should I let her go? May Hari Ray hang me high if I let this go—”

--- Page 123 ---
Ranur ma said, “That’s enough, now let Sejdidi handle it a bit—what a spectacle you’ve made—”

Tuni’s ma said, “Oh my, if I’d known it would come to this, who would have mentioned the box at all?...I don’t want my box—let her go, Sejdidi—”

Whether Sej Thakrun (elderly aunt) would have let go so easily is hard to say, but public opinion began to turn against her... So she was forced to release the accused.

Ranur ma took her by the hand, opened the side door, and led her out into the little courtyard by the back gate. She said, “You’ve hardly been out of the house today, after all! Go on, go slowly—Tepi, make sure you open the back gate properly—”

Durga, bewildered, slipped out through the back gate, and all the women and girls present watched her go.

Someone said, “Still, she didn’t confess—did you see her? Not a single tear from her eyes—”

Ranur ma said, “Tears? She’s dried up from fear. Is there any water left in those eyes? Sejdidi beats her like that!”

**Pather Panchali**

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Two**

The time for the village’s Barowari Charak Puja (a communal spring festival) had arrived. Baidyanath Majumdar of the village came collecting subscriptions, notebook in hand, going house to house. Harihar said, “No, khuro (uncle), it’s really too much to expect a whole taka from me this year—am I in any state to give a taka?” Baidyanath replied, “No, no, this year it’s Neelmoni Hajra’s troupe. No one in this region has ever seen such a group. This year, Mahesh Sakar’s boys’ kirtan troupe will sing at the Palpara market, we must match them—”

Baidyanath made it seem as though the very life and death of the people of Nishchindipur depended on the success of this competition.

Apu, dragging a bamboo stick behind him, came into the house and said, “A ripe stick, Baba, it’ll make you a fine pen, I found it by the bamboo grove near the pond, brought it home—” Then, smiling, he held it up a little higher and said, “Won’t it do for your pen, Baba? See how sturdy it is?”

There wasn’t much time left before Charak. The gajan sannyasis (ascetic dancers of the festival) had set out, dancing from house to house. Durga and Apu, forsaking food and sleep, wandered from neighborhood to neighborhood, trailing behind the sannyasi troupe. Other households gave old clothes, rice, coins—some even gave pitchers—but their own house could offer nothing but a handful of rice or two—so the troupe never came to their door! After ten or twelve days of sannyasi dancing, on the eve of Charak, Neel Puja arrived.

--- Page 124 ---
On the evening of Nilpuja, the sannyasis break thorns from a small date palm tree—this year, Durga came with news: that tree, which every year was chosen for the ritual, would not be used this time. The sannyasis had already decided, from the very beginning, to pick another tree by the riverside. Durga and Apu, joining forces with the neighborhood boys, made their way there. After the thorn-breaking dance was done, everyone went for a stroll around the charaktala—the fairground where the Charak festival takes place. The Nilpuja mandap was fenced with date palm fronds—charaktala’s field had been cleared, the wild sheora groves and other thickets cut away.

There, they met the girls from the house of Bhuban Mukherjee—Rani, Puti, Tunu—girls whose home was ruled by strict discipline, who were not allowed to wander about freely like Durga. With much pleading, they had managed to come as far as the charaktala.

Tunu said, “Tonight the sannyasis will go to keep vigil at the cremation ground—”

Rani said, “Oh, don’t you know? There’ll be a corpse. They’ll tie it up and take it to the shatimtala—the old satinwood tree—at the cremation ground. Then they’ll bring it back to life, and after that, they’ll bring the skull—chanting their rhymes as they come—they know all the mantras—”

Durga said, “I know their rhymes. Want to hear? I’ll tell you:

From the heavens came the chariot  
Descended to the field below  
Twenty-four huts, monsoon’s arrows, march with Shiva—  
A corpse from the age of truth, and earth from the age of awe  
Chant Shiva’s name, brother, and strike the drum—”

Then, laughing, she said, “How wonderful the dolls are this year at Goshtabihar, Niluda! I went to see them at Dashu the potter’s house—didn’t you see, Ranu?”

Puti asked, “Is it a real skull, Ranudi?”

“Of course! If you come late at night, you’ll see it. Come on, let’s go home—tonight isn’t a good night—come, Apu, come, Dugga-di.”

Apu asked, “Why isn’t it a good night, Ranudi? What will happen tonight?”

Ranu replied, “Those things shouldn’t be spoken of—just come home.”

Apu did not go, but Durga went off with their group. Then suddenly clouds gathered, and the darkness of evening grew thick with storm. Apu was returning home; there was not a soul on the path. After hearing tales of the cremation ground and the skull since dusk, a strange fear crept over him. Near the bamboo grove at the bend, he thought he smelled something foul in the air. He hurried his steps. Just a little further on, he met Nerar Thakuma—the old woman who tended the mongrel dog. Nerar Thakuma was on her way to offer the Nilpuja naivedya—the ritual offering—at charaktala. In the darkness, Apu did not recognize her at first; then, realizing, he asked, “What’s that smell, Thakuma?”

--- Page 125 ---
The old woman said—“Do you think they’ve all gone out tonight?... That’s their scent, isn’t it—”

Apu asked—“Who, Thakuma?”

“Who else—Shiva’s retinue, his whole band. One mustn’t utter their names at twilight—Ram Ram—Ram Ram—”

A shiver ran down Apu’s spine. All around, the evening darkness pressed in, black clouds hung in the sky, the scent of bamboo groves and cremation grounds, Shiva’s ghostly attendants—spirits and phantoms—filled the air. The little boy’s heart brimmed with wonder, fear, mystery, and the thrill of the unknown. In a trembling voice, he said—“How will I get home, Thakuma?...”

The old woman scolded him—“And why did you stay out so late today, child?... Come with me. Let me take this plate for the Nil Puja, then I’ll see you home. Goodness gracious—”

Beneath the communal banyan, the grass had been trimmed, a great bamboo frame lashed together, and a canopy stretched overhead. The jatra troupe was on its way—hadn’t arrived yet; as evening deepened, people said they’d come by tomorrow morning’s cart, and when morning passed, they pinned their hopes on the afternoon. Apu’s daily bath and meals were on the verge of being forgotten... At night, sleep eluded him, swept away by a flood of curiosity and joy, wild and unstoppable as a river in spate! He tossed and turned in bed. Jatra is coming! Jatra is coming! Jatra is coming!

Mother had forbidden Durga, now such a big girl, from leaving the neighborhood, but she would slip out quietly, come back and tell Rajlakshmi stories about the festival preparations and the bright red and blue paper decorations hanging from the bamboo poles. To Apu, it seemed impossible that the very spot at Panchanan Tala, where he played marbles twice a day, that utterly familiar, humble place, would soon witness something as extraordinary and unreal as a performance by Neelmoni Hajra’s jatra troupe. Could such a thing truly happen? He could hardly believe it.

Then suddenly, word spread that the troupe would arrive this very afternoon. A flush of excitement surged from his chest, leaping up into his head like a rush of blood...

Since noon, Apu had been standing with all the other boys at the Kumar Para crossroads, when at last he spotted a bullock cart in the distance, piled high with boxes of costumes—one, two, three, four, five carts! Patu counted them off on his fingers, his voice bubbling with delight—“Apuda, let’s follow them to their house and have a look, shall we?” Behind the carts, the troupe members walked along, each with a bundle on his head, many carrying their shoes in their hands. Patu pointed out a bearded man—“That must be the one who plays the king, right, Apuda?”

--- Page 126 ---
The color of the sky and air changed completely—Apu, brimming with excitement, returned home to find his father sitting on the veranda, writing something on sandpaper and humming a tune. He thought, surely Baba has heard about the jatra (folk theater troupe) coming, that's why he’s in such high spirits. Waving his hands in enthusiasm, Apu exclaimed, “Baba, there are five whole carts of costumes! Such a troupe!”

Harihar, who was writing protective charms on sandpaper to distribute at the disciple’s house, looked up in surprise and asked, “What costumes, Khoka (affectionate term for child)?”

Apu was astonished—such a grand event, and Baba didn’t know about it! He regarded his father as a pitiable soul.

In the morning, Apu had to sit down and study. After a while, on the verge of tears, he pleaded, “Baba, I want to go to the Baroaritola (community gathering place), everyone’s going, and I have to just sit here and study? What if the jatra starts right now?”

His father replied, “Study, study, sit and read for now. When the jatra begins, won’t you hear the sound of the drums? Then you can go. For now, stay here.” A man of mature years, Harihar was often away from home these days, and now, back for a short while, he didn’t want to let his son out of his sight. In wounded pride and anger, tears welled up in Apu’s eyes, and in a choked voice, he began reciting his lesson again—“For as many months as there are in a year, how many days follow?”

But the jatra didn’t start in the morning; word came that it would be held in the afternoon. That afternoon, Apu went to his mother, and in a tearful voice, recounted every detail of his father’s tyranny. Sarbajaya came and said, “Let the boy go, won’t you? It’s the annual festival—you're not home for nine months of the year, and will he become a scholar in just one day’s study?”

Apu was granted leave. He spent the entire afternoon at the Baroaritola. In the evening, before the jatra began, he returned home to eat. His father was sitting on the veranda, writing charms. On other days, at this hour, Apu would have to sit beside his father and read. Fearing his son might get upset, Harihar tried to keep him happy with all sorts of jokes. He said, “Khoka, quickly write ‘Sylhet’ for me, will you? Oh, what a ghostly father I am!...” Hearing all these strange things, Apu burst out laughing, quickly wrote it down, and showed it to him. “Baba, once this is done, I’m going to leave, okay?” he said. His father replied, “Go now, go now, Khoka—alright, quickly write it for me—” and then said another odd thing. Apu laughed again.

--- Page 127 ---
Today, though, Apu felt as if some immense force from outside had come and swept him away from his father. Baba sits alone in the shade-drenched, bamboo-grove-encircled house, writing in the solitude of dusk, but he no longer has the strength to keep Apu by his side. Now, if he were to say—“Khoka, come, sit and study”—at once, it would be as if a terrible uproar of protest would rise from all around. Everyone would seem to cry out—No, no, this cannot be! This cannot be! The jatra (folk theater) is about to begin!... Some wild, jubilant force has rendered his father utterly helpless, innocent, and weak. He cannot even bring himself to utter a word about studying. Apu’s heart aches for his father.

Durga said—“Apu, won’t you ask Ma if I can go too?”  
Apu replied—“Ma, why shouldn’t didi (elder sister) come with me? They’ve fenced it off with chik (bamboo screens), she can sit there.”

Ma said—“Not now, let the girls from their house go, you’ll go with them—right now, you’ll come with me.”

As he was leaving for the Barowaritola (the community fairground), Durga called out to him from behind—“Listen, Apu!” Then she came closer, her face bright with a smile—“Hold out your hand!” As soon as Apu held out his palm, Durga placed two coins in it, then clasped his hand within both of hers and said—“Buy some murki (puffed rice sweets) with these two coins, or if they’re selling nichu (wild berries) cheap, then buy those and eat.”

About a week earlier, Apu had come and quietly asked his didi—“Do you have any coins in your doll’s box? Will you give me one?”  
Durga had replied—“What do you need a coin for?”  
Apu looked at her face, smiled a little, and said—“I want to eat nichu”—then, embarrassed, he smiled again. In a tone of explanation, he added—“The boshtom (Vaishnavi mendicants) have built a macha (platform) in their garden, they’ve picked lots of nichu, two whole baskets—six for a paisa, this big, bright red like sindur (vermilion), if you buy from Satu or Sadhan—” He paused, then asked—“Do you have any, didi?”

There had been nothing in Durga’s doll’s box that day, so she couldn’t give him anything. Seeing Apu walk away with a disappointed face had pained her deeply. That’s why, yesterday evening, she’d asked Baba for two coins, saying she wanted to see the chark (festival swing). Her little brother, golden as a coin, if she can’t fulfill his wishes, Durga’s heart aches terribly.

After Apu left, their mother came up from the ghat (river steps) and said—“Durga, do something, will you? Go to Ranu’s garden and find two white leaves of gandhabhedali (a medicinal plant)—Apu’s not feeling well, I’ll make a little broth for him!”

--- Page 128 ---
At his mother’s bidding, he runs off to Ranu’s garden—into the thicket of weeds, tall as a grown man, searching for the leaves of the fragrant vetiver grass. In the joy of the hunt, he shakes his head, reciting a rhyme he learned as a child from Pishima’s (aunt’s) lips—

  In the yellow woods, in the yellow woods—
  My nose-ring is lost, my heart is heavy with sorrow—

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Three**

The journey begins. The world is gone, everyone is gone—only Apu remains, and before him, the Jatra (folk theatre) troupe of Nilmoni Hajra. Before dusk, the violinist plays an alap in raga Iman—a fine violinist. A village boy like him has never heard such music before—its wistful, plaintive notes suddenly stir his heart... It seems as if Baba is still sitting at home, writing something, just as before—Didi wanted to come, but could not. 

When, for the first time, the king’s ministers enter the stage, dressed in zari-embroidered costumes, under the light of hanging lanterns and strings of cowrie shells, Apu thinks—Baba never saw such things! Everyone from the village is here, not a soul from their para (neighborhood) is missing! Why isn’t Baba here yet...? The play moves swiftly. He remembers the time he heard the boys’ kirtan troupe perform Jatra—how different that was from this! What costumes! What faces!...

Suddenly, someone behind him says—Khoka, can you see well?... Apu hadn’t realized when his father had come and sat in the audience. Turning to him, he asks—Baba, has Didi come?... Is she behind the screen?

When, through the minister’s secret plot, the king is banished and sets off for the forest with his wife and son, the violin’s accompaniment turns to a mournful tune. Then, to prolong the mood of pathos, the king, holding his wife and son’s hands, takes a step, pauses, takes another step, and so on—surely, in the real world, no king about to go into exile would behave so, unless he were quite mad, to do such a thing in front of a crowd! The loyal royal general shakes with such rage that even an epileptic patient would be envious. Apu sits, eyes wide and unblinking, utterly enchanted and amazed—he has never seen anything like this before.

--- Page 129 ---
After that, where did the king go, where did the queen vanish!... In the dense, shadowy forest, only the royal siblings—Prince Ajay and Princess Indulekha—wander about! There is no one to look into their faces, no one to guide their path through the lonely woods. Indulekha, going a little distance to fetch fruit for her younger brother, does not return. Ajay searches the forest for his sister—then, by the riverbank, he suddenly finds Indulekha’s lifeless body—driven by hunger, she has eaten a poisonous fruit and died. Ajay’s plaintive song—“Where have you gone, leaving me in this wilderness, my dearest companion of life”—hearing this, Apu, who had been gazing on, entranced, can bear it no longer, and bursts into tears, sobbing and sobbing.

What a swordplay in the battle between King Kalingaraj and Bichitraketu!... The swords flash, it seems the bushes will be chopped to bits, or else some unlucky spectator’s eyes might be lost! The crowd shouts—“Mind the bushes! Mind the bushes!” But what strange battle tactics—he spares everything—glorious Bichitraketu!

Amidst the long song of Juri and the strains of the violin, after quite some time, Apu’s father calls him—“Feeling sleepy… want to go home, khoka (little one)?... Sleepy.” Oh no! Disaster! No, he will not go home. Calling from outside, his father says—“Here, take these two paisa, son, buy yourself something to eat, I’m going home.” Apu feels he would like to buy a paan (betel leaf) with one paisa, but as he approaches the paan shop, he sees a great crowd gathered—what a strange sight! General Bichitraketu, still armed, is buying a bird’s eye chili—surrounded by the throng of the Rath Yatra (chariot festival). Wonders upon wonders!... Prince Ajay appears from somewhere and, placing his hand on Bichitraketu’s elbow, says—“Won’t you buy me a paan, Kishorida (elder brother Kishori)?” The general’s loyalty to the prince is nowhere to be seen—shaking off his hand, he says—“Bah! I don’t have that much money—remember when you both used up the whole bar of soap, what did you tell me then?” The prince insists again—“Buy me a paan, Kishorida? Have I never given you anything?” Bichitraketu shakes off his hand and walks away.

--- Page 130 ---
He must be Apu’s age. Rosy-cheeked, pleasant to look at, with a singing voice as sweet as a koel’s call. Apu gazes at him, utterly enchanted—an ache of longing stirs within, a wish to speak, to know him. Suddenly, drawn by some invisible thread, Apu gathers his courage and steps forward—blushing a little, he asks, “Will you have some paan?” (betel leaf, a traditional treat)

Ajay looks a bit surprised. “You’ll give me some? Go on then, bring it.”  
Just like that, a bond forms between the two.  
No, to call it just a bond would be wrong.  
Apu is spellbound, overwhelmed!  
This is the one he has yearned for in secret all these days—this prince Ajay.  
Through a hundred fairy tales told by his mother, through the dreamy haze of a hundred childhood fantasies, his heart has always sought this—these eyes, this face, this voice!  
Exactly what he has always wished for.

Ajay asks, “Where’s your house, bhai (brother)? Someone’s given me a place to eat, but they serve food so late. Who eats at your house?”

Apu’s whole body tingles with happiness. He replies, “Bhai, someone comes to eat at our house, but today I saw him playing the dholak (drum). From tomorrow, you come—I’ll fetch you myself. If the dholak-player wants, he can eat at the other house where you used to go—”

After wandering here and there for a while, Ajay says, “I’ll go now, bhai. I have a song in the last scene—how did you like my part?”

Late at night, when the jatra (folk theater) is over, Apu returns home.  
As he walks, every conversation feels like a scene from the jatra.  
At home, his didi (elder sister) asks, “O Apu, how was the jatra?”  
To Apu, it feels as if, deep in a silent forest, Princess Indulekha herself has spoken.  
He is caught in a strange, wondrous trance.  
With great delight, he announces, “From tomorrow, Ajay—the one who dressed up as the prince, Ma—he’ll come to eat at our house—”

His mother asks, “Both of them will eat? Where will I find food for two?”

Apu replies, “No, no, one will leave—only Ajay will eat.”

Durga says, “How was the jatra, Apu? I’ve never seen anything like it—how did he sing when the princess died?”  
At night, Apu feels as if the air around him is filled with the music of violins.  
He wakes late in the morning—he’d slept only at the end of the night, and even then, not with the deep satisfaction of true rest. The sun’s sharp rays prick his eyes like needles. Tears sting when he tries to wash them.  
Yet in his ears, the harmony of violin, dhol, and mandira (small cymbals) still seems to play—  
it’s as if he’s still sitting in the midst of the jatra’s magic.

--- Page 131 ---
As the girls of the neighborhood walked along the path by the ghat, talking amongst themselves, Apu felt as if one was Dhiravati, another the queen of Kalinga, yet another Basumati, mother of the prince Ajay. In every word his didi spoke, in the gestures of her hands and feet, it was as if the princess Indulekha herself was embodied! True, yesterday when Indulekha was dressed up, it suited her not badly at all, but in his mind, the image of Princess Indulekha he had built—he had shaped it after his own didi: that same complexion, those large, luminous eyes, that beautiful face, that lovely hair!

Indulekha, with all her compassion and sweetness, seemed to have returned from some bygone age, from some distant land, now reborn as his didi—yesterday, in every inflection of Indulekha’s words, in every step, it was as if didi herself was shining through. When, deep in the forest, she had wrapped her little brother in a hundredfold affection, and, going to gather fruit to feed him, had lost her way in a lonely wood—that one day’s incident with the wild makal fruit kept returning to Apu’s mind again and again.

At noon, Apu went to call Ajay for lunch. His mother sat the two boys down to eat together and began to ask Ajay about himself. He was a Brahmin’s son, had no one left, only a mashi (maternal aunt) who had raised him, and she too had died. For about a year now, he had been working with a traveling theater troupe. Sarbajaya felt a deep tenderness for the boy—again and again she urged him to eat. There wasn’t much to offer, but the boy ate with such delight. Afterwards, Durga whispered quietly to her mother—Ma, ask him to sing that song from yesterday—the one that goes, “Where have you gone, my beloved, leaving me alone in this forest wilderness?”

Ajay sang out in a clear voice—the song enchanted Apu—Sarbajaya’s eyelids grew moist. Ah, such a boy has no mother! Then he sang more songs. Sarbajaya said, “In the afternoon I’ll fry some muri (puffed rice), be sure to come and have some—don’t be shy, all right? Come whenever you like, think of this as your own home, understand?”

--- Page 132 ---
Apu took him along for a walk by the riverbank. There, Ajay said, “Bhai, your voice is so sweet—won’t you sing a song?”… Apu felt a strong urge to impress him with his singing. But he was terribly nervous—this boy was from a jatra (folk theater) troupe—how could he sing in front of him? 

Under the great shimul tree by the river, a little distance from the footpath, hidden behind a bamboo grove, the two of them sat together. With great effort, overcoming his shyness, Apu sang a song—“Shreecharane bhar ekbar ga tolo he ananta”—a panchali (narrative song) of Dashu Ray, which he had written down after hearing it from his father’s lips.

Ajay was astonished. He said, “Such a voice, bhai? Why don’t you sing more?… Sing another one.” 

Encouraged, Apu started another—“Kheyar ashe bose re mon, dublo bela kheyar dhare.” His didi (elder sister) had learned it somewhere and used to sing it; Apu, enchanted by the melody, had learned it from her. Whenever no one was home, the two of them would sometimes sing it together.

When the song ended, Ajay burst out in praise. He said, “With a voice like yours, any troupe would offer you fifteen rupees a month, I’m telling you—just imagine if you trained a little!”

Whenever no one was home, Apu would sing in front of didi and ask her, “Well, didi, do I have a good voice? Can I sing?” Didi had always reassured him. But however promising her encouragement was, to hear such praise today from the lips of a true musician, a famous medal-winning singer from a jatra troupe—Apu was so overjoyed, he didn’t know what to say in reply.

He said, “Will you teach me that song of yours?”

Then the two of them sang that song together, their voices blending.

A long time passed. The boat moved along the river with a soft slap-slap, and down by the water’s edge, someone was searching for something. Ajay said, “What’s he looking for, bhai?”

Apu replied, “He’s looking for tadpoles, to fish with a rod”—then added, “Tell me, bhai, why don’t you stay here with us? Don’t go anywhere, will you stay?”

Such eyes, such sweetness in his voice! And to Apu, he was that very prince Ajay! As if, wandering through some forest, he had suddenly met and befriended the beautiful, wandering son of a king—a friend for life! How could he ever let him go?

--- Page 133 ---
Ajay, too, poured out many secrets of his heart. He had never found such a companion before. He had saved up nearly forty taka. When he grew a little older, he would leave this troupe. The Adhikari beat them too much. He would join Ashutosh Pal’s troupe—there, life was sweet, every night there were luchis (fried bread). If you didn’t eat, they’d give you three annas as food allowance. When he left this troupe, he’d come again to Apu’s house and stay for a few days then.

A little before evening, Ajay said, “Come, brother, there’ll be another gathering soon today, let’s go back early. If they perform ‘Parashuramer Darp-Sanghar’ (the Humbling of Parashuram’s Pride), then I’ll play the role of Fate—just wait, there’s a song for it—”

Three more days of jatra (folk theater) passed. In the whole village, there was no talk except of the jatra. On the roads, at the ghats, in the fields, the village boatmen, as they rowed their boats, and the cowherds, as they grazed their cattle, sang the newly learned songs from the jatra’s plays. The village women called the boys from the troupe to their homes and requested to hear whichever songs they liked best, listening with rapt attention. Apu learned three or four new songs himself.

One day, he went with Ajay to the troupe’s quarters, and there, everyone surrounded him—they insisted he must sing a song. They had all heard from Ajay that he could sing very well. After much coaxing, Apu, eager to prove his skill, sang a song. Everyone then took him to the Adhikari. There too, he had to sing. The Adhikari, a dark-skinned, pot-bellied man, played the jester in the performances. After listening, he said, “Come, khoka (little boy), will you join the troupe?” Apu’s heart swelled with joy and pride, tenfold. Everyone joined in—“Come, let’s take you into our troupe!” Apu wanted nothing more than to go right then. To work in the jatra troupe—was this not the highest purpose of human life? How had he not known this all these days? That was the real wonder.

He whispered secretly to Ajay, “Tell me, brother, if I join now, will they let me play a role?”

Ajay replied, “For now, it’ll be these sakhis and little boys’ parts and such, but once you learn well—”

--- Page 134 ---
Apu, my friend, does not wish to dress up—she would rather don a crown of zari embroidery and play the part of a general, sword at her side, ready for battle. When she grows up, she will certainly join a jatra (folk theater) troupe; that is the unwavering star guiding her life. 

Ajay, in a conspiratorial whisper, pointed out a boy with the color of touchstone and said, “See him? His name is Bishtu Teli. He never gets along with me. I buy matches with my own money and keep them under my pillow at night, but he takes them away to light his cheroot and never returns them. I tell him I’m afraid at night, ask for the matches back. The darkness makes my heart tremble, so the other day when I asked, he slapped me so hard! But because he’s a good dancer, Adhikari-dada treats him with such favor, no one dares say a word—”

Five days later, when the jatra troupe’s performances ended, they prepared to leave. Ajay, who came and went as freely as a son of the house, had, in these few days, become like another brother to Apu. He was a boy of Apu’s own age, and hearing that he had no family, Sarbajaya had cared for him these days as she did for Apu. Durga too had looked upon him as a brother—she had learned songs from him, told him countless stories, shared tales of her pishima (paternal aunt). The three of them had drawn a big house in the courtyard and played Ganga-Yamuna (a traditional game) together, and at mealtimes, they had forced him to eat more than he wanted. 

He lived with the jatra troupe—who knew where he slept, what he ate, or who looked after him? Ah, perhaps from birth he had never known the gentle touch of family affection, and now, having tasted it unexpectedly, he clung to it with the greed of one who had never had enough.

When the time came to leave, he suddenly opened his bundle and, with some embarrassment, tried to press five hard-earned rupees into Sarbajaya’s hand. In a shy voice he said, “With these five rupees, please buy a nice cloth for didi’s wedding—”

Sarbajaya replied, “No, child, no—your words are enough, you don’t need to give money. You need it more now—you must marry and make a home someday—”

Still, he would not let go. Only after much persuasion did he finally relent.

Then everyone walked with him a little way down the path in front of the house to see him off. As he left, he kept repeating that they must, without fail, send him a letter when didi’s wedding was arranged.

In the dappled shade of the gabtala tree, his delicate boyish figure disappeared behind the wild vetch bushes. Suddenly, Sarbajaya’s heart was filled with a pang—such a child, ah, out in the world at this age, earning his own living. If only my Apu had turned out like that—oh, Ma!

--- Page 135 ---
**Pather Panchali  
Chapter Twenty-Four**

When Harihar first returned from Kashi, everyone said his future was bright indeed—no one in this region had ever come back with such learning. His scholarly reputation was on everyone’s lips; all said that this time, surely, he would accomplish something. Sarbajaya too believed that soon, they would summon her husband and offer him a good job (though her notion of who bestowed such jobs was as vague and misty as the surface of a fog-shrouded sea). But months slipped by, then years, a long stretch of time passed, and no horseman in brocade, bearing an appointment letter for the post of chief pandit, came galloping through the midnight hours; nor did any genie from an Arabian tale fly in, bearing a jewel-encrusted magical palace to set down in their crumbling home.
