Instead, the worm-eaten doors of their house grew ever more decrepit, the rafters sagged lower, and what little they once had now dwindled away. Yet, she did not abandon hope entirely. Each time Harihar returned from his travels, he spoke of some new prospect with such conviction—as if all was settled, there was only a slight delay, and fortune was just about to turn. But did it ever?

Life is so sweet, perhaps, because so much of its sweetness is woven from dreams and imagination. Let the dreams be false, let the fantasies have not a trace of reality—let them lack all practical fulfillment—still, they are life’s greatest treasures. Let them come, let their place in life be eternal; petty fulfillment, petty gain—these are trivial beside them.

Harihar had been away from home for nearly two or three months now. He hadn’t sent money or provisions in a long time. Durga’s illness had worsened—she would eat, fall ill, feel a little better for a day or two, then suddenly relapse.

Sarbajaya often pressed her husband about their daughter’s marriage. She had made him write two or three letters to Nirendra’s father, Rajyeshwar Babu. She still clung to hope in that direction. Harihar would say, “Are you mad? That’s the way of the rich—do you think Rajyeshwar Kaka will even consider us?” Still, Sarbajaya would not give up. “Write just one more letter—what’s the harm? Didn’t Niren himself say he liked her?” A month or two would pass, no particular reply would come, and again she would urge her husband to write.

This time, when Harihar left for distant lands, he promised that he would return only after arranging for them to move elsewhere—he would not come back without making some definite plan.

--- Page 136 ---
On one side of the neighborhood, a few small, neatly swept thatched huts stand. In the cowshed, plump, milk-giving cows are tethered; the loft is full of straw, the granary brimming with rice. The fresh, green scent of the pea fields at the edge of the meadow drifts in on the open breeze, flowing across the courtyard. Birds call out—nilkantha (Indian roller), babui (weaverbird), shyama (magpie-robin). In the morning, Apu rises and, from a large earthen pot, pours out a vessel of freshly frothed, dark cow’s milk, eating it with hot, puffed rice before sitting down to study. Durga does not suffer from malaria. Everyone knows her, everyone treats her with affection, coming to touch her feet in greeting. No one looks down on them for being poor; no one scorns or belittles them.

...She only dreams, day or night, Sarbajaya only dreams. She feels, after all these years, that something real will finally come to pass. Someone inside her heart whispers.

Why did it not happen all this time? Why only after so many years? Since those childhood days, wandering beneath the jamun and drumstick trees, this longing has been entwined in her heart with the ritual patterns drawn by Senjuti’s hand, with the hope that one day, in a courtyard marked by Lakshmi’s red, alta-painted footprints, she would set up her own household in her in-laws’ home. Who had ever wished for such a crumbling, old house by the bamboo grove?

Durga, having somehow procured a small taro root, sits knocking at the kitchen door. Her mother says, “What’s wrong with you, Dugga? Do you want rice today? You had a fever just last evening, didn’t you?”

Durga replies, “Let it be, Ma, that fever—wasn’t it just a little chill?... If you could just cook this taro with the rice and give me a little—”

Her mother says, “Oh, your appetite only grows when you’re sick. If you’re well today and tomorrow, I’ll give it to you the day after—”

After much pleading and coaxing, Durga finally gives up and sets the taro aside. She sits quietly for a while, thinking to herself, Today I feel so well, today the fever won’t come—at lunch I’ll have two rotis and some fried potatoes. After a while, she yawns; she knows this is the first sign of fever. Still, she tries to convince herself: So what if I yawn, people yawn all the time, the fever won’t come back. Gradually, she feels cold, wants to go sit in the sun. But instead of going, she reassures herself that feeling cold is just a normal thing, what does it have to do with fever?

But no reassurance works. Before the sun can reach her, the fever comes; she sneaks out to sit in the sunlight, lest Ma notices. Her heart aches, she thinks—It’s only because I’m thinking about fever that I feel this way, it isn’t really fever—

--- Page 137 ---
The crimson sunlight falls upon the moss-covered, crumbling wall. Evening shadows deepen. Durga feels that if she lets her mind drift, the fever will slip away. She says to Apu, “Bose diki ekṭu āmār kāchhe, āy golpo kori”—Sit with me a while, let’s talk.

One day, in another year, on a night thick with monsoon rain, she and Apu, plotting together, crept out in the last hours before dawn to the garden behind Sej Thakrun’s house to gather palm fruits. Suddenly, a thorn pierced Durga’s foot with a sharp prick. She recoiled in pain, and where she set her left foot, another thorn struck—again, a sharp sting. In the morning, it was discovered that Satu, fearing someone might steal the palm fruits in the night, had planted rows of bel-kanta—thorny branches—straight along the path to the palm grove.

And another day—what a wondrous thing happened!

From somewhere, an old Bengali Muslim man arrived, carrying a large, brightly painted tin box inlaid with colored glass, to show his magic. He was performing in the courtyard of Jibon Chowdhury’s house across the way. Durga stood nearby. She had no coins. The others, one by one, paid a paisa each and peered through a horn-shaped tube into the box, marveling at what they saw.

The old Muslim man played his box, chanting in a sing-song voice, “Taj Bibika roza dekho, hathi baghka loraai dekho!”—See the Taj Queen’s tomb, see the battle of elephant and tiger! As each child finished and drew their eyes from the tube, Durga, brimming with excitement, would ask, “Ki dekhli re or modhye? Shob shotyikarer?”—What did you see inside? Was it all real?

Ah! What marvelous sights they had seen—they could not even describe them… Such wonders.

One by one, everyone had their turn. Durga was about to leave when the old Muslim man called out, “Dekhbe na khuki?…”—Won’t you look, little girl?

Durga shook her head, “Na—amar kachhe poisha nai”—No, I have no money.

The man said, “Esho esho khuki, dekhe jao—poisha lagbe na”—Come, little girl, have a look—you don’t need to pay.

Durga felt a little shy, and said, “Na—” But inside, her heart beat fast with curiosity and longing.

The man said, “Esho esho, dosh ki?… Esho, dyaakho—” Come, come, what’s the harm? Come, see.

Durga, her face alight, stepped up to the box, but still could not summon the courage to press her face to the tube. The man said, “Ei noltār modhye diya takao diki khuki?”—Look through this tube, little girl.

Durga brushed the wild strands of hair from beside her ear and peered in. Of the next ten minutes, she could give no account. How could real people be seen in pictures? So many sahibs, mems, houses, wars—she could not say what she saw! What a thing it was she had witnessed!

--- Page 138 ---
I so long to show Apu, how many times Durga has searched for him, but he never comes to play anymore.

Before the story can even finish properly, Durga, struck by a wave of fever, can no longer sit; she gets up and lies down in the room, wrapping herself in a quilt.

These days, Baba is not at home, and it’s nearly impossible to find Apu. His books in the satchel are almost being eaten away by worms. In the morning, he leaves with a little pouch of cowries, and doesn’t return until well past noon, just in time for lunch. His mother scolds him—"To hell with this boy—have you given up on your studies entirely? When your father comes home, I’ll tell him everything, just you wait—"

Apu, frightened, sits down with his satchel. The books are scattered all around. He says to his mother, “Ma, give me a little catechu (khoyer), I want to mix it in the inkpot—”

Then he sits and writes, and puts the pages out in the sun to dry. When the catechu-soaked ink dries, it glistens—Apu gazes at it with great delight—he thinks—Tomorrow I’ll add a little more catechu—Oh! Just look how it sparkles! He sneaks a big chunk of catechu from the betel box, hiding it from Ma, and drops it into the inkpot. After writing, he leaves it to dry, watching with curiosity to see how much it will shine today. He wonders—What if I add a little more?

One day, he gets caught by his mother. She says, “You care nothing for your writing. All you want is lumps and lumps of catechu every day—put that catechu back—”

Caught, he replies a little sheepishly, “If there’s no catechu, how can there be ink?...I mean, can it work just like that—”

“No, why would there be ink without catechu? These are the sons of kings, not studying at all—there must be a shop somewhere that supplies them with pounds and pounds of catechu every day! Off with you—”

--- Page 139 ---
Apu sits and sits, writing a play in a notebook. He has written so much that the notebook is nearly filled—through the minister’s betrayal, the king abandons his kingdom and flees to the forest, the prince Nilambar and princess Amba fall into the hands of bandits in the woods, a fierce battle ensues, and later, the princess’s lifeless body is found on the riverbank. In the play, a complex character named Satu is created, and soon after, despite no grave crime being described, he is sentenced to death.

Near the end of the play, the princess Amba is revived by Narada’s boon, or she is married to the loyal general Jibanketu—such events lead some to say that, except for a few names, nothing in this is truly different from the jatra (folk theater) performance seen last Baishakh (the first month of the Bengali calendar), or that it is taken wholesale from there. But they forget that if, on some silent, moonlit night of the past, in the dim lamp-lit solitude of a chamber, the dream of a distant peacock-haunted forest—visible like an ancient poet’s blue cloud—could inspire Kalidasa to describe the journey of the free-floating clouds, then what of it?... That forgotten hymn to a blessed night—humankind has, unknowingly, been singing it for a thousand years.

It is only with fire that fire can be kindled. Who, pressing a torch into a mound of ashes, lights a flame?

--- Page 140 ---
There is a book in the office—a book called Charitmala, written by Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar. An old book, collected by his father, who has a habit of gathering books from various places for his son; from somewhere or other, he had brought this one home, and sometimes Apu reads a bit of it. In that book, the stories are of people whom he wishes to become like! When sent to the market to sell potatoes, the farmer’s son Roscoe would sit by the fence and practice algebra, working out sums with a blunt piece of charcoal on a strip of leather for lack of paper; the shepherd Dubal, letting his flock of sheep wander as they pleased, would sit absorbed beneath a tree, engrossed in the study of geography—he wants to be like that. …What is ‘algebra’? He wants to study algebra like Roscoe. He does not want to write this kind of handwriting, nor does he care for arithmetic or Shubhonkari (a traditional Bengali math primer)—none of that appeals to him. He wants to sit just like that, in the solitude beneath a tree, in the shade of the forest, or by the fence, spreading out a “geography” (what is that thing?) and reading, reading great big books, becoming a scholar like them. But where will he find such things? Where is this ‘geography’, where is this ‘algebra’, where even is Latin grammar?—Here, there is only the Arya for counting coins, and the third multiplication table.

What use is it if Ma scolds him, when what he wants to read is nowhere to be found here?

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Five**

For days now, the rains have been pouring. In the evenings, a gathering sits in Annada Ray’s Chandimandap (a covered pavilion for worship). The other day, the talk began with tall tales of the indigo factory, and moved on to stories of some temple in Puri, on whose spire sits a five-maund (about 200 kg) heavy lodestone, whose magnetic pull is so strong that ships passing nearby are often drawn off course and wrecked upon the rocks near the shore—stories as fantastical as those from the Arabian Nights, a stream of wild and wondrous tales. None of the listeners wished to get up; no one’s heart was ready to leave such marvelous stories and go home. From geography, the flow of tales soon arrived at astrology. Dinu Chowdhury was saying—There’s no book like the Bhrigu-Samhita! You just go, give your birth sign, your father’s name, your lineage, and it will tell you everything—past and future—just check for yourself—everything about planets and the zodiac, whatever you can imagine—it’s all written there, even your past lives—

--- Page 141 ---
Everyone was listening intently, but suddenly Rammo sh glanced outside and said—“No, let’s get up, we can’t stay after this—don’t you see—don’t you see what’s happening? Unless there’s a big jolt or shake, we won’t survive, things are looking bad, come on, everyone—”

There was no break in the rain. It would pause for a moment, then return with renewed force, the spray turning everything around into a misty haze.

Harihar had sent a total of five rupees, and since then, there had been neither letter nor money. That too was a long time ago—every morning, Sarbajaya would wake up thinking, today surely the remittance will arrive. She would tell her son, “You’re always running about and playing, but can’t you keep an eye out? Sit by the mailbox—when the postman comes, ask him right away—”

Apu would say—“Oh, as if I don’t sit there! Yesterday too, Puti’s letter came, and he brought our newspaper—go and ask Puti if you don’t believe me! Then how did our newspaper come yesterday? Wasn’t I there?”

The monsoon had truly descended. At his mother’s bidding, Apu would sit steadfastly in the Ray family’s Chandimandap, waiting for the postman. From the shed of Sadhu the blacksmith’s house, a flock of wet pigeons would flap and flutter their way to the cornice of the Rays’ western room, glancing about as they went. He was terribly afraid of the thunder in the sky. When lightning flashed, he would think to himself—“Have you seen how the gods are playing with their firesticks? Now they’ll surely call out—” Then he would squeeze his eyes shut and plug his ears with his fingers.

Returning home, he would find Ma and Didi, soaked all afternoon, gathering heaps of taro greens and piling them up on the kitchen veranda.

Apu would say—“Where did you get these, Ma? Oh, so many!”

Durga would laugh and say—“So many—! Hmph! You have it easy, just sitting around!... We got them from the jam tree by their pond—knee-deep water, this much! Want to come?”

In the morning, at the ghat, Sarbajaya would meet the barber’s wife. From within her sari, she would take out a brass tray and say, “Look at this piece, it’s very good—not for daily use, nothing like that, pure brass. You mentioned it, so I thought I’d bring it—this isn’t just any old thing, it was a wedding gift—nowadays you can’t find things like this—”

After much haggling, the barber’s wife would untie a half-rupee coin from her anchal and, hiding the tray in her sari, take it away. Sarbajaya would ask her again and again not to tell anyone.

--- Page 142 ---
Here is your requested translation, following your instructions for style and cultural terms:

---

In just a day or two, the monsoon thickened and descended. Hoo-hoo, the wind from the east, every hollow and pond brimming and swelling—on roads and at the ghats, water up to the knees, day and night the soughing sound, storms raging through the bamboo groves—the bamboo tops bowing, sweeping the ground—nowhere in the sky is there a gap—sometimes it grows even darker than before—black, black masses of cloud, rushing, swirling, flying from east to west—as if somewhere in the distant sky, a great war between gods and demons has broken out, and under the command of some cunning general, monstrous armies, rank after rank, division after division, under the leadership of unseen heroes and mighty charioteers, surge forward with the force of the storm—blazing, furious divine thunderbolts, hurling fire, in the blink of an eye tearing and rending the vast black hosts, scattering them this way and that—then again, from somewhere, the terrible brood of Raktabeej (a demon from mythology), in a dreadful black shadow, comes encircling earth and sky in darkness.

A mighty storm!

Day and night, the soughing sound—the river swells—how many houses, how many places have collapsed!...Rivers and streams have overflowed—cows and calves stand drenched, huddled under trees, in bamboo groves, beneath the eaves of houses, rain pouring down in torrents, and not a sound from any bird or nestling anywhere! Four or five days passed just like this—only the sound of the storm and the ceaseless downpour!—Apu, climbing up onto the veranda, hurriedly wiping his wet hair, said—“There’s water in our bamboo grove, didi, will you come see?”

Durga, wrapped in her quilt, was lying down—without getting up, she said—“How much water has come, re?”

Apu said, “When your fever is gone, you can come see tomorrow. …On the tamarind lane, water up to the knees!” Then he asked—“Where’s Ma, re?”

There isn’t a grain of rice in the house—only two handfuls of stale fried rice remain. Apu began to cry—“That won’t do, Ma, don’t you see I’m hungry? I want to eat some rice—hmm!”

His mother said, “My precious, my little jewel—how can we do that! I’ll mix up plenty of fried rice for you now—how can I cook, can’t you see what a state the stove is in? There’s a whole stoveful of water!” Then, from within her clothes, she brought something out and, smiling, showed it—“Look, see, a koi fish! I saw it crawling by the earthen embankment in the bamboo grove—since the floodwaters came, everything’s coming up from the river—the pond behind the betel garden has overflowed and joined with the river, you see? That’s why everything’s coming up—”

Durga threw off her quilt and got up—astonished. She said, “Let me see the fish, Ma? Really, Ma, do koi fish crawl about by the embankment? Are there more?”

--- Page 143 ---
Apu is about to dash out into the rain again—only with great difficulty does his mother manage to stop him.

Durga says, “Once your fever’s gone, tomorrow morning let’s go, Apu, you and I—we’ll bring fish from the bamboo grove now.” Then she wonders in surprise—Fish in the bamboo grove! How did they get there? Amazing!—Did Ma even look properly? If she had, there would be more there—Didn’t I see how the koi fish walk on their ears—Tomorrow morning I’ll see—By morning the fever will be gone—

All around, the woods and gardens close in as evening falls. In the dark of the thirteenth night, the clouds of dusk blur the world into one. Durga is lying down on her bedding, and beside her, her mother and Apu sit. Sarbajaya thinks—What if, right now, a letter came from Niren Babaji? Who knows, couldn’t it happen? Niren did like her after all—Who knows what fate decided! No, could such luck ever be mine? You’re just the same! If that were so, would there be any more worries?

Meanwhile, brother and sister are deep in a fierce argument. Apu shifts closer to his mother—The chill wind makes him shiver. He laughs and says, “Ma—what? That—does Shyamlanka grind spices, her hair trailing in the dust?”

Durga says, “By then, my mother has left the country—”

Apu says, “Oh, come on—Ma, is that so? By then, my mother has left the country?”—and as he says it, he laughs at his sister’s foolishness.

The laughter of her son’s innocent delight pierces Sarbajaya’s heart like an arrow. She thinks to herself—Not seven, not five—just this one boy—What ill fate brought me here—I can’t even grant his wishes—not ghee, not luchi, not sandesh—nothing, just a little rice—nothing more!... Then she thinks—This broken house, this life of want—If Apu grows up, this sorrow will be gone—May God make him a man...

Then, sitting there, she tells stories—how, when she first came to live in Nishchindipur, one year during such relentless rains, the river swelled so much that even the big, loaded boats came right up to the path by the Mukherjee’s garden.

Apu asks, “How big were the boats, Ma?”

“Enormous—the Khottas’ lime boats, the boats that bring clay for pottery, you’ve seen them sometimes, haven’t you?—that big—”

Suddenly Durga asks, “Ma, do you know how to braid hair in four strands?”

Late at night, Sarbajaya wakes—Apu is calling, “Ma, oh Ma, wake up—water’s falling on me—”

--- Page 144 ---
Sarbojaya rises and lights the lamp—the terrible sound of rain thunders outside. Water drips everywhere through the leaky roof. She moves the bedding aside and lays it down again. Durga lies there, burning with a relentless fever—her mother touches her, feels the quilt on her body, soaked and heavy with damp. Calling out, she says—“Durga—oh Durga, can you hear me?... Get up a little, will you? Move the bedding—oh Durga—quickly, everything’s getting completely soaked…”

Even when the children fall asleep, Sarbojaya cannot. The night is dark—this dense monsoon… her heart flutters with unease—a fear that something will happen… something will happen. A strange feeling stirs in her chest. She wonders—what happened to that man? Not a single word has come—let the money go, never mind. Has it ever been like this before?... Is he well? Ma Siddheshwari, I’ll offer you a five-anna offering, just bring good news, Ma—

The next morning, the rain eases a little. Sarbojaya steps outside and sees the small pond in the bamboo grove has filled to the brim. Along the path by the ghat, Nibaran’s mother is walking somewhere, drenched. Sarbojaya calls out—“O Nibaran’s ma, listen—” Then, a little shyly, she says—“Didn’t you once mention, that Bindabuni shawl for your son—do you want it?...”

Nibaran’s mother says—“You have it? Let me go and get my son, I’ll come right now—is it new, Ma-thakrun, or old?...”

Sarbojaya replies, “Why don’t you come over—you’ll see for yourself?... It’s a little old, but no one’s worn it—it’s freshly washed—” Then, pausing a moment, she asks—“You all don’t husk rice these days?...”

Nibaran’s mother says—“How can paddy dry in this rain, Ma-thakrun… I keep just a couple of handfuls aside for food—”

Sarbojaya says—“Why don’t you do this—bring me half a measure or so today?...” She moves a little closer, her tone pleading—“Because of the rain, I can’t find anyone to fetch rice from the market—I’m going around with money, but no one will agree—really in a fix, Ma—”

Nibaran’s mother relents, saying—“I’ll bring it now, but will you be able to eat rice from this year’s paddy, Ma-thakrun?... It’s very coarse—”

Durga, weakened by fever, can no longer eat the boiled neem leaves. Her illness lingers on unchanged. There is no medicine, no nourishing food, no doctor, no vaidya. She says—“Ma, will you get me a one-paisa biscuit, the salty kind, it tastes nice in the mouth.”

Even sago is hard to come by, let alone her biscuit.

--- Page 145 ---
From evening, once again, a terrible rain began to fall. The storm seemed to come with even greater force—an incessantly rain-soaked solitude, water everywhere, the wind howling from the east, a Bhadrā twilight swallowed in darkness and cloud! Once more, those black, cotton-wool clouds drifted across the sky… The sound of the rain was so loud one could not hear a thing—through doors and windows, with the slap of cold wind, the spray of rain came rushing in—what chance did a tattered sack, a patched cloth, or the broken shutters have to stand against the monstrous assault of the storm?

Late at night, when everyone was asleep, the rain grew heavier. Sarbajaya could not sleep—she sat up in bed. Outside, only the ceaseless hiss of water; the storm’s gusts battered the house with a continuous, growling roar, like an enraged demon! The frail old house seemed to shiver and tremble with each blast… Fear made her heart flutter and flee… Helpless, with her little children, on the edge of the village, amidst the bamboo grove! In her heart she prayed—Thakur, if I die, it does not matter—but what will become of them?—Where could I go in this night? Sitting there, she thought—Well, if the house collapses, perhaps the wall of the main building will fall first—at the very sound, I’ll pull them out through the door of the thatched kitchen—

She felt she could not sit still any longer—these past days, she had been boiling wild greens and taro leaves to get by—starving herself, giving whatever little food there was to the children—her body weak from worry and hunger, her head spinning.

The storm’s growl, the wind’s howl, grew fiercer as the night wore on. What a jolt came from outside! What could she do! At one great gust, frightened, she carefully opened the door of the main house to gauge the storm’s mood, and leaned out onto the veranda… The spray of rain soaked her clothes and hair—over the unbroken roar of the wind, the sound of the rain and storm was lost—nothing could be seen outside—darkness, clouds, sky, air, trees, all blurred into one! In the tumult of storm and rain, nothing else could be heard.

--- Page 146 ---
This fierce darkness, this cruel, tempestuous night—its very soul seems to be rushing forth as the messenger of the god of destruction, Bhim Bhairav, to devour creation with a terrible, relentless force. In the darkness, in the night, through the trees, the sky, the earth, its velocity is met with resistance, raising a sound—su-ish, su-u-u-u-ish… su u u u i sh… In the first part of that sound, it is as if the world-devouring messenger is drawing back, gathering its strength—su u u—and in the final part, with all the tumult and churning of the earth’s every layer of air, it whips up a colossal storm, and with the full force of its demonic power, it hurls itself against the crumbling walls of Sarbojaya’s old hut—i i sh…! The hut shudders and sways… it cannot hold much longer! Yet within all this, there is no restlessness, no chaos, no confusion or error—as if this is the resolute, practiced, methodical duty of a being bound to its purpose! The one who has taken on the task of shattering the world and scattering it to the winds within a set time, who, age after age, has destroyed so many smiling creations and strewn them like fireworks into the endless darkness of the sky—that mighty, all-powerful messenger of destruction—this is its accustomed work… Frenzy or impatience would not suit it.

In terror, Sarbojaya bolts the door… But what if something enters the house now? Is it a person, or some other creature? All around, dense bamboo groves, jungle, not a soul in sight—Oh Ma! The spray of water threatens to float the house away… She reaches out and feels that sleeping Apu’s body is soaked through, limp and sodden… What is she to do? How many hours of night remain? She gropes around the bed, searching for matches, lights the kerosene lamp. She calls—O Apu, get up, won’t you? Water is dripping— Apu mumbles something in his sleep, the words unclear. She calls again—Apu? Can you hear me, O Apu? Get up, please! She tells Durga—Turn over and sleep on your other side, Dugga. The water is falling so badly—move a little, turn over, please—

Apu sits up, blinking sleepily, looks around—then lies down again. Suddenly, there’s a loud, jarring noise, and Sarbojaya hurriedly opens the door again, peering outside—the bamboo grove looks strangely empty—has the kitchen wall collapsed? Her heart leaps in her chest—now, perhaps, the old hut itself—? Who is there, whom can she call now? In her heart she prays—O Thakur, let this night pass somehow, O Thakur, look upon their faces—

--- Page 147 ---
It was not yet fully dawn. The storm had passed, but rain was still falling, softly, gently. Nilmoni Mukherjee’s wife was coming to the cowshed to check on the cattle when, hearing repeated knocks at the side door, she opened it and exclaimed in astonishment—“Nutan bou!…” (the new bride)

Sarbajaya spoke hurriedly, “N’di, could you call Bototakur (the village priest) for me? Please, just once, ask him to come to our house quickly—see how Dugga’s doing!”

Nilmoni Mukherjee’s wife, startled, asked, “Dugga? What’s happened to Dugga?”

Sarbajaya replied, “She’s had a fever for a few days now—it comes and goes—malaria, I think. Since yesterday evening, the fever’s been so high—and then, you know what happened last night—such strange goings-on—please, just once, quickly, Bototakur—”

Seeing her disheveled hair and the wild, red, sleepless eyes darting about in confusion, Nilmoni Mukherjee’s wife said, “Don’t be afraid, bou—wait, I’ll call him right away—come, I’ll go too—last night, the cowshed roof nearly collapsed—oh, I’ve never seen anything like what happened last night—at the end of the night, everyone got up, moved the cows out, and then lay down again, can you imagine? Wait, I’ll call him—”

A little later, Nilmoni Mukherjee, his eldest son Phoni, his wife, and their two daughters all came to Apu’s house. In the darkness of night, it was as if that giant—some monstrous force—had trampled, crushed, and churned the whole village before vanishing into the sky. Broken branches, leaves, thatch from roofs, tender bamboo leaves, bamboo splinters blocked the paths—storm-bent bamboos barred the way. Phoni said, “Did you see, Baba, what happened? The wind has carried leaves all the way from the English totka tree by the paved road in Nawabganj!” Nilmoni Mukherjee’s younger son dragged a dead sparrow out from among the bamboo leaves.

Apu was sitting by Durga’s bed. Nilmoni Mukherjee entered the room and asked, “What’s happened, Baba Apu?”

Worry was written on Apu’s face. He replied, “Didi was muttering all sorts of things, Jethamoshai (uncle).”

--- Page 148 ---
Nilmoni sat down beside the bed and said—“Let me see your hand?... The fever’s a bit high, but don’t worry—Phoni, could you quickly run to Nababganj and fetch Dr. Sharat? Bring him back here right away.”  
Then he called out—“Durga, oh Durga?”  
Durga, lost in a feverish stupor, made no sound or movement.  
Nilmoni said, “Ah, just look at the state of the house! Last night’s rain flooded everything... And really, what’s there for Bouma [daughter-in-law] to be embarrassed about—couldn’t she have just come over to our place? And that Harit, he’ll never have any sense in this life—leaving things in such a mess and not even making arrangements to fix up the house, who knows what he’s doing—he’s always been the same, all his life—”

His wife said, “Fix the house? There’s not even food in the house, who would leave their family in such trouble and go off to a foreign land? Ah, that frail girl was soaked all night—let’s heat some water—Phoni, open that window, will you?”

A little later, Dr. Sharat arrived from Nababganj—he examined her and arranged for medicine. He said there was no particular cause for alarm, though the fever was high, and instructed them to keep a cold compress on her forehead regularly. No one knew where Harihar was—but still, a letter was sent to his last known address.

The next day, the storm and rain stopped—the clouds began to clear from the sky. Nilmoni Mukherjee came twice a day to check on them. But from the day after the storm, Durga’s fever worsened. Dr. Sharat was at a loss. Another letter was sent to Harihar.

Apu sat by his didi’s [elder sister’s] head, placing a cool cloth on her forehead. He called her a couple of times—“O didi, can you hear me? How are you, didi?”  
Durga seemed lost in a daze. Her lips moved—as if she was muttering something to herself, in a feverish haze. Apu leaned close to her ear and tried a few times, but couldn’t make out what she was saying.

By evening, the fever subsided. After so long, Durga could open her eyes and look around. She was terribly weak, her voice a faint whisper—one had to listen closely to understand her words.

When Ma went off to do chores, Apu stayed sitting by his didi. Durga lifted her eyes to him and asked—“What time is it?”

Apu replied—“It’s still early—have you seen, didi, the sun’s out today? There’s still sunlight on the top of our coconut tree—”

--- Page 149 ---
For a while, neither of them spoke a word. After so many days, the sun had come out, and Apu was filled with delight. He gazed out the window at the sunlit treetop, his eyes lingering there.

After a moment, Durga spoke—“Listen, Apu—listen, I have something to ask—”

“What is it, didi?” he replied, bringing his face even closer to hers.

“Will you show me the train one day?”

“I’ll show you right now—when you get better, I’ll ask Baba, and we’ll all go to bathe in the Ganga by train—”

Day and night passed by. It was as if there had never been any storm or rain. All around, the fierce sunlight of autumn blazed.

At ten in the morning, Nilmoni Mukherjee, after many days, was sitting down to oil his body before going to bathe in the river, when his wife’s excited voice reached his ears—“Ogo, come here quickly—listen, there’s a sound of crying coming from Apu’s house—”

Everyone rushed to see what had happened.

Sarbojaya was bent over her daughter’s face, calling out—“O Durga, give me some tea—oh, give me some tea, won’t you—O Durga—”

Nilmoni Mukherjee entered the room and said—“What’s happened—move aside, move aside—ah, why are you all standing here blocking the air?”

Sarbojaya, forgetting the presence of the elderly neighbor who was her brother-in-law by relation, cried out—“O God, what’s happened, why is my daughter like this?”

Durga did not look up again.

Piercing through the blue veil of the sky, sometimes comes the beckoning of the infinite—children, restless and eager, run from the bosom of the earth and lose themselves in that endless blue—far beyond the familiar and well-trodden paths, on some pathless way—at the moment of Durga’s restless, turbulent soul, the call of life’s greatest unknown had arrived!

Once more, the village doctor was called—he said—“It’s the last stage of malaria—after the high fever, there’s a kind of lull, and then suddenly heart failure—just the other day, there was a case just like this in Dashghara—”

Within half an hour, the people of the neighborhood crowded into the courtyard.

**Pather Panchali  
Chapter Twenty-Six**

Harihar had not received any letter from home.

--- Page 150 ---
Here is the translation, following your instructions:

---

This time, leaving home, Harihar Ray first went to Goari Krishnanagar. He knew no one there. It was a town—a bazaar place—and he went there lured by the hope that, somehow or other, something would work out. After staying in Goari for a few days, he discovered that in the town, at the houses of lawyers or zamindars (landlords), one could often get work reciting the Chandi (a sacred Hindu text) on a daily or monthly contract. Clinging to this hope, he passed about fifteen days, and in that time, whatever little money he had brought from home for the journey ran out, yet nowhere did any opportunity arise.

He fell into dire straits—an unfamiliar place, not a soul to offer even a paisa in help. When his money was gone, he had to leave the small hotel by the market where he had been staying. From someone, he heard that at the local Harisabha (a religious community center), a newly arrived, needy Brahmin traveler could stay and eat for free. Disclosing his plight, he managed to secure a spot in one corner of a room at the Harisabha, but there, too, troubles abounded. Many idle ganja-smoking men would gather there at night, spending almost the entire night in loud revelry. Sometimes, deep into the night, he began to notice the comings and goings of certain women whose appearance did not exactly suggest that they were respectable ladies come to visit a Hari temple.

Enduring great hardship, he began to visit the homes of the town’s prominent lawyers and wealthy householders. After wandering all day, he would often return late at night to find that some unknown, ill-bred fellow had dragged away his bedding and was snoring away in his spot. For several days, Harihar slept out on the verandah. As this happened more and more often, he had a bit of a quarrel with the ganja-smoking crowd. The next morning, who knows what they complained to the Harisabha’s secretary—but the secretary-babu summoned Harihar to his own house and said, “There is a rule at our Harisabha: no one may stay more than three days. You must find lodging elsewhere.”

After dusk, Harihar had to gather his belongings and leave the Harisabha.

He set down his small bundle at a quiet spot by the banks of the Khore river and washed his hands and face in the river’s water.

--- Page 151 ---
All day, nothing had passed his lips—  
That day, he’d sat in a wooden granary, singing songs of Shyam, his voice weaving through the dusk. The owner of the granary, moved by the melody, pressed a single rupee into his hand. From that rupee, he broke some coins and bought puffed rice and curd from the market. Yet, the food seemed to stick in his throat, refusing to go down—he had left home with barely ten days’ worth of provisions. Now nearly two months had passed, and he had not been able to send home even a single paisa. How were they managing all this time?  

When Apu had left for home, he’d said again and again—  
“Bring me a copy of the Padmapuran, Baba, don’t forget!”  
The boy loved to read above all else—sometimes, Harihar could tell, he would sneak books out from his father’s trunk and read them in secret. The clumsy way the books were put back, the disorder inside the trunk—Apu never knew exactly where his father kept which book. He would try, in vain, to rearrange them and cover his tracks. But as soon as Harihar returned and opened the trunk, he could tell what his son had been up to.

Before leaving home, Harihar had borrowed a cheap, Bat-tala* edition of the Padmapuran from Yugipara to read. Apu immediately seized the book and made it his own—reading it every day. He was especially delighted by the tale of Shiva Thakur going fishing in Kuchuni Para.  
Harihar would say, “Give the book back, baba, the owners are asking for it!”  
But Apu would only return it after extracting a promise: that his father would buy him a copy of the Padmapuran.  
He’d said it over and over before Harihar left—“Bring that book, Baba, this time you must, you must!”  

Durga, with her lofty indifference, had only asked for a length of green hawaii cloth and a strip of good alta* to take home. But all that seemed a distant dream now—the real problem was how the family was even getting by!  

After dusk, Harihar returned to the familiar wooden granary and took shelter there for the night. Sleep would not come—he tossed and turned on his makeshift bed, mind churning with worry over how to send something, anything, back home.

---

*Bat-tala: Refers to the cheap, mass-produced books from the Battala area of Kolkata, known for popular literature.
*Alta: A traditional red dye worn on the feet by Bengali women, especially during festivals.

--- Page 152 ---
In the morning, wandering here and there, he stood aimlessly at a spot on the path. Across the road stood a red-brick house with an iron gate. After gazing at it for a long while, he felt somehow that if he went to this house and shared his sorrow, perhaps a solution would be found. Like a wind-up doll, he slipped through the gate.

A neatly arranged sitting room, flower pots set in tiers on marble steps, stone figurines, palms, a doormat laid out at the entrance. An elderly gentleman was reading the newspaper in the sitting room—seeing a stranger, he set the paper aside, sat up straight, and asked, “Who are you? What do you want?”

Harhihar replied humbly, “Sir, I am a Brahmin—I have studied Sanskrit, I recite the Chandi, I can perform readings—besides that, I can recite the Bhagavat and the Gita—”

The elderly gentleman, barely listening, made it clear that his time was extremely valuable, and in a manner that left no room for idle talk, said, “No, none of that will be of use here now, try somewhere else.”

Harhihar, desperate, pleaded, “Sir, I have just arrived in this new city, I have absolutely nothing—I'm in great trouble, for days now I have only—”

The elderly man, eager to send him off, lifted a cushion from beside him, took something out, and holding it out to Harhihar, said, “Here, take this, go, nothing else can be done, take it.” Whatever kind of coin it might have been, had it been offered in a different tone, Harhihar would have had no objection to accepting it—he had taken such things in many places before—but now he said humbly, “Sir, please keep it, I do not take alms from anyone like this—I recite the scriptures—besides, from anyone—no, let it be—”

Perhaps a stroke of good fortune occurred. In the timber shed of Rakkhit Mahashay, one day, some news was found. In a village near Krishnanagar, a prosperous mahajan (merchant) was seeking a Brahmin to perform daily worship and recitations for the household deity, someone who would stay on permanently. Through Rakkhit Mahashay’s connection, Harhihar went there without delay—the master of the house took a liking to him. He was given a room to stay in, and there was no lack of care or hospitality.

After working for a few days, the festival arrived. When it was time to return home, the master of the house gave him a ten-rupee honorarium and money for travel expenses. When he went to the timber shed to take leave of Rakkhit Mahashay, there too he received a five-rupee honorarium.

--- Page 153 ---
The scent of hot sunlight lingers in the sky and air; gazing up at the blue, cloudless heavens, a sudden joy stirs in the heart. In the lush, rain-soaked green vines and leaves at the end of the monsoon, there is a kind of happiness clinging to the footsteps of the traveler. On either side of the railway tracks, the wild kash flowers bow and tumble in the rush of the train, and as the journey goes on, thoughts of home return again and again.

A group of businessmen from Shantipur had gone to Calcutta before the festival to buy bundles of cloth. Now, boarding the ferry at Churnighat, they chatter and laugh—a sense of festivity and jubilation is everywhere. In the Ranaghat bazaar, he bought cloth for his wife and children. Durga loves to wear red-bordered saris, so he carefully chose a fine one for her, and, after much scrutiny, a few leaves of alta—the red dye for her feet. Despite much searching, Apu’s ‘Padma-Puran’ could not be found; at last, for six annas, he bought a copy of ‘Chitrita Chandi-Mahatmya or the Tale of Kalketu,’ richly illustrated. Sarbajaya had mentioned a couple of little household things—a wooden rolling board and rolling pin—he bought those too.

Getting off at the village station, he walked on, reaching the village by late afternoon. On the way, he hardly met anyone; even if he did, he hurried past, anxious, paying little heed to anyone, intent only on home. Entering the doorway, he muttered to himself—Ah, just look at this! The bamboo grove is leaning right over the wall, and Bhuban-kaka won’t cut it—what a bother! Then, stepping into the courtyard, he called out in his usual eager tone—O Ma Durga! O Apu!

Hearing his voice, Sarbajaya came out from the house.

Harihar smiled and said—Is everything well at home? Where is everyone? Are they not home?

Sarbajaya calmly took the heavy bundle from her husband’s hand and said, Come—come inside.

Though Harihar noticed his wife’s unusual calmness, it did not trouble him—his thoughts were racing elsewhere, carried away by anticipation—any moment now, the children would come running—

Durga would come, smiling, and say—What’s in there, Baba? At once, Harihar would hurriedly open the bundle, showing his daughter the sari and the leaves of alta, and his son the illustrated ‘Chandi-Mahatmya or the Tale of Kalketu’ and the tin toy train, astonishing them both. Entering the house, he said, I’ve brought a fine jackfruit-wood rolling board and pin this time. Then, with a gaze tinged with disappointment and longing, he looked about and said, Where—Apu, Durga—have they all gone out?

--- Page 154 ---
Sarbojaya could hold back no longer. With a voice choked in emotion, she burst into tears—“O Durga, are you still here?—Mother has tricked us and left—where have you been all this time!”

The Ganguli family’s puja is a tradition of many years. During these days, not even the poorest in the village go hungry. Everything is arranged in the old, aristocratic way: at the appointed time the potter comes to shape the idol, the painter decorates the backdrop, the garland-maker brings his ornaments, and from the marshes of Barase and Madhukhali, the Bauri folk gather armfuls of lotus blooms.

As in every other year, Dinuda the shehnai player from Anshmali arrived to play the rasonchouki. In the morning sky, the joyous notes of the Agomoni song ring out—the loving welcome of the coming Hemanta season—of new sheaves of paddy, of fresh shefali leaves, of the migratory shyama birds flying in from beyond the Himalayas, of autumn evenings adorned with dew-kissed lotus blooms.

Dressing his son in new clothes, Harihar sets out to attend a feast. The small face, framed by a tangle of unkempt hair, hides a quiet, earnest plea that drifts into the breeze by the doorway—Harihar, stepping onto the path, seems lost in thought—he tells his son, “Walk ahead, it’s getting late, baba.”

The courtyard of the Ganguli house is transformed for the festival, filled with the laughter and bright faces of children. Apu looks and sees Satu and his brother dressed in orange-hued shirts—how well Ranu didi looks, in her green sari and beautifully tied hair! Sunayani, the Ganguli daughter, has tucked a tuberose in her bun and stands in the puja hall with five or six other girls, chatting and laughing. Except for Sunayani, Apu doesn’t know the other girls—perhaps they’ve come from elsewhere to stay at the house for the festival—city girls, perhaps, judging by their dress and manner! Apu gazes at them, transfixed. Meanwhile, someone is shouting—“Hasn’t anyone arranged to bring the big canopy yet?—Well! Just look at your work—wait and see what happens next. And then the Brahmins will sit down to eat at five o’clock, will they?”

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Seven**

Day after day slipped by. Winter, too, was drawing to a close.

--- Page 155 ---
Since Durga’s death, Sarbajaya had been constantly urging her husband to leave this village, and Harihar, too, had left no stone unturned in seeking opportunities elsewhere. Yet, nowhere did they find any advantage. Of late, Sarbajaya had more or less given up hope.

Last winter, Nilmoni Ray’s widow—Harihar’s cousin’s wife—had come here, and as their own homestead had become overgrown with jungle, she had taken up residence at Bhuban Mukherjee’s house. Harihar had shown considerable eagerness to bring his boudidi (elder brother’s wife) to stay at his own home, but Nilmoni Ray’s wife had not agreed. With her now were her daughter Atasi and her younger son Sunil. The elder son, Suresh, was studying at a school in Calcutta and would not be able to come here before the summer holidays.

Atasi was about fourteen years old, Sunil was eight. Sunil was not particularly good-looking, but Atasi was quite comely—though it would be too much to call her truly beautiful. Still, they had always lived in Lahore, where Nilmoni Ray had worked in the Commissariat. Both children had been born and brought up there, and so, in every limb, they bore the robust, wholesome health typical of the western provinces.

--- Page 156 ---
When they first arrived here, Sarbajaya had tried to befriend the grand lady of the house. Upon learning that Sunil’s mother owned ten thousand rupees in cash and company bonds, her heart filled with reverence for the lady, and she spared no effort in striking up a conversation, trying to draw closer. But in the end, even in her simplicity, Sarbajaya realized that Sunil’s mother was not prepared to give her much importance. Her husband had always held a high post; she and her children were accustomed to a different way of life. From the very beginning, she maintained such a distance from the poor relative’s family—Harihar’s family—that Sarbajaya was forced to withdraw on her own. In words, in manners, in every little thing, she made it clear that Sarbajaya was in no way fit to mingle with them as equals. In their speech, their dress, their bearing, there was a constant display of the fact that they belonged to a well-to-do household. The children were always neatly dressed, their clothes never so much as a bit dirty, their hair always combed, Atasi wore a necklace around her neck, gold bangles on her wrists, gold earrings in her ears. No one left the house in the morning without first having tea and a bite to eat. They had a servant from the city who did all the household chores—on the whole, in every matter, there was a great gulf between the ways of Sarbajaya’s poor household and theirs.

Sunil’s mother hardly let her son mix with any of the village boys, not even with Apu—for fear that, mingling with these uneducated, uncouth village children, her own children might be led astray. She had not come to live in this village; her purpose was to oversee their property during the time of the survey. The Bhuban Mukherjees kept some of their valuables in her care, and for that reason, had given up two rooms in the western wing for them; their cooking and meals were kept separate as well. In her dealings with the Bhuban Mukherjees, there was no difference to be seen, for the Mukherjees had money. But Sarbajaya—she did not count her among people at all.

--- Page 157 ---
During Dol (the festival of colors), Nilmoni Ray’s eldest son Suresh came home from Calcutta and stayed for nearly ten days. Suresh was of the same age as Apu, studying in the fifth standard at the English school. He was not very fair-skinned, but had a luminous dusky complexion. Because he exercised regularly, his body was quite sturdy and healthy. Though only a year older than Apu, in build and stature he looked like a boy of fifteen or sixteen. Suresh, too, did not mix much with the boys of this side of the village. On the other side, the son of Ganguli-bari, Ramnath Ganguli, was his classmate. Ganguli-bari celebrated Ramnavami Dol with great festivity, and on that occasion, Ramnath had also come to visit his maternal uncle’s house. Suresh spent most of his time there, and perhaps he also considered none of the other village boys worthy of his company.

That abandoned homestead, overgrown with jungle beside the house, had been there as long as Apu could remember—Suresh’s family belonged to that place. For this reason, Apu felt a peculiar attraction towards them. Suresh, his peer, studied in Calcutta—Apu had long been waiting for the holidays, hoping to get acquainted with him. But after coming home, Suresh did not really befriend him; moreover, Suresh’s manner and way of speaking were such that at every step he seemed to want to show he was far above the village boys. Though they were the same age, the shy Apu felt even more intimidated and kept his distance.

Apu had not yet attended any school; when Suresh asked him about his studies, he replied, “I study at home with Baba.” On the day of Dol, sitting under the olive tree by the bathing ghat at the Gangulis’ pond, Suresh was questioning the village boys in the manner of a triumphant logician. He asked Apu, “Tell me, what is the boundary of India? Do you know geography?”

Apu could not answer. Suresh asked again, “Have you done arithmetic? Can you solve decimal fractions?”

--- Page 158 ---
Apu doesn’t know all that much. But does it matter? Is his little tin box any the poorer for books? There’s that manual of daily rituals, an old physical geography, a battered copy of Shuvankari, a tattered volume of Birangana Kavya, and his mother’s Mahabharata—all these. He’s read them all—though he’s read them many times over, he reads them again and again.

His father is always searching here and there, thinking and bringing home books for him. The boy must study, must become learned, must grow up to be a real man—about this, his father has an unquenchable, relentless thirst, like a man possessed by fever. But he has no money, not enough means to send his son to board at a distant school, and he himself knows little of books. Still, as long as he’s at home, he sits his son down beside him and teaches him this and that, tells him stories, and to teach the boy arithmetic, he himself brushes up his own long-forgotten childhood lessons with the help of that Shuvankari, before setting sums for his son to solve. Whatever he thinks will add to his boy’s knowledge, that’s what he lets him read—or else he reads it aloud to him.

He’s been a subscriber to Bangabasi for many years; old issues have piled up in their house. Harihar had bundled them up carefully and put them away, thinking that when the boy grew up, he would read them. Now, at last, they’re coming in handy. But since he can’t pay for it anymore, no new papers come to them; the paperwallahs have stopped delivering. Harihar knows all too well how mad his son is for that Bangabasi paper—how, on Saturday mornings, Apu forsakes his games and sits open-mouthed by the postbox at Bhuban Mukherjee’s Chandimandap, waiting for the peon to come. And because he can’t provide his son with this most cherished thing, Harihar’s heart aches with a dull, persistent pain.

Yet Apu has learned many stories from those old Bangabasi papers. He tells Potu about them—about the eruption of Mount Pelée on the island of Martinique, about the golden-skinned magician, and so many other tales. But as for his schoolwork, he’s learned nothing. He knows arithmetic only up to division, no history, no grammar, has never even heard the names of geometry or mensuration, and his English is stuck at the horse pictures in the First Book.

--- Page 159 ---
She held a different vision for her son's future than most. Sarbajaya was a daughter of the village. She harbored no lofty hopes that her boy would grow up to be a learned man through schooling. In her world, no one she knew had ever set foot in a school. Among the few disciple-households they served, her greatest hope was that, in a few years, her son would begin to visit those homes, maintain those ties, and keep their modest tradition alive.

Sarbajaya cherished one more hope. The village priest, Dinu Bhattacharya, had grown old. None of his sons were fit to succeed him. Rani’s mother, Gokul’s wife, the eldest daughter-in-law of the Ganguli household—all had voiced their wish that, after Dinu Bhattacharya, they would entrust their rituals to Apu. Instead of Dinu’s ganja-smoking son Bhombol, it would be this innocent, gentle, handsome boy who would become their companion during the village’s Manasa Puja, Lakshmi Puja, and all their sacred arrangements. This was the wish of the village women. Everyone loved Apu. Sarbajaya had heard this desire expressed many times at the bathing ghat, on the village paths, in the conversations of her neighbors. And now, this had become her highest hope. She was a daughter of a poor home, a bride in a poor home—she could imagine no brighter future than this. If this came to pass, it would be as though she had caught the last dream of the night in the palm of her hand.

One day, this very topic arose at Bhuban Mukherjee’s house. After lunch, many from the neighborhood had gathered there for a game of cards. Sarbajaya, eager to please everyone, said, “If our boro khuri (eldest aunt), Thakuma (grandmother), and Mejdi (second eldest sister) show their kindness, then next Phagun (spring), after Apu’s sacred thread ceremony, he could begin helping with the village pujas. What more could I want for him? There are eight or ten disciple-houses, and if, by Ma Siddheshwari’s grace, the Ganguli household’s puja becomes a regular affair—well, that would be everything.”

Sunil’s mother hid a smile behind her hand. Her own son, Suresh, would grow up to study law—her cousin was a famous barrister in Patna, and Suresh would go there to apprentice under him. That uncle was childless, but a man of great influence. Even now, the family hoped to keep Suresh close and see to his education. But why should Sunil’s mother send her son to live in another’s house? Without ever making a fuss like the naïve Sarbajaya, she had already made her intentions clear to everyone in the course of casual conversation.

After leaving Bhuban Mukherjee’s house, Sarbajaya said to her son, “Listen to me—” Then, lowering her voice, she whispered, “Don’t go to your Jethima (paternal aunt) and say, ‘Jethima, I don’t have any shoes—won’t you buy me a pair?’”

--- Page 160 ---
Apu said, “Why, Ma?”

—“Don’t say that. They’re rich, maybe if you ask, they’ll give you a nice pair of shoes—haven’t you seen the ones Suresh wears? That kind of red shoes would suit you well—”

Apu replied shyly, “I feel so embarrassed, I can’t ask… What would they think… I…”

Sarbajaya said, “What’s there to be embarrassed about? For your own sake—just ask—what’s the harm?”

“Hm… uh… I can’t, Ma. I can’t speak in front of Jethima (aunt).”

Sarbajaya, now annoyed, said, “And why not? All your cleverness is tucked away in the corners of the house—wandering around barefoot, it’s been two years since you’ve had shoes, that’s fine, the rich folks, maybe they’d buy you some if you asked—but you can’t get a word out—king of the tongue-tied—”

On the day of the full moon, Apu went to Rani’s house to bring back the prasad (offerings) from the Satyanarayan puja. Rani called him over with a smiling face and said, “You used to come to our house all the time before, why don’t you come anymore?”

“Why wouldn’t I come, Ranudi (elder sister Ranu)—I do come, don’t I?”

Ranu, with a note of hurt in her voice, said, “Oh yes, you come! As if! I think about you so much. Do you ever think about me, about us?”

“Of course I do! Just ask Ma if you don’t believe me!”

Other than that, he couldn’t come up with any satisfactory excuse. Rani left him standing there, went inside, and brought out fruit prasad and sandesh (a Bengali sweet) for him, placing them in his hands. Smiling, she said, “Take the whole plate, I’ll go tomorrow and get it back from Khurima (aunt).”

In Rani’s smile, Apu felt a deep sense of trust settle over him. How beautiful Ranudi has become these days—he’s never seen another girl as lovely as her. Atosidi (elder sister Atosi) is always well-dressed, true, but she doesn’t compare to Ranudi. Besides, Apu knows, among all the girls in the village, none has a heart like Ranudi’s. If there’s anyone he loves after Didi (elder sister), it’s Ranudi. And doesn’t he know that Ranudi is drawn to him too?

As he was about to leave with the plate, he hesitated a little and said, “Ranudi, the books in the cupboard in your west room—Satuda (elder brother Satu) doesn’t let me read them. Will you give me one to read? I’ll return it as soon as I’m done.”

Rani said, “Which book, I don’t know—wait, let me see—”

--- Page 161 ---
Satu at first refused outright, but at last he said—“Alright, I’ll let you read, if you do one thing. Fish are being stolen from our field’s pond every day—Jethamashai (uncle) told me to go there at noon and keep watch—but I don’t like sitting there all alone. If you go in my place, then I’ll let you borrow the books—”

Rani protested—“Is that fair? He’s just a child, and you want him to sit guard in that wild place? You’re the older boy, if you can’t do it, how can he? No need to give him any books, I’ll ask Baba for them myself—”

But Apu agreed. Rani’s father, Bhuban Mukherjee, was abroad, and it would be a long time before he returned, but Apu’s longing for those books was immense. For days he had wandered, heart full of yearning, to Satu’s western room, drawn by the lure of those volumes. He had managed to read a few pages here and there. But Satu himself never read, nor would he let Apu read. Just as the hero reached his moment of crisis, Satu would snatch the book away—“Put it down, Apu, these are Chhoto Kaka’s (younger uncle’s) books, they’ll get torn, give it here.”

Now, Apu felt as if heaven had fallen into his hands.

Every day at noon, he would carefully select a book from the almirah, ask Satu for it, and then, laying out a bed of fresh shaora branches beneath the shade of the bamboo grove, he would sprawl on his stomach and read with rapt attention. There were so many books—Pranoy-Pratima, Saroj-Sarojini, Kusum-Kumari, the illustrated play Yauvane Jogini, Dasyu-Duhita, Prem-Parinam or Amrite Garal, Gopeshwarer Guptakatha...how many names could he list! He would take up each one, unable to let it go until he reached the end. His eyes would burn, his temples throb, and meanwhile, the shadow of the bamboo grove by the lonely pond would lengthen, creeping over the cracked, mossy bank of the dried-up pond—and he would have no idea where the afternoon had gone!

--- Page 162 ---
What a story! With Sarojini by his side, Saroj is traveling by boat to Murshidabad, when, along the way, the Nawab’s men seize their boat, loot it, and take them captive. By the Nawab’s order, Saroj is sentenced to death, and Sarojini is locked away in a dark room, the door bolted and chained. In the depths of night, the door to her chamber swings open, and the Nawab, drunk and reeling, enters and declares—“Beautiful one, by my command Saroj is dead, now why…,” and so on. Sarojini, with proud defiance, turns her head and replies—“O fiend, you have yet to know a Rajput woman; as long as there is life in this land…,” and so on.

At that very moment, with a thunderous blow, the prison window shatters. The Nawab, startled, looks up to see—a towering ascetic, matted locks coiled atop his head, his body ablaze with spiritual power, flanked by four or five strong men, fierce as messengers of Yama, the god of death. The ascetic, eyes blazing with wrath, fixes the Nawab with his gaze and says—“Wretch, a protector turned predator?” Then, turning to Sarojini, he says—“Mother, I am your husband’s guru—Swami Yogananda. Your husband’s life has not been lost; with the water from my kamandalu (ascetic’s water pot), he has been revived. Now, come, Mother, to my ashram—your Saroj awaits you there.” …The author’s penmanship is exquisite—so as to portray Saroj’s miraculous resurrection in even greater detail, he stirs the reader’s curiosity for the next chapter—“Come now, reader, let us see, after Saroj’s execution at the place of sacrifice, by what means his life was restored…” and so on.

At the end of each chapter, Apu’s eyes grow misty—something catches in his throat. He gazes up at the sky for a minute or two, lost in wonder and delight, in such excitement that it feels as if flames are leaping from his ears. Then, holding his breath, he plunges into the next chapter. Evening falls, shadows stretch long on every side, and above his head, the bamboo grove fills with the calls of birds. Though he feels the urge to get up, he keeps his eyes fixed just an inch above the page, reading as long as the letters are visible.

He has never read a book like this before! How pale seem Sita’s exile, or the tales of Dubala, beside it!

When he returns home, his mother scolds him—“Are you really such a simpleton? You give away other people’s fish, and then go sit all alone in the forest just to read a book? Well, someone’s made a fool of you, that’s for sure.”

--- Page 163 ---
But from the direction where foolish Apu’s fortune arrives, his mother has not the faintest inkling. Lately, he has come into possession of two books—‘Maharashtra Jiban-Prabhat’ (The Dawn of Life in Maharashtra) and ‘Rajput Jiban-Sandhya’ (The Twilight of Rajput Life). …Against the backdrop of termite mounds and the tangled thickets of the baichi forest, in the enchanted stillness of the afternoon, scene after scene unfurls—he sits by the Jelekha river, tending to the wounded Noren; he imagines himself swelling with pride as Shivaji, seeing himself among the five-thousand mansabdars in Aurangzeb’s court—Shivaji, a mere five-thousander? Go to Pune once, count how many five-thousand mansabdars there are in Shivaji’s army and come back!...

On the desert ridges of the royal palace, in the painted halls of Delhi and Agra, in the palace of mirrors, among the beauties draped in orna and peshwaz, his days pass in their company. What world is this—where there is only moonlight, the play of swords, the friendship of beautiful faces, and at the aheriya festival, riding horseback with a long spear through barren valleys and fields of maize?...

What a hero could do, what a Rajput could do, what a man could do—Pratap Singh had done it all. Upon every stone slab of the mountainous paths of Haldighat, his story is inscribed. In the battlefield of Dewar, in the heart’s blood of twelve thousand Rajputs, his tale is written in undying glory.

Even after many years, the ancient warriors would sit by the fire on winter nights and tell their grandchildren the wondrous tales of Haldighat’s valor...

A spear, hurled by an invisible hand, comes flying—Apu, raised in the shade of this village’s ever-green woods, amid the dense tangle of vines and leaves, in the scent of wet earth—yet he knows every place in the Bhil lands of Rajputana, or the Aravalli-Mewar hills, he knows well the wild, peerless beauty of Magro. …How beautiful seems the image of the armed Tej Singh descending from the mountains!

“In those fifty-six provinces, for many days, in the lonely caves and lofty peaks of that Bhil village, at midnight, the song of a woman’s voice could be heard. In the earliest dawn, in the deserted wilderness, travelers would sometimes catch sight of a woman’s pale face and restless eyes—people would say, ‘It must be some anxious, tireless forest goddess’…” The faint, plaintive melody of that song seems to drift to Apu’s ears from behind the bamboo grove…

--- Page 164 ---
Komolmir, the battle of Surjagarh, General Shahbaz Khan, the beautiful Noorjahan, Pushpakumari, the wild land of the Bhils, the brave boy Chandansingh—distant, far-off imaginings. And yet, how near, how real they seem. On the desert sands of the royal palace, and on the lofty peaks of the blue Aravalli mountains, the trees of Chenar have bloomed and shed their flowers; the crimson footprints of goddess Mibar-Lakshmi, stained with alakta dye, remain imprinted on the stones along the banks of the Bunas and Bari rivers, on the rocks above the waterfalls, in the fields of bajra and jowar, and in the tangled wild forests...

Chittor could not be saved! Rana Amarsingh accepted the emperor’s honor. The dispossessed father, Pratapsingh, who for twenty-five years had fought in the forests and mountains with his band of Bhils—where, with his wounded, anguished heart, had he witnessed all this from...?

Through the hot tears in his eyes, the pond, the termite mounds, the thickets of baichi, the bamboo groves—all blur and fade away.

That day, in the afternoon, his father showed him a paper-wrapped parcel with a smiling face and said—“Look, Khoka, what do you think this is?”

Apu quickly climbed up onto the bed and asked eagerly—“A newspaper? Is it, Baba?”

That day, having written a protective charm and received three rupees from Behari Ghosh’s mother-in-law, Harihar had, without telling his wife, sent two rupees from that money to pay for the newspaper. If his wife had known, there would have been no way to save those two rupees from the clutches of all the other pressing needs.

Apu snatched the paper parcel from his father’s hand and tore it open. Yes—it was indeed a newspaper. There, in big bold letters, was written “Bangabasi”; that new-paper smell, that print, all of it—the very thing for which, about a year ago, he used to sit every Saturday with bated breath like a pilgrim crow, waiting by the letterbox at Bhuban Mukherjee’s Chandimandap for the postman to arrive! A newspaper! A newspaper! What new stories might it hold? What unknown wonders are written in its broad pages?

Harihar felt—that the joy he had brought to his son’s face for the price of two rupees was worth more than the satisfaction of redeeming any pawned brass pot!

After reading for a while, Apu said—“Look, Baba, there’s a letter from a ‘traveler to Bilat’ (England) published here, it’s a new feature starting today. Our paper has arrived at just the right time—hasn’t it, Baba?”

--- Page 165 ---
Yet, a sadness lingered in his heart that last year, when they suddenly stopped the magazine, he never got to read the ending of the story about the Japanese Spider-Demon. After Raiko went to the royal court, what happened to him—he never found out…

One day Rani said, “What are you writing in your notebook, re?”

Apu replied in a tone of surprise, “Which notebook? How did you—”

“Didn’t I come to your house that afternoon? You weren’t there, I sat and chatted with Khurima (paternal aunt) for quite a while. Why, didn’t Khurima tell you? That’s when I saw, in your stack of books, in that red notebook of yours—you’d written something—my name is there, and Devi Singh or something—”

Apu, blushing with embarrassment, said, “Oh, that’s just a story.”

“What story, re? You have to read it to me, okay?”

The next day, Rani handed Apu a small bound notebook and said, “Write me a story in this—make it a really good one. Will you? Atashi was saying you can write well! Write one for me, I’ll show it to Atashi…”

At night, Apu sits and writes in the notebook. He says to his mother, “Ma, give me a little more oil—just a bit, so I can finish writing today…”

His mother replies, “No more reading tonight—there’s only two ladles of oil left, what will I cook with tomorrow? I’m cooking right here, sit and read in this light.”

Apu argues.

Ma scolds, “Eh! When night falls, the boy suddenly gets the urge to study—can’t see a hair of him all day. What do you do in the morning? No, I won’t give you any oil.”

In the end, Apu brings his notebook and sits by the stove, writing by the light of the burning wood. Sarbajaya thinks—when Apu grows a little older, I’ll find him a good match. A new brick house will rise on this homestead. Next year, after his sacred thread ceremony, if the Ganguli family’s puja becomes a tradition—

…Four or five days later, when he returned the notebook to Rani, she opened it eagerly and said, “Did you write something?”

Apu, with a smile, said, “Why don’t you open it and see?”

Rani, seeing the pages, exclaimed with delight, “Oh! You’ve written so much! Wait, let me call Atashi and show her.”

Atashi, after looking, said, “Apu hasn’t written anything new—tsk! He just copies from books.”

Apu protested, “Ish, do I copy from books? I make up stories—ask Potu if you want, Atashidi! Don’t I sit by the river in the evenings and tell him stories I make up on the spot?”

Rani said, “No, I know he wrote it himself. He writes like that. He wrote a wonderful jatra (folk theatre) play in his notebook, read it out to me… Later she told Apu—You didn’t write your name? Write your name on it.”

--- Page 166 ---
Apu, with a hint of embarrassment, said that his story wasn’t finished yet—he’d write his name down once it was. Though he had begun the tale in the style of the play “Sochitra Jaubane-Yogini,” he couldn’t quite figure out how to end it. Yet, fearing that if he kept the notebook too long, Ranudi—especially Atosidi—might begin to doubt his poetic talent, he returned it unfinished...

His father wasn’t home. In the morning, he’d gone with the rest of the villagers to a neighboring village for an adyashraddha feast (a ritual meal in memory of ancestors). Sunil went with him. From various villages, groups of Brahmins had walked five or six krosh (ancient measure, several miles) to attend. Each man had brought along five or six children, and as everyone tried to seat themselves comfortably, a commotion nearly broke out. After four luchis (fried flatbreads) had been served to each plate, the servers came around with fried eggplant, only to find that some plates were empty—everyone had tucked their luchis away on the edge of a cloth or gamcha (thin towel) beside them... The little boys, not understanding such subtleties, started to tear into the luchis on their plates—Apu’s father, Bishweshwar Bhattacharya, quickly snatched the luchis from his son’s plate and placed them on the cloth beside him, saying, “Keep these aside! They’ll serve more soon, eat then.”

After that, an uproar broke out—“The luchi basket’s on this row!” “Why is all the pumpkin on my plate?” “Hey, look, they’re piping hot!” “Sir, what did you serve, check with your hand, it’s just raw dough!” and so on. There was a fierce argument between the Brahmins and the organizers about the amount of chhada (ritual offering or share of food)! Someone shouted, “Then don’t invite gentlemen there! Five luchis per head, that’s the fixed chhada rate—it’s been set since the days of Ballal Sen! We don’t want your chhada, Kandarpo Majumdar would never—”

The organizers, with folded hands, managed to appease Kandarpo Majumdar.

Apu, too, brought home a little bundle of chhada. Sarbajaya hurried out, smiling, and said, “Oh my, how much you’ve brought—let’s see, open it! Luchi, pantua (syrupy sweets), gaja (fried sweets)—so much! Let’s cover it up and save it, eat in the morning now.”

Apu said, “But you have to eat too, Ma—I asked for pantua twice just for you.”

Sarbajaya replied, “Really? Did you say, ‘Give some for my Ma to eat’?—You’re such a silly boy!”

--- Page 167 ---
Apu shook his head and hand, saying—“Yes, that’s exactly what I’m saying! The way I said it, they must have thought I’d eat it all.”

Sarbojaya, delighted, picked up the little bundle and took it inside the house.

A little while later, Apu went to Sunil’s house. The moment he set foot on their veranda, he heard Sunil’s mother scolding Sunil—“Why did you bring all that home? Who told you to bring it?”

Sunil, like everyone else, had tied up his share in a cloth, and replied—“Why, Ma, everyone took some—Apu took some too.”

Sunil’s mother retorted—“Why wouldn’t Apu take it—he’s that Pholar Brahmin’s son! Next he’ll be performing puja and wandering around with his little bundle—such is their way. His mother is just as greedy. That’s why I didn’t want to come to this village with you all. Bad company leads to bad habits! Go, call Apu and give it all back to him—or just throw it away. If you’re invited, you eat—why bring things home like low-caste folks?”

Frightened, Apu didn’t dare enter Sunil’s house. As he walked home, he wondered—Why did Jethima (aunt, father’s elder brother’s wife) get so angry seeing the very things that made his mother so happy? Was the food so worthless that it had to be thrown away? His mother, greedy? He, Pholar’s Brahmin son? Oh, but Jethima must have eaten plenty of sweets and fried treats—his own mother never got to eat any of that. And hadn’t he himself eaten these things for the past few days? What was wrong for Sunil was somehow not wrong for him—how could that be?

He didn’t study much; he spent his days doing just these things. Eating Pholar’s food, tying up little bundles, going to disciples’ houses with his father, fishing. That little boy, Patu—from the fishermen’s quarter, who once got beaten up while playing shells—he was Apu’s companion in all these adventures. These days, he had grown taller, his head reaching higher, and he always tagged along with Apuda (elder brother Apu). He only came from the other neighborhood to play with Apuda, hardly mixing with anyone else. He still hadn’t forgotten how Apuda had taken a beating from the fishermen’s boys trying to protect him.

--- Page 168 ---
Apu’s passion for fishing is boundless. Down below the Sonadanga fields, where the Ichhamati river bends and the Kanchikata canal meets its mouth, the fish bite eagerly at the line. He often comes here, settling himself beneath the great chatim tree by the riverbank to fish. The place enchants him—utterly secluded, with all manner of trees and plants leaning over the river’s edge on both sides, their branches dipping into the water. Across the way, a dense green thicket of reeds, here and there wild kadam and shimul trees tangled with vines, clumps of purple forest water-spinach flowers, and in the distance, the bamboo groves of Madhabpur village. The calls of birds mingle with the shadows of the woods and the lushness of the reeds, blending into a gentle, tranquil solitude.

From the very first day of his boyhood arrival at the kuthir-math—the cottage field—Apu has been spellbound by the magic of these fields, woods, and rivers. Casting his line, sitting in the shade of the chatim tree, his heart fills with an exquisite delight as he gazes all around. Whether or not he catches any fish, whenever the thick shadows of evening gather, and the air grows heavy with the scent of ripe dates from the clumps of date palms at the field’s edge, when the gentle breeze carries from all directions the calls of bou-kotha-kao and papiya birds, and the sun, scattering clouds of color, leans behind the scraggly banyan tree in the Sonadanga field, turning the river’s water black, and the gangs of myna birds chatter their way home—at such moments, his mind is swept away. With eyes brimming with wonder, he looks around and feels—fish or no fish, he will return here every day, to sit just beneath this great chatim tree.

Often, he catches nothing. The float on his line stands motionless in the still water, unwavering as a flame in a windless night. He doesn’t have the patience to sit in one place for long; he fidgets, wandering here and there, searching the bushes for birds’ nests. When he returns, perhaps he notices the float trembling ever so slightly! He lifts the rod and mutters, “Ugh! Just a school of pesky zhena fish—nothing will come of this.” Then he moves a little farther off, casting his bait near a patch of moss. The deep, dark water makes him think—surely a great rohu or katla will bite any moment now! But the illusion doesn’t last long; the float soon returns to its meditative stillness…

Some days, he brings along a book to read as he sits.

--- Page 169 ---
Casting his line, he opens his book. From Suresh, he has borrowed an illustrated English reader from a lower class, along with its translation guide. He cannot understand English, so he pores over the translation to grasp the Bengali version of the stories, and in the English book, he gazes only at the pictures. Tales of distant lands and all manner of greatness have stirred his heart since childhood, and this little book holds many such stories. There is the tale of a traveler, lost in a blizzard on some open plain, wandering in circles until he perishes in the cold; the story of how Christopher Columbus crossed unknown oceans to discover America—stories like these. The two English children, a boy and a girl, who, while collecting eggs from a seagull’s nest on a seaside cliff, found themselves in peril; the brave girl, Praskovia Lopuloff, who, hoping to win her exiled father’s pardon, set out alone across the desolate, snow-covered roads of distant Siberia—he feels as if he could recognize them at once, should he ever see them.

When he reads the little story of Sir Philip Sidney, his eyes fill with tears. He goes to Suresh and asks, “Sureshda, do you know this story? Will you tell it to me, but not in a grown-up way?”

Suresh says, “Oh, the story of the Battle of Zutphen!”
