Apu, astonished, asks, “What, Sureshda? Zutphen! Where is that?”

But Suresh cannot say any more than that...

About a month later, one day—

While fishing, somehow a large sarputi fish (a kind of river fish) bites his line.

Greedy for more, he refuses to leave that spot—breaking off branches from trees, he makes himself a bed and sits there.

Time passes; once again, that extraordinary silence settles over the riverside fields. Beyond, on the far side of the embankment, in the vast green stretches of ulubon (wild grass) and kash-thickets, atop the kadam and shimul trees, the last rays of the evening sun—so familiar, so old a companion of his childhood’s moments of delight—return once more.

He remembers that beautiful story he read in Bongobashi, in the letter from the traveler to England...

--- Page 170 ---
He knows where Suresh-dada has shown the Mediterranean Sea on his English map; he knows that just across its waters lies the country of France. Long ago, when foreign armies pressed down upon the heart of France, the nation was imperiled, the king powerless, chaos and plunder reigned on all sides! In those dark days of national humiliation, in a tiny, secluded village of the province of Lorraine, a poor farmer’s daughter would take her father’s flock to graze. Letting the sheep wander here and there, she would sit quietly on the grassy edge of the remote countryside, her blue eyes lifted to the sky, pondering in solitude the misfortunes of her land. Day after day, as she sat thus, a thought arose in her innocent maiden’s heart—someone seemed to be telling her: “You are the savior of France. Go, gather the king’s army, take up arms, the fate of your country and people rests in your hands.” The Virgin Mary was her inspirer—her call came from far-off heaven, day after day. Then, how the newly emboldened French army drove the enemy from their land, how the visionary maiden herself took up arms and set the king upon his throne, and how, in the end, the ignorant and blind masses burned her alive as a witch—he has read all these things today.

In this evening hour, by the tranquil riverbank, as he ponders this tale, how wondrously full his heart becomes!—The maiden’s battles, her victories, the rest he does not dwell upon so much. But the image that returns to him again and again is only this: a solitary, thoughtful girl in the quiet fields, her flock of sheep wandering at will around her, the dusky green grass below, the boundless blue sky above. On one side, fierce foreign enemies, cruelty, the arrogance of conquest, rivers of blood—on the other, a simple, divinely inspired, blue-eyed village girl. This image captivates his growing boyish mind.

Other images come to mind as well. The distant, sea-girdled island of Martinique. All around, fields of sugarcane, the blue sky overhead—far, far away—only blue sky and blue sea!—only blue and blue! There is so much more, but it cannot be explained—it cannot be told.

--- Page 171 ---
Winding up his fishing line, he prepared to head home. Along the riverbank, the bowed heads of babla and saibaba trees leaned over, their branches heavy with blossoms, shedding their fragrant burden into the river’s gentle, dark waters. In the middle of the golden Sonadanga fields, behind the scraggly old banyan, a vast blood-red sun slanted low—like some divine child, who, from the burning, foaming golden sea of Alaka, had blown a bubble and sent it drifting across the sky, and now, just this moment, it was falling behind the western horizon, sinking into the world’s distant forests!

Suddenly, someone came up behind him and covered his eyes. As he struggled, prying the hands away, Patu burst out laughing and came around in front of him. “I’ve been looking for you everywhere, Apuda! Then I thought, you must be out here fishing, so I came. No luck with the fish? Not even one? Come on—let’s untie a boat and go for a ride—want to?”

At Kadamtala, at the sahib’s ghat, boats came from far-off lands—boats loaded with golpata leaves, boats heavy with rice, boats filled with shells, all moored in neat rows. Out on the river, the fishermen’s shell-collecting boats cast their great nets. Every year, at this time, they came from the south to gather shells, anchoring their boats side by side in the middle of the river. Apu sat on the bank watching—a dark-skinned man dove again and again, searching for shells, and after a while, surfaced beside his boat, sifting through his pouch to pick out a few shells from the piles of sand and mud, tossing them into the boat’s hold. Apu, delighted, pointed them out to Patu—“See, Patu, how long he stays under? Let’s count—one, two, three! Could you stay under that long?”

The riverbank, thick with soft grass, sloped down to the water’s edge, and here and there, the loaded boats were anchored, their mooring poles stuck deep in the mud. Who knows from how far they had come, crossing great rivers and canals, braving the tides and storms of the vast salty estuaries—Apu longed to sit with the boatmen and listen to tales of those distant lands. All he wanted was to wander from river to river, from sea to sea—nothing else. Ever since reading about sailors from faraway countries in Suresh’s book, that longing had grown fierce within him! Patu went up to one of the boats and started bargaining—“Hey, majhi (boatman), how much for a bundle of these golpata leaves? Where’s your rice boat from, majhi? Jhalakathi? Which way is that, how far from here?”

Patu said, “Apuda, let’s go to the tamarind grove’s ghat and see if there’s a dinghy—let’s go for a little ride!”

--- Page 172 ---
The two of them, from the tamarind grove’s ghat, unmoored a small dinghy, gave it a gentle push, and climbed aboard. The river water rose with its cool, damp scent; water hyacinths sat quietly on the surface, their stems thick with the smell of kolmi-shak (water spinach). Along the edges of the sandbars, farmers weeded their pointed gourd fields, some cut grass and tied it into bundles. At the bend by the jhau and wild olive thickets, a flock of gangshalik (river mynas) chattered noisily. In the late afternoon, clouds of many colors piled up along the eastern sky.

Patu said—“Apu-da, won’t you sing a song? That one you sang the other day!”

Apu replied—“Not that one. I’ve learned a really good song from Baba, with the proper tune. I’ll sing that one. But go a little further, brother, not here—the people on the bank might hear us.”

“You’re so shy, Apu-da! The people are so far away, and you’re afraid to sing—come on, sing it!”

After they had drifted a little, Apu began to sing. Patu, picking up the bamboo paddle, sat quietly at the prow, listening intently. There was no need to row; the dinghy floated on its own, spinning gently as the current carried it toward the wide bend of La-Bhanga. When Apu’s song ended, Patu started up a song of his own. Now Apu was rowing. The boat hadn’t gone far—the bend of La-Bhanga was already in sight.

Suddenly Patu, pointing to the northeast, exclaimed—“O Apu-da, look at those clouds! Looks like a storm’s coming—should we turn back?”

Apu said—“Let the storm come! It’s best to row and sing in a storm—let’s go a little further.”

As they spoke, a dense black cloud rose from the direction of Madhabpur’s fields, spreading to fill the whole sky, its dark shadow falling across the river. Patu gazed up at the sky with eager eyes. Far off, a whistling sound began, a vague tumult mixed with the cries of many birds; a chill wind blew, carrying the scent of wet earth. The winged seeds of akanda (milkweed) came swirling from the fields in countless numbers. In no time, the trees bent and tossed their heads, the wild nor’wester storm broke with a furious violence.

The river water turned a deep, dense black. The sainsbabla and tall chatim trees along the bank seemed about to snap, their branches thrashing. Flocks of white egrets, strung out in long lines, fled beneath the dark sky! Apu’s chest swelled, his heart pounding with excitement and wonder. He let go of the oar, turning in all directions to watch the wild spectacle of the storm. Patu, untying the end of his cloth, held it up to the wind like a sail, and the wind caught it, billowing it out wide.

--- Page 173 ---
Patu said—“The wind is too fierce, Apu-da, the boat can’t go any further. But what if it capsizes? Thank goodness I didn’t bring Sunil along!”

But Apu wasn’t listening to Patu’s words; his ears weren’t tuned to them—nor was his mind. Sitting at the prow of the boat, he gazed fixedly at the storm-tossed river and the sky. All around him—the dancing black waters of the river, the flock of flying egrets, the billowing masses of storm clouds, the piles of oyster shells from the southern boatmen, the drifting mats of water hyacinth in the current—everything seemed to blur and fade away! He imagined himself as that England-bound traveler from the ‘Bangabasi’ newspaper! His ship had set sail from Calcutta, leaving Sagar Island behind at the mouth of the Bay of Bengal, passing by so many unknown little islands in the middle of the sea, beholding the emerald coconut groves along the Ceylonese coast, the distant blue mountain ranges of wondrous lands receding into the horizon, bathed in the crimson glow of sunset, journeying on—on—on—through ever-changing, ever-new scenes of foreign shores!

Just like the waters of this Ichhamati—dark, deep, agitated—he imagined that unseen distant ocean, its surface dotted with green thickets, like that island in the Arabian Sea. There, on an evening like this, he would sit beneath a tree at the port of Aden, and, just like that England-bound traveler, he would ask a beautiful Arab maiden for a glass of water and drink from her hand. Looking toward the bend at Chaltipota, he could almost clearly see, as if from the pages of the newspaper, the flock of flying seabirds trailing behind the ship…

--- Page 174 ---
He will go to all those places, he will see all those wonders—he will travel to Bilat (England), to Japan, he will set out on merchant voyages, become a great trader, forever journeying across seas and oceans, facing peril after peril. When, in the midst of the China Sea, his ship is caught in a furious storm like today’s mesmerizing nor’wester, the Kalboishakhi, and is on the verge of sinking—just like the sailors in “My Unparalleled Travels”—he too will escape in a jali-boat (a small fishing boat), roasting and eating gugli-snails clinging to the submerged reefs, crossing boundless waters!

There—just above the bamboo grove of Madhabpur village, where a mountain of mulberry-colored clouds leaned a little while ago—beyond that lies all those blue seas, unknown shores, coconut groves, volcanoes, snow-laden fields, Jelekha, Sarayu, Grace Darling, Zutphen, those graceful English boys and girls gathering seagull eggs, the golden-skinned magician of Batgar, the blue-eyed village maiden Joan of Lorraine, lost in thought in a lonely meadow—oh, what marvels await! The books in his tin box, the books from Ranu-didi’s house, the one borrowed from Suresh-dada, the old issues of ‘Bangabasi’ newspaper—all speak to him of those distant lands, whispering that somewhere, someone is waiting for him there. One day, a call will come for him from those faraway places—he too will go!

It never occurs to him how distant those lands are, who will take him there, or how such a journey might ever be possible! Soon, he will have to go from house to house performing puja to keep the household running; at night, he will be scolded by Ma for wasting oil while reading; he, who has never seen the inside of a school, who does not know what good clothes or fine things are—who will invite such an unknown, unlettered, penniless village boy to join the grand festival of life?

If such questions ever arose in his mind, perhaps the chariot of his youthful imagination—the irresistible enchantment of his hope-filled path—could conquer all fear, all doubt. But such thoughts never come to him. He simply feels—when he grows up, everything will happen; as he moves forward, all opportunities and fortunes will be found along the way… For now, he must only wait to grow up! When he is older, opportunities will come, warm invitations will arrive from every direction—he will set out to conquer the world, to know its people, to discover life.

--- Page 175 ---
Immersed in dreams of a colorful future life, he spends the rest of his journey in a daze. The rain has ceased; the storm sweeps away the masses of dark clouds, leaving the sky clear and bright. It is only when the dinghy bumps against the landing at the tamarind grove that his reverie breaks. Tying up the boat, he strides ahead of Potu, whistling with delight as he takes the bamboo grove path homeward. He too, like his mother and didi (elder sister), has learned to dream.

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Eight**

In truth, Apu had not slept; he had been awake. Lying with his eyes closed, he had listened to the conversations his mother and father had at night. They were planning to leave this land and go to Kashi (the holy city of Varanasi). Father was telling mother about the many advantages of living in Kashi compared to here. He had spent many years there in his youth, was acquainted and friends with everyone in that land, and everyone knew and respected him. Things were cheaper there, too. His mother showed great eagerness; in that golden land, no one ever lacks for anything—here, sorrow clings to them all twelve months of the year, but if only they could muster the courage to go there, all suffering would end. Mother said she would leave today if she could, she had no wish to stay even a single day longer. In the end, it was decided—they would leave around the month of Baishakh (April–May)…

At the Siddheshwari Thakur-bari (temple-house) in Ganganandpur, Sarbajaya had a vow to fulfill. Who would travel three krosh (about six miles) to offer worship?—for this reason, the vow had not yet been fulfilled. Now, before leaving this land, it was necessary to perform the worship, but no one could be found for the task. Apu said—he would go and offer the worship, and his pishima (father’s sister) lived in that village; he had never met her, so he would visit her as well. His mother said—Nonsense! You think you’ll go alone? It’s nearly four krosh from here!

Apu began to argue with his mother—So am I to sit in this house forever? Am I never to go anywhere? Do you think I have no eyes, no ears, no feet?

—Oh, you have everything, and you’ll go all alone to Ganganandpur—such a brave man, aren’t you!

But at last, overwhelmed by Apu’s persistence, she had to let him go.

--- Page 176 ---
The high earthen path cuts through the heart of the Sonadanga fields. On either side, stretching into the fields, grow wild thickets of akanda flowers—their long, pale stalks, heavy with blooms, bow down and sprawl across the soft blades of durba grass. There is not a soul on the path; noon is not far off, and the shadows of the trees are shrinking. The sun-warmed, sandy earth feels pleasant against Apu’s bare feet—a gentle comfort. In the thickets by the path, so many kinds of flowers have blossomed; the fresh blooms of the saibaba tree lift their faces toward the sun, and on a small, unknown shrub, clusters of red, wild figs ripen in abundance, glowing crimson. From the earth rises a sun-baked, loamy scent, rich and heady... Now and then, Apu bends low, searching through the undergrowth, gathering baichi fruit and filling both pockets of his hand-stitched red satin shirt.

As he walks, his heart swells with delight. He could never explain to anyone just how much he loves this fresh, sun-drenched scent of earth, this shadow-dappled durba grass, the sunlight-washed fields, the winding path, the trees, the birds, the wild thickets, the swaying clusters of flowers and fruit, the alkushi vines, the wild kalmi, the blue aparajita blossoms. He never wants to stay indoors; how wonderful it would be if Baba said to him—Khoka, you just wander from path to path. Then, beneath these wildflower-laden, shadowy thickets, with eyes fixed on the distant woods where doves call, he would walk along this earthen path—just walk, and walk...

Sometimes, perhaps, there would be the whisper of wind in the bamboo grove, the golden vermilion of late afternoon sunlight, and the songs of birds in every shade and color.

--- Page 177 ---
Apu’s childhood was spent in intimate communion with nature. As one season slipped away and another arrived—its message spread through the trees and plants, the sky and the wind, the chorus of birds. With each passing season, his awareness of the ever-changing forms of the Ichhamati river awakened—he had come to know well how each season transformed the trees and plants, the water, the land, and the sky with its flowers and fruits. He cherished this close bond with nature; he could not imagine life without it. Growing up, he kept this vast, wondrous panorama before his eyes.

After the blazing heat and suffocating stillness of summer, the whole horizon would be draped in the solemn, beautiful attire of deep blue clouds. At sunset, above the golden fields of Sonadangar, clouds of every hue played across the sky. At the end of Bhadra, the distant stretches of Madhabpur’s sandbars bloomed with swaying kash flowers. On moonlit nights, beneath the latticed shadows of the bamboo grove, silver light pooled and danced. Into Apu’s fresh, eager, untroubled heart—on the verge of blossoming into youth—these scenes pressed an indelible stamp of beauty, opened his eyes to the nectar of radiance, and softly whispered into his ears the secret mantra of immortality. Apu never forgot this lesson in his life. The vow to be a lifelong worshipper of beauty—unknowingly, nature herself, in her unfettered glory, was slowly initiating him into this path.

At the baor (oxbow lake) of Natidanga, some people were fishing. He stood there for a while, watching. In the village, a blind beggar was singing for alms, plucking at his ektara—a song Apu knew well, one he had sung many times himself—

“In broad daylight the moon rises, the night has come to an end…”

Bostom dadu (elderly Baul or wandering minstrel) sang that song beautifully.

Entering Harishpur, he saw a little thatched hut by the roadside where a pathshala (village school) was in session; the children were chanting their multiplication tables in sing-song voices. He stopped to listen. The gurumoshai (teacher) was not very old—much younger than the cheerful gurumoshai of their own village.

Another thought kept returning to him. Here he was, grown up now, no longer a child. If he were still little, would Ma ever let him go off alone like this? Now it was only forward, always moving ahead. Besides, where would they be, how far would they have traveled, on this very day next month? Where was that Kashi—so far away, waiting for them?

--- Page 178 ---
Toward evening, he arrived at Ganganandapur. The moment he set foot in the neighborhood, a wave of shyness swept over him so fiercely that he could not bring himself to look in any direction. With great effort, he kept his gaze fixed on the path ahead and walked on, somehow putting one foot in front of the other. It seemed to him that everyone was staring at him. As if everyone already knew he would be coming today—perhaps, all this while, they had been saying to themselves, “There he goes, look, look at him…” It felt as if they all knew he was carrying coconut-naru (a sweet made of coconut and jaggery) tied up in his bundle. He could not even bring himself to ask anyone which way was his pishimeshayi Kunja Chakraborty’s house.

At last, finding an old woman alone, he gathered the courage to ask her. She showed him the house. In front of the house was a walled enclosure. He entered the courtyard but found no one there. He coughed once or twice, but could not manage to let any words escape his lips. How long he stood in the blazing Chaitra sun in the outer yard, he could not say, but after a while, an eighteen or nineteen-year-old dusky-skinned girl came out for some reason and, as soon as she set foot on the verandah, she saw him—a strange, sweet-faced boy standing by the door, bundle in hand, overcome with shyness. The girl exclaimed in surprise—

“Who are you, khoka (little boy)? Where have you come from?...”

Apu, awkward and uncertain, stepped forward and managed to stammer out, “My home is in Nishchindipur—my name is A-Apu.”

He felt then that it would have been better not to come at all! Perhaps his pishi (aunt) would be annoyed by his unexpected arrival, perhaps she would think, “Where has this new trouble come from now?”… Besides, who could have known that it would be so hard to speak in a strange, unfamiliar place? Sweat broke out on his forehead.

But the girl immediately ran up to him, took his hand with great affection, and led him up onto the verandah. She asked after his mother and father. She cupped his chin in her hand and spoke words of such tenderness. Though he had never seen didi (elder sister) before, she spoke of didi by name and grieved deeply for her. With her own hands, she took off his shirt, washed his face and hands, dried him with a clean gamchha (thin cotton towel), and quickly brought him a glass of sugar sherbet. His pishi was not as he had imagined—she was young, just a little older than Rajib’s didi.

--- Page 179 ---
His pisi (father’s sister) too kept glancing at him, again and again. Perhaps she had never noticed before how beautiful her nephew was, or how young he still seemed. So when a neighbor woman from the next house came by and asked who Apu was, she replied with a touch of pride—“My nephew, from Nishchindipur, my cousin-brother’s son, very close to us by relation, but we don’t visit much, so!...” Then she looked at Apu again with those proud eyes. Her expression seemed to say—Look at my nephew, how he has the face of a young prince! Now you see what worth—what lineage—I come from!

After dusk, Kunj Chakraborty came home. Hawk-nosed, sharp-jawed, his age impossible to guess. Just as Apu had felt shy before his pisi, he now felt a kind of fear before his pishemoshai (uncle by marriage). He looked just like the cheerful Gurumoshai (teacher) Apu had studied with as a child. It seemed as if this man might say at any moment—You’re quite the mischievous boy, aren’t you?

The next morning, Apu got up and wandered a little along the village paths. All around, thick jungle, empty land—almost no soft grass, such was the wildness. Here stood one house, then a winding path through the woods, and far off, another house. Often, the path ran right across someone’s courtyard. He saw two or three children his own age playing, but they all stared at him, mouths agape, so intently that he could not even think of trying to speak to them—he couldn’t even look them in the face.

Even returning to his pisi’s house was a dilemma. Back home, at this hour, Ma would give him chira (flattened rice), muri (puffed rice), nadu (sweet coconut balls), or leftover rice. Would they give him such things here? Last night, at dinner, they had bought him sandesh (a sweet) to eat with his milk. If he went back now, so early, perhaps they would think—this boy is terribly greedy, always running home for food! Is it good to eat at someone’s house every day?... What should he do now? No, he would not go home yet. He would wander a little longer, and only return just before it was time for the midday meal. But in this unfamiliar place, where could he even stand and wait for so long?

Step by step, he finally found himself back at the house.

A girl of six or seven came into the yard, a brass bowl in her hand, and called out from the courtyard—“Nau (cook), have you finished cooking, Jethima (aunt)? Will you give me a little?”

Apu’s pisima (aunt) called out from inside the house—“Who is it, Ghulki? No, I’ll cook later, come back then…”

--- Page 180 ---
Gulki set down the bowl and stood by the edge of the veranda. Her hair was tousled, cropped short like a boy’s. She wore dirty clothes, no oil in her hair, her skin a dusky shade. Looking at Apu, she seemed to understand something, and suddenly let out a little giggle, then picked up the bowl and walked away.

Apu asked, “Whose pishima (aunt) is that girl?”

His pishi replied, “Who, Gulki? Her home isn’t here—she has no parents anywhere. Nibaran Mukherjee’s wife—the house next door—she’s a distant aunt of hers. Gulki stays there.”

The next day, a boy from the neighborhood befriended Apu and took him all around the village, showing him every lane and corner. On the way home, Apu saw that same orphan girl, Gulki, sitting alone by the roadside, legs stretched out, eating something. When she saw him, she hurried to gather up her anchal (the loose end of her sari)—inside it was a heap of half-ripe bakul fruit. By now, Apu had learned more about her from his pishima: Nibaran Mukherjee’s wife did not treat her well, and people said she wasn’t a good person. Pishima had said, “She’s not even really her aunt, just a distant relation. Sometimes she doesn’t even give her food for days—Gulki eats here and there, wherever she can. She has her own brood to feed—barely enough for them, let alone someone else!” Seeing Gulki, Apu didn’t feel shy at all—such a little girl, ah, with no one of her own! He felt a strong urge to befriend her. He went up and said, “What are you hiding in your anchal, let me see, khuki (little girl)?”

Suddenly, Gulki gathered up her anchal, let out a quick giggle, and darted away, bending low as she ran. Watching her antics, Apu couldn’t help but laugh. As she ran, the bakul fruits in her anchal tumbled out one by one. Picking them up as he went, Apu called out, “They fell, all your bakul fruits fell! Take them, khuki, I won’t say anything, here, khuki!”

But by then, Gulki had vanished.

After his bath in the pond, Apu was sitting quietly when he noticed Gulki peeking out from behind the back door, her face appearing for a moment before she hid again. When their eyes met, Gulki broke into a giggle. Apu stood up and said, “Wait, I’m going to catch you in one dash!” With that, he ran toward the back door.

Gulki didn’t look back, but darted straight down the path toward the pond. But how could she outrun Apu? Seeing no escape, she stopped, and as she did, Apu grabbed a handful of her wild, curly hair and said, “You were running so fast! Think you can outrun me, khuki?”

--- Page 181 ---
Gulki’s first fear was that perhaps he would hit her! But when Apu let go of her hair and burst out laughing, she understood—it was a game. She, too, laughed in that same way.

Apu felt a great tenderness. In her smile was a hint, a glimmer, that made Apu feel she wanted to be friends with him—to play with him, but being a child, unable to speak her heart, she expressed her wishes in this way—peeking and darting about, giggling, running away—this was how she showed what she wanted. She knew no other way. It was just like his didi (elder sister) at this age—just like this, tying up wild berries and bel fruit in the corner of her sari, wandering about lost in her own world, no one understanding, no one noticing, just as greedy, just as guileless—a little girl without a care!

Apu thought—no one plays with her, let me play with her a little. Ah, poor girl, orphaned of mother and father, wandering alone in her own world!—He let go of Gulki’s hair and took her hand, saying, “Will you play, little one? Come, let’s go to the edge of the pond. No, do one thing, little one, I’ll try to catch you—and you run to that jackfruit tree over there, old lady. Come—”

The moment he let go, Gulki didn’t stand still; she bent low and darted off again. Apu shouted, “Alright, go on, let’s see how far you get—I’ll catch you for sure, just watch! There you go—see now—” Saying this, he held his breath and took off running—chuuuuu! Gulki glanced back and, seeing Apu chasing her, tried with all her small strength to run as fast as she could—but Apu, after only a short sprint, caught her. “You’ve learned to run fast, haven’t you, little one? But can you beat me? Come, let’s play thief-and-watchman—you’ll be the thief—steal this jackfruit leaf and run away, understand?... And I’ll be the watchman, I’ll catch you.”

Gulki’s face could barely contain her smile—perhaps all this while, she had secretly wished to befriend this lovely boy. Nodding her head, she said in a reassuring tone, “Will you take kaibichi?” (a kind of wild berry)

Apu thought to himself, living in a farmer’s village, she’s learned to speak like this—just as the children of the goala (cowherd) or sadgop (farming) families speak in their villages.

--- Page 182 ---
At noon, when her pishima (father's elder brother's wife) called, Gulki trailed in behind her. Once Apu had finished eating, his pishi asked, “Will you have some rice, Gulki? Sit next to Apu’s plate—there’s mocha’r ghonto (a curry of banana blossoms)—I’m serving dal.” Apu thought, Ah, if I’d known she would eat, I’d have saved her two pieces of fish.

Without a word of protest, Gulki shamelessly sat down to eat. She asked for a large helping of rice, mashed it with dal, and then, after sitting for a long while, found she could not finish so much. She pushed the uneaten heap to the side of her plate. Still, she made no move to get up. Apu’s pishima laughed and said, “You don’t have to eat any more, Gulki—you’re gasping for breath—come, get up, just look how much rice you’ve wasted! You’re always hungry with your eyes, not your stomach.” Then she added, “Look at your jethima’s (father’s elder brother’s wife) ways—see how late it’s gotten—such a young girl—and she doesn’t even call her to eat? So what if she’s a stepmother—even so, she’s just a child…”

On Saturday, Apu went to offer puja at the temple of Siddheshwari. The acharya thakur (priest) had a long white beard flowing down his chest, quite an impressive figure. His widowed daughter came with him, arranging the puja offerings, helping her aged father in every way. The girl said, “Why only four poysha (small coins) for dakshina (offering), khoka (affectionate term for a boy)? That won’t do, for the weekly puja you need to give at least two anna (a larger coin).”

Apu replied, “But my mother only gave me four poysha, that’s all I have.”

The girl selected a few bananas and roots, wrapped them in a leaf, and handed them to him, saying, “Here’s the prasad (blessed food) from Thakur, and I’m giving you some bel leaves and sindur (vermilion) too—give them to the women at your house.” Apu thought, What good people they are. If I had more money, I’d have given another two poysha.

Returning to his pishima’s house, he sat on the outer veranda in the moonlight, chatting with her about the puja. Suddenly, from the neighboring Gulki’s house, Gulki’s thin voice rang out, shattering the sky—“Oh jethi (father’s elder brother’s wife), don’t beat me like that—oh baba re—oh jethi, my back is bleeding—don’t hit me, jethi—”

Immediately, a harsh voice responded—“You wretch—scoundrel—you went to the Chowdhurys’ house to eat at their invitation, and this is how your mouth runs? If I don’t burn your tongue with a ladle today—going around eating at other people’s houses, and those hundred-eyed women can’t see a thing, saying we don’t feed you at home—what a curse you are—don’t you get food at home?...Today I’ll—”

Apu’s pishima said, “See, do you hear how she’s taunting her with every word? If you speak the truth, people won’t keep relations with you anymore—then you’re the bad one…”

--- Page 183 ---
Apu’s heart was writhing with longing. Because his throat was choked with tears, no words could pass his lips.

The next day, a little before evening, after finishing his meal and other chores, Apu set out towards the cowshed quarter—Goyalapara. The day before, his uncle had arranged everything: from this village, a cart laden with tobacco would be going to Nawabganj. If he boarded it at dusk, they would let him off near Nischindipur by morning.

A short way down the road, at the bend leading to Bamunpara, he met Gulki. She was returning home after playing that evening. Apu said, “I’m going home today, Khuki—where were you all day? You didn’t come to play at all—” Then, seeing Gulki’s disbelieving smile, he added, “It’s true, I swear, look—here’s my bundle. I’m going to Kartik the cowherd’s house to catch the cart—won’t you come a little way with me?”

Gulki followed behind him for quite a distance. They left Bamunpara behind, crossed a stretch of open field. Beyond that lay Goyalapara. Gulki came as far as the edge of the field. Pointing at Apu’s red satin shirt, she asked, “How much did your anga-jama cost?”

Apu replied with a smile, “Two rupees—do you want it?”

Gulki burst out laughing. Meaning, if you give it, I’ll take it right now...

Suddenly, as Apu glanced ahead, he saw that at the far end of the field, light was breaking through the trees—at once, he wondered where, how far away, they would be on a day like this next month! Then he said to Gulki, “Don’t come any further, Khuki, you go back now—you’ve come a long way already—your folks might scold you—go on, Khuki—if you come again, we’ll meet, all right? Maybe I won’t come back, we’re going to Kashi in the month of Boishakh, we’ll live there—” Gulki giggled again.

That day was either the full moon or the fourteenth night—some such tithi. He had never come this way before, but for many days, the image of this first solitary childhood journey into the unknown lingered in his mind—a straight path across the field, the full moon (or perhaps the fourteenth night’s moon, he couldn’t quite recall) rising through the gap in the distant trees, and behind him, the little girl with wild hair—an orphan, innocent, barely known to him—had come to see him off.

Pather Panchali

Pather Panchali, Chapter Twenty-Nine

--- Page 184 ---
At the beginning of the month of Boishakh, Harihar made all the arrangements to move out of Nishchindipur. The things that could not be taken along, he sold off, using the money to pay off various small debts. In those days, the house had many large jackfruit-wood bedsteads, chests, and low stools; hearing the news, buyers even came from the next neighborhood and bought them up at low prices.

The elders of the village came and tried to dissuade Harihar. They recited, from memory, a comparative list of how cheap the milk and fish of Nishchindipur were, and how little it cost to run a household here. Only Rajkrishna Bhattacharya, who had come to invite them for a feast on the occasion of his wife’s Savitri Brata (a ritual fast), after much conversation, said, “Bapu, what is left in this land that I should ask you to stay? Besides, there’s no use sinking your roots into the same patch of mud forever—I know this from my own life. The mind shrinks, the doors of the heart close. This time, I hope to make a pilgrimage to Chandranath, if God grants me the days.”

Rani, having heard the news, came to Apu’s house. She said to Apu, “Is it true, Apu, that you all are leaving this village? Really?”

Apu replied, “It’s true, Ranudi. Ask Ma if you want.”

Still, Rani could not believe it. At last, hearing everything from Sarbajaya’s own lips, Rani was left astonished. She called Apu out to the courtyard and asked, “When will you go?”

––“The Wednesday after next Wednesday.”

––“Will you never come back again?”

Rani’s eyes filled with tears as she said, “You always say, Nishchindipur is our beautiful village—where else is there such a river, such fields? How can you leave this place?”

Apu said, “What can I do? It wasn’t my idea to leave. Baba wants to live there; we can’t manage here anymore. I’ll leave my writing notebook with you, Ranudi. Maybe, when we’re grown, we’ll meet again.”

Rani said, “But you haven’t finished the story in my notebook, nor signed your name in it. Are you a good boy, Apu?”

Holding back her tears, Rani hurried out of the house. Apu could not understand why Ranudi was angry for no reason! Was he leaving the village by his own choice?

At the bathing ghat, Apu had a long talk with Potu. Potu hadn’t known either; hearing it all from Apu, his heart sank. With a pale face, he said, “For you, I waded into the water myself, scraped away the moss with so much trouble to clear the steps—won’t you fish there even one more day?”

--- Page 185 ---
Here is your requested translation, preserving the lyrical, evocative style and all content, with Bengali and cultural terms retained and briefly explained where needed:

---

This year, Ramnavami’s Dol festival, Charak Puja, and Goshtobihar all came one after another, in quick succession. Every year, this season fills Apu’s heart with an extraordinary, unrestrained joy. He and his didi (elder sister) would forsake food and sleep at this time. Of course, from Apu’s side, there was no shortcoming this year either.

On the day of Charak, the village’s Aturi budi (old Aturi woman) passed away. The new field where the Charak fair is held these days—right beside it stood Aturi budi’s little two-roofed hut. Seeing the crowd that had gathered, he too went there to see. He remembered how, once, he had dashed through the bamboo grove in terror of the witch Aturi—he’d been small then—and now, thinking of it, he felt like laughing. Today it seemed to him that Aturi budi was no witch, nothing at all. She lived alone on the edge of the village, outside the settlement—poor, helpless, with no son, no daughter, no one to look after her. If there had been anyone, would she have lain dead in her hut all day, with no one to perform her last rites? Panchu the fisherman’s son brought out a pot and tipped it over outside—a potful of dried mango slices, amchur. The old woman used to gather storm-fallen mangoes, make them into tangy amchur, and sell them in the markets to get by. Apu knew this, for he had seen her last year at the Rath fair, sitting with her basket, selling amchur.

The Charak festival felt strangely empty this year. In other years, at the Charak market, didi would buy new painted scrolls and rejoice so much. He remembered how that morning he’d quarreled with his didi. In the afternoon, his didi had said—“I’ll give you money, Apu, if you see a Sita-haran (abduction of Sita) scroll at the fair, will you get it?” Apu, still seeking revenge, had said—“You always want those insipid, silly scrolls, you’ll have to buy them yourself, I can’t—why not get a Ram-Ravana battle scene instead?” His didi replied—“You’re always about battles and fighting—such a boy you are! Aren’t scrolls of gods and goddesses beautiful too?”...Apu had never had much respect for didi’s artistic sensibilities.

When the rajnigandha (tuberose) flowers bloomed red along their fence, her face would come to mind; in the calls of birds, in the swaying of freshly blossomed orkalam (a local wildflower)—his heart would ache for didi. It seemed the one who would be happy if he ran to her and told her these things—where had she gone, how far away!...And never, never again—would she come to play these games with him...

--- Page 186 ---
Amidst the clamor of the fair, who is playing such a wonderful flute? The new melody delights him greatly—he searched and found—the flute-seller Haran Mal from Malpara has brought a bundle of bamboo flutes to sell, and as advertisement, he is playing one himself. Apu asked, “How much for one?” Haran Mal knows him well. How many times has he passed by their kitchen, covered in ash. He asked, “So I heard, you folks are leaving the village, khoka (little one)? Where are you off to—ha, go?”

Apu bought a slender bamboo flute for one and a half paisa. He said, “Which holes do I press my fingers on, Haran-kaka (uncle Haran)? Will you show me once?”

He remembers, once, waking in the deep of night, he lay awake for a while. Far away, on the river, in the darkness, the fishermen’s lights flickered, and the monotonous, continuous thok-thok of their fishing nets echoed. At that moment, he heard, from far off—perhaps along the path by the kuthir field—someone walking late at night, singing aloud as they went. Seldom did anyone walk that field path so late, yet, half-asleep, how many nights had he heard, in the moonlight of midnight, the broken strains of a stranger’s song, the sweet notes of Madhukan’s verses drifting from far to farther away—yet what he heard that time was entirely new. He could never master that tune—in the haze between sleeping and waking, the goddess of melody, graceful and elusive, slipped away down the path between dreams, never to be found again—yet could Apu ever forget her?

After the Charak festival, children of farmers from many villages, dressed in bright clothes, some in new, crisp saris, walked home in lines. The boys played their flutes as they went. People had come even from four or five krosh (ancient measure of distance) away to see the fair at Goshtibihar. Shola birds, wooden dolls, paper fans, painted pots, clay toys—everyone had something in hand. The sweet vendor, the Vaishnava from Chinibas, had opened a stall selling purple-hued fuluri (fried snacks); from his shop, Apu bought two paisa’s worth of oil-fried treats, and, holding them in his hand, set off for home. As he walked, he wondered, would there be such a Goshtibihar where they were going? Perhaps he would never see the Charak fair again! He thought—if there’s no Charak there, I’ll tell Baba, “I want to see the fair, Baba, let’s go to Nishchindipur—if nothing else, we can stay at Khurima’s (aunt’s) house for a couple of days!”

The day after Charak, the packing and tying up of belongings began. Tomorrow, after lunch, they would have to set out.

--- Page 187 ---
At dusk, on the kitchen veranda, his mother was frying hot, puffed parathas for him. The leaves of the coconut tree in Neelmoni jetha’s (elder uncle’s) yard shimmered in the moonlight—seeing this, Apu’s heart filled with sorrow. All these days, he had been so eager to go to the new place, but the closer the day of departure drew, the more his heartstrings resonated with the deep ache of impending separation.

This was their home—the house, the bamboo grove, the Salte-Khagir mango orchard, the riverbank, the very spot where he and didi (elder sister) had their little picnics—how dearly he loved it all! Would there be such a coconut tree where they were going? For as long as he could remember, he had seen this coconut tree here, its leaves so beautiful on moonlit nights! On bright moonlit nights, sitting on this veranda, gazing at the moon-drenched coconut fronds, how many nights had he played “ten-twenty-five” with didi, how many times had he thought what a wonderful land this Nishchindipur of theirs was! Where they were headed, would there be a coconut tree by the kitchen veranda, by the edge of the woods, just like this? Would he be able to catch fish there, to gather mangoes, to row boats, to play at trains, would there be a ghat (steps down to water) like the one at Kadamtala’s Saheb’s ghat? Would Ranudi be there? Would there be the fields of Sonadangar? They were doing just fine here—why leave all this behind for nothing?

Something happened at noon.

His mother had gone to a Sabitri Brata (a ritual fast and gathering for married women) invitation, Harihar was in the next room, having finished his meal and now asleep, and Apu was rummaging through the things on the shelves inside the house, seeing what he could and couldn’t take. As he tried to move a clay pot from the high shelf, something rolled out from inside and fell to the floor. He picked it up and turned it over in his hands, astonished. Even though it was covered in dust and cobwebs, there was no doubt left in his mind about what it was or its history.

It was that little golden box, the very one that had been stolen from Sej Thakrun’s (second eldest grandmother’s) house last year.

No one was home at noon; standing there with the box in his hand, he lingered for a long time, lost in thought. In the scorching, sun-drenched stillness of the Boishakh (Bengali month of April-May) afternoon, the whispering of the bamboo grove sounded in his ears like a message from far away. To himself he murmured—Didi, poor thing, had stolen it and hidden it inside that pot!

--- Page 188 ---
He paused for a moment, then slowly walked over to stand by the backdoor—the bamboo grove stretched far into the distance, dozing in the midday sun, while that shankhachil (brahminy kite) was crying out, tugging at the top of some tree, and in the hidden Dipayan Lake, the sorrowful, tragic noon of that ancient, defeated, ill-fated prince seemed to linger! Standing there for a little while, he took the tin box in his hand, gave it a sharp shake, and flung it deep into the dense bamboo forest. 

His didi (elder sister) called out to Bhulo, the stray dog, who came panting and running through the thick undergrowth. The box rolled and tumbled, coming to rest somewhere by the pile of dry bamboo and leaves, near the edge of the baichi-bush (wild berry thicket), right beside where Bhulo had stopped.

He thought to himself—It’ll stay there, no one will ever know anything, who else would go there?

Apu never told anyone about the golden box, never—not even his mother.

As the afternoon wore on, Hiru the cart-driver set off with his bullock cart. There had been a few clouds in the sky that morning, but before ten o’clock they had all drifted away, and now the full, abundant midday sun of Boishakh (the first month of the Bengali calendar, peak of summer) was pouring down on the trees, the fields, the road, as if raining fire. Patu had followed the cart for quite a distance, and said—Apuda (elder brother Apu), this time a really good jatra (folk theater) troupe has been booked for the Barowari (community festival), didn’t you hear about it?

Apu replied—You take an extra copy of the pala (theater script), send it to me, will you?

Again, the road ran along the edge of that same Chataker Maath (the field of the chatak bird)! All across the field, as remnants of the fair, the shells of cut green coconuts rolled about; someone had cooked and eaten at one end of the field, blackened lumps of earth from the fire and a soot-stained new pot lay discarded to one side. Harihar sat silently, feeling a strange unease. Was this the right thing to do? The ancestral homestead of so many years, on the other side the ruined house where all the old festivities had already faded away—what little lamp of clay still flickered there, tonight, from dusk, it would be extinguished forever. What would his father, Ramchand Tarkabagish, think, watching from heaven?

--- Page 189 ---
The last house of the village is that two-roofed hut of Aturi budi (old woman Aturi); as long as it was in sight, Apu stared open-mouthed in that direction. Then, passing by a large grove of date palms, the cart climbed up onto the embankment road that led straight out to Asharu (the world beyond the village). As soon as the village ended, Sarbajaya felt as if all the poverty, all the humiliation, all the deprivation had been left behind—now ahead lay only a new household, a new way of life, newfound prosperity!...

Gradually, sunlight spread—the cart was now passing through the fields of Sonadangar. Harihar pointed to a large banyan tree in the middle of the fields and said, “Look there, didi, that’s the banyan tree by Thakurjhi’s pond.” Sarbajaya quickly leaned out to see. Not far from the road, on the edge of a low-lying field, the immense banyan tree sat, its aerial roots hanging all around. She had heard the story of that old Brahmin and his young son so many times. Fifty years ago, her father-in-law’s ancestors, at just such an evening hour, had cruelly murdered that innocent Brahmin and his helpless son beneath that very banyan tree, out of greed for money, and buried them in the adjacent lowland—which, in those days, was Thakurjhi’s pond. Perhaps the boy’s mother had waited in vain for months, for years, hoping her son would return home, but he never did—oh, Ma! Suddenly Sarbajaya’s eyes blurred with tears, something caught in her throat.

--- Page 190 ---
The field of Sonadanga is the largest in this region. Here and there, thickets of wild shrubs, shimul and babul trees, date palms heavy with hanging clusters of dates, the sōndali flowers swaying in the breeze, and all around, the call of the bou-kotha-kao bird, the papia, echoes. Over the vast expanse of the field, the deep blue sky—like the color of linseed flowers—has toppled down, pressing close, with nothing to block the gaze. Amidst the undulating field, wrapped in dense green grass, there is no sign of cultivation—only an abundance of trees and wild groves, and the tranquil expanse of the great field. Ahead, the wide earthen path winds on, like a wandering, world-weary baul, drawing its own meandering course into the distance.

A little further on lies the Barase-Madhukhali bil, a great wetland. Some ancient river, dried up long ago, has left its footprints on the journey of life; the vast channel of the vanished river is now a lake filled with blooming lotuses. Apu sat in the cart, watching the field and the exquisite colors of the sky all around. In the dreamscape of late afternoon, how many childhood fancies come and go! Here he is, leaving the village behind! Who knows how far he will travel now—his journey has only just begun, and perhaps now he will see those lands, that wondrous life glimpsed in dreams!

Harihar pointed out a distant village with his finger and said, “That is Dhonche-Polashgachi, and just beyond it is Natabere. There, under the banyan at the shrine of Bonbibi, there’s a grand fair in the month of Shraban—nowhere else will you find pumpkins so cheap!”

At the ferry below Ashadhu Bazaar, as they crossed the Betroboti river, the moon rose, its light making the water shimmer and gleam. Today is the market day at Ashadhu; a few market-goers, chattering among themselves, are coming across from the far bank in the ferry-boat. Apu and the cart crossed over to the other side. Apu, asking his father’s permission, got down to see the Ashadhu Bazaar. A small market, rows of shuttered stalls; from the sugarcane vendor’s shop, bits of conversation could be heard; in front of a wholesale date-molasses shop, a crowd of bullock carts had gathered.

Majherpara station was still nearly four krosh away; though the road was unpaved, it was quite wide, lined on both sides with banyan, peepul, and mulberry trees, planted in the days of the indigo-planter sahibs. At the start of the month of Boishakh, somewhere among the branches of the roadside banyan and peepul, the koel calls and calls, filling the air. The whole way is lined with ancient banyans, their aerial roots swaying. The clusters of tender new leaves, bathed in moonlight, appear almost transparent.

--- Page 191 ---
In the fields, forests, and gardens of Bengal during Basanta (spring), Chaitra, and Baisakh, everywhere and anywhere, in the disordered calls of the koel, in the drooping branches of the nagkeshar tree weighed down with countless blossoms, in the southern breeze heavy with the scent of wildflowers and bathed in moonlight—an ecstatic dance of joy has begun. Never before in his life had Apu witnessed such an exquisite vision of spring. Even at this tender age, the enchanting image of Bengal’s fields, rivers, and the moonlit solitude of forest edges had been etched into his heart; it was this very magic that would, in later years, become the richest source of sweetness and inspiration for the moments of imagination in his life as an artist.

It was around ten at night when the train finally arrived at the station. For a long while now, Apu had been sitting there, waiting in hope for the train’s arrival; as soon as it stopped, he jumped down and dashed to the platform. The eight-thirty evening train had long since departed—he had learned from his father that there would be no more trains that night. All this had happened because of those two bullocks belonging to Hiru the cart-driver; otherwise, he would have seen the train already. On the platform, a heap of tobacco bundles was laid out—two railway men were pressing the bundles with a contraption that looked like an iron box but had a very long handle; what they were doing, Apu could not tell. The moonlight fell on the railway tracks, making them glisten. Over there, by the side of the tracks, two red lights shone on a tall post; on this side, two more red lights, just the same. In the station office, a four-legged oil lantern burned on the table. A pile of bound registers lay there. Apu went and stood near the door, watching for a while; the station master was tapping away at a little thing that looked like a wooden sandal, making a clacking sound.

Istrishan! Istrishan! Not much longer now—tomorrow morning, not only will he see the train, he’ll ride it too!...

He could hardly tear himself away from the platform. But his father came to call him. That little thing like a wooden sandal, his father told him, was the telegraph key.

Apu turned and saw that, by the station pond, preparations were underway for cooking dinner. Another cart had been standing there since earlier. Among the passengers were a young woman of about eighteen or nineteen and a youth. Apu heard that the woman was from the Biswas family of Habibpur, returning to her father’s house with her brother. She had quickly struck up a friendship with Apu’s mother. His mother was washing rice and lentils for khichuri, while the young woman was peeling potatoes. They would cook the meal together.

--- Page 192 ---
The train arrived at half past seven in the morning. For a long while, Apu had been standing at the edge of the platform, mouth agape, craning to catch sight of the train. His father said, “Khoka, don’t lean out so far, come back this way.” A khalasi (railway porter) was also shooing people back.

How enormous the train is! What a terrifying sound! Is that thing in front called the engine? Oh, what a marvel!

The wife from Habibpur, her ghunghat (veil) drawn back, gazed with curiosity at the approaching train.

There was a great commotion as all the luggage and bundles were loaded onto the carriage. The wooden benches were arranged facing each other. The floor of the carriage felt as if it were made of cement. It was just like a room, with windows and doors and everything, exactly so! This heavy carriage, which had come to a halt—how could it possibly move again? Apu could hardly believe it. Who knows, maybe it wouldn’t move at all, maybe they would suddenly say, “Ogo, all of you get down, our train won’t run today.” On the other side of the wire fence, a man stood with a bundle of ulughash (wild grass) on his head, waiting for the train to pass. To Apu, the man seemed an object of pity! On a day like this, if one hasn’t ridden the train, how could one go on living? Hiru the carter stood outside the gate, gazing at the train.

The train began to move. Strange, marvelous swaying! In the blink of an eye, Majherpara Station, the crowds, the bundles of tobacco, Hiru the carter standing with his mouth agape—everything was left behind as the train sped out into the fields of ulukhud (reeds and wild grass). The trees and plants flashed by, racing past the windows on both sides—what speed! This is what they call a railway train! Oh, the whole field seems to be spinning! Bushes and trees, thatched roofs of ulukhud, the small huts of the farmers—all whirled together in a blur! Beneath the carriage, there was a ceaseless grinding sound, like a millstone turning—and up ahead, what a noise the engine made!

The distant signal of Majherpara Station was slowly fading away!...

That day, so long ago!

That day when he and didi, searching for the calf, had raced breathlessly across fields and marshes to catch a glimpse of the railway track! That day—and now?

There, where beneath the sky the line of trees along the monsoon-washed Durgapur road receded farther and farther into the distance, where the path from their village curved and rose into the Sonadanga fields—right at that very bend in the road, at the old jamun tree at the edge of the village, it seemed to Apu that his didi stood, face pale, gazing after their departing train...

--- Page 193 ---
No one had come to bring her back; everyone had left her behind. Even after didi (elder sister) passed away, on the paths and byways where they played, in the bamboo groves, beneath the mango trees, he had felt her presence close by all this time. The invisible caress of didi’s affection lingered in every corner of the crumbling ancestral house of Nishchindipur— but today, truly and irrevocably, he had parted from didi forever!...

It seemed to him that no one else had ever loved didi, not Ma, not anyone! No one was sorrowful for leaving her behind.

Suddenly, Apu’s heart filled with a strange sensation. It was not sorrow, not grief, not longing—he did not know what it was. So many things flashed through his mind in that brief moment... Aturi the witch... the river’s ghat... their old house... the path beneath the wild olive trees... Ranudi... so many evenings, so many afternoons... so many days of laughter and play... Patu... all of didi’s unfulfilled wishes...

Didi was still gazing at him, unblinking—

And then, the astonished language of his heart seemed to well up in tears, as if to say, again and again— I didn’t want this, didi, I haven’t forgotten you, I didn’t leave you behind on purpose— they are taking me away—

Truly, he had not forgotten.

In later life, he would come to know intimately the blue, jewel-girdled earth and the wide, cloud-draped sky. But whenever the thrill of movement sent a shiver through his whole being, whenever from the deck of a seafaring ship he beheld the ever-changing magic of the blue heavens, when perhaps a vine-clad mountain peak faded into the distant, dissolving horizon of the sea, when the faint, shadowy outline of a far-off shore conjured a sweet, enchanting music in his imaginative mind—at those very moments, always, he would remember: a night of heavy monsoon rain, the ceaseless patter of drops, a dark room in an old house, and a poor village girl lying sick in bed—

—Apu, when I get better, will you take me to see the train, just once?

Watching the distant signal at Majherpara station, he saw it grow fainter and fainter, until at last it disappeared.

**(From "Pather Panchali," Chapter Thirty)**

--- Page 194 ---
After noon, they had to change trains at Ranaghat station. Even though twice coal dust had fallen into Apu’s eyes, he kept leaning out the train window, gazing all day at the world outside. At every station—what do they call those things? Signals? Why do they rise and fall? Where the train stops, the ground is built up with bricks, raised like a porch. Is that called a platform? On the wooden boards, the names of all the stations are written in big letters, in both English and Bengali—Kurulgachhi, Govindpur, Banpur. When the train is about to leave, a bell rings—dhong dhong dhong dhong—four strokes—Apu counted them. There’s a big iron wheel with handles all around it; when that is turned, the signal drops—Apu noticed this at Kurulgachhi station.

This was only the second time Sarbajaya had ever ridden the train. The first time was long ago—back when she had just come from new Kashi and settled down to make a home in the country. It was in the month of Jaishtha, when she had gone to Aranghata to see Jugalkishore Thakur. That feels like another lifetime. Today, she watched with delight as people got on and off at each station—bouthans (sisters-in-law) boarding and alighting—what faces they had, what clothes, what ornaments! At Jagannathpur station, seeing a vendor selling good muri’r moa (puffed rice and jaggery sweets), she said to her son, “Apu, would you like some muri’r moa? You love them, don’t you? Shall I get some for you?” On the telegraph wires at the station, birds were swinging and swaying. Apu pointed and said, “Look, Ma, a mynah bird has escaped from someone’s cage!”

At Naihati station, they changed trains again, and as they crossed the great bridge over the Ganga, the sun was setting. Sarbajaya gazed, transfixed—the wind was rushing in from the far bank, boats drifted on the river, and on both sides, beautiful houses and gardens lined the shore. She had never seen such sights in her life. She pointed them out to her son: “Apu, do you see, a steamship?” Then, pressing her joined fingers to her forehead, she murmured to herself, “Mother Ganga, we are crossing over you—please forgive us, Ma. When we reach Kashi, I will worship you with flowers and bilva leaves. Keep Apu safe. Let the purpose for which we go be fulfilled. Grant us shelter there, Ma—”

--- Page 195 ---
With joy and trembling, with the mystery of uncertainty, his heart swayed—never before had he felt such a mood. Whether in comfort or in hardship, the delight of an unfettered, free life—he tasted it now for the very first time. In his small, fenced-in world, bounded by the bamboo groves of his village, he had never witnessed such a living panorama, such a novel rush of movement, such an intimate acquaintance with the thrill of uncertainty. The life that had always built walls and fences around itself, making itself smaller and smaller, was now moving, moving, moving forward—towards that setting sun in the western sky—crossing rivers and lands, near and far—racing ahead. The reality of this ceaseless journey, he felt today with every beat of his heart!

Was it not just the other day, only a year ago, that lying awake at night in the house at Nischindipur, whenever he thought, “If only there were a chance, I’d go to Gangasnan at Chakdah or Kaliganj”—even that had seemed something far beyond the realm of possibility or certainty. And now?

Just before their train arrived at Bandel station, a great train thundered past on the main line ahead, roaring like a storm. Apu gazed at it in wonder. What a sound!—Uff! Reaching Bandel station, they got down from the train. Engines were running this way and that, huge freight trains shook the station as they rumbled by every five minutes without stopping. The clamor!—Here, the engine’s whistle rang so loud it seemed to pierce the ears; there, another passenger train was pulling out, the guard waving his green flag. At dusk, signals clustered east and west along the tracks—red and green lights blinking—rail, engines, trains, people!

A little later in the night, the train for Kashi arrived with a deafening roar and halted at the platform. The vast station, the immense crowd—Sarbajaya felt utterly bewildered. Flustered, unaccustomed, her feet stiff with anxiety, she followed her husband to the door of a carriage. Harihar, with great difficulty, pushed through the relentless crowd and, with much effort, managed to seat his bewildered wife and disoriented son on a bench in the train, then, with the help of a porter, loaded their bundles and bags aboard.

--- Page 196 ---
Toward dawn, Sarbajaya’s drowsiness fled, startled away. The train was rushing forward with the speed of a storm—fields, earth, trees and foliage all blurring together in its flight—as if, being a night train, they had all boarded the same carriage together. Harihar had not put her in the women’s compartment. The carriage was less crowded than before—on each bench, a single person lay stretched out, sleeping deeply. On the upper bench, a Kabuliwala (an Afghan trader) was snoring loudly. Apu, already awake, had his mouth open, head stuck out the window, staring fixedly outside.

Harihar, having woken up, said to his son, “Don’t stare outside like that, khoka (little one), coal dust will get in your eyes any moment now—”

--- Page 197 ---
Coal dust is such a harmless thing, yet even if someone were to pluck out both his eyes and carry them away, still Apu would not have the power to turn his gaze from the window now. He sits like this, motionless, for almost the whole night. Father and mother are asleep—oh, how much he has seen! At so many stations the train did not stop; the station, with all its lights and people, would suddenly whoosh past, slipping by like a firework in the night—at some point, drowsiness had crept over him, but when he awoke with a start, he poked his face out the window and saw: in the deep moonlit night, the train was hurtling across a small bridge over some river—a tall, dark mound loomed ahead, atop which grew many trees and bushes. The moonlight shimmered on the river’s water, white clouds drifted across the sky—then came more of those large mounds, more trees just like before. After that, a big station, people, lights—a train stood waiting on the next track—a quarrel broke out between a panwala (betel-leaf seller) and another man! There was a large clock at the station—he had learned from his master, Nirenbabu, how to read the time by the trains; counting carefully, he saw it was twenty-two minutes past three in the night. Then the train started again—more trees, more of those tall mounds—often, on either side of the railway line, those same kinds of mounds—everyone in the carriage was asleep; if they are not going to see anything, then why did they even board the train! Whom could he ask what all those mounds were? Now and then, he leaned out the window, craning his neck to try and judge how fast the train was going—his hair whipped across his face in the wind, the ground was invisible, as if someone were drawing straight lines across the earth—oh! How fast the train goes! In curiosity and excitement, he stuck his head out first from this window, then that, peering out to see.

From time to time, as he gazed eastward at the swiftly vanishing, moonlit fields, it seemed to him—how far they had come! Through what strange country of undulating fields were they traveling?

Toward morning, he had dozed off again, but when the train thundered to a halt at a huge station, his drowsiness vanished—the name was written on a stone plaque on the platform—Patna City.

--- Page 198 ---
After that, so many stations passed by. What enormous bridges! The train kept moving, moving, it seemed as if the bridge would never end—so many kinds of signals, so many mills and factories, and inside the room of some station, on the iron pillar, a trumpet-like device—through it, a railway babu was speaking—Private number?... Yes, alright—sixty-nine—sixty-nine—yes?... Sixty-nine... six with a nine on its back—yes—yes—

Apu asked his father in astonishment, “What is that, Baba? Why is he speaking into it like that?”

By then, it was quite late in the day. Just then, Harihar said, “Now we’re about to reach Kashi. Keep looking to the left—once the train climbs onto the bridge over the Ganga, you’ll see Kashi—”

Apu had been pondering something for a long while. All along the journey, he had been watching the telegraph wires and poles—never before in his life had he looked at them so intently, except that one time. If he ever got the chance to play his railway game again, he would set up poles just like those. How mistaken he had been before! In the place they were going, would there be gulanch-lata vines (a kind of creeper) in the woods?

About fifteen days passed. In a medium-sized three-story house in Banshfotka Lane, Harihar had rented a ground-floor flat. He could not find any trace of old acquaintances. Those who used to live in certain places before, no one could say where they were now. Only the old haluikar (sweetmaker) Ramgopal Sahu of Vishweshwar Lane was still alive.

On the upper floor of the house lived a Punjabi family, and on the middle floor, a Bengali businessman. The outer room was his shop and warehouse—the surrounding two or three rooms were his kitchen and bedroom.

In these five or six days, Sarbajaya had gone around with her husband to see all the nearby places. Never even in her dreams had she imagined such sights—such temples! Such gods and goddesses! So many houses! Until now, the temple of Jugalkishore at Aranghata had seemed to her the pinnacle of architectural art—but the temple of Vishwanath?—the temple of Annapurna? The red-stone temples above Dashashwamedh Ghat?

--- Page 199 ---
One evening, she had gone with the Punjabi gentleman’s wife to witness the arati at Vishwanath’s temple—what an experience it was, she could never put it into words. The temple grew dark with the smoke of incense and burning dhuno—seven or eight priests began chanting mantras together—the crowd, the grandeur, the throng of women from the grandest households who had come to see, the splendor of their attire! A queen from somewhere had arrived—accompanied by four or five maidservants. She wore an expensive Banarasi sari, the golden-embroidered anchal (the loose end of the sari) blazing like fire in the light of the arati’s five lamps—those long, almond-shaped eyes—those eyebrows, that face—she had never seen a real queen before, only heard of them in stories—yes, beauty fit for a queen indeed! She could not tell whether she spent longer gazing at her, or at the deity’s arati.

And what were the homesteads themselves, if not temples of the gods!… Invited for Durga Puja, she had once gone to the Ganguli house in Nishchindipur, and seeing their natmandir (temple pavilion), their two-storied house, the stone-paved bathing ghat by the pond, she had felt such envy in her heart—she remembered once telling Durga—“Have you seen the prosperity of the rich folks’ homes?”—But now, the houses she saw lining the roads on either side—compared to them, even the Ganguli house…

Never before had she seen so many carriages and horses together. And the kinds of carriages! On the day they arrived, she had seen horse-drawn carriages at Ranaghat and Naihati, but never so many kinds as these. Even the two-wheeled carts—how many there were!… She longed to stand by the roadside for a while, just to watch them all pass by—but the Punjabi lady was always with her, and out of shyness, she could not.

Apu, meanwhile, was utterly astonished. He could never have imagined such sights. Their lodgings were not far from Dashashwamedh Ghat, and every afternoon he went there to wander. Every day, it seemed as if a fair was in full swing. Here, music played; there, people conversed; over yonder, someone recited the Ramayana. Crowds of people, smiling faces, festivity everywhere—Apu simply wandered from place to place, taking it all in, and after dusk, he would return home and recount everything with great excitement.

--- Page 200 ---
The servant of the Kahas ties a rope around the waist of a small boy and brings him out for a walk every day. Apu has decided—his name is Poltu, he cannot speak properly, is terribly restless, so the family, fearing he might get lost, has made this arrangement as if he were a jailbird. Apu laughs quietly. He had requested the servant to untie the rope, but the man, out of fear, refuses. The prisoner is utterly small and innocent—he has no idea that such treatment deserves protest. In fact, Sarbajaya scolds him every day—“Why do you go off alone like that? This is a city, a marketplace—what if you lose your way?” That his mother’s fears are entirely unfounded, he tries to assure her twice a day with a wave of his hand and persistent explanations.

After coming to Kashi, Harihar’s income increased. Roaming about here and there, he managed to secure the job of daily reciting the Puranas at several temples. Besides that, one day Sarbajaya said to her husband, “Why don’t you sit at Dashashwamedh Ghat every afternoon with your manuscript? So many people make money with their tricks, and you just sit around making plans—”

Prodded by his wife, Harihar takes his copy of the Kashikhanda and sits at Dashashwamedh Ghat in the evenings. Reciting the Puranas is no new business for him; back in the village, he had done this many times at disciples’ houses on the occasion of various vows and festivals. Opening the manuscript, he begins to sing the hymns in his melodious voice—

barhapīḍābhirāmaṃ mṛgamadatilakaṃ kuṇḍalākrāntagandam...

...smita-subhaga-mukham svādhare nyasta reṇum...

...brahma-gopāla-veśam...

The crowd is not insignificant.

Back at home, he writes something on a piece of sandpaper. He says to his wife, “If you just recite shlokas, no one wants to listen—look, that Bangal (a Bengali from East Bengal) storyteller draws a bigger crowd than I do—I’m thinking of writing a few performances, with songs, and some storytelling too; otherwise, people won’t gather. Met the Bangal the day before yesterday—he can’t even read Devanagari, just makes up rhymes to amuse girls and collects money... I pick up a tray and get six or eight annas, but he never gets less than a rupee... Want to hear a bit of what I’ve written?”

He reads out a portion. “I’m thinking of copying the forest description from that storyteller’s manuscript—will you get it for me?”

“Tell me, where do you sit and tell your stories? I must come listen one day—”

“No, don’t come. I sit just below the Shasthi temple—come tomorrow, I’ll perform the new piece; tomorrow is Ekadashi, it’s an auspicious day—”

--- Page 201 ---
––On your way back, bring four-paise worth of panpholer jilipi from Bishweshwar’s alley shop for Apu, will you?––That day, the Khotta bou* upstairs called me over after her puja and gave me water to drink. She said, “Panpholer jilipi, you can get it in Bishweshwar’s alley.” As I tasted it, I thought, Apu loves jilipi so much––but she only gave me water, so what could I say?––Bring some today, four-paise worth.

[*Khotta bou: the upstairs neighbor, married woman from a particular community.]

For the past few days, quite a crowd has been gathering to listen to Harihar’s storytelling. In a large barqosh* basket, the maid from the Naradghat Kali temple brought down a big, straight coconut to Apu’s veranda. Sarbajaya smiled and said, “Is it the bar’s puja today? You’ve come home, have you, jhi*?” When the maid left, she called her son, “Come here, Apu––look, your favorite coconut shell! Do you like it? You’ve seen raisins, bananas, such big mangoes––come, eat, I’ll give you––sit here––”

[*barqosh: a large, woven bamboo basket. *jhi: maidservant.]

Encouraged, Harihar brought out his old bundles of notebooks again. Sarbajaya said, “People’s ears are worn out from hearing that same Baichifol* story, can’t you try something new?”

[*Baichifol: a traditional folk tale.]

All morning and afternoon, Harihar sat absorbed, finishing the tale of Jarabharata* in the form of a storytelling performance. He remembered, it was here in Kashi, twenty-two years ago, that he sat translating the verses of Gita Govinda, when he was just twenty-four. Returning home, the purpose of his life seemed to reveal itself to him more clearly. In Kashi, there was not so much of this––but back in the village, surrounded by the songs of Dashuray, Dewanji’s songs, the duels of Govinda Adhikari’s Shuk-Sari*, the widespread tradition and popularity of Loka the washerman’s group’s Mati Juri songs, all these cast a new kind of spell on his mind.

[*Jarabharata: a character from Indian epic tales. *Shuk-Sari: traditional folk song duels, named after the parrot and the mynah.]

At night, he would tell his wife stories––“Do you know, the kabir’s* songs are being sung at the market’s barowari*? I sat and listened, you know?…Such simple verses, nothing at all, just wait, let me settle the household a bit, get well––I’ll compose a new kind of performance––they all sing those ancient songs from Mandhata’s time––that’s what I was telling Raju yesterday.”

[*kabir: folk singer or bard. *barowari: community event, often for worship or performance.]

Some nights, Harihar would get up very late and sit outside on the veranda, lost in thought, his mind buoyant as a feather with some vague, indefinable joy.

What a radiant future lay before him!...

--- Page 202 ---
In the wavering light of the hurricane lamp, he sees before him a grand gathering—across the land, from village to village, his rhymes, songs, Shyama-sangeet, and verses are sung night after night. From distant places, people cross fields and meadows, tying up their little bundles of food, and come to sit and listen. The leaders of the troupe have come to his house, earnestly requesting, and have taken him away to perform.

Ah! How wonderful! Whose composed rhyme is this?—“The poet’s guru, Thakur Haru—” Haru Thakur’s?—No. It is Harihar Ray Mahashoy of Nishchindipur.

Sitting right here on this Dashashwamedh ghat, twenty-two years ago, how many times he had built and rebuilt in his mind—then, slowly, when did he begin to forget all that? When did those new notebooks and bundles of papers, neglected, find shelter in some secret, disregarded corner of the box, hiding away from the light of day? The web of youthful dreams, by life’s noon, faded into the horizon like mist.

Looking back towards that lost youth, something stirs in his chest, so many memories come flooding back—can those days of life ever be brought back, even once more?

At Dashashwamedh ghat, Apu has made friends with many boys. But here, all the boys his age go to school—only he still does not. In Nishchindipur, it was fine to spend the days fishing and boating, but here, among his peers, he feels ashamed to admit he does not study.

Besides, the boys he has befriended at Dashashwamedh ghat all come from well-off families. One day, Poltu’s dada (elder brother) casually mentioned that his father often has to travel abroad. Apu asked, “Why, do you have a lot of shishya-bari (disciples’ homes)?”

Poltu’s dada replied in surprise, “Shishya-bari? What do you mean, brother?…”

Before Apu could answer properly, he said, “My father is in contracting, you know? Besides, we have a small zamindari (landholding) in Kanthi—but these days, what’s left after paying the installments?”

--- Page 203 ---
Some evenings, Apu would wander down to the Dashashwamedh Ghat and listen to his father recite tales from the Puranas. Sitting on a mat in the Shasthi temple, he would lose himself in the story—the tender, aching sorrow of Rajarshi Bharat, the deer-boy, mourning the death of a fawn at the jaws of a wild beast, and finally, the tale of his own death. As he listened, Apu’s eyes would fill with tears. And then, when the king of Sindhu-Sauvira, Rahugan, not knowing Bharat’s true nature, made the Brahmarshi carry his palanquin—Apu’s heart would pound with curiosity and dread, certain that something momentous was about to happen, must happen. At the end of the storytelling, he especially loved the benediction sung in the lilting Purabi melody:

kāle varṣatu parjanyaṃ pṛthivī śasyaśālinī  
lokāḥ santu nirāmayāḥ...

As evening fell, the sound of conch shells and temple bells mingled with the red glow of the setting sun, and with the strains of the Purabi raga. It was as if the pain of the bereaved Rajarshi, grieving for the lost deer-boy, was woven into the very air.

At home, Apu would take paper and pen from his father and say, “Baba, won’t you write it down for me? That song you sing—kāle varṣatu parjanyaṃ?”

Harihar, delighted, would say, “So you listen, khoka (little one)?”

“I’m there every day! Yesterday, when you were telling about Bharat’s mother dying, I was sitting right behind you—on the steps of the Shasthi temple.”

“And how does it make you feel—do you like it?”

“Very, very much. I listen every single day.”

But Apu kept one thing hidden. On days when his friends were with him, he never went to his father. Once, as he was passing by his father with his companions, his father spotted him and called out, “Khoka, O khoka—”

One of his friends asked, “Does he know you?”
