Apu only nodded, yes. But that day, he did not go to his father. He was afraid his friends might find out that his father was the one telling stories at the ghat! Except for Poltu’s dada (elder brother), he told his other friends that they had a house in Kashi, that they had come to Kashi for a change of air, that they had a big house back home, that his father was a contractor, and that they even owned zamindari (landed estate) in the village. In the end, he would add, “But what’s the use of zamindari? After paying the installments, what’s really left?”

Perhaps because his friends were not much older than he was, they never noticed the mismatch between the stories he told and the simplicity of his clothes. Especially with his beautiful face, everything seemed to fit, and all was forgiven.

--- Page 204 ---
On the day of the full moon, there was quite a crowd at Kathak Thakur’s place. After dusk, when Harihar finished his tale, he sat down on the steps by the ghat’s kitchen, resting. Kathak Thakur went down to the water to wash his hands and face. Seeing Harihar, he said—“Ah, so you’re here too! Did you see the spectacle, this full moon day? I say, today’s been a good day, managed to arrange a Brahmin-bhiksha (ritual alms for Brahmins)—back in the day, every month in Kashi, if there was a Brahmin-bhiksha, fifteen seers, half a maund of rice would pile up. But these days, dada, even at a grand Brahmin-bhiksha, people don’t bother—there’s not a single grain of rice—barely five annas, and among those, two are old, worn coins! Where’s the learning in people these days?”

—“There was learning here in Kashi, long ago. But I’ve been in my own country all these years—only now have I come and settled here…”

—“Is your house nearby, moshai (sir)? Could you offer me a little tea? I’ve been thinking for days I’d like a cup—look, I carry tea tied in the corner of my chador, wandering about, thinking maybe I’ll get some hot water at a sweet-seller’s shop… My throat’s gone hoarse, a little salted tea would soothe it…”

—“Yes, yes, come along, my house is just nearby—shall we go?” Taking Kathak with him, Harihar brought him home.

Tea was not a custom here from long ago. Water was heated in a pan, and tea was prepared. Apu brought out the tea in a brass glass, with some snacks on a tray, and set them before the storyteller. Seeing the food, Kathak Thakur was delighted—he hadn’t expected any.

—“Is this your son? Ah, what a fine boy you have! So handsome—wonderful—come, come, baba (child), stay well, may you be blessed—did you make salted tea, moshai? Let me see—”

Harihar said, “Are all your children here too—?”

—“No family at all, so no children… Even the ten bighas of land are gone, and at the root, I’m still a pauper—if only a few bighas of land had remained, would I have come so far away—just like you! Is this even home anymore, moshai? May Lord Vishweshwar watch over us—such a winter, moshai—not a drop of date palm sap, not a bit of patali gur (jaggery)—back home, I had twenty date palm trees myself—”

—“Where is your home, moshai?”

—“Near Satkhira—do you know Badhur-Shitalkathi? The Chakkattis of Shitalkathi are quite a renowned family—”

Harihar packed the tobacco, took a few puffs himself, then handed it to Kathak Thakur, saying—“Have some.”

--- Page 205 ---
––It’s nothing, moshai, I’m heading out around the time of Phagun (spring)—there’s a garden, I’ll go sell it off—after all, we’re Shrotriya Brahmins, you see?... So, I had ten bighas of land, mortgaged it to arrange the dowry—got married too—moshai, I kept house for ten years—do you know what happened? One evening, she went up to the kitchen roof to cut a pumpkin—there, moshai, a snake was lying in wait for me—bit her hand—of course, I wasn’t home that day—who knows which quack or village healer they called, what they tried—crossing the ghat at Patuli, Mahesh Sadhukhan from the village was coming from the other side, he told me—Go home quickly, moshai—there’s great trouble at your house—what trouble, he wouldn’t say—when I reached home, I saw that my wife had died the night before!—So that’s how it went, moshai...the land went too—this way and that—since then I keep saying, what’s the point of staying in the village—where would I get three or four hundred rupees to marry again?...So I go to Bishwanath’s place...at least I won’t starve...it’s been eight years now—there’s a cousin—whatever little land is left, he’s taken it over—says I have no share—well then, so be it, if I have none, I have none—I’ll never get into a quarrel—let him keep whatever he’s grabbed. I’ll take my leave, moshai—had a nice cup of tea at your place—where’s your boy?...Good boy, fine boy—

The old pair of canvas shoes, patched with worn leather, he dusted off and slipped onto his feet as he came to the door—turning to go, he said—Tomorrow too, I’ll go begging from door to door—let’s see what happens—

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-One**

Harihar’s lodgings were nothing special. On the lower floor, two damp rooms, so dark that coming in from outside, you couldn’t make out a thing. Sarbajaya had never lived in such a place before; their old house in the village, though ancient, had wide doors and windows where sunlight and wind played, the high-raised rooms of the old days, dry and echoing underfoot. Here, the damp floors and darkness gave Sarbajaya headaches. Apu, of course, never stayed indoors at all—like a young tree reaching for the sun, his face always turned toward the light. Raised in the open fields and river air of Nischindipur, his soul suffocated in the gloom of these closed rooms; he couldn’t bear to stay there even for a moment.

Seeing Kashi, he felt a little disappointed. What use are grand houses if there are no forests at all?

One evening, around dusk, the storyteller came to Harihar’s lodgings. After some small talk, he said—Haven’t seen your boy around, have I?

--- Page 206 ---
Harihar said, “He’s gone out to play somewhere, probably towards the Dashashwamedh ghat—”

Kothakthakur, busy untying some bundle at the edge of the verandah, said, “He’s become quite close with your son, moshai. The other day, I called him over at the ghat and we sat and talked for a long while. He loves to play with shells, so those two big sea cowries that someone gave as offerings for a votive ritual the other day—I thought I’d bring them for him. Here, you keep them. Give them to him when he comes back.”

At the end of the month of Agrahayan, Apu caught hold of his father and insisted he wanted to go to school. He said, “Everyone goes to school, Baba, I want to go too. There’s a good school just past the bend in the lane, not far at all.”

Harihar enrolled his son in the school. Though it was a scholarship school, they taught English there as well. After leaving Prasanna Gurumoshai’s pathshala—it had been nearly four or five years—this was his first time enrolling in another school.

Around mid-Magh, Kothakthakur arrived at Harihar’s house one day, holding a scrap of brown paper. Showing the piece of paper, he said, “Take a look, moshai—can you read it? If I write it like this, will it do?”

Harihar read it. It was a deed of gift, written in the name of one Kashibasi Ramgopal Chakrabarti, granting ten bighas of land in Kothakthakur’s native village to him, with such-and-such witnesses, at Dashashwamedh ghat, on such-and-such date. Kothakthakur said, “Do you know what this is about? In our country, in the village of Kumure, Ramgopal Chokkatti was a great scholar. About a year before he died, he said to me, ‘Ramdhon, you have nothing, I’m thinking of giving you ten bighas of land—will you take it?’ Well, I thought, he’s a good Brahmin, if he wants to give, what harm is there? Then, just like that, he gave me the land by word of mouth. He gave it, he gave it—but all this time, I didn’t pay it much mind. I was going to stay in Kashi, not back home—what use was land? Then Chokkatti moshai passed away. The gift of land remained only in words. Now, after all this time, I’m thinking of going back to the village—after all, moshai, what is a man without wife and children? To tell you the truth, I’ve managed to save up about three hundred rupees—earned it by reciting the Jaladhar. And with another two hundred, I could get a girl from a good Shrotriya family. If that happens, then I’ll need the land, won’t I? But a gift given by word of mouth—will Chokkatti moshai’s sons accept it? So I sat down and wrote out this paper myself—wrote it all, signatures and everything—two witnesses, all made up. Let’s see if they’ll accept a written deed. I’ll go and say, ‘Look, your father gave me this land—here’s the proof.’”

--- Page 207 ---
When it was time to leave, Kathak-thakur said, “Very well, moshai, on Tuesday, on the day of Maghi Purnima, I’ll take your boy with me to that Raja’s Thakur’s house by the Teota, right next to the Manmandir itself. Every year, after dusk, they host a grand Brahmin feast, you see. With a touch of pride, he added, “They send me an invitation card every year, treat me to a fine meal—splendid, truly. I’ll come and fetch him that day, mind you.”

On the day of Maghi Purnima, from the last hours of the night, Sarbajaya was left astonished by the crowds of pilgrims on the road. In groups, men and women alike, chanting “Jai Vishwanathji ki jai,” “Bolo Bom,” “Bolo Bom,” braved the biting chill of Magh to make their way to the river for the holy bath. A little later, Sarbajaya too went to bathe, accompanied by the Punjabi lady—the waters of the Ganga at the ghat, the steps, the temple, the path, all were dressed in festive finery, thronged with celebrants. Entering the water was no easy feat. At the temple of Shashthi, red pennants fluttered in the wind.

Before evening, Kathak-thakur came to fetch Apu. Sarbajaya said, “Send him along, will you? There’s no one else, and Apu’s taken a shine to him—at Dashashwamedh Ghat, he calls Apu over, sits him down, tells him stories, even bought him papaya to eat one day, I hear—send him, he’s a good man.”

Apu first went with Kathak-thakur to his house. A bare room, with sums and accounts chalked onto the mud walls in various places. Samples:

“Bhagavat recital at the Rani of Searsole’s house... 4”

“At Musammat Kunta’s Thakur’s house... that... ... 2”

“One day’s rations for Dharak Lalji Dobe... 4.0”

There was little in the way of furniture. A narrow cot laid out, a small tin trunk, a rope-strung hanger, a pair of wooden sandals. On a nail in the wall hung a large garland of lotus seeds.

Kathak-thakur said, “Will you have an orange?”

Apu shook his head and asked, “Do you have one?”

For some reason, Apu felt no shyness or hesitation with this Kathak-thakur. As he peeled the orange, he asked, “Do you know ‘Kale varshtu parjanyam’?”

“‘Kale varshtu parjanyam’? Of course I do, I recite it every day. Haven’t you heard me?”

“Won’t you say it for me now, just once?”

Kathak-thakur did recite it in a sing-song voice, but Apu felt it sounded better from his father’s lips; the thakur’s voice was too deep.

For taking home, Kathak-thakur had gathered various little things made of clay and stone—dolls, toys, Shiva lingam garlands, wooden birds. Showing them to Apu, he said, “Things from Kashi—everyone will ask, ‘What have you brought?’ That’s what I’ll take back.”

--- Page 208 ---
Crossing several narrow lanes, Kathak-thakur came to a halt before the door of a dark house. Squeezing through the low doorway with great difficulty alongside the storyteller, Apu felt as though there was no one in the house at all—everything was silent, hushed. Kathak-thakur cleared his throat once or twice, and then someone, roused from sleep on a cot in the corridor, asked something in a gruff voice in Hindi, which Apu could not understand. Even after Kathak-thakur introduced himself, it seemed the man neither recognized him nor had expected their arrival. After that, the man, with a hint of irritation, went off to ask someone something. But he took so long to return that Apu began to think perhaps they would say, “You weren’t invited—go away.” 

Whatever the case, after standing in the darkness for a good fifteen minutes, the man finally returned and, in a half-lit corner of the corridor, spread out a few sal leaves and had them sit down. He brought water in a thick brass lota. Kathak-thakur, as if in fear, sat gingerly on the makeshift seat. “It’s the Raja’s house—who knows what they’ll serve?” In restless anticipation, Apu sat leaf to leaf for nearly another twenty minutes—still, no one appeared. Just as Apu’s doubts about whether they would actually get to eat here began to resurface, the unexpected happened: the arrival of the meal. Thick, coarse wheat puris, and a bland, scentless eggplant ghonto—finally, enormous laddus. When Apu tried to bite into a laddu, his teeth could not pierce it—it was that hard. Kathak-thakur, glancing at Apu now and then, ate ten or twelve of those thick puris with great relish. From time to time, he would look at Apu and say, “Eat your fill, don’t be shy, they’re serving plenty—aren’t the laddus fine? My teeth are still strong, I can chew them well.”

Even if one lives together for a hundred years, someone may still remain outside my heart unless, by some special event, they are able to open the door to it. Even as a boy, Apu understood the neglect and disregard with which they had been received at this feast. Yet, for the sake of this food, for the joy of it, a wound was struck deep within Apu’s innermost heart. He thought, this Kathak-thakur is truly unfortunate. He thought, Kathak-thakur must never get enough to eat—ah, that’s why he’s eating these laddus with such gusto. One day, I’ll ask Ma to invite him to our house for a meal—

--- Page 209 ---
Compassion and love are the most precious spices of all, their weaving is ever so deft and sure. In her childhood heart, this foreigner, this Bengali storyteller known only for a few days, became entwined with her didi and Gulki in the same way—just as one, in the throes of longing, yearns to devour a whole laddu at once.

Not long after, the storyteller returned to his homeland. At Rajghat station, at the storyteller’s persistent urging, Harihar took Apu along to see him off and help him board the train. In Harihar’s mind, it seemed that twenty-two years ago, when he had gone home to do the very same thing—this man, at least eight years older than he is now, was also going home to begin life anew, to set up a household afresh. So then, what of his own age? How could it be that he was already too old, that he might lack time to do anything?

When the train left, tears welled up in Apu’s eyes. Sometimes, in a child’s heart, a deep and lasting affection for an elder takes root. Precisely because it is rare, it is all the more precious.

Toward the end of the month of Magh (late winter), one day Harihar suddenly entered the house and sat down by the edge of the courtyard. Sarbajaya, whatever she was doing, dropped her work in haste and came running, asking, “What’s happened? Why have you sat down like that?” Looking at her husband’s face, the words caught in her throat. Harihar’s eyes were red as hibiscus flowers, his right hand seemed to tremble. As Sarbajaya reached out to help him up, he muttered in a daze, “Where’s Khoka? Khoka?” (Khoka: affectionate term for a young boy, here meaning Apu.)

Sarbajaya touched his body and felt the fever burning through him. Gently, she took his hand and led him inside, laying him down on the bed. She said, “Apu is coming, Nandababu from upstairs called him away, I think he’s taken him to his shop at the Godhuliya crossing—”

--- Page 210 ---
Apu hasn’t gone to the shop; he was sitting on the roof in front of Nandababu’s house, reading a book. It’s been about a month now that Apu has become quite friendly with Nandababu. He hasn’t quite figured out how old Nandababu is, but he seems younger than Apu’s father. In Nandababu’s upstairs room, Apu has discovered a great many books—when Nandababu is home, he takes a book and sits on the roof to read. But he’s always a little afraid that Nandababu might snatch the book away and not let him read, because something like that had happened once before. One day, Apu was sitting in a sunny corner of the roof, reading, when Nandababu, searching for something in his room, came outside, saw him, and scolded him sharply—“Hey, put that down! All you do is sit and read these books, and you never put things back where they belong. When I need something, I can’t find it—go on, put the book back, go!”

He never touches anything else in the room, so why should he be scolded? Since then, he always takes the books with a bit of fear.

In the evenings, Nandababu would trim his nails, put on nice clothes, dab on some fragrant oil from a little bottle, and go out for a walk every day. One day, he had sprinkled a little of that scent on Apu as well—the fragrance was strong and heady.

Even after dusk, Apu used to go up to Nandababu’s room to read. But after evening, Nandababu would take a bottle out of the cupboard and drink a reddish kind of medicine. Once, when Apu went into the room at that time, he was scolded very harshly. The stairs to Nandababu’s room were on the other side—and one night, suddenly going upstairs, Apu saw a woman sitting in the room. Seeing him, Nandababu said, “Now go, Apurba, this is my shali—my wife’s younger sister—she’s come to visit, she’ll be leaving soon.” As Apu was coming back down, he heard Nandababu saying, “He’s the son of our downstairs tenant—doesn’t really understand much.”

Nandababu often asks him about his mother. He says, “Tell your mother to send up some paan (betel leaf)? My servant doesn’t know how to prepare it properly—”

Apu, after pleading with his mother, often brings paan upstairs. Sometimes Nandababu asks, “Does your mother ever say anything about me? No?”

Apu comes home and tells his mother, “Nandababu is a good man, Ma—he asks about you every day—”

—“About me? What does he ask about me—?”

—“He said, tell your mother I ask about her—he’s a good man—”

—“Let him ask, but why do you, you little rascal, keep going upstairs to his room? What do you do sitting up there in the afternoon?”

--- Page 211 ---
Harihar’s fever had eased a little. Apu, just returned from school, was putting down his books. Hearing the sound of his footsteps, Harihar called out—  
“Khoka, come here, sit with me for a bit, baba—”

Apu sat down and began to recount stories from school. Smiling, in a soft voice, he said—  
“These two months I’ve been going to school, and already, baba, everyone there likes me a lot. Every day I get to sit in the first row. In our class, they’re going to print a little magazine, once every month. They’ve taken me onto the team—I’ll show you, baba, when it comes out—”

Inside Harihar’s chest, a strange ache of tenderness and sorrow welled up. Apu pulled out a sheet of paper and said—  
“I’ve written something—They said they’ll print it, under my name—but only for those who give two taka as a subscription—they’ll only print the writing of those who pay. Will you give two taka, baba?”

With eager curiosity, Harihar took the paper from his son’s hand and began to read. He hadn’t known that his son wrote. The story of the prince’s hunt was beautifully crafted; delighted, Harihar propped himself up on his pillow and asked—  
“You wrote this, khoka?”

“I’ve written so many more, baba—ghost stories, tales of princesses—when we were back home, I used to write them out for Ranudidi in her notebook—”

Hearing talk of money, Sarbajaya wanted to protest. With her husband ill, even what little they had would not be enough for household expenses; there was no need for a printed magazine. But Harihar, gently explaining, insisted on giving the money, saying—  
“Let him go, let khoka’s writing be printed. Once I recover and do the Bhagavat reading at the Thakur-bari, we’ll get ten whole taka from that—”

A couple of days later, Apu came quietly to his father, lips pouting in disappointment—  
“It didn’t work, baba. The printing people asked for more money, so today at school they said the subscription is four taka—”

The look of disappointment on his son’s face pierced Harihar’s heart. After a while, changing the subject, he said—  
“See, khoka, where has your mother gone…?”  
He took the key-bunch from under his pillow and handed it over—  
“Quietly, open that wooden box, the one with my bundle of bamboo pens, open it, will you? See how much money is in the corner?”  
Then Harihar, with loving eyes, watched his son, busy opening the box. Innocent, innocent, utterly innocent! That beautiful, pure moonlike forehead—his mother’s forehead; those eyes—his mother’s eyes. When Harihar, in his youth, first brought his bride home, the same smile that lit up the face of the young bride Sarbajaya now shone, unclouded and new, on eleven-year-old Apu’s face.

--- Page 212 ---
For no reason at all, a sea of tenderness would suddenly surge and swell within Harihar’s chest, bringing tears to his eyes.

Apu seemed like the first tender leaf of spring, the joy on his face like the fresh crimson glow of dawn; in the gaze of those wide, deep-blue eyes, Harihar saw the boundless dreams of his own lost youth, the jubilant rustle of young sal trees on distant blue hills... the faraway music of a shoreless sea.

Apu, in a hushed voice, pointed out to his father—“I have four taka, Baba!” Harihar had set aside a few coins for times of need, hidden away in his own box, unknown to his wife, so he could answer without worry—“Take it, khoka, pay the subscription, but don’t tell your mother.”

Apu replied in a bright, lilting tone—“When it’s printed, I’ll show you, Baba! They said they’ll print it in my name—it’ll come out the Monday after this Monday—”

The next morning, Harihar’s illness worsened again.

Sarbojaya, frightened, said to her son—“Go call Nandababu, will you—let him come and see once—”

Nandababu came, examined Harihar, and said—“A doctor needs to be called, Apurba. Tell your mother.”

That evening, it was Nandababu who brought a doctor along. The doctor examined and listened, then said—“He’s caught a chill, it’s bronchopneumonia—he needs good nursing—does he stay in this downstairs room like this?... Khoka, bring a bottle to my dispensary, I’ll give you medicine—”

For several days, Apu went to the doctor’s dispensary above Dashashwamedh Ghat to fetch medicine.

There was no noticeable improvement. Day by day, Harihar grew weaker. The few coins they had were spent on doctor’s visits and remedies. The doctor said, unless he was given at least a seer of milk and other fruits daily, the patient would become even more frail. He also prescribed a tonic from abroad, costing three and a half taka. In this foreign, unfamiliar place, there was no one to offer even a word of encouragement. All around, Sarbojaya saw only darkness.

--- Page 213 ---
In this time of trouble, Sarbajaya found herself ensnared in yet another predicament. If one stood on the upper terrace and leaned over, the kitchen could be seen below. She had noticed before that Nandababu would sometimes sneak glances from the terrace toward her kitchen, but ever since Harihar had fallen ill, Nandababu had grown bolder. On various pretexts, he would come into the house ten times a day—at first, he would ask after Apu, keeping his questions indirect and discreet. Lately, however, he addressed her directly, speaking to her without hesitation.

At first, Sarbajaya had thought nothing of it—rather, in this time of crisis, she felt quietly grateful that this unrelated man was offering so much help and attention. But gradually, she began to sense that this increasing familiarity—this overstepping—felt somehow out of place, discordant. Nandababu would buy betel leaves himself, lay them out before her, and say, “Don’t let the servants prepare the paan—my life is wasted, Bou-thakrun (a respectful term for the lady of the house)—why don’t you make it for me, just once?” Even then, Sarbajaya found no fault; if anything, she felt a touch of pity for this exiled, affection-starved man. But the intimacy was slowly crossing the bounds of propriety.

These days, he would bring the paan and say, “Keep it, Bou-thakrun.” He seemed to want her to take it directly from his hand. Apu, ever the scatterbrain, was hardly ever to be found at home—most of the time, Harihar lay unconscious in the other room—and it was precisely at these moments that Nandababu would come, ostensibly to see the patient! On some pretense or another, he would linger for half an hour, never leaving quickly. He would say, “Don’t worry, Bou-thakrun—I’m upstairs—whether Apu is here or not—just call me from the stairs if you need anything! In times of trouble, you can’t be too particular… Give me a little lime, will you? No stem? Oh, just dab a bit on my finger, won’t you—just like that—”

Whenever Harihar regained consciousness, he would become restless for his son. Looking around with a feeble voice, he would call, “Khoka, where’s Khoka! Where’s my boy!” Sarbajaya would reply, “He’s coming, what a wretched child, won’t even sit by his father for a moment… Must have gone off to the ghat again.” When the boy returned home, Harihar would say, “Can’t you sit by me for a bit?... He’s mad for his boy—can’t sleep for worrying about him—go on, sit, can’t you run your hand over his head and body? Are you his son or will you ring the temple bell in heaven?”

--- Page 214 ---
Apu sits awkwardly by his father’s pillow. But after a little while, he thinks—Oh! How long must I sit here—really? Am I not allowed to go out for a bit, to play? In the biting cold, his feet are growing numb. His mind fidgets—he wants to dash straight to that Dashashwamedh Ghat. The king of the waters, the pure, free air, the crowd of elegantly dressed men and women. Poltu… Subir… Gulu… Patal—Poltu’s dada (elder brother). And today, again, at four in the afternoon, the peacock-barge of the Raja of Ramnagar! Restless, he lets the evening slip by out of shyness, and doesn’t dare go out for fear of his mother.

One morning, Sarbajaya said to her son—Hey, do you know that chhattar (community dining hall) beside the white house?

—No…

—You’ve never eaten at the chhattar, not even once since you came here? In Kashi, you must eat at the chhattar, you know! Won’t you go eat there today?... At least go see it?

—Why must one eat at the chhattar in Kashi?

—It brings punya (spiritual merit)—today, after your bath at Dashashwamedh Ghat, go eat at the chhattar, understand!

At noon, after eating at the sattr (community kitchen), Apu returned home. His mother was sitting on the kitchen veranda, eating something from a bowl—at first, seeing him, she tried to hide, but Apu had come too close—if she tried to hide now, it would arouse suspicion, so she tried to speak in a casual tone—You’ve eaten? How was the food?

Ma is eating soaked arhar dal (split pigeon peas).

—Not good—the pumpkin curry was just ashes—sitting there was tiresome—so many dirty, ragged people go there to eat—I’m not going again, I don’t need any punya—what are you eating, Ma? Is that your berto (leftover)? Didn’t you cook?

—Today is my Kuluichandi (a ritual fast)—just these two soaked arhar dals—they taste quite good—I love them… Will you have some this afternoon?

There was no cooking at night either. His mother said—Try some soaked arhar dal, will you? You’ll like it now—I didn’t cook this time, you eat so much, it’s not like you just sit at the table for a few grains of rice—after eating that, could you really eat anything more?

--- Page 215 ---
The next afternoon, Nandababu handed a bundle of betel leaves to Apu and said—“Go get them prepared by your mother, will you?” In the room next to the patient’s, Sarbajaya was sitting, making paan (betel leaf parcels). Nandababu, his shoes clacking on the floor, came down from upstairs and entered the patient’s room, and after only a brief moment, he emerged and entered the room where Sarbajaya was preparing the paan. 

Having spent the whole night awake, Sarbajaya was nodding off; the sound of shoes startled her awake, and seeing Nandababu standing right in front of her, she shrank back and pulled her veil (ghomta) over her head. 

Nandababu said—“Is the paan ready, bou-thakrun (a respectful term for the lady of the house)?” 

In silence, Sarbajaya arranged the prepared paan parcels on a tray and pushed it towards him. Nandababu picked one up, popped it into his mouth, and said—“You always put too little lime (chun) in your paan, bou-thakrun. Move aside, let me take some myself—”

The betel box was right by Sarbajaya’s lap. There was no one else in the house; Apu had gone out somewhere. In the next room, Harihar was sleeping under the influence of medicine. The afternoon was utterly still. 

Suddenly, Sarbajaya felt as if Nandababu, under the pretext of taking some lime, was unnecessarily—trying to come much too close to her. With a vague cry, in a flash, she stood up and went outside the room. A current, like a bolt of lightning, shot from her feet to her head. Pointing to the stairs with her finger, she said in a piercing voice—“Go upstairs this instant—never come down again—if you come down, I’ll dash my head and die—why do you come here? Don’t you dare come again—”

Sarbajaya found herself in dire straits. In this foreign place, in the patient’s house—helpless, without a single coin in hand, not a familiar soul anywhere, her son only about eleven years old—and even then, lacking sense, utterly naïve. And now, on top of all this, these troubles.

--- Page 216 ---
Here is the translation, following all your instructions:

---

The Punjabi woman upstairs rarely came down—once or twice she had taken Sarbajaya up to her room, but even after five or six months in Kashi, Sarbajaya could neither speak Hindi nor understand it well, so no real friendship had blossomed. Today, going to her, Sarbajaya recounted Nandababu’s incident in detail and broke down in tears. The Punjabi woman’s name was Surajkuari; both husband and wife were from the Roalsar district of Punjab, her husband worked as an overseer on the railways. Though she was not very young, she looked youthful—fair-skinned, wide-eyed, with a slender, taut frame. Hearing everything, she said—“Don’t be afraid, you stay without fear. If you see any more mischief, tell me. I’ll have my husband cut off his nose and cool him down for good…”

It was exactly noon. After several sleepless nights, Sarbajaya had spread her anchal (the loose end of her sari) on the floor and fallen asleep. Through the window of the north room, a slant of sunlight fell crookedly across the narrow courtyard. Apu had planted marigold saplings in a clay pot; two or three marigolds had bloomed together, looking thoroughly annoyed—beneath them, a kitten sat. Apu was sitting right beside his father’s bed. His father had been a little better since morning—the doctor had said perhaps there was hope for life. Even so, the patient did not seem fully conscious, more in a stupor. Suddenly, his father opened his eyes and looked at him for a while, then said something. Apu felt as if Baba was asking him to come closer. As Apu moved nearer, Harihar, with his disease-wasted, thin arms, drew his son’s neck close and silently gazed into his face for a long time, unblinking. Apu was a little surprised—he had never seen such a look in his father’s eyes before.

At ten o’clock that night, Apu was woken from sleep by some sound. A dim light burned in the room—Ma was sleeping deeply, but from Baba’s throat came a strange, many-toned sound. A sense of dread crept over him. The soot-blackened rafters, the damp floor, the bone-breaking cold, the smoke from the coal fire—all together seemed like a hard, unyielding nightmare. If only Baba’s illness would go away, then life could go on!

In the last hours of the night, he was jolted awake by his mother’s push!—“Apu, O Apu, get up, quickly go upstairs and call the Hindustani bou (daughter-in-law; here, the Punjabi woman)!”

Apu got up and heard that the sound in Baba’s throat had grown even louder. Not long after Surajkuari came down from upstairs, at four in the morning, Harihar died.

--- Page 217 ---
On a rain-laden, mist-shrouded day in the heart of the monsoon, it seems as though the sunlit days of the world—were they dreams, or were they ever real? These clouds, this relentless gloom, will they remain the only companions on the endless road into the future? Like the mirage on the horizon, have those days of Chaitra and Baisakh—those bright, dry months of spring and early summer—truly vanished into the past, never to return?

From all sides, a strange fog seemed to close in around Sarbojaya. Through its depths, neither path nor companion could be seen, nor could she tell where she was. She wondered if this mist would ever lift, even if the sun were to rise at midday; behind it, she sensed the sky was veiled in the pale gray of unseasonal rainclouds that would linger all day.

In this time of peril, the Punjabi overseer, Jalim Singh, and his wife were of great help. Jalim Singh skipped work at the office and wandered through the Bengali quarter, seeking people for the funeral rites. Hearing the news, a few volunteers from the Ramakrishna Mission also arrived.

At Manikarnika Ghat, after the cremation, as evening fell, Apu bathed and, shivering in the cool western breeze, climbed up onto the platform. One of the Ramakrishna Mission volunteers and Nandababu were helping him put on his uttariya (a traditional upper cloth). The day had grown late; the faint light of sunset glimmered only on the thresholds of the stone temples. Lost in the confusion of the day’s events, it seemed to Apu as if, in his father’s familiar voice, someone was sitting before an eager audience, reciting—

Kale varshatu parjanyam prithivi shasyashalini...

Lokah santu nirāmayāh...

The father whom they had all brought to Manikarnika Ghat today for cremation—defeated by illness, by the battles of life—that father was now only a dream. Apu no longer knew him, could not recognize him—his lifelong refuge, his familiar, smiling father, whose gentle, melodious voice had always been with him, singing blessings in the wistful tune of an old Purabi song, as if seated somewhere, just as he had every day—

Kale varshatu parjanyam prithivi shasyashalini...

Lokah santu nirāmayāh...

*Pather Panchali*, Chapter Thirty-Two

--- Page 218 ---
About a month passed, somehow. During this month, Sarbajaya thought of many ways out, but none seemed right to her. It isn’t true that she never considered returning home once or twice, but whenever the thought arose, she quickly suppressed it. First of all, except for that one homestead, everything else back home had been sold off—some to pay debts, some simply sold and bought—there was no land left. Secondly, before leaving that place, she had painted so many pictures of their future happiness to the bou-jhis (sisters-in-law) along the roads and lanes, showing them in so many ways how bright things would be. The moment they left the soil of Nishchindipur, no one in this wretched, ignorant land understood her husband’s worth, but wherever they were going, everyone would surely welcome him with open arms, and it wouldn’t even take a year for their fortunes to turn—how many gestures and words Sarbajaya had used to make them believe this! Now it was Chaitra month, not even a full year had passed. And already, in such destitute, penniless circumstances, the very thought of returning there in widow’s garb and standing before everyone made her want to sink into the earth with shame and embarrassment. Let whatever is to happen, happen here—she would take her son by the hand and beg from street to street in Kashi to raise him—who would come to see them here?

After about a month, a bit of fortune arrived. A gentleman from Kedar Ghat informed the mission office that an acquaintance of his from a wealthy family was in need of a Brahmin girl—one of their own caste—to come into their house and help with the household work. Could the mission find someone suitable? In the end, through the mission’s arrangements, the gentleman agreed to send Apu and his mother there. For Sarbajaya, it was as if she had found the shore after being lost in a boundless sea. A day or two later, the gentleman sent word that they should be ready to move house entirely, for the wealthy family’s home was not in Kashi—their people had come to Kashi for a visit, and would take Sarbajaya and her son back with them when they returned.

The house was enormous, painted a grand shade of yellow. Just like the big houses one finds in Kashi, this was a truly large one. Sarbajaya, with her son in tow, entered the house timidly, following behind everyone else.

As soon as she set foot in the inner quarters, a wave of welcome rose up—not for her, but for the group that had just returned from their outing in Kashi.

--- Page 219 ---
When the crowd and commotion had lessened a little, the mistress of the house, Sarbajaya, appeared before her. She was quite stout, one could tell she had once been very beautiful, her age over fifty. As soon as Sarbajaya bowed to her in greeting, the mistress said—“Enough, enough, come, come—ah, at such a young age, this—Is this your son? What a fine boy—what’s his name?”

To someone else she said—“Your home is in Kashi, isn’t it? No?—Then perhaps—”

In front of everyone’s curious gaze, Sarbajaya felt a great shyness and discomfort. When, at the mistress’s command, a maid led her to the room set aside for her, she breathed a sigh of relief...

From the next day, Sarbajaya began her work in the kitchen as agreed. She was not the only cook; there were four or five others. Three or four kitchens: one for fish and meat, one for vegetarian dishes, one for milk-based dishes, one for bread, and a separate kitchen for the food of the outside men. The number of maids and servants was endless. Though the kitchen was within the women’s quarters, it was a little set apart. That area seemed to be the kingdom of the maids, servants, and Brahmin cooks. The women of the house came only to give instructions and explain the work; unless there was some special reason, they rarely stayed in the kitchen.

There was a discussion about what Sarbajaya should cook. Sarbajaya had always believed she could cook very well. She said, perhaps she should take charge of the vegetarian dishes. The Brahmin cook Mokshada gave a sly smile and said—“You’ll cook for the babus? That would be a disaster.” Then, calling out to Panchhi, she said, “Did you hear, Panchhi, this lady from Kashi says she’ll cook the babus’ dishes! What’s your name again? I keep forgetting—” The mocking smile at the corner of Mokshada’s lips left Sarbajaya overwhelmed with embarrassment that day, but within a day or two she realized that none of her village-style curries would do here. That so much sugar had to be mixed into the broth, or that there was such a dish as cabbage fritters—these were things she heard of for the first time.

The mistress had taken good care of Sarbajaya for the first couple of months—giving her light work, checking in on her. Gradually, she had to stand equal with the other cooks. After working until two in the afternoon, at first she would feel utterly exhausted; she had never been used to being by the fire for so long, and at such a late hour she rarely felt any appetite. The other cooks would hide away fish and curries for themselves, eat some, take some outside somewhere. She would only sit down at her plate once, and that was all!

--- Page 220 ---
Sarbojaya is left astonished at the enormity of the cooking arrangements; never in her wildest dreams had she imagined such a grand affair. She wonders in amazement, *Three seers* of oil spent twice a day? Every day, the expense of oil and ghee as if for a sacrificial ritual! ... With her experience of a small, poor household in a village, she cannot quite grasp all this.

One day, while taking down the large pot of rice made from fine-grained rice, she called out to Mokshada Bamini, “O mashima (auntie), could you hold the pot for a moment?”

Mokshada, though she heard, paid no heed.

Meanwhile, seeing the rice begin to stick, Sarbojaya tried to take down the heavy pot herself. She tilted it, and the hot froth spilled onto the top of her foot, raising a blister at once. That very day, the mistress of the house transferred her to the bread room, telling her she need not do any work until her foot healed.

Sarbojaya stays with her son in a room downstairs. The room is right beside the western wing of the house. But it is so low-roofed, the floor so damp, and there is always such a strange smell in the room that even their house in Kashi was much better than this. The lower part of the wall is crusted with salt, large reddish stains blotched across it. Every time they enter from outside, Apu says, “Ugh, do you smell that, Ma? It’s just like the smell of old rice or something, isn’t it?”... These lower rooms were never meant for people to live in; that’s why the servants, helpers, and cooks are housed here.

Apu has wandered through all the rooms of the upper mansion, peering in from outside—such big windows and doors! Glass panes set in every window. In every room, large, cushioned chairs, gleaming tables polished so bright you could see your face in them. At Apu’s home, there had been old mats for sitting, but these carpets are much better—thick, almost new, spread across the floor. Mirrors hang on the walls, so large that Apu’s whole figure is reflected in them. He wonders to himself—where do they get such big pieces of glass? Maybe they join them together...

There is another large room on the upstairs verandah—almost always locked, only opened now and then by the servants to let in some air and light. Apu’s curiosity about what lies within is irrepressible. One day, seeing the door open, he slipped inside. What huge paintings! Stone statues! Large, cushioned chairs—mirrors—he wandered about, looking at everything. Just then, Chhutu the khansama (household steward) caught sight of him in the room and came in, blocking his way. “Who are you?... Why have you come in here?”

--- Page 221 ---
Perhaps that day he would have been beaten, but just then one of the household maids, passing by the verandah, saw and said—“Hey, little one, let him go, don’t say anything—his mother lives here—let him watch if he wants—”

When everyone had finished eating, Sarbajaya would come to her own room around half past two and lie down for a while. Of all the hours in the day, only at this time could she truly open her heart and talk with her son, and so sometimes Apu would come to the room then. His mother longed to see him at least once during the day. Ever since coming to this house, Apu seemed to have drifted far away. All day, it was nothing but work, work—she had to stay away from her son. By the time she finished her chores late at night and returned, Apu would already be asleep, and there would be no chance to talk. She yearned for this one afternoon hour.

There was the sound of footsteps at the door. Sarbajaya said—“Who is it, Apu! Come in—” Pushing open the door, Bamni mashi entered the room. Sarbajaya said—“Come in, masima, have a seat.” At the same moment, Apu also arrived. Bamni mashi was a relative of the babus, so she was given a seat with proper respect. Bamni mashi’s face was heavy and grave. After a moment of silence, she said—“Did you see what happened today, boro-bouma? Tell me, what was the fault... You’ve always been in the sickroom, haven’t you? She brought fish and ghee and left them in a basket, I thought it was for the cabbage—did you see the kind of insult? If it was for the polao, she could have just told the maid. Is Sadhu’s wife any less of a mischief-maker? The mistress’s favorite maid, is she? She won’t even set foot on the ground, always putting on airs upstairs—wasn’t that Chirikantha Thakur there too—let him say, why not?”

The afternoon drifted by in conversation. Mashi said, “I’ll go now, have to knead the flour for the snacks—it’s already four o’clock—”

After mashi left, Apu nestled close to his mother. She stroked his chin lovingly and asked—“Tell me, where do you spend your afternoons?...”

Apu smiled and replied—“There’s a gramophone playing in the upstairs sitting room, Ma—I was listening from the verandah—”

Sarbajaya was pleased.

“Tell me, do you get along with the babus’ children?... Do they call you to sit with them?...”

“Ve-e-e-ry much!...”

--- Page 222 ---
Just before their train arrived at Bandel Station, a great engine thundered past on the main line ahead, roaring with the speed of a storm. Apu gazed at it in wonder. What a sound!—Uff! When they reached Bandel Station, they got down from the train. Engines were running this way and that, massive freight trains shook the station as they rumbled past every five minutes without stopping. The air was alive with clamorous noise—here, the engine’s whistle pierced the ears, there, another passenger train was leaving, the guard waving his green flag—at dusk, signals clustered east and west along the tracks—red and green lights flickered—rails, engines, carriages, people!—

A little later at night, the train to Kashi arrived with a deafening roar and halted at the platform. The station was immense, packed with a tremendous crowd—Sarbajaya felt utterly bewildered—unaccustomed to such haste, she followed her husband with stiff, uncertain steps and stood at the door of a compartment. Harihar, after much struggle, forced his way through the relentless crowd and, with great effort, seated his bewildered wife and dazed son on a bench, then, with the porter’s help, loaded their bundles and bags.

Towards dawn, Sarbajaya’s drowsiness slipped away. The train was racing like a storm—fields, earth, trees all blurred together as they sped by—since it was a night train, they had all boarded the same compartment—Harihar had not put her in the ladies’ carriage. The crowd was less than before—on each bench, someone lay stretched out, sleeping. On the upper berth, a Kabuli (an Afghan trader) snored loudly. Apu had already risen, mouth agape, his face stuck out the window, staring intently.

Harihar, waking up, said to his son—“Don’t keep looking out like that, khoka (little one), coal dust will get in your eyes any moment—”

--- Page 223 ---
Coal dust is such a harmless thing, yet even if someone were to gouge out his eyes, Apu would still not be able to tear them away from the window right now. He sits like this, unmoving, for almost the entire night. Baba and Ma are asleep—oh, how much he has seen! At how many stations the train did not stop, the station itself, with all its lights and people, would whoosh by like a firework, vanishing in a flash—at some point in the night he must have dozed off a little, but suddenly waking, he stuck his face out and saw, in the deep moonlit night, the train was hurtling across a small bridge over some river—a tall, dark mound loomed ahead, with many trees atop it, the moonlight shimmering on the river’s water, white clouds drifting across the sky—then more such great mounds, more trees just like that. Then a big station, people, lights—on the next track, another train had come to a stop—a quarrel broke out between a panwala (betel-leaf seller) and another man!—there was a big clock at the station—he had learned how to read train times from his master, Nirenbabu, and counting carefully, he saw it was twenty-two minutes past three in the morning. Then the train started again—more trees, more tall mounds just like before—often, on both sides of the railway line, those same kinds of mounds—everyone in the carriage was asleep; if they would not see any of this, then why did they even board the train! Whom could he ask what all those mounds were? Every so often, he would lean out the window, craning to look down at the ground, trying to guess how fast the train was going—his hair whipped into his face by the wind, the ground invisible, as if someone were drawing straight lines across the earth—ah! How fast the train goes!—with curiosity and excitement, he would peer out one window, then the other, again and again.

Now and then, gazing out toward the east at the swiftly vanishing, moonlit fields, he would wonder, how far they had come! Through what strange land of rising and falling fields were they traveling?

Toward morning, he dozed off again, but as the train screeched to a halt at a massive station, his drowsiness vanished—the name was written on the stone slab of the platform—Patna City.

--- Page 224 ---
Apu looked back, his face stricken, and in the sing-song tone of reciting multiplication tables, he said—“I’ve been sitting here since evening, it’s crowded behind, where should I go?...” Before he could finish, Girish Sarkar grabbed his trembling hand, gave it a rough shake, pulled him up, and said—“What’s wrong with you, you little brat, don’t you have any sense? Sitting right in front—this is for the babus! And here comes the cook’s boy, wanting to sit right up front! Tell him where to go—useless rascal—get out of here, go sit by that pillar or somewhere else—”

From behind, a couple of officials called out—“What’s going on, what’s the matter, Girish—what’s the commotion? Who is it?”

“Just look, Manager-babu, this little brat is sitting here among the babus, right in front—those gentlemen from Chandannagar have come, there’s nowhere for them to sit—I tell him to get up, and he dares to argue!”

Manager-babu said—“Just give him a couple of slaps—”

Apu, shrinking in on himself, not looking anywhere, stunned, slipped out of the gathering. Suddenly, it seemed to him that everyone’s eyes were on him, all were watching him with curiosity! At first, he thought he’d break into a run, escape anywhere out of sight of the crowd. Then he went and stood behind a pillar. His body was trembling—out of fear, humiliation, shame—a strange shiver ran through the delicate curtains of his sensitive heart. After a moment, composing himself a little, he peeked out from behind the pillar... But all around were servants and attendants, on the upper verandah, behind the curtains, women; downstairs, the maids and cooks were standing—surely they had all witnessed the scene, what must they be thinking! Without knowing, what a mess he had made! He hadn’t known that was the babus’ place! Again and again, he tried to reassure himself—surely no one recognized him. So many outsiders had come—who would know who he was?

---

Then the theatre began. But he paid no attention to it. The crowd of people in front, the stifling air, the blaze of lights, the commotion of the doormen and servants—none of it registered with him. Chhutu, the young khansama (household servant), was moving along the row of invited guests, distributing betel leaves from a silver duck-shaped container—just the sight of it made Apu’s body tingle strangely. He glanced up at the enclosed balcony above—was Ma there? What if Ma found out about this? But Apu’s fear was entirely unfounded; his mother was nowhere near that place at the time, and none of this had even reached her ears.

Pather Panchali  
Chapter Thirty-Three

---

For the first time in her life, Sarbajaya found herself living like a thief, utterly dependent, in someone else’s house. Whether in happiness or sorrow, until now she had always been the sole mistress of her own home. The queen of a poor household—her word there was as effective as that of the mistress of this grand house, no less than the bou-ranis (the principal daughters-in-law). But here, it was as if she must always be on guard, always attentive, always watching someone else’s face for cues, walking on eggshells, never letting the slightest thing go amiss—every little thing, even the tiniest! It was becoming unbearable for her. She worked herself to the bone—her lips would bleed from exhaustion—yet here, her labor had no value. She toiled with all her might—yet no one would even mention her name. When they did give her something, it was tossed at her with pride and disdain—they would never give it as an equal, as if in payment for her work. She would have to beg for it, kneeling.

This was becoming more and more intolerable for her. But what could she do? Where was the opportunity to go outside? Who would give her shelter? Where could she stand?

Would it always be like this? For as long as she lived? Like that Brahmin mashi (maternal aunt) over there?

---

The aftertaste of the wedding festivities had not yet faded; today was the women’s feast. From evening onwards, the carriages of the invited women began to arrive at the back gate. Passing through the great inner doors, they would come to the wide marble staircase, carpeted with blue-flowered designs, leading up to the women’s quarters on the second-floor veranda. Gaslights glowed all along the veranda and the stairs, and at the head of the staircase, a great gas chandelier blazed. The two bou-ranis and the women of the house greeted everyone and sent them upstairs. The invited women ascended the stairs—some with a sly smile, some with ripples of laughter, some slow, some swift, some with a beauty and grace beyond compare.

--- Page 226 ---
Apu had been standing for quite some time near a pillar on the lower verandah, watching. This was the first time in his life he had witnessed such a scene; on the night of the wedding, he had fallen asleep early and hadn’t seen much. Of everyone, he liked Sujata, the daughter of this house, the most. She would descend the carpeted marble stairs from time to time, glancing at the guests, her face bright with smiles as she said—“Oh, really, Moni-di? Exactly at eight at night? Did Bakulbagan’s boudi (elder brother’s wife from Bakulbagan) not come?”

The beautiful hostess replied with a laugh—“I’ve been sitting ready in the car since six o’clock… It’s not so easy to leave, you know, not until everything’s ready… You know how it is—”

Sujata, in her sleeveless, precious china crepe dress the color of golden kanchan flowers, wrapped her white, round, flawless arm around the guest from behind, resting her face affectionately on her right shoulder as they began to ascend together. She chatted as they went—“Ma was saying that Bakulbagan’s boudi is supposed to go to Kolkata next month—Ma went on Wednesday, you know—did anything get decided?”

On the upper step of the stairs appeared the middle daughter-in-law, Mejo Bou-rani. She was a little older, perhaps over thirty, of exquisite beauty. There was no excess in her attire; the end of her wide, pale champa-colored silk sari with a red border was pinned to her hair with a diamond clip, and in the light of the great chandelier on the stairs, the slender gold chain at her throat glimmered. Her figure was graceful, her manner a little slow, grave—yet even at this age, her complexion glowed with the radiance of milk and alta (a traditional red dye). About a month ago, the death of her husband had cast a slight shadow of sorrow over her mature beauty, lending it a composed grace.

As Moni-di ascended, she stopped on the stairs, facing Mejo Bou-rani—“How are you feeling these days, Mejo boudi? See, I keep saying I’ll come by… Yesterday they all arrived from Etowa, so it was late into the night with all that…”

Apu had never imagined people could be so beautiful. He saw her for the first time now, for she had not been here all this while; only a few days ago, after her husband’s death, she had come from her father’s house. He gazed at her, spellbound, his eyes wide with wonder. This light, this gathering of beauties all around, the gentle, intoxicating fragrance of expensive flowers, the waves of music and laughter like the notes of a veena—Apu felt as if he were in a dream. What if this went on all day…?

--- Page 227 ---
Mejo Bou-rani had been watching for quite some time—a strange boy was standing in the corner by the stairs. She didn’t know everyone here—her own father was a wealthy man, and she often stayed at her father’s house. Stepping down two steps, she called out in a gentle voice, “Khoka, come up, won’t you? Why are you standing there? Where have you come from?”

Apu, meanwhile, had been gazing at another group of newcomers—when he suddenly turned and saw that Mejo Bou-rani was calling to him, at first he was startled—as if he couldn’t quite believe it. Then, as a wave of shyness swept over him, he wondered whether he should go up or run away—just then, Mejo Bou-rani herself came down to him. Coming close, she asked, “Where have you come from, khoka?”

With great difficulty, after much effort, words stumbled out of Apu’s mouth—“I—I—um—my mother—she stays in this house—” At once, a terrible fear seized him: that he was standing here—the cook’s son—now, hearing this, perhaps she would call someone and say, “Throw him out of here at once!”

But Mejo Bou-rani did nothing of the sort. With a look of surprise, she said, “Your mother stays in this house?... Tell me, who is she? What does she do? How long have you been here?”

Apu, in broken, halting words, gave a muddled introduction. Perhaps Mejo Bou-rani had heard something about them by now—she said, “Oh, so you’ve come from Kashi, have you?... What’s your name?” Looking into his beautiful, innocent eyes, perhaps she felt a sudden tenderness. She said, “Come on, don’t stand here—why stay in the corner? Come upstairs—”

Like a little thief, Apu followed behind Bou-rani, keeping close to the wall as he went up, and stood pressed into a corner.

Upstairs, the women’s gathering was in full swing—the whole veranda was carpeted. Along the edges, in large glazed pots, grew rose bushes and erica palms. In the corner stood the big organ of the drawing room. After some coaxing, one of the girls went and sat on a small, cushion-topped stool by the organ, pressed the keys lightly a few times—and then, after a moment’s silence, began to sing with a smiling face. The girl was not particularly pretty, her complexion was middling, but her singing voice was truly lovely. After her, another girl sang—this one was not as good-looking. Mejo Bou-rani’s daughter, Leela, recited a humorous poem, nodding her head as she spoke, making everyone laugh heartily. She was a very beautiful girl, lovely like her mother. And what a sweet smile she had!

--- Page 228 ---
Apu was wondering, why didn’t his mother come upstairs to see, just once, at this moment? Where was Ma, lost in some kitchen, perhaps busy with chores—would she ever see all this again?

The women’s gathering was in full swing when suddenly a commotion erupted downstairs. Girish Sarkar’s voice could be heard above all the rest.

Sadu jhi came up the stairs, laughing, and said—“What a mess! Just look at this! Hee hee… says he put it in the hookah… hee hee…”

A couple of the invited women asked, “What happened, re? What?”

“That thike thakur—a Brahmin cook from somewhere—came to fry luchis. He’s sitting in the Sarkars’ dining courtyard, frying luchis. Says he just stepped outside for a moment… put it in the hookah… hee hee… trying to steal it, slinking away… over half a seer… Gomosta moshai caught him… Ramnihor Singh is beating him—just look at that! Not even holding him by the hair—”

Since morning, Sarbajaya hadn’t had a moment to catch her breath. The responsibility of frying nearly two maunds of fish fell on her alone—since eight o’clock she’d been at it in the fish room. Hearing the shouting, she came outside and saw: in the middle of the courtyard, a thin, dirty, twenty-five or thirty-year-old Brahmin youth in soiled clothes was being slapped and punched by two or three people. The man was a thike radhuni—a hired cook, brought in just for today’s work. They said he was trying to steal ghee by hiding it inside a hookah. His hookah had been flung to one side, the ghee spilled on the ground nearby—his dhoti had come undone in the scuffle. The man, desperate, was trying to defend himself, insisting that finding ghee inside a hookah was the most natural, everyday thing in the world, nothing to be suspicious or astonished about—he was trying to explain this to the frenzied crowd. But before he could finish, Shambhunath Singh, the gatekeeper, shoved him so hard that he cried out, “Baba re!” and tumbled toward the corner of the house, his head striking the pillar with a loud thud—blood seemed to be coming out.

Sarbajaya asked Kshemi jhi, “What happened, Kshemi-mashi? Ah, why are they beating him like that? He’s a Brahmin boy…”

Kshemi replied, “Why wouldn’t they beat him! They’ll grind his bones to dust… This is nothing yet… They’ll hand him to the police, a jackal’s den in a tiger’s lair—”

But Kshemi jhi’s words trailed off, unfinished.

--- Page 229 ---
May the rains fall in due season,  
May the earth be rich with grain,  
May the people be free from illness...

In the evenings, as the conch shells and bells resound from temple to temple, the crimson glow of the setting sun mingles with the strains of the Purabi raga, and it seems as if the sorrow of the deer-like boy’s separation and the grief of the royal sage blend together in the air.

At home, I take paper and pen from Baba and say—  
“Won’t you write it down for me, Baba? That song you sing—‘kale varshatu parjanyam’?”

Harihar smiles with delight—  
“So you listen, Khoka?”

—“Of course I do, every day. Yesterday, when you were telling about Bharata’s mother’s death, I was sitting right behind you—on the steps of Shashthi’s temple—”

—“How does it make you feel? Do you like it?”

—“Very, very much. I listen every single day—”

But Apu hides one thing. On days when his friends are around, he doesn’t go to his father. That day, as he was passing by his father, his father spotted him and called out—  
“Khoka, hey Khoka—”

His companion asked,  
“Does he know you?”

Apu only nodded, yes. He didn’t go to his father that day. His father was telling stories at the ghat, and he was afraid his friends might find out! Except for Poltu’s dada, he told the other boys that they had a house in Kashi, that they’d come here for a change of air, that they had a grand house back home, his father was a contractor, and besides, they owned zamindari lands too. In the end, he’d say—  
“But what’s the use of zamindari, what’s left after paying the installments?”

Perhaps because his friends weren’t much older than him, they never noticed the mismatch between his stories and his simple clothes—especially since his beautiful face made everything seem believable.

On the day of the full moon, there was quite a crowd at Kathak Thakur’s place. After dusk, when Harihar finished his tale and was resting on the ghat’s steps, Kathak Thakur went down to the water to wash his hands and face. Seeing Harihar, he said—  
“Ah, you’re here too! Did you see the crowd today, on the full moon? I’d say today’s a good day—got some Brahmin alms going. If every month in Kashi we had Brahmin alms like this, we’d get fifteen seers—half a maund—of rice each time. But these days, even during the grandest Brahmin alms, people don’t fry anything—can’t get a single grain of rice! And out of the five coins, two are fake double-annas!… Babu, where’s your teaching now?”

—“I did teach here in Kashi, a long time ago. But I’ve been back in the village all these years—now, for the first time, I’ve settled here again…”

--- Page 230 ---
A few days later, as Apu was passing by the verandah, he saw Leela, the daughter of Mejo Bou-Rani (the second eldest sister-in-law), coming down the stairs. Seeing him, she called out—“Wait, won’t you? What’s your name—isn’t it Apu?”

Apu replied, “Ma calls me Apu—my good name is Shri Apurba Kumar Ray…”

She looked a little surprised. None of the children of this house had ever called out to him or spoken to him before. Leela came closer and stood beside him. What a beautiful face! Ranudi, Atosidi, Amoladi—they were all pleasant to look at, but until then, he had never seen anyone lovelier than them. Ever since coming to this house, all his previous notions had completely changed. Especially, he had never even imagined a girl as beautiful as Mejo Bou-Rani. Leela, too, was as beautiful as her mother—just the other day, when Leela was reciting a humorous poem in the girls’ gathering, Apu had gazed fixedly at her face; he hadn’t really listened to the poem at all.

Leela said, “How long have you all been coming to our house? I didn’t see you last time you came?”

“We came in the month of Phalgun, this very Phalgun month—”

“Where have you come from?”

“From Kashi. My father passed away there, you see—that’s why—”

It was as if Apu himself could hardly believe it. The whole incident still seemed unreal, impossible. Leela, Mejo Bou-Rani’s daughter Leela, had called out to him and was standing here, talking to him! His whole body tingled with happiness.

Leela said, “Come, let’s go sit in my study room, it’s almost time for Mastermoshai (the tutor) to arrive—come—”

Apu asked, “Shall I come?”

Leela laughed and said, “Why, I’m telling you to come, aren’t I? You’re so terribly shy!—Come—you haven’t seen my study room? It’s in the corner of the western verandah…”

The room wasn’t very big, but it was nicely arranged. On either side of a small stone table were two leather-cushioned chairs. There was a large calendar with pictures. A small timepiece in a green glass case. A little drawer for keeping books. Four or five framed photographs on this wall and that. Leela opened a leather attaché case and said—“Look, these are my watercolor paintings, Mastermoshai bought them for me, if I learn fractions well he’ll get me more, do you know how to do fractions?”

Apu said, “You don’t know fractions?”

“Do you know? Have you done fractions?”

Apu curled his lip with disdain and said, “Of course, ages ago!…”

In that gesture, Apu’s beautiful face looked even more strikingly handsome.

--- Page 231 ---
—Oh, it’s nothing, moshai, I’m just heading out as the month of Phagun (spring) approaches—there’s a garden I own, I’ll go and sell it—after all, we are shrotriya (a Brahmin lineage), you see?... So, there were ten bighas of land, I mortgaged it to arrange the dowry—got married too—moshai, I kept house for ten years—do you know what happened? One evening, she went up to the kitchen roof to cut a pumpkin—moshai, there was a snake lying in wait for me—bit her hand—of course, I wasn’t home that day—who knows which quack or village healer they called, what they did—crossing the ghat at Patuli—the village’s Mahesh Sadhukhan was coming from the other side, he told me—Go home quickly, moshai—there’s great trouble at your house—he didn’t say what kind of trouble—when I reached home, I saw that my wife had died the night before!—That’s how it went, moshai... and the land was lost too—on that side as well— Since then, I keep saying, what’s the point of staying in the village—where will I get three or four hundred rupees to marry again?... So I go to Bishwanath’s place... at least there won’t be any hunger... It’s been eight years now—there’s a cousin—whatever little land there is, he’s taken it over—says there’s no share for me—well, fine, if there’s none, there’s none—I’ll never get into a quarrel—let him keep what he’s grabbed. I’ll take my leave, moshai—had a nice cup of tea here—where’s your boy gone?... Fine boy, a splendid boy—

Brushing off his old, patched canvas shoes, the storyteller slipped them on and came to the door—As he was leaving, he said—Tomorrow too, I’ll go begging as a brahmin—let’s see what happens—

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-One**

Harihar’s house was nothing special. The rooms on the ground floor were damp, and there were only two of them, so dark that if you came in suddenly from outside, you couldn’t make out a single thing. Sarbajaya had never lived in such a place before; even though their ancestral house in the village was old, it had big doors and windows where sunlight and wind played, the high foundations of the old days, echoing and dry. The damp floors and darkness of this house gave Sarbajaya headaches. Apu never stayed indoors at all, his face always turned toward the light like a young sun-fed tree; raised in the open fields and river air of Nischindipur, his soul suffocated in the darkness of this closed room, he couldn’t bear to stay there even for a moment.

Seeing Kashi, he felt a little disappointed. What use are big houses if there are no forests here at all?

One evening, as dusk fell, the storyteller came to Harihar’s house. After some small talk, he said—Where’s your boy? Haven’t seen him around?

--- Page 232 ---
Leela looked a little sad. She said, “Won’t you take it?... I’ll get another one from Baba, you take this, let me see your hand? There!... And you can’t give it back.”

The whole matter seemed utterly unreal to Apu. He said, “But what if someone scolds you?”

Leela replied, “For giving away a fountain pen? No one will scold me, I’ll tell Ma I gave it to Apurba—I’ll get another one from Baba—do you want to see Baba’s photo?... There, hanging next to the calendar—wait, I’ll show you—”

Then Leela showed him two or three more photos. She took out a few books from the cupboard and said, “Mastermoshai (respected teacher) bought these for me—which school do you go to?”

Apu had only attended school in Kashi for a few days, and never since. He said, “I used to go to school in Kashi, but now I don’t—” He felt embarrassed saying it, so the last part came out in a tone as if not going to school was something to be proud of. One of the books was full of pictures. Apu asked, “Will you let me read this book, just once?”

Leela said, “Take it, won’t you? I have many more picture books, three years’ worth of bound Mukul magazines, in Ma’s cupboard, I’ll bring them for you, read—”

Apu said, “I have books too, should I bring them?”

Leela said, “Let’s go, let’s visit your house—”

Apu felt shy about taking Leela to their own room. There was hardly any furniture, the pillowcases were torn, and an old quilt hung on the rack. Still, Leela went. Apu opened his tin box and, with a smile of pride, showed her a book. “I wrote this, look, my name is printed here—”

Leela quickly took it from his hand. “Let me see, let me see?”

It was the magazine from his school in Kashi. Harihar had never seen his son’s story in print; the paper came out three days after his death. Leela began to read. Apu sat beside her, his face alight with excitement, following Leela’s eyes as she read each line, silently reading along in his own mind. When she finished, Leela looked at Apu with eyes full of admiration and said, “It’s really good, I’ll take this and show it to Ma—”

Apu felt terribly embarrassed. He said, “No—”

Leela didn’t listen. She kept the paper in her hand. She asked, “It says Nishchindipur here, where is Nishchindipur?”

“Nishchindipur is our village—that’s where our real home is—we’ve only been in Kashi for about a year—”

--- Page 233 ---
At that moment, little Mokshada came to the doorway, poked her head inside, and said—“Oh my didimoni, you’re sitting here? My goodness! Masterbabu is sitting there getting tired, and I’ve searched every room upstairs and down—who would have guessed you’d be in this damp, musty storeroom—come, come—”

Leela said—“Go on, I’m coming, you go—”

Little Mokshada said—“Is this any place to sit? That’s why we all get headaches—do those stable-hands ever sweep or wash the horse stalls? Ugh, just smell it—come, didimoni, quickly—”

Leela said—“I’m not coming, go away; I’m not studying today, say what you want—who told you to come chatter here? Go tell Ma, go—”

Little Mokshada stomped off in a huff. Apu said—“Won’t your Ma scold you? Why did you talk to her like that?”

The next afternoon, he was napping in his own room. Awakened by someone’s nudging, he opened his eyes to see Leela sitting by his bed, smiling. He had been sleeping on a mat on the floor; Leela was kneeling beside him, nudging him awake, still gazing at him with those playful, wide eyes. With a smile, she said—“Well, well, so you nap like this in the middle of the day? I called from outside, and when I came in, there you were, fast asleep—”

Apu quickly sat up, rubbing his eyes with the edge of his cloth. He said—“Didn’t you come to teach me this morning? I looked all over the study rooms, but no one was anywhere—”

Leela handed Apu the school paper—“I read it to Ma last night, she read it herself too.”

Apu’s whole body tingled with delight. He felt terribly shy and embarrassed. The middle bou-rani (elder sister-in-law) had read his writing!

Leela said—“Come to my study room, I’ve brought out the bound ‘Sakha-Sathi’ (a children’s magazine) just for you—”

Apu glanced at the clothes rack. His good clothes weren’t dry yet, and the ones he was wearing weren’t fit to go out in. He said—“I can’t come now—”

Leela asked in surprise—“Why not?”

Apu pressed his lips together and shook his head with a mischievous smile. He didn’t know how beautiful his face looked in that moment.

Leela pleaded—“Come, come—”

Apu stifled another smile.

—“Oh, what a stubborn boy you are! If you say no, there’s no way to get a yes, is there? All right, wait, I’ll bring the book here—”

Apu couldn’t hold back his laughter and burst out giggling.

Leela said—“What’s so funny? What happened—come on, you have to tell me—tell me the truth—”

--- Page 234 ---
Apu only glanced toward the clothesline, his eyes brimming with laughter, but said nothing.

This time Leela understood. She went over to the clothesline, touched it with her hand, and said, “It’s dried a little, you sit, I’ll bring the book—are you writing with a fountain pen? How is it, does it write well?”

After that, for a long while, they looked at the book Leela had brought. Spreading the book out on the mat, the two of them sat side by side, knees folded, leaning over the book, poring over its pages. Leela’s hair, soft and fine as silk, brushed against Apu’s bare arm, sending a shiver through him. Suddenly, Leela lifted her face from the book and asked, “Do you know how to sing?”

Apu shook his head.

“Then sing something—”

“Do you know how?”

“A little, a little. Didn’t you hear on the wedding day?”

Little Mokshada, the maid, poked her head into the room and said, “Here’s didimoni (elder sister)—I was just thinking, not upstairs, not in the study, then surely—come now, have this milk, it’s gone cold—searched everywhere, wore myself out—”

A glass of milk in a small silver tumbler! Leela said, “Leave it here—come back later and take the glass—”

The maid left. They went back to looking at the pictures in the book for a while longer. At one point, Leela picked up the glass of milk and said, “You drink half—”

Apu replied in an embarrassed tone, “No.”

“I have to coax you for everything—why are you like this? This is milk from our Multani cow—drink it—it’s thick as kheer (rice pudding), good boy—”

Apu squinted and said, “Ugh, good boy? That’s so—what’s this now—”

Leela pressed the glass of milk to Apu’s lips, shook her head, and said, “No need to be shy—I’ll close my eyes, go on—”

Apu took a quick sip, drank some of the milk, then lowered his face, hurriedly wiped the milk mustache from his upper lip with the edge of his cloth, and burst out laughing.

Leela took a sip from the glass and finished the rest of the milk, then she too broke into peals of laughter.

“Isn’t the milk sweet?”

“Why did you drink after me? Isn’t there more for you?”

“I wanted to—” She paused a moment, then said, “You said you know how to paint watercolors, but you don’t, do you? Come, paint a few watercolors for me today?”

**From Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-Five**

--- Page 235 ---
Compassion and love are the most precious spices of all, their weaving together forms a strong, enduring braid. In her childhood heart, this foreigner—this Bengali kathak-thakur (storyteller), known only for a couple of days—became entwined with her didi and Gulki, all together, as if in the eager, impatient gesture of reaching for a single laddu to eat.

Not long after, the kathak-thakur returned to his homeland. At Rajghat station, at the storyteller’s insistent urging, Harihar took Apu along to see him off and help him board the train. Harihar felt that, twenty-two years ago, he himself had gone home to do just what this man, at least eight years older than Harihar is now, was doing—to begin a new household, to start life afresh. So then, what of his own age—was it really so much? Could he truly lack time to do anything?

As the train departed, tears welled up in Apu’s eyes. Sometimes, in a child’s heart, a deep and lasting affection for an elder takes root. Precisely because it is rare, it is all the more precious.

Toward the end of the month of Magh, one day, Harihar suddenly entered the house and sat down by the edge of the courtyard. Whatever Sarbajaya had been doing, she dropped it at once and hurried over, asking, “What’s happened? Why have you sat down like that?” Looking at her husband’s face, the rest of her words caught in her throat. Harihar’s eyes were red as hibiscus flowers, and his right hand seemed to tremble. As Sarbajaya reached out to help him up, he spoke in a dazed, feverish voice, “Where’s Khoka? Khoka?”

Sarbajaya touched his body and found it burning with fever. Gently, she took his hand and led him inside, laying him down on the bed. She said, “Apu is coming, Nandababu from upstairs took him, probably to his shop at the Godhuliya crossing—”

--- Page 236 ---
Apu did not go to the shop; he was sitting on the roof in front of Nandababu’s house, reading a book. For about a month now, Apu had grown quite friendly with Nandababu. He could not quite figure out how old Nandababu was, but he seemed younger than Apu’s father. In the upstairs room of Nandababu’s house, he had discovered many books—when Nandababu was home, Apu would take a book and sit on the roof to read. But he was always a little afraid that Nandababu might snatch the book away and forbid him from reading, because something like that had happened once before. One day, Apu was sitting in a corner of the roof, reading in the sunlight, when Nandababu, searching for something inside the house, came out and saw him. Scolding, he said, “Hey, put that down! Always sitting and reading those books, you never keep things where they belong, and then when I need something, I can’t find it—go on, put the book back, go—”

He never touched anything else in the house, so why should he be scolded? Since then, he always took the books with a bit of fear.

In the evenings, Nandababu would shave, put on good clothes, and dab himself with some fragrant oil from a little bottle before going out for a walk. One day, he had sprinkled a little of that scent on Apu too, and the fragrance was strong and heady.

Even after dusk, Apu used to go to Nandababu’s room to read. But after evening, Nandababu would take a bottle from the cupboard and drink a reddish medicine. One time, when Apu went into the room at that hour, Nandababu had scolded him harshly. The stairs to Nandababu’s house were on the other side—and one night, going upstairs suddenly, Apu had seen a woman sitting inside the room. Seeing him, Nandababu had said, “Now go, Apurba, this is my shali (sister-in-law)—she’s come to visit, she’ll leave soon.” As Apu was coming back down, he heard Nandababu say, “He’s the son of our downstairs tenant—doesn’t really understand much.”

Nandababu often asked him about his mother. He would say, “Tell your mother to send up some paan (betel leaf)? My servant doesn’t know how to prepare it—”
