Apu would plead with his mother and often bring paan upstairs. Sometimes Nandababu would ask, “Does your mother ever say anything about me—no?”

Apu would come home and tell his mother, “Nandababu is a good man, Ma—he asks about you every day—”

—“About me? What does he ask about me—”

—“He said, tell your mother I ask after her—he’s a good man—”

—“Let him! But you naughty boy, why do you keep going upstairs to his room? What do you do sitting up there in the afternoons?”

--- Page 237 ---
Harihar’s fever had eased a little. Apu, just returned from school, was putting down his books. Hearing the sound of his footsteps, Harihar called out—“Khoka, come here, sit with me for a bit, baba—”

Apu sat down and began to tell stories from school. Smiling, in a soft voice, he said—“I’ve been going to school these past two months, and already, baba, everyone at school likes me a lot. Every day, I get to sit in the first row. In our class, they’re going to print a little paper, once every month. They’ve taken me into the group—I’ll show you, baba, when it comes out—”

Inside Harihar’s chest, a strange ache of tenderness and sorrow stirred. Apu pulled out a sheet of paper and said—“I wrote something—a story. They said they’ll print it, with my name—but only the writings of those who give two taka as a subscription will be printed—will you give two taka, baba?”

Harihar, eager and curious, took the paper from his son’s hand and began to read. He hadn’t known that his boy could write. The story of the prince’s hunt was beautifully crafted. Pleased, Harihar propped himself up on his pillow and asked—“You wrote this, khoka?”

—“I’ve written so many more, baba—ghost stories, tales of princesses—when we lived at home, I’d write them all out for Ranudi in her notebook—”

Hearing talk of money, Sarbajaya did not want to give it. With her husband ill, even what little they had would not be enough for the household expenses. There was no need for a printed paper. But Harihar, gently persuading her, gave the money, saying—“Let him be, let Khoka’s writing get printed. Once I recover and perform the Bhagavat reading at the Thakur-bari (the local zamindar’s house), we’ll get a good ten taka from that—”

A couple of days later, Apu came quietly to his father, his lips pouting in disappointment—“It didn’t work out, baba. The press people asked for more money, so today at school they said the subscription will be four taka—”

The look of disappointment on his son’s face pierced Harihar’s heart. After a little while, talking of other things, he said—“See where your mother’s gone, khoka… Take this—” He pulled out a bunch of keys from under his pillow and handed them to Apu. “Quietly open that wooden box, the one with my bundle of bamboo pens—open it, will you? See how much money is in the corner?” Then Harihar gazed at his son, busy with the box, with eyes full of tenderness. Innocent, innocent, utterly innocent! That beautiful, pure, moon-like forehead was his mother’s; those eyes, his mother’s eyes. When Harihar, in his youth, first brought his bride home, the very same smile that once lit the face of the new bride Sarbajaya now shone on the unclouded, youthful face of eleven-year-old Apu.

--- Page 238 ---
For no reason at all, a sea of tenderness would suddenly surge and swell within Harihar’s chest, bringing tears to his eyes.

Apu seemed like the first tender shoot of spring, the joy on his face like the fresh crimson glow of dawn; in the gaze of those wide, blue-tinged eyes, Harihar saw the boundless dreams of his own lost youth, the jubilant rustling of young sal trees on sunlit blue hills… the distant music of a shoreless sea.

Apu, in a hushed voice, showed his father—“I have four rupees, Baba!” Harihar had set aside a few coins for times of need, hidden away in his own box, unknown to his wife. So, with an easy heart, he could say, “Take it, khoka, pay the subscription. But don’t tell your mother.”

Apu replied in a lilting tone, “When it’s printed, I’ll show you, Baba. They said they’ll print it in my name—it’ll come out the Monday after this one—”

The next morning, Harihar’s illness worsened again.

Sarbojaya, frightened, said to her son, “Go tell Nandababu, will you—ask him to come and take a look—”

Nandababu came and, after seeing Harihar, said, “A doctor needs to be called, Apurba. Tell your mother.”

That evening, it was Nandababu who brought a doctor with him. The doctor examined Harihar and said, “He’s caught a chill, it’s bronchopneumonia—he needs good nursing. Does he always stay like this in the downstairs room?... Khoka, bring a bottle to my dispensary, I’ll give you medicine—”

For several days, Apu went to the doctor’s dispensary above Dashashwamedh Ghat to fetch medicine.

But there was no noticeable improvement. Day by day, Harihar grew weaker. The little money they had was spent on doctor’s visits and medicine. The doctor said, unless the patient was given at least a seer of milk and other fruits daily, he would become dangerously weak. He also prescribed a foreign tonic costing three and a half rupees. In this strange, distant land, there was no one to offer even a word of comfort. Sarbojaya saw darkness closing in on all sides.

--- Page 239 ---
In the midst of this calamity, Sarbajaya found herself caught in yet another predicament. From the upper terrace, if one leaned over, the kitchen could be seen below. She had noticed before, now and then, that Nandababu would peer down from the terrace, sneaking glances toward her kitchen. But ever since Harihar had fallen ill, Nandababu’s visits had grown more frequent, more blatant. On various pretexts, he would come into the house ten times a day—at first, he would ask after Apu, trying to keep his questions discreet. Lately, though, he addressed her directly, speaking to her without hesitation.

At first, Sarbajaya thought nothing of it—rather, she felt a quiet gratitude, believing that in this time of trouble, this unrelated man was offering genuine help and care. But gradually, she began to sense that this growing familiarity—this excess—felt somehow out of place, discordant. Nandababu would buy betel leaves himself, set them before her, and say, “The servants can’t prepare a proper paan—my life’s wasted, bou-thakurun (a respectful term for the lady of the house)—won’t you make one for me, just this once?” Even then, Sarbajaya found no fault in him; if anything, she felt a twinge of pity for this lonely, affection-starved man, far from home. But the intimacy was slowly crossing the bounds of propriety.

These days, he would bring the paan and say, “Here, bou-thakurun, keep this.” It was as if he wanted her to take it directly from his hand. Apu, of course, was a wild one—most of the time, he was nowhere to be found in the house. In the other room, Harihar lay unconscious, and it was precisely at these times that Nandababu would come, ostensibly to check on the patient! With one excuse or another, he would linger, spinning out half an hour in idle conversation before leaving the room. He would say, “Don’t worry, bou-thakurun—I’m upstairs. Whether Apu is here or not—just call me from the stairs if you need anything! In times of trouble, you can’t be too particular... Pass me a bit of lime, will you? No stem? Ah, just give me a little with your fingertip, then—like that—”

Whenever Harihar regained consciousness, he would become restless for his son. Looking around weakly, he would call out in a faint voice, “Khoka, where’s Khoka!” Sarbajaya would reply, “He’s coming, what a wretched boy, can’t he sit by you for a bit... Must have gone off to the ghat again.” When the boy returned home, Harihar would say, “Can’t you sit by me for a while!...” He would call, “Khoka, Khoka,” like a madman—Khoka couldn’t sleep for worrying about him—“Go on, sit with him, can’t you run your hand over his head and body a little? Is this how a son behaves, ringing the bell in heaven while his father lies here?”

--- Page 240 ---
Apu sits by his father’s pillow, awkward and ill at ease. But after a while, he thinks—Oh! How long must I sit here—really? Am I not allowed to go out for a bit, to play? In the biting cold, his feet grow numb. His heart squirms—he longs to dash off, all the way to that Dashashwamedh Ghat. The king of the waters, the pure, free air, the throng of well-dressed men and women. Poltu… Subir… Gulu… Patal—Poltu’s dada. Today, once again, at four o’clock, the peacock-barge of the Raja of Ramnagar! Restless, he lets the evening slip by out of shyness, and for fear of his mother, he doesn’t dare go.

One morning, Sarbajaya says to her son, “Hey, do you know that chhatr (community kitchen) beside the white house?”

—“No—”

—“You’ve never eaten at the chhatr, not even once since coming here? In Kashi, you must eat at the chhatr, you know! Won’t you go eat there today?… Or at least go see it?”

—“Why must one eat at the chhatr in Kashi?”

—“It brings punya (spiritual merit)—today, after bathing at Dashashwamedh Ghat, go eat at the chhatr, all right?”

At noon, after eating at the chhatr, Apu returns home. His mother is sitting on the kitchen veranda, eating something from a bowl—at first, she tries to hide it on seeing him, but Apu has come too close—if she hides it now, it’ll arouse suspicion, so she tries to speak in a casual tone—“You’ve eaten? How was the food?”

Ma is eating soaked arhar dal (split pigeon peas).

—“Not good—the pumpkin curry was just ashes—sitting there was exhausting—so many people in dirty clothes come to eat—I’m not going again, I don’t care for punya—What are you eating, Ma? Is that your bertho (leftovers)? Didn’t you cook?”

—“Today is my Kuluchandi (a fasting ritual)—just these two soaked arhar dals—it tastes quite nice—I really like it… Want to eat some this evening?”

There is no cooking at night either. His mother says, “Try some soaked arhar dal, will you? You’ll like it now—I didn’t cook this time, you eat so well, it’s not as if you sit down to so many plates of rice—after eating that, could you eat anything more?”

--- Page 241 ---
The next afternoon, Nandababu handed a bundle of betel leaves to Apu and said, “Go get these prepared by your mother, will you?” In the room next to the sickroom, Sarbajaya was sitting, making paan. Nandababu, his shoes clacking on the floor, came down from upstairs and entered the sickroom, and after only a brief moment, emerged and stepped into the room where Sarbajaya was preparing the betel leaves. 

Having spent the whole night awake, Sarbajaya was nodding off; the sound of shoes startled her awake, and seeing Nandababu standing right in front of her, she shrank back, hastily pulling her sari’s veil over her head. 

Nandababu said, “Is the paan ready, Bou-thakrun?” (Bou-thakrun: a respectful term for a married woman of the house.) Silently, Sarbajaya pushed the prepared paan rolls toward him on a tray. Nandababu picked one up, popped it into his mouth, and said, “You always put too little lime in your paan, Bou-thakrun. Let me see, I’ll take it myself—”

The betel box was by Sarbajaya’s lap. There was no one else in the house; Apu had gone somewhere outside. In the next room, Harihar was sleeping under the influence of medicine. The afternoon was utterly still. Suddenly, Sarbajaya felt as if—under the pretext of taking lime—Nandababu was unnecessarily, uncomfortably, trying to come too close to her. With a vague cry, in a flash, she stood up and stepped outside the room. 

A current, like a bolt of lightning, shot from her feet to her head. Pointing to the stairs with her finger, she said in a sharp, trembling voice, “Go upstairs this instant—never come down again—if you come down, I’ll dash my head and die—why do you come here? Don’t you dare come again—”

Sarbajaya was thrown into great distress. In this foreign place, in the sickroom, utterly helpless, not a coin in her hand, not a single familiar soul anywhere, her son only about eleven years old—and even he, so naïve, so utterly innocent. And now, on top of everything, these new troubles.

--- Page 242 ---
The Punjabi woman upstairs rarely came down—once or twice, she had taken Sarbajaya up to her room, but even after living in Kashi for five or six months, Sarbajaya could neither speak Hindi nor understand it well, so no real friendship had blossomed between them. Today, Sarbajaya went to her and, recounting Nandababu’s incident in detail, broke down in tears. The Punjabi woman’s name was Surjakumari; both husband and wife were from the Roalser district of Punjab, her husband worked as an overseer for the railways. Though she was not very young, she looked youthful—fair-skinned, wide-eyed, with a tall, slender, tightly-knit frame. Hearing everything, she said, “Don’t be afraid, stay here without worry. If you see any more mischief, tell me—I’ll have my husband cut off his nose and teach him a lesson…”

It was exactly noon. After several nights without sleep, Sarbajaya had spread her anchal (the loose end of her sari) on the floor and fallen asleep. Through the window of the north room, a slant of sunlight fell crookedly across the narrow courtyard. Apu had planted marigold saplings in a clay pot; two or three marigolds bloomed there, looking thoroughly annoyed—beneath them, a kitten sat curled up. Apu was sitting right beside his father’s bed. Since morning, his father seemed a little better—the doctor had said perhaps there was hope for life. Yet even in this better state, the patient did not seem fully conscious, more like a stupor. Suddenly, his father opened his eyes and gazed at him for a while, saying something indistinct. Apu felt as if his father was asking him to come closer. As Apu moved nearer, Harihar, with his disease-worn, feeble hands, drew his son’s neck into an embrace and, in silence, gazed long and intently into his face. Apu was a little surprised; he had never seen such a look in his father’s eyes before.

At ten o’clock that night, Apu awoke to some sound. A faint light glimmered in the room—Ma was sleeping deeply, but from Baba’s throat came a strange, many-toned sound. Apu felt a creeping fear. The cobwebbed beams, the damp floor, the bone-breaking cold, the smoke from the coal fire—all together, it felt like a hard, unyielding nightmare. If only Baba would recover, then life could go on!

In the last hours before dawn, his mother shook him awake—“Apu, O Apu, get up, quickly go upstairs and call the Hindustani bou (daughter-in-law, here meaning the Punjabi woman)!”

Apu got up and heard that strange sound from his father’s throat had grown louder. Not long after Surjakumari came down from upstairs, at four in the morning, Harihar died.

--- Page 243 ---
On a mid-monsoon day, drenched with rain and shrouded in mist, it seems as if the sunlit days of this earth were but a dream—were they ever real? These clouds, this gloom, these endless days of sorrow—are they to be my only companions on the path to an infinite future? Like the mirage on the horizon, have those days of Chaitra and Baisakh—days of golden spring and summer—vanished into the past, never to return?

From all sides, a strange fog seemed to close in around Sarbojaya. Through its thickness, neither the path could be seen, nor could one recognize a companion, nor even know where one was. She wondered if this mist would ever lift, even when the sun rose at midday; behind it, there lingered the pale, gray clouds of an untimely monsoon, stretching across the sky all day long.

In these days of peril, the Punjabi overseer, Jalim Singh, and his wife offered much help. Jalim Singh skipped work at the office and wandered through the Bengali quarter, searching for people to help with the funeral rites. Hearing the news, a few volunteers from the Ramakrishna Mission also arrived.

After the rites at Manikarnika Ghat, as evening fell, Apu bathed and, shivering in the cool western breeze, climbed up onto the platform. One of the Ramakrishna Mission volunteers and Nandababu were helping him put on his uttariya, the ritual scarf. The day was drawing to a close; the faint light of the setting sun glimmered only on the thresholds of the stone temples. Lost in the tumult of the day, Apu felt as though, in his father’s familiar voice, someone sat reciting before an eager gathering of listeners—

kāle varṣatu parjanyaṃ pṛthivī śasyashālinī...

lokāḥ santu nirāmayāḥ...

The father whom they had all brought today to the burning ghat at Manikarnika—defeated by illness, vanquished in the battle of life—that father was now but a dream. Apu no longer knew him, could not recognize him. The vessel of his lifelong trust, his familiar, smiling father—ever since Apu could remember, in that easy, melodious voice, in that sweet, resonant tone, as if sitting somewhere, he sang every day, in the wistful melody of a Purabi, the song of blessing—

kāle varṣatu parjanyaṃ pṛthivī śasyashālinī...

lokāḥ santu nirāmayāḥ...

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-Two**

--- Page 244 ---
About a month passed somehow. In this one month, Sarbajaya thought of many ways out, but none seemed right to her. It’s not as if the thought of returning home didn’t cross her mind once or twice, but whenever it did, she quickly suppressed it. First of all, except for the homestead, everything else back home had been sold off—some to pay debts, some simply sold away—there was no land or property left. Secondly, before leaving there, she had painted so many pictures of their future happiness to the other wives and daughters-in-law along the roads and lanes of the village. She had told them, just wait until we leave Nishchindipur, here in this wretched, ignorant place no one recognizes her husband’s worth, but wherever they were going, everyone would welcome them with open arms, and their fortunes would change in less than a year—how many ways had Sarbajaya tried to make them understand, waving her hands and gesturing! Now it was the month of Chaitra, not even a full year had passed. And yet, in this destitute, penniless state, to return there in widow’s garb and stand before everyone—just the thought of it made her want to sink into the earth with shame and embarrassment. Let whatever is to happen, happen here; she would take her son by the hand and beg through the streets of Kashi if need be, and raise him—who would come to see them here, anyway?

After about a month, a bit of good fortune came their way. A gentleman from Kedar Ghat informed the mission office that a wealthy family he knew was looking for a Brahmin girl—someone of their caste, who would come to their house and help with the household work. Could the mission help find such a person? In the end, through the mission’s arrangements, the gentleman agreed to send Apu and his mother there. Sarbajaya, lost in a boundless sea, suddenly found a shore. A day or two later, the gentleman sent word that they should be ready to move house entirely, because the wealthy family’s home was not in Kashi—their relatives were only visiting Kashi, and would take Sarbajaya and her son back with them when they returned.

It was a huge house, painted a bright yellow. The kind of grand house you see in Kashi, this was one of those—very large indeed. Following behind everyone, Sarbajaya entered the house with her son, shrinking in on herself.

As soon as she set foot in the inner quarters, a wave of welcome rose up—not for her, but for the group that had just returned from their outing in Kashi.

--- Page 245 ---
When the crowd and commotion had quieted a little, the mistress of the house, Sarbajaya, appeared before her. She was quite stout, though one could tell she had once been very beautiful; her age was above fifty. As soon as Sarbajaya bowed to her in greeting, the mistress said—“Enough, enough, come, come—ah, at such a young age, this—Is this your son? What a fine boy—what is his name?”

She turned to someone else and asked—“Your home is in Kashi, isn’t it? No?—Then perhaps—”

Standing before everyone’s curious gaze, Sarbajaya felt deeply embarrassed and uncomfortable. When, at the mistress’s command, a maid led her away to the room set aside for her, she breathed a sigh of relief…

From the next day, Sarbajaya began her work in the kitchen as agreed. She was not the only cook; there were four or five others. There were three or four kitchens: one for fish and meat, one for vegetarian dishes, one for milk-based preparations, one for bread, and a separate kitchen for the food of the outside staff. There seemed to be an endless number of maids and servants. Though the kitchens were within the women’s quarters, they were a bit apart—almost like a realm ruled by the maids, servants, and Brahmin cooks. The women of the house would only come to give instructions or explain something; unless there was a special reason, they rarely stayed in the kitchen.

There was much discussion about what Sarbajaya should cook. Sarbajaya had always believed she was a very good cook. She said, perhaps the responsibility for the vegetarian dishes should be given to her. The Brahmin cook Mokshada gave a sly smile and said—“You’ll cook for the babus? That would be a disaster!” Then, calling out to Panchhi, she said, “Hear that, Panchhi? This lady from Kashi says she’ll cook for the babus! What’s your name again? I keep forgetting—” The mocking smile at the corner of Mokshada’s lips left Sarbajaya deeply embarrassed that day, but within a few days she realized that none of her village-style dishes would be of any use here. That so much sugar should be added to a curry, or that there was such a thing as ‘cabbage fritters’—these were things she was hearing for the first time.

For about two months, the mistress treated Sarbajaya with great care—giving her light work, checking in on her. Gradually, she had to become equal to the other cooks. After working until two in the afternoon, at first she would feel utterly exhausted; she had never before been used to standing over the fire for so long, and at such a late hour she rarely had any appetite. The other cooks would set aside fish and curries for themselves, hiding some to eat and some to take elsewhere. Sarbajaya would sit down to her plate only once, and that was all.

--- Page 246 ---
Sarbojaya is astonished by the enormity of the cooking arrangements; never in her wildest dreams had she imagined such an elaborate affair. In wonder, she thinks to herself, “Three seers of oil for two meals a day? Every day, the expense of oil and ghee is like a yajna (ritual sacrifice)!” With her experience of a small, poor household in a rural village, she cannot quite grasp any of this.

One day, as she was taking down the large pot of rice cooked with fine-grained rice, she called out to Mokshada Bamini, “O masima (auntie), will you hold the pot for a moment?”

Mokshada, though she heard, pretended not to.

Meanwhile, seeing that the rice was starting to stick, Sarbojaya tried to take down the heavy pot herself. She tilted it, and the hot froth spilled onto the top of her foot, instantly raising blisters. That very day, the mistress of the house transferred her to the bread room and told her she would not have to do any work until her foot healed.

Sarbojaya lives with her son in a room downstairs. The room is right beside the western wing of the house. But it is so low-ceilinged, the floor so damp, and there is always such a musty smell in the room that even their house in Kashi was better than this. The lower part of the wall is crusted with salt, with large reddish stains. Every time they enter from outside, Apu says, “Ugh, do you smell that, Ma? What is it—smells just like old rice or something, doesn’t it?” These lower rooms were never meant for people to live in; that’s why the servants, cooks, and attendants stay in them.

Apu has wandered through all the rooms of the upper wing, peering in from outside—such big windows and doors! All the windows have glass panes. In every room, there are large, cushioned chairs, gleaming tables—so polished you could see your face in them. At Apu’s house, there were old mats made from worn-out carpets, but here, the carpets are much better—thick, almost new—spread across the floor. Mirrors hang on the walls, so large that Apu can see his whole self in them. He wonders to himself, “Where do they get such big pieces of glass? Maybe they join them together…”

On the second-floor veranda, there is another large room—almost always locked, sometimes opened by the servants to let in air and light. Apu’s curiosity about what lies within is insatiable. One day, seeing the door open, he slipped inside. Such enormous paintings! Stone statues! Huge cushioned chairs—mirrors—he wandered about, looking at everything. Just then, Chhutu the khansama (household steward) caught sight of him in the room and came up, blocking his way, “Who are you? Why did you sneak in here?”

--- Page 247 ---
Perhaps that day he would have been beaten, but just then one of the housemaids, passing by the verandah, saw what was happening and said—“Hey, little one, let him go, don’t say anything—his mother lives here—let him watch if he wants—”

When everyone had finished eating, at around half past two in the afternoon, Sarbajaya would come to her own room and lie down for a while. Only at this time of day could she truly open her heart and speak with her son, so sometimes Apu would come to the room at this hour. Once a day, his mother wanted to see him. Ever since coming to this house, it was as if Apu had drifted far away. All day, it was work and more work—she had to stay apart from her son. By the time she finished her chores late at night and returned, Apu would already be asleep; there was no chance to talk. Her heart thirsted for this one afternoon.

There were footsteps at the door. Sarbajaya called out—“Who is it, Apu! Come in—” Pushing the door open, Bamni mashi entered the room. Sarbajaya said—“Come in, masima, sit down.” Right then, Apu also came in. Bamni mashi was a relative of the babus (the landlord’s family), so she was treated with special respect. Bamni mashi’s face was heavy and grave. After sitting silently for a while, she said—“Did you see what happened today, boro-bouma (eldest daughter-in-law)? Tell me, what was the fault... You were always in the sickroom, weren’t you? She brought fish and ghee and left them in the basket, I thought it was for the cabbage curry—do you see what kind of insult that was? If it was for the polao, couldn’t she have just told the maid? Is Sadhu’s wife any less of a sly fox?... The mistress’s favorite maid, is she? She walks as if she doesn’t touch the ground, goes upstairs and acts like a queen—wasn’t that Chirikantha Thakur there too—let him say, then?”

The afternoon passed in conversation. Mashi said, “I should go, need to knead the flour for the snacks—it’s already four o’clock—”

After mashi left, Apu nestled close to his mother. She stroked his chin affectionately and asked—“Tell me, where do you spend your afternoons?...”

Apu replied with a smile—“There’s a gramophone playing in the upstairs sitting room, Ma—I was listening to it—from the verandah—”

Sarbajaya was pleased.

—“So, do you get along with the babus’ children?... Do they call you to sit with them?...”

—“Very much!”

--- Page 248 ---
Apu told a lie. No one ever calls him to sit with them. Whenever he hears the sound of the gramophone playing in the upstairs sitting room, he hesitates a little, then, with a flutter of fear, climbs the stairs. He stands silently by the door, listening to the music. Every moment, he’s afraid—maybe this time they’ll tell him to go back downstairs. When the song ends and he’s on his way down, he thinks—No one scolded me, did they? Why would they? I just stood outside and listened to the music; I didn’t go inside the babus’ room, did I? They’re good people, very good—

He didn’t get to mix with the boys of this house either. They didn’t pay him any attention at all. That day, Romen, Tebu, Samir, Santu—they had set up a square wooden board like a low stool in front of them and were playing a game with black wooden discs, a game called carrom, apparently. He stood a little distance away, watching them play. Beegun-bichi, the game with eggplant seeds, was much better than this.

At the start of Boishakh, the house grew lively in preparation for the wedding of the elder babu’s daughter. From Gaya, Munger, Allahabad, Kolkata, Kashi—relatives began to arrive from all over. All of them daughters and daughters-in-law of wealthy families, each accompanied by their own maids and servants. At night, they took over the downstairs verandas and halls. All night, there was a commotion.

In the morning, the mistress called Sarbojaya and said, “O, Apurbo’s mother, do one thing—let the kitchen work be yours for a couple of days. Offerings are coming from all sorts of places. You and little Mokshada arrange everything and store them in your bread-room’s pantry. Keep the sweets there; if you see any fruit or fried treats that look like they’ll spoil, send them to Sadu-jhi, otherwise keep them—Bamuni mashi will bring them out at breakfast time—”

From morning till evening, so many gifts arrived on the heads of maids and bearers from so many places that Sarbojaya couldn’t even count them. There was no more space for sweets; the little silver and sandalwood bowls piled up to fifteen or sixteen. Mangoes hadn’t even come into season yet, but still, a big basket was filled to the brim with them.

As Sarbojaya handed food to Bamuni mashi, she thought—So much good food, so much fuss, but is any of it for the boy? Ah, my poor child sits timidly in the corner of the babus’ dining room and eats a little rice. I can’t even serve him two good pieces of fish, or a bit of good curry, or a ladleful of milk. At that very moment, that wretch Sadu will pile everything from the babus’ kitchen onto her own son’s plate—

--- Page 249 ---
On the day of the wedding, the house was teeming with people. The groom’s party had arrived by the morning train and were staying at another house in the city. Just before evening, the groom arrived in a grand procession.

The outer courtyard was overflowing with invited guests. The entire yard was spread with thick rugs; in one corner stood the high bridal seat, wrapped in broad, gold-bordered red velvet, beneath a canopy of blue satin with golden tassels swaying gently. On either side were bolsters of brocade, and from arch to arch of the canopy hung three thick garlands of large white bel flowers. All around were chairs and couches for the groom’s party. Sprays of imported perfume and rosewater were being squirted about frequently.

Apu had not seen much of all this; he had fallen asleep. He had gone inside the house only once, when the women’s rituals were taking place, late at night. He could not find his mother anywhere—who knows what task she was busy with amidst the festival’s crowd. Not an inch of space was empty in the courtyard, packed as it was with women draped in expensive Benarasi saris. Aruna, Chotobabu’s daughter, was calling to someone, asking them to bring the big organ from the outer drawing room into the house.

Two days after the wedding, there was another great commotion for the much-anticipated amateur theater. A stage had been erected in one corner of the courtyard. It was beautifully decorated with roses and orchids. The enormous chandelier with five hundred branches had been set up on the stage. These past few days had already left Apu wide-eyed with wonder; as for today’s theater, he had no idea what it even was. Eager and curious, he took his seat at the front of the gathering from early evening, determined to secure a good spot.

Gradually, the invited gentlemen began to arrive, and lights blazed up all around. The house’s doormen, dressed in gold-embroidered uniforms, stood outside the gathering and by the doors. The stewards bustled about, overseeing the arrangements. The concert began. When it was nearly time for the curtain to rise, the house’s gomosta (estate manager), Girish Sarkar, came over, bent down, and peered at him, saying, “Who’s this?” Apu looked up, but being shy, could not speak for a moment. Without waiting for his reply, Girish Sarkar said, “Get up, get up, the babus will sit here—get up.” Girish Sarkar had recognized him by guesswork.

--- Page 250 ---
Apu looked back, his face stricken, and in the singsong cadence of reciting multiplication tables, he said—  
“I’ve been sitting here since evening, it’s all crowded behind, where should I go?...”  
But before he could finish, Girish Sarkar seized his trembling hand, gave it a rough shake, and pulled him up, saying—  
“You haven’t done anything, eh? What a cheeky brat, no sense at all, sitting right in front—this is where the babus (gentlemen) will sit, and here comes the cook’s son, plopping himself down in front! Tell him where to go—useless brat—go on, get out of here, go sit by that pillar or somewhere—”

From behind, a couple of officials called out—  
“What’s happened, what’s the matter, Girish—what’s the commotion? Who is it?”

Girish replied—  
“Look here, Managerbabu, this cheeky boy has come and sat right here among the babus, right in front—people from Chandannagar have come, there’s nowhere to seat them—I told him to get up, and now he’s arguing back!”

Managerbabu said—  
“Just give him a couple of slaps—”

Apu, shrinking into himself, eyes fixed on nothing, stunned, slipped out of the gathering. Suddenly, it seemed to him that every eye in the assembly was on him, everyone was staring at him with curiosity! At first, he thought he might bolt—make a dash to any place out of sight of all these people. Then he went and stood behind a pillar. His body was shaking, trembling with fear, humiliation, shame—a strange, discordant shiver ran through the delicate screens of his feelings.  
Steadying himself a little, he peered out from behind the pillar... but everywhere there were servants, helpers, on the upper verandah, behind the bamboo screens, women, and downstairs, maids and cooks standing and watching—the whole affair had played out before their eyes, what must they be thinking! What a mess he had made, unknowingly! He hadn’t known that was the babus’ place! Over and over, he tried to reassure himself that perhaps no one had recognized him. So many outsiders had come—who would know who he was?

--- Page 251 ---
Here is the translation, following your instructions:

---

Then the theater began. But Apu paid no attention to it. The crowd of people in front of him, the stifling air, the blaze of lights, the commotion of the doormen and servants—all of it faded from his awareness. Chhutu, the young khansama (household servant), was moving along the rows of invited guests, distributing betel leaves from a silver duck-shaped box. As Apu gazed at it, a strange sensation ran through his body. He glanced up at the enclosed upper balcony and wondered, Is Ma there? What if Ma finds out about this? But Apu’s fear was entirely unfounded—his mother was not even in that part of the house at the time, and none of this had reached her ears.

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-Three**

For the first time in her life, Sarbajaya found herself living in another’s house, utterly dependent, like a thief. Whether in happiness or in sorrow, until now she had been the sole mistress of her own humble home. The queen of a poor household—her authority there was no less effective than that of the mistress or the bou-ranis (young mistresses) of this grand mansion. But here, it was as if she had to be ever on guard, always alert, forever watching another’s face for cues, treading carefully so as not to make the slightest misstep—even the tiniest, tiniest thing!… It was becoming unbearable for her. She worked herself to exhaustion—sometimes until her mouth tasted blood—but here, her labor had no value. She toiled with all her might—yet no one would speak her name. When they did give her something, it was tossed to her with pride and disdain, as if to say, “We’re giving you this because you’ve worked,” but never as an equal. She would have to kneel and accept it.

This was becoming more and more intolerable for her. But what could she do?… Where was the opportunity to leave? Who would give her shelter? Where would she go?…

Would it always be like this? For as long as she lived? Like that old Brahmin widow, Bamni Mashi?…

The festivities of the wedding had not yet faded; today was the women’s feast. From evening onwards, the carriages of the invited ladies began arriving at the back gate. Passing through the large inner door, they would ascend the broad marble staircase to the women’s quarters on the second-floor veranda, the steps covered with a carpet embroidered with blue flowers. Gaslights illuminated the entire veranda and staircase, and at the landing to the second floor, a grand gas chandelier blazed. The two bou-ranis and the women of the house greeted everyone and sent them upstairs. The invited ladies, some with sly smiles, some sending ripples of laughter, some slow, some swift, some with a beauty and grace beyond compare, ascended the stairs in their own unique ways.

--- Page 252 ---
Apu had been standing for quite some time by one of the pillars on the lower veranda, watching. He was witnessing such a scene for the very first time in his life; on the night of the wedding, he had fallen asleep early and hadn’t seen much of anything. Of everyone, he liked Sujata, the daughter of this house, the most. She would descend the carpeted marble stairs now and then, and, looking at one or another of the guests, she would say with a bright smile—“Oh, that’s wonderful, Moni-di! You’re coming exactly at eight at night? Didn’t Bakulbagan’s boudi (elder brother’s wife) come?”

The beautiful hostess replied with a laugh—“I’ve been sitting in the car, all dressed up, since six o’clock... It’s not so easy to leave, you know, not until everything is ready... You know how it is—”

Sujata, through the opening of her sleeveless, expensive China crepe dress the color of golden kanchan flowers, encircled the guest from behind with her fair, round, flawless arms, and, resting her face affectionately on the guest’s right shoulder, began to ascend the stairs together. She chatted as they went—“Ma was saying that Bakulbagan’s boudi might go to Kolkata next month—Ma went on Wednesday, you know—has anything been decided?”

On the upper step of the stairs appeared the middle daughter-in-law, Rani. She was a little older, perhaps over thirty, of extraordinary beauty. There was no excess in her attire; the end of her pale champa-colored silk sari with a broad red border was pinned to her hair with a diamond clip, and in the light of the great chandelier on the stairs, the fine gold chain at her throat gleamed. Her figure was graceful, her manner a little slow, a little grave—yet even at this age, her complexion glowed with the luster of milk and red dye. Only a month ago, her husband had died, and a faint shadow of sorrow had lent a restrained grace to the maturity of her beauty.

As Moni-di ascended, she saw the middle boudi before her and stopped right there on the stairs—“How are you feeling these days, Mejo boudi? See, I keep meaning to come by... Yesterday, they all came from Etowa, so it went on very late...”

Apu had never imagined that people could be so beautiful. He was seeing her for the first time, for she had not been here all this while; only a few days ago, after her husband’s death, had she come from her father’s house. He gazed at her, spellbound, with wide-eyed wonder. This light, this gathering of beauties all around, the gentle, intoxicating fragrance of expensive garlands, the waves of music and laughter like the shimmering notes of a veena—Apu felt as if he were drunk on it all. What if this went on all day...?

--- Page 253 ---
Mejo Bou-rani had been watching for quite some time—there, in the corner by the stairs, stood an unfamiliar boy. She did not know everyone here—her own father was a very wealthy man, and she often stayed at her father’s house. Stepping down two stairs, she called out softly, “Khoka, come up, won’t you? Why are you standing there? Where have you come from?...”

Apu had been looking the other way, his gaze fixed on another group of strangers—suddenly, turning around, he saw that it was he whom Mejo Bou-rani was calling. At first, he was startled—as if he could not quite believe it. Then, as a wave of shyness swept over him, he hesitated—should he go up, or should he run away? At that very moment, Mejo Bou-rani herself came down—coming closer, she asked, “Where have you come from, khoka?...”

With great difficulty, after much effort, the words finally tumbled out of Apu’s mouth—“I—I—um—my mother—she lives in this house—” Instantly, a terrible fear seized him: here he was, standing in this place—just the cook’s son—what if, upon hearing this, she called someone and said, “Throw him out of here at once!”

But Mejo Bou-rani did none of those things—instead, her face filled with surprise, she said, “Your mother lives in this house?...Who is she, tell me...What does she do? How long have you been here?”

In broken, halting words, Apu gave a rambling account of himself. Perhaps Mejo Bou-rani had just recently heard something about them—she said, “Oh, so you’ve come from Kashi, have you?...What’s your name?” Looking into her beautiful, guileless eyes, perhaps she felt a sudden surge of pity. She said, “Come, why are you standing here—come upstairs—stand up there—why here?—come up—”

Like a little thief, Apu crept up behind Bou-rani, hugging the wall, and stood quietly in a corner.

Upstairs, the women were gathered in a lively assembly; the entire veranda was carpeted. Along the edges, in large glazed pots, grew rose bushes and erica palms. In the corner stood the organ from the big drawing room. After a little coaxing, one girl went and sat on a small, cushion-topped stool by the organ, pressed the keys lightly a couple of times, and then, after a moment’s silence, began to sing with a smiling face. The girl was not particularly pretty, her complexion somewhere in between, but her singing voice was truly lovely. Then another girl sang—a girl not quite as good-looking. Mejo Bou-rani’s daughter, Leela, recited a humorous poem, nodding her head as she spoke, making everyone laugh heartily. She was a very beautiful girl, as lovely as her mother. And what a sweet smile she had.

--- Page 254 ---
Apu was wondering, at this moment, why didn’t his mother come upstairs to see him just once? Where was Ma, lost in some kitchen, perhaps busy with chores—would she ever get to see all this again?

The women’s gathering was in full swing when suddenly a commotion erupted downstairs. Girish Sarkar’s voice could be heard above all others.

Sadu jhi came up the stairs, laughing, and said—“What a mess! Just look at this… hee hee… says he put it in the hookah… hee hee…”

Two or three of the invited women asked—“What happened? What is it?”

—“That hired cook, you know, one of those Brahmin boys, came from somewhere… was frying luchis… sitting in the Sarkars’ dining courtyard, frying luchis… says he just stepped out for a moment… and in the hookah… hee hee… he was sneaking away with it, stealing, more than half a seer… Gomosta-moshai caught him… Ramnihor Singh is giving him a beating… not even letting go of his hair—”

From morning, Sarbajaya hadn’t had a moment to catch her breath. The burden of frying nearly two maunds of fish was hers alone—since eight in the morning she’d been at it in the fish room. Hearing the uproar, she came out and saw that in the middle of the courtyard, among a crowd of people, a thin, dirty, twenty-five or thirty-year-old Brahmin boy in soiled clothes was being slapped and punched by two or three men—he was the hired cook, brought in for today’s work—apparently, he’d tried to steal ghee by hiding it inside a hookah. The hookah had been flung to one side, the ghee spilled across the courtyard—the knot of his dhoti had come undone in the scuffle—the man, desperate, was trying to defend himself, insisting that finding ghee inside a hookah was a perfectly ordinary, everyday occurrence, nothing suspicious or surprising at all—he was trying to explain this to the furious crowd. But before he could finish, Shambhunath Singh, the gatekeeper, shoved him so hard that he fell, gasping “baba re,” into the corner of the veranda, his head striking the pillar with a loud thud—perhaps even drawing blood.

Sarbajaya asked Kshemijhi, “What happened, Kshemimashi?… Oh, do they have to beat him like that?… A Brahmin boy…”

Kshemijhi replied, “Beat him? They’ll grind his bones to dust… this is nothing yet… they’ll hand him to the police, a jackal’s den in a tiger’s house—”

But Kshemijhi’s words trailed off in her mouth.

--- Page 255 ---
He stood there, tense and unmoving, his gaze fixed on the staircase that led upwards. Sarbajaya looked and saw: an old woman of about sixty-five or seventy was descending the stairs, beside her the mistress of the house, behind them two young daughters-in-law, and the daughters of this house, Aruna and Sujata. All the maids and servants, lined up in anxious anticipation, stood in rows on the veranda at the foot of the stairs—each craning their necks to catch a glimpse. Sarbajaya whispered softly to Kshemi, the maid, “Who is it, Kshemi-mashi?” Kshemi-mashi murmured something in reply—something about a Rani-ma from somewhere—but Sarbajaya could not quite catch the words. Yet she felt, somehow, that she had seen a face like this before, somewhere in her past.

The mistress of the house told someone to go and see whether her palanquin had arrived at the back gate. The old woman had brought two or three of her own maids with her; they followed close behind. There was an exchange of farewells and courtesies, smiles of deep humility blossomed across faces, and suddenly the maids and servants of the house fell prostrate on the ground, offering their pranam, as if for a moment they had merged with the very earth itself.

Sarbajaya thought to herself—these people are so wealthy, if they show such honor, then she must be someone truly important...

The old woman’s enormous palanquin, borne by sixteen bearers, had been waiting all this time at the back gate; now the old woman climbed into it. Her guards stood at attention before and behind the palanquin. After bidding her farewell, the mistress of the house and the other women went back upstairs.

Masima came quietly into the bread-room and whispered, “Money, dear, did you see how money is honored? Her own vast zamindari, she’s donated two lakh rupees for some college somewhere in Bengal—money is what gets respect—and here I am too... but they are her own people... no one dares to cross them!”

But Sarbajaya’s mind was elsewhere. Just now, a memory had come flooding back. Someone of much the same appearance, of much the same age—her own old Thakurjhi, Indir Thakrun, with her patched and knotted sari, eating half-rotten hog plums with rice on a broken stone, suffering such humiliation for a mere salty fruit, neglected, unacknowledged, sent away from the house at midday, cast out onto the road, and there, in utter destitution, meeting her lonely death...

Sarbajaya’s tears would not be restrained.

She did not know if the pain within a human heart could reach beyond death, yet today, over and over, she begged forgiveness in her heart, seeking to atone for the sins of her own unripe youth.

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-Four**

--- Page 256 ---
A few days later, as Apu was passing by the verandah, he saw Leela, the daughter of the second wife, coming down the upper stairs. Catching sight of him, she called out—  
“Wait, won’t you? What’s your name—Apu, isn’t it?”

Apu replied,  
“Ma calls me Apu—but my full name is Shri Apurba Kumar Ray…”

She looked a little surprised. None of the children of this house had ever called out to him or spoken to him before. Leela came closer and stood near. What a beautiful face! Ranudi, Atasidi, Amaladi—they were all pleasant to look at, to be sure, but never before had he seen anyone more beautiful than her. Ever since coming to this house, all his previous notions had been completely overturned. Especially, he had never even imagined a girl as beautiful as the second wife, Mejo Bou-Rani. And Leela was just as lovely as her mother—on that day, when Leela had recited a humorous poem in the girls’ gathering, Apu had gazed at her face the whole time, barely listening to the poem itself.

Leela said,  
“How long have you all been at our house? I didn’t see you the last time you came.”

“We came in the month of Phalgun, this Phalgun—”

“Where did you come from?”

“From Kashi. My father passed away there, you see—so—”

It was as if Apu himself could not quite believe it. The whole event still seemed unreal, impossible. Leela, Mejo Bou-Rani’s daughter Leela, was calling out to him, coming over to speak with him! His whole body tingled with happiness.

Leela said,  
“Come, let’s go sit in my study room, it’s almost time for Mastermoshai (the tutor) to arrive—come—”

Apu asked,  
“May I come?”

Leela laughed and said,  
“Oh, I’m telling you to come, aren’t I? You’re so shy, aren’t you?—Come—you haven’t seen my study room? It’s in the corner of the western verandah…”

The room wasn’t very big, but it was nicely arranged. On either side of a small stone table were two leather-cushioned chairs. There was a large calendar with pictures. A small timepiece, encased in green glass beads. A little drawer for keeping books. Four or five framed photographs on this wall and that. Leela opened a leather attaché case and said—  
“Look, these are my watercolor paints, Mastermoshai bought them for me. If I learn fractions, he’ll buy me more. Do you know how to do fractions?”

Apu said,  
“You don’t know fractions?”

“You do? You’ve done fractions?”

Apu curled his lip with a touch of disdain and said,  
“Long ago!…”

In that pose, Apu’s beautiful face looked even more striking.

--- Page 257 ---
Leela burst out laughing and said—“You really do know how to say funny things, don’t you?” Then, placing her hand beneath Apu’s lower lip, she asked—“What’s this? A mole? It looks good on your face, the mole suits you well. How old are you? Thirteen? I’m eleven—two years younger than you—”

Apu said—“That day you recited from memory, that funny rhyme—I really liked it—”

—“You know poetry?”

—“I do—Baba has a book, I learned from that—”

—“Will you say one?”

How sweet the tone of Leela’s voice was—Apu had never heard such a melody in any girl’s voice before.

Apu shook his head and began—

“The one who spends his days
Lying on straw, licking dates and sleeping away,
If you give him a bed, a mattress, a fine mosquito net,
Will he really sleep any better?”

At the end, he tilted his head in a questioning gesture. He said—“It’s a rhyme from Dashu Ray’s Panchali, I have the book—”

Leela rolled over laughing, what else. She said—“You really know such funny things? You can make me laugh like this!...”

Apu’s heart brimmed with delight at Leela’s praise. In an eager tone, he said—“Shall I say another? I know more—” Then, glancing up at the rafters, he thought for a moment, then shook his head again and began—

“The sage’s only thought is the philosopher’s stone, no other hope,
The idle man’s thoughts are cards and dice.
The rich man thinks of wealth, and the ninety-nine’s troubles,
The yogi thinks of Jagannath, the fakir thinks of Mecca,
The householder worries to keep up the four-pillared house,
The child always thinks of Ma, the beast only of its belly.”

Leela couldn’t understand the meaning of every line of this rhyme. But again, she was about to roll over laughing. She said, “Wait, I’ll write it down—”

Leela took a pen out from her attache case and said—“Say it again?”

Apu began reciting again. After a bit, he looked surprised and said—“You haven’t got any ink, how will you write?”

Leela said—“This is a fountain pen—no need for ink, it’s filled inside—don’t you know?”

Leela handed the pen to Apu. Turning it over and examining it, Apu said—“This is nice, you don’t have to dip it in ink at all!”

—“No, the ink stays inside, you just have to fill it—here, let me show you—”

—“Wow, that’s great! Let me see it once—”

Leela, smiling, handed the pen to Apu—“I’m giving it to you, just like that—”

Apu looked at Leela in astonishment. Then, with a shy face, he said—“No, I can’t take it—”

Leela asked—“Why?”

—“No—”

—“Why?”

—“No!”

Leela looked a little hurt. She said—“Won’t you take it?... I’ll get another from Baba, you take this one, let me see your hand? There!... And you can’t give it back.”

--- Page 258 ---
The whole thing felt utterly unreal to Apu. He said, “But what if someone scolds you?”

Leela replied, “For giving away a fountain pen? No one will scold me. I’ll tell Ma I gave it to Apurba—I’ll get another one from Baba. Want to see Baba’s photo?… There, hanging beside the calendar—wait, I’ll show you—”

Then Leela showed him two or three more photos. She took out a few books from the cupboard and said, “Mastermoshai (respected teacher) bought these for me. Which school do you go to?”

Apu had only attended school for a few days in Kashi, and never since. He said, “I used to study in Kashi, but not anymore—” He felt embarrassed saying it, so he said the last part in a tone as if not going to school was something to be proud of. One of the books was full of pictures. Apu asked, “Will you let me read this book, just once?”

Leela said, “Why not? I have lots more picture books, three years’ worth of bound Mukul magazines, in Ma’s cupboard—I’ll bring them for you, read as much as you want—”

Apu said, “I have books too, should I bring them?”

Leela said, “Let’s go, let’s visit your room—”

Apu felt shy about taking Leela to their own room. There was hardly any furniture, the pillowcases were torn, and a patched kantha (quilt) hung on the rack. Still, Leela came. Apu opened his tin box, took out a book, and with a smile of pride said, “This is my writing, look, my name is printed here—”

Leela quickly took it from his hand. “Let me see, let me see!”

It was the school magazine from Kashi. Harihar had never seen his son’s story in print; the magazine came out three days after his death. Leela began to read. Apu sat beside her, his face alight with joy, following Leela’s gaze along the lines and silently reading them to himself as she did. When she finished, Leela looked at Apu with eyes full of admiration and said, “It’s really good, I’ll take this and show it to Ma—”

Apu felt terribly shy. He said, “No—”

Leela didn’t listen. She kept the magazine in her hand. She asked, “It says Nishchindipur—where is Nishchindipur?”

“Nishchindipur is our village—that’s where our real home is. We’ve only been in Kashi for about a year—”

Just then, little Mokshada came to the doorway, poked her head into the room, and said, “Oh, didimoni (respected elder sister), you’re sitting here? My goodness! Masterbabu (the teacher) is sitting and waiting, I’ve searched every room up and down—who would have thought you’d be in this dark, stuffy little room—come, come—”

Leela said, “Go on, I’m coming, go—”

--- Page 259 ---
Chhoto Mokshada said—“So, is this a place to sit, then? That’s why we get headaches—do those stable boys ever sweep or wash the horse stalls? Uff, just smell that—come, didimoni, quickly—”

Leela said—“I won’t go, jaa, I’m not studying today, go on, go—who asked you to come here and chatter? Go tell Ma, go—”

Chhoto Mokshada stomped off with a rustle. Apu said—“Won’t your mother scold you? Why did you speak to her like that?”

The next afternoon, he was sleeping in his own room. Awakened by someone’s persistent nudging, he opened his eyes—Leela was sitting by his bedside, smiling. He had been sleeping on a mat spread on the floor; Leela was kneeling beside him, having shaken him awake, and was still looking at him with those playful, wide eyes. With a smile, she said—“Well, well, do you always sleep like this in the afternoon? I called from outside, but when I came in, you were fast asleep—”

Apu hastily sat up, rubbing his eyes with the edge of his cloth. He said—“Didn’t you come to study this morning? I looked all over the study room—there was no one anywhere—”

Leela handed Apu that school paper of his and said—“I read it aloud to Ma last night, she read it herself too.”

Apu’s whole body tingled with delight. He felt a deep shyness and embarrassment. The middle bou-rani (elder sister-in-law) had read his writing!

Leela said—“Come to my study room, I’ve got ‘Sakha-Sathi’ (a Bengali children’s magazine) bound and waiting for you—”

Apu glanced at the clothes rack. His good clothes were still not dry; what he was wearing now wasn’t fit for going outside. He said—“I won’t go now—”

Leela asked in a tone of surprise—“Why not?”

Apu pressed his lips together and shook his head with a mischievous smile. He didn’t know how extraordinarily beautiful his face looked in that moment.

Leela pleaded—“Come on, come on—”

Apu smiled again, stifling his laughter.

—“Oh my, what a stubborn boy you are! If you say no, there’s no way to get a yes, is there? Fine, wait, I’ll bring the book here—”

Apu couldn’t hold back his laughter any longer and burst out giggling.

Leela said—“Why are you laughing so much? What happened—come on, you have to tell me—tell me the truth—”

Apu only glanced at the clothes rack with eyes full of laughter, saying nothing.

This time Leela understood. She went over to the rack, touched the clothes and said—“They’re almost dry, you sit here, I’ll bring the book—do you write with a fountain pen? How is it, does your writing come out well?”

--- Page 260 ---
Here is the translation, following your instructions:

---

After that, for a long time, they both looked through the books Leela had brought. Spreading the books out on the mat, the two of them sat side by side, knees drawn up, leaning over the pages. Leela’s hair—soft and fine as silk—brushed against Apu’s bare arm, sending a shiver down his skin.

Suddenly, Leela lifted her face from the book and asked, “Do you know how to sing?”

Apu shook his head.

“Then sing something—”

“Do you know how?”

“A little, a little. Didn’t you hear on the wedding day?”

Just then, little Mokshada jhi (maidservant) peeked into the room and said, “Here you are, didimoni (respected elder sister). I was just thinking—she’s not upstairs, not in the study, then surely— Come now, have this milk, it’s gone cold— I’ve been searching everywhere, my hands are tired—”

A glass of milk in a small silver tumbler! Leela said, “Leave it here—come back for the glass later—”

The jhi left. They went back to looking at the pictures in the book for a while longer. At one point, Leela picked up the glass of milk and said, “You drink half—”

Apu replied in an embarrassed tone, “No.”

“I always have to coax you for everything—why are you like this? This is milk from our Multani cow—drink it—milk as thick as kheer (rice pudding), good boy—”

Apu squinted and said, “Ugh, good boy? That’s so—what’s this now?”

Leela pressed the glass to Apu’s lips, shook her head, and said, “No need to be shy—my eyes are closed, go on—”

Apu, in one gulp, drank some of the milk, then quickly lowered his face and, rubbing the milk mustache off his upper lip with the edge of his cloth, broke into a smile.

Leela took a sip from the glass and finished the rest of the milk, then she too burst out laughing, her laughter ringing like bells.

“Isn’t it sweet milk?”

“Why did you drink after me? Do you eat other people’s leftovers?”

“I wanted to—” She paused for a moment, then said, “You said you know how to paint watercolors, but you really don’t—come on, today, will you paint a few watercolors for me?”

**Pather Panchali, Chapter Thirty-Five**

It was mid-Jyaistha (a summer month in the Bengali calendar) when Sarbajaya, after much thought and worry, somehow managed to arrange for Apu’s upanayan (sacred thread ceremony). In someone else’s house, in a corner of the thakur-dalan’s (family shrine’s) veranda, the rituals were completed, all in a hush of nervousness. Bamni mashi (elderly woman, aunt-like figure) helped fry the naru (sweet coconut balls), a couple of cooks and Brahmin priests were invited, and among the respectable outsiders were Biru Gomosta and Dinu Khatanji. 

A few days after the upanayan was over, Apu was sitting in his own little room, reading the bound volume of ‘Mukul’ that Leela had given him. Through the open door, someone entered the room. Apu could hardly believe his own eyes, and then he exclaimed—“What’s this, oh!—when—”

--- Page 261 ---
Leela stood there, mischief and laughter sparkling in her eyes. Apu said, “Well! That’s just like you. You said you’d come on Monday from Kolkata—so many Mondays have passed, and still no sign of you coming back—”

Leela laughed and sat down on the floor. She said, “How could I come? I’ve been admitted to school, Baba got me admitted. Baba’s health isn’t good, so now we’ll probably stay at our house in Kolkata. I only came now because I have a few days’ holiday—came with Ma. I’ll go back again on Wednesday.”

The smile faded from Apu’s face. He asked, “So you won’t stay here anymore?”

Leela replied, “If Baba gets better, we’ll come again—”

Then, with a bright smile, she said, “Will you close your eyes for a moment?”

Apu asked, “Why?”

“Just do it, won’t you?”

Apu closed his eyes, and at once he felt the touch of something heavy in his hands. The moment he opened his eyes, Leela burst into peals of laughter. There was a cardboard box in his lap. Opening the box, Leela showed him a fine, local dhuti and chador, and a red silk panjabi. Leela said with a smile, “Ma gave these—how are they? For your poitey (sacred thread ceremony)—”

The dhuti and chador, and especially the panjabi, were precious things—not just unused, but Apu had never even seen such things before coming to this house.

Leela looked at Apu’s face and said, “Your face has changed in a month! You look even bigger—let’s see the new Brahmin’s poitey?—And did it hurt when they pierced your ear? My little cousin brother had his poitey too, and he cried—”

Suddenly, Apu pointed to a copy of ‘Mukul’ and asked, “Have you read this story?”

Leela said, “Let me see?”

Apu began to read aloud. Somewhere beneath the sea, off the coast of Spain, a treasure-laden ship had sunk two or three hundred years ago—many have searched for it till now, but no one has been able to find the spot. He had just read the story and was still full of excitement.

He said, “No one’s been able to find it—do you know how much money there is? Units, tens, hundreds, thousands, ten thousands, lakhs—five million pounds’ worth of gold and silver… Thirteen rupees to a pound—can you do the math? Then he quickly scribbled a calculation on a scrap of paper and showed her—‘Look, this much money!’ He’d done the calculation before, too. With a shining face he said, ‘When I grow up, I’ll go—I’ll see for myself—I’ll definitely find it, you’ll see—no one’s discovered it yet—’”

Leela, doubtful, said, “You’ll go? How will you find the place?”

“Look, it says here, ‘in the depths of the sea near Porto Plata’—I’ll search and find it…”

--- Page 262 ---
He had read that story and thought to himself, It’s just as well—no one has managed to uncover it. If everyone took everything out, what would be left for him? What would he have to discover when he grew up? Let it stay there until he goes—let it wait for him!...

Leela, though young, was very clever. Thinking it over, she said—Where would you get a ship like theirs? You need your own ship—just like theirs—

—That’ll happen, I’ll buy one. Won’t I have money when I’m grown up?

This time, it seemed Leela believed him quite a bit. She didn’t argue about it anymore.

After a while, she asked—Have you been to Kolkata?

Apu shook his head—No, I’ve never seen it—Is it a very big city?—Bigger than this?

Leela laughed—Much, much bigger—

—Bigger than Kashi?

—I haven’t seen Kashi—

Then she brought Apu into her study room. Showing him a notebook, she said—Look, see what kind of flower plant I’ve drawn, how’s the drawing?

After a moment, Apu said—I want to lie down, my head hurts a lot—

Leela said—Wait, I know a mantra to cure headaches—shall I try? Then, pressing her fingers on his forehead in such a way that Apu burst out laughing—Oof! That tickles so much.

Leela laughed—My eldest cousin brother is learning wrestling from a pehlwan (wrestling master), I learned this from him—Isn’t it good? All better now?

A few days later, Leela’s family left for Kolkata again.

Apu, after persuading his mother, started going to a small school. The big road ran by their house, and from there, a little way off, down a narrow lane to the left, stood a one-story house—the school. Five or so teachers, broken benches, chairs with splintered arms, a blackboard stained with old ink, a few battered maps—these were the school’s furnishings. Right in front of the school was an open drain; from Apu’s classroom window, if you looked out, you’d see the next house’s unfinished wall of bare, lime-streaked bricks. On his way to school, he’d see laborers cleaning the drains, piles of garbage gathered here and there. All day, the school was filled with a kind of stale, closed-in smell; after noon, a Hindustani bhuja-wala (street vendor selling roasted snacks) next door would fire up his coal stove, and the sweet, raw scent of burning coal would drift in. It muddled Apu’s head, and even after leaving school, it seemed to cling to him, refusing to let go.

He didn’t like it, not at all; he felt stifled by all these brick-and-cement contraptions of the city, as if he couldn’t breathe. Something was missing, his heart ached and fluttered with longing, but he couldn’t quite tell what it was he lacked.

--- Page 263 ---
There is little grass along the path, hardly any trees or greenery—just one or two here and there. The road is surfaced with red gravel, a proper drain runs alongside, and in the gap between two houses lies a heap of rubbish: dirty water, torn cloth, scraps of paper. One day, he had gone to a classmate’s house. It was a single-storey, open room. Around the cramped courtyard, each room was rented out to a different family. An old jute curtain hung at the doorway. The floor of the room was barely a hand’s span higher than the courtyard, so the dampness never left. Inside, there was no trace of light or air. The courtyard itself was filthy and unpleasant, every family had stoked their coal fires at once, and yet, amidst it all, that faint, sweet smell lingered. Altogether, Apu felt terribly uncomfortable, as if his heart had shrunk to a tiny, tight knot. Even though they had asked him to sit, he couldn’t stay long. Only when he returned to the main road did he feel some measure of relief.

But even at home, that sense of suffocation remained—if anything, it was worse. Here, everything was bound by brick, cement, and marble, even the courtyard was paved. Apu could not bear it if he could not see earth; and the little soil that was here seemed somehow different, not the earth he knew. Besides, where was the freedom to move about in their own home? They had to live in fear, like thieves—always anxious about what someone might say, unable to raise their voices, always afraid.

Sometimes, Apu would go to the office room and see the old khata-nji (the ledger-keeper) sitting in a cage-like iron-grilled enclosure, shrouded in darkness. Piles of straw-bound account books were stacked to one side. With a small wooden cash box in front of him, the old man would sit all day, leaning against a grimy, oil-stained cushion. The room was so dark that even in broad daylight, a tiny kerosene lamp sometimes burned. Girish Gomosta (the clerk) sat in the accounts office. On a low wooden cot, covered with a dirty sheet, lay heaps of office files tied up with cloth in every corner. That room was not as dark as the ledger-keeper’s, with two or three large windows, but beneath the cot were piles of tobacco, torn papers, and on the rafters, cobwebs and soot from the kerosene lamp. When Biru Muhuri (the scribe) would call out, “Hey, Ramdayal, can you check how much was spent on the musicians in last year’s accounts?”—at that very moment, for some reason, a deep disgust would well up in Apu’s heart once again.

--- Page 264 ---
Morning. Apu came near the gateway and saw the boys of the house pushing the chair-cart, playing with it. The cart was newly made—a sturdy iron chair with handles, a leather cushion on top, big wheels—gleaming and handsome to look at. He stood close by, watching, when Romen said, “Hey, come help us push for a bit—”

Ever since the cart arrived, Apu had secretly coveted it, and now, delighted, he said, “I’ll push! But will you let me ride it once?”

Romen replied, “Alright, you will. Now push—give it a good, strong shove—”

After quite a while of playing, Romen suddenly said, “Okay, that’s enough for now—let’s stop—”

When he saw everyone leaving with the cart, Apu asked, “Won’t I get a turn to ride?”

Romen said, “Alright, alright, not this time. If you ride too much, it’ll break—maybe later—”

Frustration welled up in Apu’s eyes as tears. All this time, he had pushed them with all his might, hoping for his turn.

He said, “But you promised you’d let me ride when it was my turn! I pushed everyone—fine! The other day too, you didn’t let me ride in the end—”

Romen said, “Why did you push then? If you didn’t want to, you could’ve refused—who told you you’d get to ride? You think carts come free?”

Apu replied, “Why, you said so, and Santu too—my hands are sore from pushing—and I don’t even get a single turn—fine, you—”

Romen, getting heated, said, “I didn’t say anything—”

Santu said, “Foo-rrr—look at him sulk!”

Suddenly, with no warning, Tabu, the elder sahib’s son, came up behind Apu, put his arm around his neck, and started shoving him, saying, “Go on, go on—we won’t let you ride if we don’t want to—go back to your own house—why do you come here to play?”

Perhaps because Tabu was younger than Apu, or perhaps because of everyone’s mockery and taunts, Apu’s head began to spin with confusion and humiliation—he jerked his neck free and gave Tabu a shove. Tabu spun around and fell against the wall—his forehead struck the wall, splitting open and bleeding, and at once Tabu let out a piercing scream and burst into tears.

The maids and servants came running, the cook and the doorman rushed over—upstairs, in the sitting room, Barobabu (the elder master) had been holding court in the morning; now he came down with his entire retinue. Water from ten directions—buckets, cloths, commotion everywhere!

When the chaos subsided a little, Barobabu said, “Well, who hit him? Let’s see.”

Ramnihor Singh, the doorman, pushed Apu forward and stood him before Barobabu.

Barobabu said, “Who’s this? Isn’t he the son of that Kashibabu Brahmin lady?”

--- Page 265 ---
Girish Sarkar stepped forward and said––Such a naughty boy––always up to some mischief, sir, if you only knew! That day at the theater, he sat himself right in front, in the seats meant for the gentlemen, and when I asked him to move, he started arguing with me face to face! And just the other day, I saw him at the corner by the Seths’ house, strutting down the road in a red panjabi, munching on bird’s eye chilies––already so full of himself at this age––

Barababu (the head of the household) said to Romen––Didn’t your master come this morning? Wasn’t there any studying to do? Hey, someone bring me my cane, will you? Who told you to go play with him?

Romen, on the verge of tears, said––He’s the one who comes to us during our playtime, why should we go to him? Ask Santu instead––he’s the one who wants to look at the pictures in your English magazines––he even sneaks into the big drawing room and fiddles with this and that––

Girish Sarkar said––Just look at that, what a fancy he has––

Now it was Apu’s turn. Barababu said––Come here, step forward––why did you hit the table?

Apu’s heart had already flown away in fear. True, he had shoved the table in a fit of anger, but he hadn’t expected such a commotion. With a stiff tongue, he managed to stammer out––The table, it––it hit me first––I––

Barababu cut him off before he could finish––Do you know how old the table is, and how old you are?

If Apu had been able to gather his words, he could have said that even if the table was a little younger, in experience it was more seasoned than even his Jethamoshai (paternal uncle) Nilmoni Ray. He could have said that the table, like all the boys in this house, calls him “Bangali” for no reason, teases him, shows him the crane, pecks him on the head from behind––so if he tries to play a little, is that such a crime? But in front of the courtyard full of curious onlookers, especially in front of Barababu, his tongue stuck to the roof of his mouth––he could only say––The table––me––for no reason––it came and––

Barababu thundered––Stupid, insolent boy––who told you to come here and mix with them––Here, give me the cane––Step forward––come––

--- Page 266 ---
With a sharp swish, the cane landed hard across his back. At that very instant, he looked up in astonishment—his wide, bewildered eyes darting from the stern face of Borobabu to the cane, raised once more in his hand. Never before in his life had he been beaten—not even by his own father. His confused mind seemed unable, at first, to accept the reality of being struck. Then, almost without realizing it, his hands rose to shield himself from the cane. But now, there was no room left for sorrow—his turn had come, and he had fallen into the gap of fate. The swish and slap of the cane rang out so sharply that even Tebul, forgetting the ache in his own forehead, turned to watch. Borobabu made sure the cook’s son would never again dare to be insolent—he gave him a thorough lesson. Any other cane might have snapped, but this one must have been very expensive.

Catching his breath, Borobabu declared, “You old, insolent, good-for-nothing boy, I’m warning you today—if I ever hear you’ve been mixing with any of the boys in this house again, I’ll grab you by the ear and throw you out at once!” Then, turning to someone else, he said, “Just look, Dhirenbabu—widowed mother, Satishbabu brought her from Kashi to be the manager’s cook, I thought, let a woman of our own caste stay here. But look at this! The mother cooks rice, and he—he struts around in a Panjabi jacket, smoking cigarettes—”

Dhirenbabu replied, “That’s always how it goes. Next, he’ll be taking cocaine—breaking into his mother’s trunk—that’s just his way. And on top of it all, a boy from Kashi—”

Not every word spoken in the house reached every ear, but the news of Apu being beaten did reach Sarbajaya. She heard it all too clearly. The mistress of the house remarked, “If he’s going to be such a ruffian, then, my dear—” and so on.

Coming from the kitchen, she saw that Apu had gone to school, without saying a word to her. He never told her such things. Rage, sorrow, and indignation made Sarbajaya’s whole body tremble, as if a burning heat was rising from every limb. Unable to stay inside, she stepped out onto the narrow, cramped verandah.

To lay a hand on her Apu! Even now, he would say, “Ma, when you come from the kitchen through the stairs at night, one day I’ll scare you so badly…” Did he have any sense at all? How much had it hurt? Who had comforted him there? Who had heard his cries?

Sarbajaya’s heart broke, and tears came.

A dark night. A few stars flickered in the sky. The wind rustled through the branches of the amalki tree above the stable. Sitting beside the iron-hooped water tank at the corner of the house, her whole body shook with the force of her sobs as she turned the matter over and over in her mind…

--- Page 267 ---
––Thakur, Thakur, oh my most beloved treasure, you know that if I lose sight of him for even a moment, I cannot remain still. Whatever punishment you wish to give, let it fall upon me, Thakur, but say nothing to him—I cannot bear it, Thakur, my heart would break, I could not endure it––

Early in the morning, Apu’s school was let out. The boys from his class insisted that Apu must be the referee for their football match. Apu was overjoyed; before coming to this town, he had never even seen a football game, nor could he play very well. Yet, somehow, the boys in his class liked him best of all—they often called on him to be the referee in their games.

He said, “Let me bring that big whistle from home, brothers, it’s lying in the box. I’ll be at the field exactly at four o’clock—now—”

As he walked along the road, Apu remembered what had happened that morning. He had thought about it all day. It was true that he had run into Girish Sarkar while eating bird’s eye (a kind of sweet), but does he eat bird’s eye every day? That day, wearing the red panjabi (a traditional tunic) given by Mejo Bourani (second eldest sister-in-law), he had suddenly felt a fancy on his way home from school—gentlemen eat bird’s eye wearing such panjabis, he too would try it once. So, with the money meant for food, he bought the bird’s eye and ate it in secret. But even that one time, sneaking a taste in Nishchindipur, he hadn’t liked it much, and today was no different. He thought to himself—Bah! If only I’d bought a paisa’s worth of chola-bhaja (spiced roasted chickpeas) instead! Why do people buy and eat this? But why did Girish Sarkar scold him so harshly, without knowing anything?

Thank goodness Leela isn’t here. If she had seen, it would have been so embarrassing. Ma probably didn’t notice either. That’s why he’d hurried off to school that afternoon, lest Ma find out!

How long it’s been since Leela last came here! It’s been a year, and she hasn’t come again. Even if she did come now, would they let them talk together as before?

As he returned home, he heard, near the entrance hall, that a gramophone was playing upstairs in the sitting room. The moment the sound reached his ears, he stood on the road under the window, his face lifted, eyes bright with delight and curiosity. From the street, the words of the song weren’t clear, but the melody was enchanting. Listening to it, thoughts of school, games, refereeing, even the scolding he’d received earlier—all of it faded completely from his mind.

--- Page 268 ---
His heart, carried on the wings of song, would drift away on its own—at such moments, wandering along the riverbanks of Nishchindipur, how many times had he seen, across the way, the field of reeds, the young shimul trees ablaze with little red flowers, and behind them, far away, the blue canvas of the sky—the straw fields seemed painted, the red-flowered shimul saplings seemed painted, even the bird perched on the dry branch—everything seemed as if drawn by an artist’s hand. And behind them all, that land, that far, faraway land—he did not know its name, it only revealed itself in the joy of his heart.

Someone calls—who knows from how far away—an exuberant, joy-filled, familiar melody rings out—Apu—u—u—u—u—

His heart, brimming with happiness, calls back—Ja—a—ai—i—i—

Returning to their little house, his mother asked, “You’re home so early this morning?”

He replied, “The upper class boys won the ball game, so it was a half-day at school.”

His mother said, “Come, sit here.” After a while, as she gently stroked his back, she hesitated a little and asked, “Did they call you away today for something—did they scold you?”

“No, just a little accident with that table, so the headmaster called me to ask what happened—that’s all.”

“They didn’t scold you or anything?”

“No—”
