His mother was silent for a while, then said, “I’ve been thinking—what if we left this place?”

He looked at his mother in surprise. Then, suddenly, a smile broke across his face and he exclaimed, “Where, Ma, to Nishchindipur? That would be wonderful, let’s go! I’ll do the Durga puja there—the sacred thread ceremony is done now—it’s our own land, it’ll be good—let’s not stay here anymore—”

Sarbajaya said, “I’ve been thinking about that for two years now. You say you want to go there, but tell me, what’s left there? One house, and for three years now the monsoon waters have been eating away at it—what’s left of it after all this time? An old house from the days of Mandhata—there was a little paddy field, even that’s gone—there’s not even a place to lay our heads—should we go just to make our enemies laugh at us…” She fell silent for a while, then said, “Let’s do one thing, why not—shall we go to Kashi instead?”

Nothing was decided. His mother still hadn’t eaten. After her bath, she went back to the kitchen. A thought came to Apu. He had a singing voice, didi used to say so, and the friend from the jatra troupe had said so too. If he joined a jatra (folk theater) troupe, wouldn’t they take him? Here, Ma suffers so much. He would take her away from here!

--- Page 269 ---
Uff, how hot it is! Smoke coils up from the mouth of the kitchen pipe, sunlight clings to the cornice... Already, the inside of the house is dark... In the stables, the syces are making a racket, speaking in their Hindi tongue... The clatter of horses’ hooves on the stone-paved floor... That smell from the drain... His head aches so much it feels as if it might split open and fall off. He thought... Let me lie down for a bit now, then I’ll get up and go to the playing field—it's only three o’clock—right now the sun is blazing.

Lying in bed, a single thought kept coming back to him again and again. He had never looked at it this way before. All these days, it was as if, in some corner of his mind, there was always a clear certainty that, at the end of all this, their village was waiting for them—for them alone! Even though, when they left, there was never any talk of returning—he knows this, yet the enchantment has never quite left him.

But today, in everything that happened, especially in Ma’s and Borobabu’s words, the sense of their homelessness, their lack of shelter, became painfully clear to him. Will he never be able to return to their village?—never?—never?

This foreign land, this Girish Sarkar, this life of being a thief—if not that, then Ma and her son wandering hand in hand, rootless, forever—are these what are to remain?

In the stables, the two syces have started quarreling, on the kitchen roof a flock of crows gathers, greedy for rice—after a while, he feels he’s been thinking the same thought for a long time, the same thought. The clatter of horses’ hooves from the stables does not cease... It’s as if he’s sinking somewhere deep into the earth... deeper and deeper into the earth... someone below is pulling him down... such comfort... no more headache, such comfort...

Uff—how the sun is blazing! Didi and her antics—picnic in this scorching heat! He says—Didi, lie down, why have a picnic in this blazing sun?

Ranudi sits by his ear, whispering all sorts of things, so many things. Ranudi’s large, glistening eyes are full of hurt! What can he do? They can’t manage in Nishchindipur, can they? Ranudi or Leela?

Haran Kaka has come selling bamboo flutes, playing them as he goes... he plays so beautifully! He tells Baba—Baba, I want to buy a one-paisa bamboo flute, will you give me a paisa?...

His father, tucking his long hair behind his ears with affection, says—Your story has turned out well, when it’s printed, show it to me, Khoka?

He asks—Baba, what is cocaine? Girish Sarkar said I’m going to eat cocaine—

--- Page 270 ---
Around Baba’s neck hangs a garland of lotus seeds. Just like those old storytellers, the kathak-thakurs.

They are at the Majherpara station. On the big wooden plank, the name is written out: Ma-jhe-r––Pa-ra. He walks ahead, the heavy bundle slung across his back, Ma follows behind. He wears his red panjabi, the color vivid against the dusk. Their shadows stretch along the path. In the sky, the evening star has appeared. The air is thick with the scent of ripe banyan fruit.

The road to Nishchindipur seems endless… He walks on… and on… and on… He and Ma. Never before has he come this way alone; the path is unfamiliar to him… “Hey, kaka, with the sickle in your hand, do you hear me? Could you tell us the way to Nishchindipur? Yashra-Nishchindipur, across the Betrobati river?”

His Ma enters the house and calls out—“Get up, O Apu, there’s no time left, you said you’d go out to play—get up, get up!”

At his mother’s call, he starts awake, sits up on the bed, and looks around—Oh! How late it’s gotten! The sun has gone far away, vanished from the sky. His Ma says—“You said you’d go out to play, so where did you go? How soundly you’ve slept in the middle of the day! Shall I get your flute out for you?”

She takes the flute out from the trunk and lays it at the corner of the bed, but he shows no eagerness to go out and referee the game. Already, the room is filling with darkness! He gets up and stands absentmindedly by the window, gazing outside. There is no daylight left. An unbearable, stifling heat presses in. The smell from the stable’s drain seems even stronger now. From the clock by the gate, the metallic clang—perhaps it’s striking six.

Beyond the stable roof, the sky stretches, and far, far to the east, beyond that, lies their Nishchindipur.

How many days has it been since he last saw Nishchindipur—three years! How long it’s been!

He knows Nishchindipur calls to him, day and night, always. The Shankharipukur pond calls, the bamboo grove calls, the fields of Sonadanga call, the ghat at Kadamtala where the sahib’s house stands calls, Devi Bishalakshi calls.

When will he again wander in the dappled shade beneath the drumstick trees, breathing in the scent of sweet lemon blossoms from the ruined homestead? When again will he hear the birds calling from the shirish and sodali woods near their house?

By now, the monsoon floods must have come to their Ichhamati. Water must have risen under the shimul tree along the ghat path. In every thicket, the wild natas and kantas, the forest morning glories have begun to bloom. The wild aparajita’s blue flowers cover the forest’s crown.

--- Page 271 ---
Perhaps by now, at the village ghat beside the kuch-joop thicket, Raju-kaka has descended for his habitual, untimely bath; at the bend of the chaltapotar, along the edge of the new kashar forest, Atrur-majhi has set his fishing traps; today is market day there, and behind the banyan tree by Thakurjhi-pond, the sun is setting at the horizon’s edge like a foam of crimson fire, while along the earthen path beneath that glow, the village boys—Patu, Neelu, Tinu, Bhola—are all returning from the market.

By now, deep shade is gathering in the courtyard of their house ringed by forest, birds are chirping and calling, and that sweet, silent, tranquil evening descends—still, that yellow bird comes and sits just so on the twig above the wall, and perhaps by now, the lemon tree mother planted has borne its fruit...

A little while later, darkness will fall over their homestead at evening, but in that dusk, no one will light the lamps, no one will show the flame, no one will tell fairy tales. In the deserted courtyard, the jungle of kalmegh will be alive with the calls of crickets, and deep in the night, from the dense woods behind, the cry of the Lakshmi owl will echo from the jagdumur tree... No one will ever tread that way again, no one will ever know of the lemon tree, buried deep in the wild, that mother once planted, the or-kalmi flowers will bloom and fall on their own, the kul-nona fruit will ripen in vain, and the yellow-winged, restless bird will wander, crying, crying.

Those wondrous, enchanted evenings by the forest’s edge will descend forever in vain.

That day, even after being beaten without cause before the whole courtyard of people, not a single tear had come from his eyes, but now, standing alone at the window of a silent room, suddenly he wept, overcome, tears streaming down and flooding his beautiful cheeks, and as he raised his hand to wipe them away, he cried out in his heart, in a voice full of longing—Let us return to Nishchindipur—O God—let it be so, let us truly go back to Nishchindipur—else I cannot live—at your feet I beg—

The god of the road smiles gently and says—Foolish child, do you think my path ends at the bamboo grove of your village, beneath the banyan tree of thyangare Biru Ray, or at the ferry ghat of Dhalachit? Beyond your Sonadanga fields, across the Ichhamati, skirting the Madhukhali marsh filled with lotus blooms, crossing the ferry at Betrabati, my road goes on, on, always on... Leaving the homeland for foreign lands, from sunrise to sunset, beyond the bounds of knowing, toward the unknown...

--- Page 272 ---
Days and nights pass by, birth and death pass by, months, years, epochs, and great ages slip away…  
Your murmuring, dreamlike lives fill with the mossy umbrellas of memory, yet my path does not end…  
It goes on… on… on… ever onward…

Anirban listens to his veena, hearing only the music of endless time and the infinite sky…

It was by placing the invisible tilak—the sacred mark—of that wondrous journey’s joy upon your forehead that I sent you away from home!…

Come, let us move forward.
